# Fetch, cache and store: per-cell packs, tsfuse and a two-tier storage cluster (replicated NVMe hot tier, erasure-coded HDD cold tier)

Status: **proposed, for the owner's review (revision 9); not built, and nothing in it was run. The implementation of the store described here is paused (owner, 2026-11-18): SeaweedFS is trialled as the store and the agents work on the client side; this document is kept as the complete design of our own store, on paper (10.17, section 18).** This document is the design of the farm's read path and of the store behind it: how a build node fetches the packs a tree names, keeps them in its cache, serves them to jobs through a FUSE mount (an NFS front on macOS), and where the packs live when no build node has them. Target hardware (owner, 2026-11-18): **31 build nodes**, each with a local SSD cache running the tierstore cache and tierstore-fs, and **3 storage nodes**, each with one 6.4 TB U.2 NVMe and fifteen 6 TB SAS hard drives (45 hard drives in all). The storage nodes run tierstore's own store: a small, replicated **hot tier** on the NVMe and a large **cold tier** of erasure-coded 1 GiB contiguous blocks on the hard drives, redundancy of each tier set by configuration. The design has tierstore own this store end to end, with no silo or SeaweedFS underneath it; while the trial of SeaweedFS lasts, a SeaweedFS cluster stands where this store would (10.17). Its block-level truth (which blocks and stripes exist, where their copies are, which keys are deleted, the current version of each key of a mutable bucket) lives in a dedicated, replicated Postgres cluster on the storage nodes themselves (10.16), while the bytes and the per-node key index stay on the nodes' own drives; it is a general-purpose object store, whose largest user is the CAS service. It replaces the erasure-coded store "silo" (section 10). It is written as the system reads once built; each task of section 18 makes part of it true and states so in its pull request. What it says about today was read in the code of `main` and measured on the farm before this revision. It does not change [tierstore-design.md](tierstore-design.md) (the node cache, the entry protocol) or [put-without-lock.md](put-without-lock.md); it replaces the fetch and prefetch parts of [prefetch-api.md](prefetch-api.md) and [node-local-trees.md](node-local-trees.md) and the per-blob serving of [virtual-build-fs.md](virtual-build-fs.md) when the tasks of section 18 land (section 13 lists the code it deletes).

**What changed from revision 8** (the revision merged before this one). The owner's decisions of 2026-11-18, each written into the sections named (the last bullet is the pause of the store's implementation); everything else of revision 8 stands, and the data path (hot segments, records, trailers, the replication stream and its ack rule, group commit, seal and roll, destage, reclaim, promote, the drive scheduler, `LOCATE` and the client read path, budgets, hedging, the definitive-miss rule) is unchanged:
- **A Postgres metadata store across the storage nodes (10.16, 10.2, 10.3, 10.5, 24).** Block-level truth lives in one dedicated Postgres cluster of three members (a primary, one synchronous standby and one more standby) on the storage nodes, never on a machine outside the storage tier and not on the CAS service's or the job scheduler's Postgres (the CAS service runs on tierstore, which would be a cycle, and a shared instance would share blast radius). LATER: on the three storage nodes, each on its own partition of the NVMe; NOW: on three of the six combined nodes, each on its own partition of the SATA SSD. Failover is Patroni over a three-member etcd on the same nodes, with a target of at most 30 s; point-in-time backups go to an off-box target that is not tierstore. Schema, client, failover, backups, upgrades and the yearly major-upgrade runbook are in 10.16.
- **Seal by a segment-epoch fence and one compare-and-set (10.3).** Sealing a segment fences its copies at a higher epoch, chooses the sealed length by the rule of revision 8, and commits it with one conditional update of the segment's row; exactly one sealer wins. Replicas reject batches of a lower epoch. When Postgres is unavailable the fence still happens and the writer opens a new segment, so immutable-bucket PUTs continue.
- **Transactions for descriptor, free and repair (10.5, 24).** A stripe descriptor, a stripe free, a relocate, a tombstone and every repair result are single Postgres transactions conditioned on row versions; a stripe is freed only when no block of it has a live record, checked in the same statement.
- **Buckets with immutable and mutable modes, and general S3 semantics (10.9, 10.2).** The store is general-purpose: correctness never depends on content-derived keys. A mutable bucket has ordinary S3 semantics (overwrite, DELETE, prefix LIST, `If-Match`, `If-None-Match`), ordered by Postgres transactions on a `heads` table. An immutable bucket (the CAS service's) keeps revision 8's all-node create-only path with no Postgres on PUT or GET, and states its create race and its bounded staleness after DELETE honestly. Multipart upload, object versioning, lifecycle rules, ACLs and a list of other features are out of scope and answer `501`, each with its reason.
- **The reconciler (24.5).** One loop, active on the node that holds a database-clock lease, compares desired with actual redundancy and maintains a queue of repair jobs in Postgres, margin-first, with the throttle, flap damper, flap cap, adopt-on-return and refusal reasons of revision 8, and executors that commit their result as one transaction. The runbooks R1 to R16 (R11 to R16 are the metadata-store runbooks) are its policies plus the operator verbs.
- **New runbooks (24.12).** R11 metadata primary lost (automatic failover), R12 metadata replica re-init, R13 WAL partition full or stuck replication slot, R14 point-in-time restore and `metadata fsck`, R15 yearly Postgres major upgrade, R16 power event with Postgres and etcd cold start order.
- **Merged code of the retired design (18, T-I2).** The merged `tierstore-format` files `hlc.rs`, `stamp.rs`, `tomb.rs` and `seal.rs` and the descriptor state-file code (`seals/`, `stripes/*.desc`; `descriptor.rs` is edited, not deleted: it keeps the stripe-descriptor types the shard footer carries, `StripeDescriptor`, `Code`, `PlacedShard` and `ShardLoc`, and loses only the state-file code), the routes `SEAL`, `DESCRIPTOR`, `TOMB` and `INDEX_SYNC` of `tierstore-store-api` with the fake node's handlers for them, and their tests are retired by T-I2 (the harness seal model is rewritten by the T10a follow-up with T-S4, and the fake node and harness tests by the T10a follow-up with T-S6, which only rewrite them to `fence` and `tomb-apply`), not by T-S2, T-S3 or T-S6, which only build on the result. `free.rs` is edited in place by T-I2, not retired: the stripe-free record loses its stamp and owner and its majority commit, and keeps its other fields.
- **Metadata incarnation, rollback detection, freeze and `metadata fsck` (10.16).** A point-in-time restore or a promoted lagging standby could roll the metadata back and make live data look unreferenced. Every transaction checks the cluster incarnation and returns a transaction sequence; a node that sees an incarnation mismatch, a sequence that went backwards or a frozen flag freezes every destructive action. `tierstore store metadata fsck` rebuilds the rows from the nodes' on-disk facts and then raises the incarnation.
- **Power-loss protection on LATER, honest fsync on NOW (10.3, 10.16).** The owner states that the LATER NVMe drives have power-loss protection; group commit, `hot.min_acks`, `batch_max` and Postgres's default `synchronous_commit = on` are sized on that, verified by T0-S and the hardware audit (a drive found without it is refused, never silently retuned). NOW (SATA SSD, no PLP) keeps `fdatasync` and `fsync` everywhere and accepts millisecond commit latency; nothing turns fsync off.
- **Clients tolerate about 30 s of `503` (23, 10.9).** Every client of the S3 front or the peer protocol treats `503` with `Retry-After` as retriable, retries for at least 60 s with jittered backoff, never treats `503` as absence, and retries idempotently. `tierstore-client` does this itself; the CAS service's change is its own task outside this repository.
- **Tasks (18).** Changed: T-S0, T-S1, T-S2, T-S3, T-S4, T-S5, T-S6, T-S7, T-S9a and T-S9b, T-S10, T-S12, T-S14, T-S15, T-S17 to T-S19, T-S20 and T-S21. Deleted: T-S13 (health ledger and membership agreement, replaced by T-S22) and T-S16 (repair scheduler, replaced by T-S25). New: T-S22 (metadata schema and client), T-S23 (Postgres deployment and failover), T-S24 (`metadata fsck`, incarnation and the freeze), T-S25 (the reconciler).
- **What was deleted (10.12 keeps a short "why not").** Single-decree seal ballots and durable acceptors; majority-committed descriptor and stripe-free records; health-ledger facts and voter sets; the hybrid logical clock and "newest stamp wins"; per-origin tombstone logs, pull cursors, `tomb-drop` reports and the one drop rule; `prior_blocks` lists on index entries; the replicated block table as an authority (it is a cache of Postgres rows); the operator-edited membership file as the authority (it only seeds `store init`); the peer routes `POST /store/v1/seal`, `POST /store/v1/descriptor`, `GET /store/v1/tomb` and `POST /store/v1/index-sync`; the `tomb/`, `seals/` and `stripes/*.desc` files on the NVMe; and the cluster stamp carried in `x-ts`. The hot-only block-drop record is a Postgres row.
- **The store's implementation is paused; SeaweedFS is trialled as the store (10.17, 10.12, 18).** The owner decided on 2026-11-18 that the agents now work on the client side and SeaweedFS is trialled as the store; the implementation of our own store waits. This revision stays the complete design of that store, on paper, and adds 10.17 (what SeaweedFS 4.54 does, read in its source: the 10+4 default and the absent ratio flag, uniform contiguous EC blocks, 5/5/4 placement on three servers and `-force` below four nodes, whole-volume operator-run tiering with no cache or promotion, 4 MiB filer chunking, the whole-needle CRC of a ranged read, whole-needle EC reads) and reconciles 10.12 with it. Section 18 does not dispatch the storage-tier tasks; they remain the plan of record, and no task of the store is in progress.

**Revision 8's changes from revision 7** (the revision before revision 9; the interface freeze T-I, komira-ai/tierstore#352, was cut from revision 7). History: where revision 9 replaced a mechanism, the bullet says so. The owner's decisions of 2026-11-18, each written into the sections named; everything else of revision 7 stands:
- **Components, not roles (10.1, 10.14, 21).** A node runs a set of independently configured components: hot store, cold store, peer service, S3 front, node cache, fetch engine, FUSE front, NFS front and Prepare provider. Each has its own configuration section and declared dependencies, and any combination whose dependencies are met is valid. "Storage node", "build node" and "combined node" are names of example configurations in `docs/examples/` (the NOW and LATER profiles of section 21), never an enum, a configuration key or a code branch.
- **The client is a set of libraries; the daemon is a thin host (section 23).** Three layered crates, each usable alone with a stable, documented, versioned API: `tierstore-client` (membership, `LOCATE`, ranged part fetch, per-cell verification, budget), `tierstore-cache` (the local cache: entry store, single flight and claims, holds, eviction; in-process, or shared across processes through the state page and `flock` of 5.1) and `tierstore-tree` (open an L2 tree list and read files by path, from the cache or straight from the client, with no FUSE). The daemon hosts the FUSE and NFS fronts, a node-wide shared cache, the S3 front and the Prepare provider on exactly these libraries. Section 18 maps the client-side tasks onto the crates and adds a library-mode test suite (T-L).
- **Prepare's order without learned input (6.1).** Prepare fetches shared (toolchain) packs first, then in tree order, under the whole-pack rule; runtime-learned access profiles are not part of the design. eStargz and Nydus are cited as prior art (both order at build time), and a publish-time order hint written by the build system inside the L2 list is named as a later option, taken only if T10b's time to first read shows that order matters. Task T-K1c (the CAS service marks shared packs) is added.
- **Two layers of reads (4.0).** FUSE reads of at most 1 MiB are served from the build node's local cache; hard-drive reads are fetch parts of 16 to 64 MiB or whole packs, from a storage drive into that cache. The two are separate layers and never meet.
- **No client pings; load travels with requests (4.3, 4.6, 10.6, 10.8).** Clients learn nothing in the background. Storage nodes share their queue depths on the peer traffic they already exchange; `LOCATE` answers the key's copies ordered by load with a recommended one; tail latency is handled by budgeted hedging at about the p95; a client contacts nodes only in `rank(K)` order (past the key's replica nodes only to decide a miss), and a co-located client looks up its own index in-process.
- **Operations and repair (section 24).** The block is the placement group and `rank(K)` only a routing hint (10.2, 24.1); a health ledger of single-decree majority-committed facts decides `lost`, `returned`, `maintenance` and `flapping` (superseded in revision 9: the facts are rows of a Postgres `health` table written by single transactions, no voter sets); two clocks and a flap damper with a floor; a soft-state repair scheduler ordered by remaining margin (superseded in revision 9 by the reconciler, 24.5, which keeps its policies), triggered by margin at once and independent of the `lost` timer, with one throttle shared with scrub; degraded placement recorded with a debt counter and repaid by adopting the returning copy; capacity adds never move data; ten audited invariants; operator verbs generated from the API with dry-run and safety refusals; runbooks R1 to R10 (R10: two nodes rebooting together); a deterministic cluster simulator. Every blocker of the adversarial review is resolved (24.14). The prioritized operations additions (24.15) follow: the capacity invariant after losing the largest domain with PUT admission and `days_to_full`, one narrow guard on four destructive primitives with trash before delete, verified reads on every path with an independent canary prober, an off-box copy of the ledger and descriptors with a cold-start drill (superseded in revision 9 by pgBackRest backups of the metadata store with a restore drill, 10.16), a local `store status`, jitter, a hardware audit, slow-drive detection, clock rules and an upgrade rule with no on-start migration. Tasks T-S13 to T-S21 are added (revision 9 deletes T-S13 and T-S16 and adds T-S22 to T-S25).
- **SeaweedFS (10.12):** the two deciding gaps, fresh outputs read by other build nodes and promote-on-read, are stated with numbers or the experiment that measures them; `4+4` is stated as 2x raw like two replicas, its gain being tolerance, not capacity.
- **Seed bootstrap of membership (10.8, 10.6).** A client, library or daemon, is configured with seed addresses or a membership file; it fetches the membership document from any storage node (`GET /store/v1/membership`, cluster token) and follows the epoch from `x-ts-mem-epoch` on every response. The route is an addition to T-I's peer protocol (in revision 9 the document is rendered from Postgres rows; the route and the client behaviour are unchanged) (the freeze delta T-I2 of section 18 lists every change to frozen items).
- **Why the cell is 1 MiB** is stated where the cell is defined (section 2).

**Revision 7's changes from revision 5** (history) (the revision merged before revision 7, which put the cold tier on the build nodes' own hard drives): the cold tier now lives on three storage nodes, beside a replicated NVMe hot tier. Sections 1 to 9 (the read path on the build nodes) keep their design and are updated for 31 clients reading from 3 storage nodes (4.3 has the budget arithmetic, including degraded reads). Section 10 is the storage tier: components, units, hot tier, cold tier, movement, metadata, configuration, capacity, the SeaweedFS, Ceph and silo comparison and the migration. Sections 11 to 20 are updated to match. Revision 6 (not merged) introduced the storage tier; revision 7 added, after a critique of revision 6:
- **The owner's decisions on the LATER cluster:** cold code `4+4` and three hot replicas (every storage node holds every hot object, and a hot read is served by the least-loaded node without a hop); their consequences are in the decision table, 10.3, 10.6 and 10.15.
- **Deployment profiles (section 21):** one codebase and one schema serve today's six combined nodes (NOW: SATA SSD hot tier, `4+2` over six nodes on the SMR drives) and the target cluster (LATER), with a migration from NOW to LATER by configuration and membership only.
- **Evidence map (section 22):** for every main choice, the published result it rests on or the named experiment that decides it.
- **Ordering by a cluster stamp (10.2); superseded in revision 9** (no clock orders anything: Postgres transactions order writes of a mutable bucket, and record ids name what a tombstone deletes).
  The revision 7 text: Records, relocates and tombstones carry a hybrid logical clock stamp that every storage node can compare; a key is live when its newest fact is not a tombstone. A delete while the writing node is down, and a re-PUT of identical bytes after a delete, both resolve correctly.
- **One sealed length per segment (10.3); superseded in revision 9** (the sealed length is committed by one compare-and-set on the segment's row after a fence, not by a majority of the storage nodes).
  The revision 7 text: A seal, including the early seal by the writer, is agreed by a majority of the three storage nodes before anything acts on it, so two sealers under a partition cannot leave two different sealed copies. Create-only is stated precisely: it is linearizable when the nodes' views agree, and a divergence is counted, not prevented (10.5).
- **Silo retires only after repair exists (10.11, 18).** Milestone S1 now includes hot and cold-copy re-replication, scrub of copies and the margin and live-key audits; erasure-code rebuild stays in S2.
- **Degraded reads in the budget (4.3, 10.4).** A degraded part is charged `k` stream slots; capacity with one node down is stated (about 2.8 GB/s against 8.5 GB/s healthy) and measured with a pass bar (17.2).
- **Rebuild arithmetic corrected (10.4, 10.15).** It counts writes and one decode per stripe, uses the fill the capacity guard allows, and the guard keeps one drive of slack per surviving node; rebuild shards may exceed `fill_limit` up to a ceiling; margin 0 alerts and scrubs first while a node is down.
- **Stripe free has an owner and a commit (10.5); the commit is a Postgres transaction in revision 9.** Fewer open segments (one per replica pair and expiry class), an age seal only above a floor, stripes from any replica pair by similar length, a cold-fill efficiency metric, and a stated rule for objects near `block_bytes`.
- **The index is kept in an embedded ordered store (10.2)**, rebuildable from the segments (in revision 9, also from Postgres tombstones); it is a cache of the bytes, and the crate's own durability is not relied on.
- **Units are explicit (10.2):** the 1 GiB block is the unit of storage, placement, erasure coding and destage only; the unit of read, fetch and promotion is the object or a byte range of it.
- **Destage is decoupled from eviction (10.5):** destage copies dirty data to cold; reclaim only drops clean data; promoted copies live in a separate read-cache area that the destager never reads.
- **A hot-only mode (10.14):** the same code without a cold tier, `on_full = "evict"` (a cache) or `"reject"` (507, never drop), with example configurations.
- **Corrected comparison (10.12); the layout fact is refined in revision 9 (10.17):** SeaweedFS's erasure coding keeps a needle on one shard (the 1 GB blocks stated here are the legacy layout; 4.54 encodes one uniform block per shard); revision 5's reasons for not using SeaweedFS are withdrawn, and the reasons that remain are listed. Ceph RGW is added as a baseline.
- The first farm measurements of T0 (one SMR drive's sequential and scattered rates, sha256 speed without SHA-NI, the loop-device cache) are in the measured facts, and moving the cache off the loop device is now required (section 11).

Labels: **[M]** measured on the farm or in a paper, **[V]** vendor guidance, **[K]** Linux kernel behaviour from source knowledge, not yet checked on the farm (each has a named T-F0 or T0 check), **[I]** inference (a named test or measurement settles it), **[E]** arithmetic from stated inputs, **[D]** a decision of this document or of the owner.

## Decisions for the owner

**Decided by the owner (2026-11-18); this document is written against them.**
- Hardware: 3 storage nodes, each 1 x 6.4 TB U.2 NVMe and 15 x 6 TB SAS HDD; 31 build nodes with a local SSD cache.
- Tierstore owns the storage tier: no silo and no SeaweedFS underneath it. (This is the design; the implementation is paused and SeaweedFS is trialled as the store, see the revision 9 decision below.)
- The hot tier is deliberately small against the cold tier (cost). **Hot tier: replication only** (for speed). **Cold tier: erasure coding over 1 GiB contiguous blocks** (a normal read touches one drive; parity is read only on failure), with replication as the alternative.
- Redundancy of both tiers is **configuration** (`replicas = N`, or `ec = k+m` with a block size, and a failure domain of node or drive), so other installs choose; an install may also run without a cold tier (10.14).
- Small objects must be supported, though not encouraged on hard drives: the hot tier packs them into append-only segments as they arrive, sealed at about 1 GiB. The cold tier receives only sealed immutable blocks, written sequentially.
- Movement is automatic and ours: seal, then destage to cold (decoupled from eviction); promote an object back to hot when it is read again.

**Decided by the owner (2026-11-18, revision 9); this document is written against them.**
- Block-level truth lives in a **dedicated Postgres cluster across the storage nodes**: three members (a primary, one synchronous standby, one more standby), never on a machine outside the storage tier. LATER: on the three storage nodes, on their own partition of the NVMe. NOW: on three of the six combined nodes, on their own partition of the SATA SSD. Point-in-time backups go to an off-box target that is not tierstore. It is not the CAS service's or the job scheduler's Postgres: the CAS service runs on tierstore (a cycle), and a shared instance shares blast radius (10.16).
- The LATER NVMe drives have **power-loss protection**. Group commit, `hot.min_acks`, `batch_max` and Postgres's default `synchronous_commit = on` are sized on it; T0-S and the hardware audit verify it, and a drive found without it is refused, not silently retuned. NOW (SATA SSD, no PLP) keeps honest `fdatasync` and `fsync` everywhere and accepts millisecond commit latency; nothing turns fsync off (10.3, 10.16).
- Every client, the CAS service first, **tolerates about 30 s of `503` with `Retry-After`** on operations that need the metadata store during a metadata failover; the design states the requirement on every client (23, 10.9), and the CAS service's change is its own task outside this repository.
- **The store is general-purpose.** Keys are content-derived for every CAS-service object, but correctness never depends on it. Create-only (`412`) stays a capability, and ordinary S3 semantics are specified (overwrite, DELETE, prefix LIST, `If-Match`, `If-None-Match`); where content addressing is a shortcut, the text gives the general rule and the CAS-service fast path separately (10.9, 10.2).
- **The failover manager is Patroni with a three-member etcd** on the same three nodes, with a failover target of at most 30 s (10.16).
- **The store's implementation is paused (2026-11-18, the owner's decision after revision 9 was drafted).** Agents focus on the client side; SeaweedFS is trialled as the store. This document is kept and finished as the complete design of our own store, as paper: no implementation task of the store is dispatched, and the tasks of section 18 stay as the plan of record if the owner resumes it. 10.17 records what SeaweedFS 4.54 does and what each difference costs on our hardware.

**Decided by the owner (2026-11-18, revision 8); this document is written against them. Where revision 9 replaced a decision's mechanism, the bullet says so.**
- No roles in code: a node runs a set of components, each configured on its own, with declared dependencies; any combination whose dependencies are met is valid; deployment shapes are example configurations only (10.1, 10.14, 21).
- The client is a set of libraries (`tierstore-client`, `tierstore-cache`, `tierstore-tree`), each usable alone with a stable, documented, versioned API; the daemon is a thin host of fronts on those libraries (section 23).
- Prepare orders its fetches by shared packs first, then tree order, under the whole-pack rule; no runtime-learned profiles (6.1).
- No periodic client pings: liveness from request outcomes and the membership epoch, load from the storage nodes through `LOCATE` and response headers, tail latency by budgeted hedging (4.3, 4.6, 10.6).
- Operations and repair as section 24: recorded block placement, a health ledger (rows of the metadata store in revision 9), margin-first repair (the reconciler, 24.5), adopt-on-return, generated operator verbs, runbooks R1 to R16 (R11 to R16 are the metadata-store runbooks) and a deterministic cluster simulator.
- A client finds the storage nodes from seed addresses or a membership file and follows the membership epoch from responses (10.8).

**The owner's questions.** Q1 and Q2 are **decided by the owner (2026-11-18)**; Q3, Q4 and Q5 are open, with a default this document is written against. A different answer changes the sections named. Two deployments run the same code with different profiles (section 21): **NOW**, six combined build-and-storage nodes on today's hardware, and **LATER**, the three storage nodes and 31 build nodes; Q1 and Q2 are the LATER profile.

| # | Decision | Answer | Consequences, and what the other answer would cost |
|---|---|---|---|
| Q1 | **The cold code on our cluster (10.4, 10.15).** | **Decided: `ec = 4+4`, 3 + 3 + 2 shards over the three nodes, failure domain node, 1 GiB blocks.** It survives the loss of a node plus a drive. | `4+4` costs 2.0x raw, the same as two replicas: its gain over replication is tolerance (a node plus a drive), not capacity (10.12); `4+2` (2 + 2 + 2 shards) would cost 1.5x. Logical capacity at the 85% fill limit is 114.8 TB at `4+4` against 153.0 TB at `4+2`; with the node-loss guard (one node's raw plus one drive of slack per survivor kept free) it is 71.4 against 95.2 TB [E]. **What `4+4` buys:** (1) with a node down, every stripe still has at least one spare shard, so a drive failure or a latent sector error during a reboot or an outage costs nothing (at `4+2` the same event makes the stripes with a shard on that drive unreadable until the node returns, and lost if it does not); (2) after a node is lost for good, its stripes can be rebuilt onto the two survivors at 4 + 4 and survive a second node loss, where `4+2` cannot be restored beyond its tolerance until a replacement node arrives (10.4); (3) a rolling update of a storage node, one at a time, runs at one spare instead of zero. **What it costs:** a quarter of the logical capacity (`4+2` holds a third more), and destage, rebuild and degraded reads move 8 shards instead of 6 per stripe. Repair reads are 4 shards for both; a degraded read costs `k = 4` range reads for both. With under 2% of the capacity in use today the extra raw is idle disk. The code is recorded per stripe, so a later switch of new stripes to `4+2` is a configuration change, and old stripes can be re-encoded in the background (read `k`, write new parity, same data shards). |
| Q2 | **Hot replicas (10.3, 10.15).** | **Decided: `hot.replicas = 3`, `hot.min_acks = 2`: every storage node holds every hot object, on NVMe drives that have power-loss protection (the owner's statement, revision 9; T0-S and the hardware audit verify it, 10.3).** | **Consequences [E]:** (1) **reads:** any storage node holds the whole hot tier and its whole index, so a build node's `LOCATE` to the key's first node is answered from that node's index with every copy ordered by load, and the parts go to the recommended (least-loaded) node, which serves them from its own NVMe with no cross-node hop (10.6); (2) **writes fan out 3x** on NVMe: today's 34 GB/day is about 102 GB/day of NVMe writes cluster-wide (about 34 GB/day per device, far below the endurance of a 6.4 TB U.2 device [I]) and each PUT sends two network copies; a small PUT is acknowledged when two copies are durable (the writer's and the faster replica's), so its latency is the faster of two replicas, and the third copy follows in the same stream; (3) **capacity:** 19.2 TB raw is about **6.1 TB usable hot** after the 256 GiB metadata partition on each device (10.15); the dirty room between the destage-start and reject marks is 2.45 TB, about 72 days of today's ingest and about 3.6 days at 20x, and the marks, destage rate and reclaim of 10.5 and 10.15 are sized on that; (4) **durability:** an acknowledged record is on two NVMe devices at once and on three within the replication lag, so un-destaged data survives the loss of a node plus an NVMe on another node once the third copy lands (that fault also loses two of the three metadata members, so seals, destage, repair, DELETE and mutable buckets answer `503` until a member returns, while immutable PUT and GET continue, Q5); (5) a miss is definitive when two live storage nodes, neither `indexing`, answer `absent` (10.6). `2` would give 9.2 TB usable and 1.5x fewer writes, but every hot read of a key on the other pair would cross nodes, and a second fault during the destage window could lose dirty data. `1` is meant for a single-domain install (10.14). **Power-loss protection:** with PLP a drive acknowledges a flush from its protected cache, so group commit, `hot.min_acks`, `batch_max` and Postgres's `synchronous_commit = on` are sized for flushes of a fraction of a millisecond [I]; a drive found without PLP is refused by the hardware audit and the sizes are not retuned around it. NOW has no PLP (SATA SSD) and keeps honest `fdatasync` and `fsync` at millisecond latency. |
| Q3 | **Per-cell digests (the L2 list, section 3).** Every pack carries one digest per 1 MiB cell, so any cell is verified on arrival and a read waits only for its own cells. | **Yes, sha256, computed with the `ring` implementation.** | Without them a file is verified whole before its first byte (a 1 GiB `.so` waits for 1 GiB and about 3.7 s of hashing on one core of today's build nodes, which have no SHA-NI [M: about 290 MB/s per core with `ring`, about 150 MB/s with the `sha2` crate]), part order is forced, and the hedge and failover of 4.6 cannot publish part-way. The CAS service's hashing CPU roughly doubles with them (3.2). The L2 header names the digest function, so a later release may add blake3 cells (several times faster per core [I], T-FMT measures) as an additive format change; this revision keeps sha256, the hash the file table already uses. |
| Q4 | **The trust gap of the cell digests (3.4).** T-FMT Q1 found that the tree id is the sha256 of the version 2 list, which the L2 sidecar holds byte for byte and the reader re-hashes; so the id pins every path, file sha256, pack key and span, and does not pin the packs' sizes and the cell digests. For those, and for trees whose L2 sidecar was made by the backfill (3.5), the digests are as trustworthy as the sidecar and the packs it was made from. | **Leave it** (confirmed by T-FMT Q1). | Closing it makes the tree id cover `sha256(CELLS)`: every tree id changes and the farm's action cache cold-starts once. A wrong digest can make a good cell fail (a visible EIO or miss) and cannot make a wrong cell pass unless an attacker can also write the list object, which is the trust v1 spans have today. |
| Q5 | **The durability target and failure model (24.15 item 1).** | **Open; default:** the cluster survives any one storage node plus one drive with no acknowledged write lost and keeps serving (a "drive" here is a hard drive of the cold tier: a storage node plus a cold-tier drive on another node keeps full service; a storage node plus the NVMe of a second node loses no acknowledged write once the third copy has landed, but counts as two metadata members lost, so metadata commits stop, with `503` on seals, destage, repair, DELETE and mutable buckets, until a member returns, while immutable PUT and GET continue); it is designed for one failure at a time; **two storage nodes lost at once stops service**: data writes stop (the hot tier's ack rule cannot be met), and the metadata store also stops committing (two of its three members are down), so seals, destage, repair, DELETE and mutable-bucket operations answer `503` (R10 and R6); no acknowledged write is lost while their NVMe devices survive. | A stronger target (two nodes at once, or a site) needs a fourth node or another site, not configuration. The owner states the target; the exposure metrics of 24.10 (time at margin <= 1, time at margin 0) report against it. |

Decisions of the CAS service (not the owner's, recorded in T-K1a): that the CAS service treats a 404 on a pack it believes live as "tree not present" (3.5, 10.5), whether it sends the optional expiry-class hint on PUT (10.5), and whether its sweep needs the weak consistency of LIST described in 10.9 (it is implemented either way).

## Measured farm facts used throughout

Today's farm [M], read from `main` and measured before this revision:
- **Legacy store: silo** (MinIO lineage). One erasure set of 12 drives over the six existing build nodes (2 x ST8000DM004 each: 5400 rpm, drive-managed SMR, 256 MB cache, 190 MB/s vendor rate, xfs), EC 8+4, 1 MiB erasure block; about 87 TiB raw. Silo is the legacy store of this design: it stays up, read-only, for the migration window (10.10) and is then retired.
- **One silo drive, measured (T0, first results):** about 119 MB/s sequential and about 43 MB/s at 1 MiB scattered reads (36%, which matches the positioning model of 10.7); silo keeps about 10 small writes per second on every drive with no jobs running.
- **Workload on silo (1.9 days of counters, a 36-day-old bucket):** 1.34 TB live in 1.39 M objects (about 85.9 k packs); about 220 k successful PUTs/day and 34 GB/day; **99% of objects are under 64 KiB by count, about 95% of the bytes sit in 1 to 256 MiB packs**; deletes are rare (147 DELETEs against 418,823 successful PUTs, 0.035%) and no garbage collection exists yet, so **about 38% of pack bytes are dead** (14,795 packs fully dead); reads are about 88/s and 0.5 MB/s cluster-wide with a mean GET near 6 KB, from requesters not yet identified. About 74% of PUT requests are create-only rejections (4xx), about 7/s. The measured delete rate says nothing about the rate after the CAS service's GC (planned: minimum TTL 7 days, action TTL 30 days) lands; this design therefore treats GC and delete volume as unknown and sizes for them (10.5).
- Unexplained: silo's raw usage (about 9.3 TB) is about 4.6x what its logical size implies at 8+4; T0-F C-6 explains it before any capacity figure of silo is trusted. The new store does not depend on it (nothing shares its drives), only the migration's copy volume does (10.10).
- **Hashing on the build nodes (T0):** Broadwell CPUs without SHA-NI: sha256 about 150 MB/s per core with the `sha2` crate and about 290 MB/s with `ring` (4.7, Q3).
- **Local cache on today's build nodes:** `/var/lib/tierstore-cache` is xfs on a loop device backed by an image file on the root SATA SSD; budget 185 GiB. Measured (T0): the loop device turns `O_DIRECT` writes into page-cache writes (945+ MB/s that are not on the device) and stalls four buffered writers for more than 20 s. Root filesystem type unknown (T0 records it). Load average 131 on 88 CPUs: hashing, FUSE and I/O CPU compete with builds.
- Kernels: reported as Linux 7.0, not verified; T0 E records `uname -r` and `fuse.enable_uring` per node.
- FUSE baseline, one harness (`ts-fs-open-stall-load`): the fuser fork as shipped in 0.3.119, 2,769 opens/s, p50 74 ms, p99 312 ms; the fork plus the pin fix of komira-ai/tierstore#343, 28,199 opens/s, p50 7.7 ms, p99 26 ms.
- macOS: tierstore-fs on macOS is an NFSv3 server (the komira-ai/nfsserve fork) inside the tierstore process, not fuser.

Target farm (owner, 2026-11-18) [D]; its facts are specified, not measured: 31 build nodes (the six existing nodes are among them or are replaced; the cache device and size per node are T0 outputs) and 3 storage nodes of 1 x 6.4 TB U.2 NVMe + 15 x 6 TB SAS HDD. **Power-loss protection:** the owner states that the LATER NVMe drives have it (revision 9); T0-S measures `fdatasync` latency and the hardware audit checks the drive's reported volatile write cache and PLP, so the statement is verified, not assumed. **Not known and recorded by T0 on the storage nodes:** whether the SAS drives are CMR or SMR, their rpm and sequential rate (this document assumes 7.2k rpm and about 190 MB/s outer-zone and 12 ms positioning [I]), the NVMe's sustained write, `fdatasync` latency and link speed and CPU/RAM of the storage nodes (this document assumes 25 Gbit/s per storage node and at least 128 GiB of RAM [I]).

## 1. Goals and non-goals

**Goals**
- Fetch every pack's needed cells exactly once per node, as ranged parts in parallel, into one cache entry.
- Serve files as spans of that entry. FUSE reader threads never wait on the network, a fill or a hash; metadata is answered from mapped memory.
- One fetch engine for demand reads, mount Prepare prefetch and S3-front misses of large objects; one engine on Linux and macOS.
- **The client is libraries, not a daemon (section 23).** Any program reads trees and packs through `tierstore-client`, `tierstore-cache` and `tierstore-tree` in its own process; the daemon is one host of those libraries, with the FUSE, NFS and S3 fronts and the Prepare provider on top.
- **No roles (10.1).** What a node does is the set of components its configuration enables; nothing in the code asks what kind of node it is.
- **One storage tier owned by tierstore**, on three storage nodes: a replicated NVMe hot tier and a cold tier of erasure-coded (or replicated) 1 GiB contiguous blocks on hard drives, so a normal read of any object, a 1 GiB pack included, is one sequential stream from one drive (hot: one NVMe file or record range; cold: one data shard). Redundancy of each tier is configuration (10.14). There are no erasure sets on the build nodes.
- Every wait is observable, and every degradation (a stalled drive, an under-replicated pack, a slow consumer) is counted where an operator looks.

**Launch budget.** Request to command start under 200 ms, 100 ms as the target to aim for.
- Reachable only when the command's first spans are local before the action asks; Prepare at job assignment does that.
- Demand-read first-byte target, idle farm: under 10 ms [I] for a span of 1 MiB or less in a pack on the hot tier, and under 60 ms for one in a pack on the cold tier (a seek of about 12 ms plus a 4 MiB first part at about 190 MB/s on one 7.2k SAS drive [I]; T0-S measures the drives). Under load a demand preempts at the next chunk boundary (10.7), which bounds its drive wait against lower classes to about one 4 MiB chunk plus two seeks plus its own chunk (about 100 ms [I]); against equal-class demands (a cold-start storm) the wait is a slice plus up to `queue_max` queued requests, so only the idle figure is a target. T10b measures both.
- **Launch before Prepare is done.** Nothing gates launch on Prepare. A read on cells not yet present is `demand`: it promotes its part and, if the part has not started, issues a demand sub-range GET (4.5). Each such read increments `launch_before_ready_total` and records `read_wait_seconds{cause="launch_before_prepare"}`.
- Warm open and read: no upstream traffic, no RPC and no lock wait.

**Non-goals**
- Content-defined chunking or dedup across packs.
- A cluster-wide limiter service: admission is at the drive (10.7) and clients do flow control against its signals.
- Changing the pack byte layout.
- Erasure coding or any durable data on the build nodes: they keep only their cache. Redundancy is the storage tier's (10.3, 10.4). The storage tier is durable and, with a cold tier (our cluster), never evicts for capacity (10.5); a hot-only install configured with `on_full = "evict"` is a cache by choice, and its evictions are misses (10.14); a pack lost beyond the configured redundancy is a miss that the CAS service republishes (3.5, 4.6).
- Kernel fscache/cachefiles; FUSE passthrough (deleted); any reader of v1 lists.
- A complete S3 implementation. The front is a general-purpose S3 subset: PUT, range GET, HEAD, DELETE, prefix LIST and the conditional headers, over buckets that are immutable (create-only keys, the CAS service's bucket) or mutable (ordinary overwrite and DELETE ordered by the metadata store). Multipart upload, object versioning, lifecycle rules, ACLs and policies, tagging, CopyObject, object lock, server-side encryption and bucket configuration APIs are out of scope and answer `501`, each for the reason 10.9 gives.

## 2. Data model

**Identity**
- `PackId = (object key, pack_root)`. `pack_root` is computed by the reader from the list's cell digests (3.1); two lists naming the same pack bytes give the same id and a changed pack cannot alias an old entry. No HEAD is needed.
- S3-front large objects have no digests: `PackId = (key, ETag)` with `digest=none` (section 9). In the storage tier the ETag is the object's **generation id**: the 128-bit blake3 of the object's bytes, computed while they stream in (10.2). It is a function of the content, so every copy of one stored object holds the same generation, a retried or raced PUT of the same bytes is an identical PUT, and one stored object is one `PackId` whichever copy served it. In a mutable bucket (10.9) a key can later name other bytes: each write of different bytes is a new stored object with its own generation (a write of the same bytes has the same generation, and still gets a new record and `version`), and the key's current **version** (an internal order number assigned by the metadata store, not an S3 version id) says which is current. A `PackId` names exact bytes in both bucket modes, because a generation does, so an overwrite of the key never changes what an existing `PackId` holds; it creates a different `PackId`.
- One cache entry per `PackId`. Packs are immutable.

**Entry on disk** (cache filesystem, section 11)
- Data: `packs-v2/aa/bb/<hash(key, pack_root)>.pack`, a **sparse** file (`ftruncate` to the pack size, never `fallocate`; space is accounted by present cells).
- Journal and snapshot: `<hash>.jnl` (append-only) and `<hash>.meta` (snapshot, atomic rename). Section 8.
- `flock(LOCK_EX)` on the data file is held by the owning tierstore process.

**Cell** = 1 MiB of pack bytes on a grid anchored at pack offset 0 (the last cell may be short). It is the digest unit, the readiness unit and the minimum fetch unit. Cell state is one byte: **low 3 bits state, bit 7 `durable`** (set by the server after the batch `fdatasync` of section 8; meaningful only on `present`).

**Why 1 MiB [D].** Four quantities meet at this size, and none of them moves far without another getting worse:
- *Digest table size.* A sha256 digest is 32 bytes, so the list carries 32 B per MiB of pack bytes (1/32,768 of the data): 320 KiB of CELLS for a tree naming 10 GiB of packs, mapped and read in place [E]. At 64 KiB cells the same tree needs 5 MiB of digests and at 4 KiB cells 80 MiB, all of which the CAS service writes and every reader maps; the state page grows the same way (one byte per cell: 4 KiB for a 4 GiB pack at 1 MiB).
- *Time to first byte.* A read waits for its covering cells to arrive whole and hash, because a cell is verified only whole. At 1 MiB the hash is about 3.6 ms on one core of today's build nodes [M: 290 MB/s with `ring`]; the transfer is well under 1 ms from an NVMe and about 5.5 ms at 190 MB/s from a hard drive after its positioning of about 12 ms [I], so a cell is ready in about 4 to 5 ms hot and about 21 ms cold, inside the 10 ms and 60 ms first-byte targets of section 1 [E]. At 4 MiB the hash alone is about 14 ms, past the hot target, and a cold cell takes about 48 ms; below 1 MiB the positioning dominates a cold read and the hash is already small next to the hot target, so smaller cells buy little.
- *The FUSE read size.* A FUSE READ request is at most `max_pages` pages, which the kernel caps at 256 pages of 4 KiB, 1 MiB, by default [K, T-F0 records the farm kernel's `max_pages` limit]. An aligned READ of the largest size therefore covers one cell, and an unaligned one at most two, so one read never waits on more than two cells' arrival and hashing.
- *The fetch grid.* Every fetch unit of 4.4 and 4.5 is a whole number of cells: parts of 16, 32 or 64 MiB, a first piece of 2 to 4 cells, a demand sub-range of 1 to 4 cells and the ramp of 4, 8, 16 MiB pieces. A cell much larger than 1 MiB would make the first piece and the demand sub-range coarser than the first-byte lever needs; a much smaller one would multiply claims, CAS operations and hash-pool hand-offs per part without a gain in the request size the drives see.

The size is a per-pack property, not a constant of the code: the PACKS record of the L2 list carries `cell_shift` per pack (3.1), so another cell size for some packs (for example larger cells for packs of many GiB, to shrink CELLS) needs no change of the list format. The CAS service writes `cell_shift = 20` for every pack, and this release's readers accept only 20: the state page of T-I fixes its cell at `CELL_SHIFT = 20` (its header already records the shift), so a list naming another shift is refused with `ListVersionUnsupported`. Accepting other shifts is a later, additive change of the reader and the state page, made when a writer needs it.

| State | Meaning | Transitions |
|---|---|---|
| `absent` | no bytes on disk (or not trusted) | to `claimed` by CAS |
| `claimed` | a fetch attempt owns it; bytes in flight | to `present` (verified and written) or back to `absent` (attempt failed) |
| `present` | verified bytes on disk, servable by the mount without RPC | to `evicting` by CAS only |
| `recheck` | bytes are on disk from before an unclean start in another boot (section 8); re-hashed before serving | to `present` (hash matches) or `absent` |
| `evicting` | the evictor owns it; readers must not use it | to `absent` (punched) or back to `present` (evictor timeout) |
| `bad` | failed verification twice; EIO until expiry (60 s) | to `absent` |

- A cell is written once per residency. Only the claim owner writes it, only after its bytes hash to the table digest; CAS `claimed -> present` has exactly one winner, so a hedge or retry cannot publish twice.
- **Every transition is a CAS from a named predecessor state; no state is ever overwritten by a plain store.** The mount's RPC-free read path treats anything but `present` as "not ready".
- The state array lives in a shared memfd that mounts map read-only (5.1).

**Span**: `(pack, off, len)` from the list; a file is one span, or several if it crosses packs. Span readiness = all covering cells `present`. **Part**: the fetch unit (4.4): a cell-aligned run on the part grid, or a demand sub-range.

| Thing | Key |
|---|---|
| Entry | PackId |
| Readiness | cell index within the entry |
| Fill state (single flight) | PackId, in memory, sharded map |
| Hold | (PackId, holder id); holder = mount session or Prepare |
| Verification | cell index (digest from the cell table) |
| Storage-tier location | key, from `LOCATE` against the index of the key's storage nodes (10.2, 10.6) |

## 3. The tree list "L2": per-cell digests, no legacy reader

**Why.** One sha256 per file forces a whole file to be fetched and hashed before its first byte. Per-cell digests make any cell verifiable on arrival, in any order. **[D, Q3 default]** Readers accept only L2; no reader parses v1.

### 3.1 Layout
All integers little-endian, 8-byte-aligned sections addressed by `(off u64, count u64)`, fixed-size records, zero parse (the reader indexes into the mmap). T-FMT fixed the bytes (the reference is `crates/tierstore-list/docs/tree-list-l2.md`; the reader and writer are the crate `tierstore-list`). It keeps the version 2 list's (`TSTREE`, the list the CAS service writes today; "v1" in this section) entry records, file-sha table, keys, names and ranges byte for byte, so the version 2 list is a projection of the L2 list (3.5):
- **Header, 192 bytes:** magic `TSL2TREE`, major and minor version, required flags, the header's length, `cell_digest` (the digest function of CELLS: 1 = sha256, the only value this revision writes; a reader refuses a value it does not know with `ListVersionUnsupported`), `default_cell_shift` (20), `tree_id` (the identity the list is stored under, 3.5), a section table of eight `(off, count)` pairs (entries, file shas, packs, cells, keys, names, ranges, extension records), and a CRC-32C of the header. Extension records (a tag, a length, the bytes) are the additive change path: a reader skips a tag it does not know unless the tag's required bit is set.
- **Entries: the version 2 records**, 32 bytes, breadth first: a directory's children are a contiguous slice sorted by name, so an entry's index is its inode and a lookup is a binary search; a file names its pack by its PACKS index.
- **PACKS: one 48-byte record per pack**, in the order of the keys (the key's offset and length in the keys, 4 bytes each, plus):

| Field | Size | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `pack_size` | 8 | bytes |
| `cell_first` | 8 | index of this pack's first digest in CELLS |
| `cell_count` | 4 | ceil(pack_size / cell) |
| `cell_shift` | 1 | 20 |
| `pack_flags` | 1 | bit 0: pack fully covered by spans; bit 1: **shared**, set by the CAS service on a pack that at least `shared_min` trees name (6.1). A reader ignores bits it does not know. |
| reserved | 2 | zero |
| `upload_part_size` | 8 | **0 for every pack written to the storage tier** (single streaming PUT, no parts). Lists of packs uploaded to silo as multipart keep their recorded nonzero value. **No reader uses it:** it cut the parts of a fill from silo (4.4), which was deleted with silo (T11b); the bytes stay in the record, so every list stays readable, and the decoded record (`PackRecord`) has no field for them. |
| reserved | 8 | zero, ignored |

- **CELLS: a flat table of 32-byte sha256 digests**, one per cell, pack by pack: `digest[cell_first + i] = sha256(pack bytes [i << shift, min((i+1) << shift, pack_size)))`.
- **Spans** are the file records' `(pack idx, offset, len)` with the file's sha256 at the same index of the file-sha table; the per-file sha256 stays as the end-to-end content address used by `tierstore verify` and by non-pack objects.
- **Directory records are sorted by name bytes**: the version 2 image order already is (T-FMT checked it, and the reader refuses a list whose siblings are not ascending); lookup binary-searches the mapped records, 5.3.
- `pack_root = sha256("TSPACK1" || pack_size_le64 || shift_u8 || digest[0] || ... || digest[n-1])`, computed when a pack is first opened and cached per list.

### 3.2 How the CAS service computes them
1. While streaming a pack to the storage tier, the CAS service keeps a 1 MiB accumulator, hashes each full cell and appends the digest; the last cell is hashed short.
2. The CAS service records `pack_size` and `cell_shift`. **The CAS service uploads every pack with one streaming PUT** (HTTP `Content-Length` if the pack size is known when the upload starts, else chunked transfer encoding) to tierstore's S3 front (10.9). There are no multipart parts; packs written to silo before the cutover keep their recorded `upload_part_size`.
3. The list is finalized by writing PACKS and CELLS, then the header last. Per-file sha256 is still computed, so the CAS service's hashing CPU doubles: T-FMT measured ring's sha256 on a build node at 319 MB/s per core over whole files and 325 MB/s over 1 MiB cells, about 3.1 s of one core per GB more (`crates/tierstore-list/docs/tree-list-l2.md`; the hashing pipelines with the upload).
4. A deterministic-output test (same inputs, same bytes) covers the new sections.

### 3.3 How a reader verifies a cell on arrival
1. Part bytes stream into pooled 1 MiB cell buffers (a part is a sequence of whole cells).
2. A full buffer goes to the bounded hash pool (4.7), which compares sha256 with `CELLS[cell_first + i]`.
3. Match: `pwrite` at `cell << shift`, CAS `claimed -> present`, wake waiters. Bytes are verified before they touch the file, so nothing is re-read to be verified and no later write can replace verified bytes.
4. Mismatch: 4.6 (the refetch goes to the other hot replica, or to a degraded read of the cold stripe). A `digest=none` pack skips client verification (section 9).

### 3.4 What the reader does not do, and what the list's integrity rests on
No per-file hash on the read path: a file is correct if every covering cell is `present`. **What pins the list (T-FMT Q1, answered):** the tree reference is `cas/l/<id>`, `<id>` the sha256 of the whole version 2 list, and a node refuses a list that does not hash to it; so today the id pins every span and file sha256. The L2 sidecar keeps those sections byte for byte, and the reader re-hashes their projection (3.5) and refuses a sidecar whose projection is not the id: every path, mode, size, file sha256, pack key, offset and range of the L2 list is pinned by the id, as in v1. **Not pinned:** the fields the version 2 list does not have, the packs' sizes, cell counts and flags, and the cell digests. Those are as trustworthy as the sidecar object in the storage tier (the same trust as the pack bytes beside it), plus the header CRC and an offline cross-check: `tierstore verify` re-derives per-file sha256 from cells and compares with the file-sha table the tree id covers. A wrong cell digest can make a good cell fail verification (a visible EIO or miss), and cannot make a wrong cell pass unless the attacker can also write both the sidecar and the pack. Closing that residue is decision Q4 (default: leave it).

### 3.5 Rollout order, tree identity, refusal and rollback
**Tree identity [D, T-FMT Q1 answered].** What the code does today [M, read]: the CAS service writes a job's tree as the version 2 list and names it `cas/l/<id>`, `<id>` the sha256 of the list's bytes, the job's layer ref; the id is a hash of bytes, not of the logical tree, so any change of those bytes is a new id. The action key is the client's REAPI action digest (the command and the input root's Merkle digest); the tree list is made from the action after it is known and enters no action-cache key, so no format change of the list invalidates an action-cache entry (the CAS service's front keeps the action's list key in memory for at most 10 minutes; a node keys its prepared trees by the id). The tree id does not change: the CAS service keeps computing the version 2 bytes (in memory) for the id, and stores the **L2 list as an additional object, the sidecar, at `cas/l2/<id>`** (it cannot sit at `cas/l/<id>`, which must hash to `<id>`). Its header names `<id>`, and its version 2 projection hashes to it, which the reader checks (3.4). The sidecar is a function of the version 2 list and of each pack's size, cell digests, `upload_part_size` and `shared` bit (`sidecar_from_v2`); all but the `shared` bit are fixed with the pack's bytes, so when the CAS service's create-only PUT, which takes an existing key as success, finds a sidecar already written, a create-only PUT that finds the key written keeps the first sidecar, whose `shared` bit may be stale. That bit only orders Prepare's fetches (design 6.1) and is never read for correctness, so a stale bit costs fetch order, never a byte. The CAS service's list writer does not read packs: the cell digests come from where packs are written (one 1 MiB accumulator as a pack streams, `CellHasher`), or from the backfill.

**Dual emission for exactly one release.** The release that adds L2 makes the CAS service write, for every new tree, both the v1 list and the L2 sidecar. Old readers ignore the sidecar; new readers read only the sidecar. After every reader is new (`tierstore_build_info` on all nodes including the Macs), a following release of the CAS service stops storing v1 (the id is still computed from the canonical bytes in memory).

**Order.**
1. **The CAS service's dual emission ships first.** It is invisible to old readers. The CAS service also runs the id-preserving backfill (`l2-backfill`) over trees referenced in the last N days, rate limited, so recent trees have a sidecar before any reader needs it. The backfill reads the packs once to compute cell digests, about 0.9 core-hours of hashing per TiB beside the read (T-FMT's measurement), and it runs off-peak.
2. **Readers roll node by node** under the one-live-update-at-a-time coordinator. On an updated node a tree with no L2 sidecar is a miss.
3. When all nodes are new and the sidecar coverage is complete (`l2_sidecar_missing_total` zero for 7 days), the CAS service stops writing v1.

**Refusal.** A new reader never parses v1. A tree with no L2 sidecar returns a distinct `ListVersionUnsupported` status to the caller (`list_refused_total{reason}`). **The CAS service treats that status as "not mountable through tierstore" and takes its miss path (materialize the tree from the cold tier without the mount, or rebuild and republish); it never fails the job because of it.** T-FMT Q1 found no such path today: a tree the provider cannot place reaches the CAS service as a job start failure, which it retries twice as an infrastructure failure and then reports unavailable; T-K1a builds the miss path before step 2.

**Rollback.** During the dual-emission release both lists are stored, so rolling readers back loses nothing. After v1 stops, rolling readers back makes trees unreadable (misses) until readers roll forward; the runbook (T13) says to fix forward. Wrong bytes are never possible in either case.

## 4. Fetch engine

### 4.0 Two layers of reads, which never meet [D]
A read crosses two separate layers, with different sizes, devices and code:
- **Serving layer (sections 5 and 7): a job's read, from the build node's local cache.** A FUSE READ (or an NFS read on macOS) asks for at most 1 MiB (the kernel's `max_pages`, section 2) and is answered from the pack file in the node cache on the build node's SSD, after the reader protocol of 5.1 checks that its cells are `present`. It never touches the network or a storage drive; a read whose cells are not present parks and asks the fetch engine (`wait`, `ensure`), and is answered when the cells land.
- **Fetch layer (this section and 10.6): a part, from a storage drive into that cache.** The fetch engine moves **parts** of 16 to 64 MiB (4.4), a first piece of 2 to 4 MiB, a demand sub-range of 1 to 4 cells, or a whole pack (section 6), from one storage device (a hot copy on an NVMe, or a cold data shard on one hard drive, read as one sequential stream by the drive scheduler of 10.7) into the cache, verifying each cell as it arrives (3.3).

So a hard drive never sees a 1 MiB FUSE read or a random 128 KiB read: it sees ascending multi-MiB streams of one file, admitted per drive (10.7). And a job's read never waits on the network except through a parked read of a cell not yet fetched, which the demand class and the first-piece rule keep short (4.4, 4.5).


### 4.1 Single flight
One `PackFill` per `PackId` in a sharded map, no lock across I/O. It owns the cell state array, the part queue, the claim table, the pack's storage-tier location (the hot copies or the cold data shard found, with the generation, 10.6) and the fill's **pinned generation**: the generation of the hinted copy, else of the first copy that answers, set once when the location is first resolved and never changed for the life of the fill. An S3-front object pins the ETag of its `PackId` (section 9). Any reader, prefetch or S3-front miss calls `ensure(pack, cell_runs, class, rank)`, which attaches to the existing fill and raises priority; it never starts a second fill.

### 4.2 Claims
- A fetch attempt claims only the cells it is about to fetch by CAS `absent -> claimed`; `claimed` and `present` cells are skipped, so a part overlapping an in-flight demand sub-range splits into runs of still-absent cells.
- If the owner fails, its cells return to `absent` and a waiter's demand re-claims them.
- **Validation:** no cell is fetched twice except by retry after failure or verification mismatch. GETs are counted by kind (`grid_part`, `demand_sub`, `retry`, `hedge`); the acceptance bound is GETs <= grid parts + demand sub-ranges + retries + hedges, and `bytes_fetched == bytes_needed + wasted`.

### 4.3 Budget: per-drive flow control under per-storage-node and per-client ceilings
**What limits the storage tier.** Each hard drive serves at most `s*` sequential streams at a time (default 2, the server's `drive.streams`), admitted and ordered by that drive's scheduler (10.7); each NVMe is bounded by a queue depth (`hot.queue_depth`, default 64). Admission is **server-side and authoritative**. A part is one contiguous byte range of one hot file, one hot record range or one cold data shard on one device, so one part is one stream on one device; a healthy read has no striping and no erasure width (10.4). The client's budget is flow control against what each device and each storage node advertise, not a model of them.

**Quantities, stated separately.** With `D` = 45 cold hard drives on 3 storage nodes, `N` = 31 build nodes (clients), `s*` = 2, and `P` the current part size:

| Quantity | Formula | At D = 45, N = 31, s* = 2 |
|---|---|---|
| Cold stream capacity | `G = s* x D` parts | 90 parts |
| Per-client share | `share = ceil(G / clients_active)` parts (`clients_active` from the jobscheduler hint, default N if missing or older than 10 s) | 3 parts |
| Per-client floor | 1 part at depth 1 at `P_floor` = 16 MiB, plus the demand reserve | 16 MiB per client, 496 MiB across 31 clients |
| Per-client ceiling | `2 x max(share, floor)` parts until T0-S replaces it with the measured knee | 6 parts |
| Bytes in flight at share | `share x P` | 192 MiB per client and about 5.8 GiB across 31 clients at P = 64 MiB; 48 MiB per client at P = 16 MiB |
| Per-drive permits | `per_drive_max[d]`, 1 or 2 for a hard drive (a larger value for an NVMe, T0-S), **one value per drive** | 2 per drive when that drive is healthy |
| Per-storage-node permits | `node_permits[n]`, the parts in flight to one storage node, bounded by its advertised link and CPU | set from the measured knee; unbounded until T0-S |

The budget unit is the part, so a part size never exceeds the per-client share: share is at least one part whatever P is. A client's parts land on drives by the object's location, so the expected load at full share is `G / D` = `s*` streams per cold drive when reads are spread over the drives; hot reads go to the NVMe and are bounded by its depth. Overload is absorbed by the server queue (10.7), never by collapse. **With 31 clients and 90 stream slots, a cold storm of more than about three parts per client queues at the drives, which is the intended place for it to queue.**

**Degraded reads cost `k` slots [D].** A part served by a degraded read (10.4) reads the same range from `k` drives. The reconstructor admits it as `k` stream charges, one on each source drive's scheduler (10.7), and the client charges it `k` parts against its ceiling and one part against each source drive's permits (`LOCATE` and the reconstructor's response name the source drives, chosen by their advertised queues, 10.6). **With one storage node down** about a third of cold reads are degraded (the data shards on that node) and a third of the spindles are gone: drive-read load is 2/3 x 1 + 1/3 x 4 = 2.0 times the healthy load, carried by 30 drives instead of 45, so the cold read capacity falls from about 8.5 GB/s to about 8.5 x (30/45) / 2 = **2.8 GB/s** [E] (at `4+2` the same). Today's 0.5 MB/s is far below either; a cold-start storm of 31 nodes during a rolling update of a storage node is the case that meets it, and 17.2 item 5 measures it with a pass bar. There is no read drain before a planned reboot: the data shards on the node cannot be read anywhere else, so the budget's job is to charge degraded parts honestly and let the drive queues absorb the rest.

**Three levels of state [D].** The controller keeps **per-drive state**, **per-storage-node state** and a **client ceiling**:
- *Per drive d:* `per_drive_max[d]`, a baseline and a decrease state of its own, fed by signals that drive d produced (its 503s, its `x-ts-queue-wait-ms` and `x-ts-service-ms`, first-byte time of GETs it served). A 503 or a doubled queue wait from drive d decreases **only drive d's permits** (2 to 1, then pacing of d), moves d's replicas to the end of the read preference, and leaves every other drive's fills untouched. A hot pack that queues its drive therefore throttles that drive and nothing else.
- *Per storage node n:* `node_permits[n]`, decreased only by evidence about that node as a whole: more than a third of the drives of n that the client has traffic on in a decrease state at once, or the client's network time per MiB for parts from n above 2 x its baseline for two windows (n's link or CPU is the bottleneck, for example a node rebuilding). One storage node being slow does not slow reads from the other two.
- *Client ceiling:* the total parts in flight across all storage nodes, bounded by the table above and decreased only by client-wide evidence: the client's own network signal above 2 x its baseline for two windows (its link or CPU), or more than a third of all drives with traffic in a decrease state (the farm as a whole is loaded). One slow drive cannot hide inside a pooled median, because no median is pooled across drives.

**Parameters [I until T0-S]:** `s* = 2`, `per_drive_max` up to 2, P from 4.4.

**Floor is a rate floor.** Under a sustained decrease state a drive steps down its own ladder, one step per window: (1) `per_drive_max` 2 to 1; (2) pacing: a minimum gap between part starts to that drive, doubling per window up to 2 s. A storage node's and the client ceiling's ladders are: (1) fewer parts in flight; (2) P toward 16 MiB (64 -> 32 -> 16). P is one client-level value per size class (4.4), never per drive, so a hot replica and a cold shard share one part grid and parts stay interchangeable for hedges and failover. A client never stops (one part per 2 s per drive at worst) and the farm can back off below one stream per drive.

**Controller**
- Window `max(16 parts, 2 s)`, per drive and for the ceiling. Increase only with backlog (queued parts and the permits fully used); slow start (double toward the share, then +1 part per window); state persists across idle.
- **Signals, normalized per size class:** (a) network time per MiB = (last byte - first byte) / size; (b) first-byte time; (c) **the drive's own report**: every storage-node response carries `x-ts-queue-wait-ms` (time the request waited for a stream slot), `x-ts-service-ms` and `x-ts-drive-state` (10.7), so the controller sees each drive's queue directly instead of inferring it; (d) **the load in `LOCATE` answers** (10.6): the answering storage node's view of each copy's device queue. **A client sends no pings and learns nothing in the background [D]:** every signal arrives on an answer to a request it made for its own work, and a device the client has no traffic with has no state in its controller. Local write and hash time are excluded.
- Baselines: windowed minimum (10 minutes) of the median of each signal per size class, **per drive** for (b) and (c), per client for (a) and per storage node for the node level. Decrease of drive d: median of d's (b) or (c) above 2 x d's baseline for two windows, or any `503 Retry-After` from d -> d's permits x 0.75 rounded down, at least the ladder step; then a cooldown of one window with +-50% jitter. A response with `x-ts-drive-state: stalled` or `indexing` is not a load signal: the copy is skipped (4.6) and d's permits are not changed.
- Local back-pressure is separate: the dirty-byte cap (4.8).
- Topology is the membership document (10.8) and `s*`; there are no per-fill concurrency knobs.

**Demand reserve.** `max(1 demand part, 25% of the client ceiling)` is held back from `prepare` and `background`, and one permit of every drive is reserved for `demand` while that drive has queued demand. At the drive the demand class also preempts (10.7), so the reserve buys drive time and not only node bytes.

### 4.4 Part sizing and the part grid
A part is a contiguous run of one pack file on one drive read as a stream: it reads at the drive's sequential rate whatever P is, as long as it continues in ascending order; P trades request overhead against first-byte time for the waiting reader and memory. Starting values (T0-F replaces them):

| Pack size | First piece | Ramp | Steady P |
|---|---|---|---|
| <= 32 MiB | first-touch cells (>= 2, <= 4 MiB), then one GET for the rest | - | - |
| 32 MiB to 512 MiB | 4 MiB | doubling | 32 MiB |
| > 512 MiB | 4 MiB | doubling | 64 MiB |

- **Grid.** The steady grid is anchored at pack offset 0 with cell-aligned pitch P. **The ramp is defined relative to the grid:** it covers `[first-touch cell start, next grid boundary)` (or to the pack end) in doubling pieces of 4, 8, 16 ... MiB capped at P/2, the last piece trimmed to the boundary. After the ramp every part is a whole grid part. A first touch in the middle of a pack costs one ramp and no further runts; a first touch at offset 0 ramps 4+8+16+32 = 60 MiB and the next piece is trimmed at the 64 MiB boundary. Demand sub-ranges split grid parts into runs of absent cells; a run is a valid fetch unit.
- **No upload-part boundary exists** (10.2): a list's recorded `upload_part_size` is ignored, and parts are cut only by the plan and at a chain object's block boundaries. (Fills from silo were also cut at its upload parts; that rule was deleted with silo, T11b.)
- **First part small (first-byte lever).** At least 2 cells, at most 4 MiB: on an idle drive about one seek plus 4 MiB at the media rate. If Prepare entered with a plan, the first part is the plan's first run.
- **Adaptation.** P moves within {16, 32, 64} MiB. While backlog exists, 1 part in 8 is issued at the next size up (the probe arm). Per size class, goodput per in-flight byte and drive-reported service time are compared over 10 minutes; move up if the probe is >= 10% better at equal-or-lower p95 first byte, back down if >= 10% worse. P is a node-level value per size class; a node-ceiling decrease state also steps it down (4.3).
- **Striping one pack across hot replicas (optional, measured).** With 2 or more hot replicas holding the same generation, parts of a pack larger than 128 MiB may alternate between replicas on different nodes, so two NVMe devices stream one pack in parallel. A cold pack is one data shard on one drive and has nothing to stripe. T0-S arm C-3 compares one replica streaming against striping; default off unless it wins by >= 20% at equal p99.

### 4.5 Order of parts, demand sub-ranges, fairness across jobs
- Classes: `demand` > `prepare` > `background`. The class, job id and rank travel to the drive in request headers (`x-ts-class`, `x-ts-job`, `x-ts-rank`) so the scheduler (10.7) applies the same order.
- **Within a class: deficit round robin across jobs**, quantum one part of the current P, oldest job first on ties. **Within a job** the key is `(rank, offset)`, where `rank` is the first-touch rank of the first needed cell inside the part (shared packs first, then tree order; 6.1).
- **Active-pack window.** At most 3 packs are partly filled per class per node; each pack's parts go in ascending offset order, so the drive sees ascending reads of one file.
- A `demand` on a part that has not started promotes it. If the needed span is 1 MiB or less (4 MiB with alignment) and the part has not started, the engine issues a **demand sub-range GET** of exactly the covering cells (1 to 4 cells), `demand` class; later grid parts skip the claimed cells.
- A demand inside a started part waits for that cell only (cells publish as they land).

### 4.6 Retries, stalls, hedging, verification failures, lost packs
- **Generation known before the first byte.** The location of a stored pack (`LOCATE`, 10.6) carries its generation and size, because both are in the index entry and in the record header or file name (10.2, 10.3); for a mutable-bucket key the head read from the metadata store (10.16) names the record id and generation the same way. Every GET of a fill carries `If-Match: <the fill's pinned generation>` (4.1), so one fill never takes bytes of two generations, even for a `digest=none` object whose cells the client cannot verify.
- **Copies of another generation are excluded per fill.** A copy that lists or answers (412) another generation holds other bytes under the key: it is dropped from this fill's location, `store_copy_generation_mismatch_total` counts it, and parts go only to copies of the pinned generation. The fill fails only when no reachable copy holds it. What the mismatch means depends on the bucket (10.9). In a mutable bucket a new generation of a key is a legitimate overwrite: the fill pinned one generation, so a key whose head moved to another generation mid-fill is a lost location for that generation (the old record is tombstoned and its bytes go), handled as the lost-pack rule below says: `LOCATE` once more, and if the key now names a different generation the fill is lost and the job retries on the new tree or object. For a tree-named pack in the CAS service's immutable bucket, whose keys are content-derived and never overwritten, a copy of another generation is still a defect (10.5) and is counted and excluded as above.
- **Stall detection.** After the first byte: no bytes for `max(500 ms, 8 x baseline net time per MiB x 1 MiB)`. **Before the first byte** a GET is not a stall because of time alone (a queued drive is normal under load): the pre-first-byte timeout is `max(2 s, 8 x first-byte p99 baseline of the size class)`, and a response that already said `x-ts-queue-wait-ms` is not a stall while the drive is making progress. A response (its `x-ts-drive-state`) or a `LOCATE` answer saying the drive is `stalled` (10.7) ends the GET at once. A stalled part is cancelled and retried, **on the other hot replica, or as a degraded (reconstructing) read of the cold stripe when the stalled device is the data shard's drive, else on a fresh connection**, up to 3 attempts, jittered backoff.
- **Stall retries are budgeted.** A retry waits for a permit like any part, at most one stall retry per drive per window, and while that drive is in a decrease state only transport-level failures (reset, connect error, node down, drive `stalled`) are retried at once; everything else waits for the state to clear.
- **Hedge [D]** (after Dean and Barroso, "The Tail at Scale"): a part with no first byte by its size class's first-byte **p95** baseline (at least 30 ms) is re-sent to the **next copy in the `LOCATE` answer's order** (10.6), a different device; the two attempts share the claim, whichever first delivers a verifying cell publishes, and the other's bytes are dropped, never written, and its request is cancelled. For a cold range of at most 4 MiB of a `demand` part the alternative is a degraded read from the other shards when the reconstructor's sources have `k` free slots (a degraded hedge costs `k` charges, 4.3); a larger cold part has no alternative and is not hedged. **Hedges are budgeted so they cannot amplify overload:** at most 5% of the parts the client started in the current window are hedges (a degraded hedge counts as one hedge and also needs its `k` charges free) (the extra load Dean and Barroso report for hedging at the p95), at most one hedge per part, none to or from a device in a decrease state, and none at all while the client ceiling is in a decrease state; a part that cannot hedge waits, and the stall rules above still apply.
- **Verification failure:** the claim is released and the cell refetched once as a demand sub-range **from the other hot replica, or by degraded read of the cold stripe, if there is one** (else the same drive on a fresh connection). A second mismatch marks that cell `bad` for 60 s: reads covering it return EIO, other cells are unaffected. The serving node is told (`x-ts-report: bitrot`); it quarantines that copy or shard and the copy is restored by repair or rebuild (10.3, 10.4).
- **A pack lost mid-job [D].** A running job's tree may name a pack that every copy then answers 404 for (the CAS service deleted it, or every copy was lost). The storage tier with a cold tier never evicts for capacity (10.5), so a held pack disappears only through a DELETE by the CAS service's GC or through loss beyond the configured redundancy. When it happens anyway: `LOCATE` (10.6) is asked once more of the nodes of `rank(K)` that a definitive miss needs (10.6 step 3), bypassing every negative cache; a hit is served. If the miss is **not definitive** (10.6 step 3: fewer than `N - hot.min_acks + 1` live storage nodes, none `indexing`, answered), the fill waits and retries with backoff for up to `store.unavailable_wait` (default 10 minutes, longer than a routine reboot), so a reboot of the node holding the only copy delays the job and does not fail it. If the miss is definitive, or still not definitive when that wait ends, the fill is **lost**: waiters of unsatisfied cells get EIO, the tree's mount is marked lost for every holder, `store_pack_lost_total{phase="mid_job"}` counts it, and the mount provider reports each job holding the tree as failed with the **retriable** cause `tree-lost` to the jobscheduler (T-J1), which requeues it. On the retry the CAS service finds the tree not present and takes its miss path (republish or rebuild). No wrong byte is ever served, and the job is retried rather than failed. One lost pack marks the whole tree lost on purpose: a tree with a hole cannot build correctly.
- No reachable copy because storage nodes are `down`, an index is `indexing`, or the only shard's drive is `stalled` and no degraded read is possible yet is a non-definitive miss and takes the wait of the bullet above, never an immediate EIO. Other pack-level failure after retries (verification failing on every copy, a copy set the location rules exclude entirely): waiters of unsatisfied cells get EIO; the entry stays resumable and the next read retries.

### 4.7 Hash pool and CPU
Dedicated bounded pool, not `spawn_blocking`. Threads = `ceil(1.2 x target_rate / core_sha_rate)`, with `target_rate` the cache write ceiling (about 500 MB/s) and `core_sha_rate` measured at startup (about 1.5 GB/s with SHA-NI [I]; about 0.29 GB/s on today's build nodes, which have none [M]).
