The implementation is `ring`, about twice the `sha2` crate on these CPUs [M]; at 0.29 GB/s per core a 500 MB/s cache write ceiling needs 3 hash threads. Queue bound 32 cells; when full, receive pauses (`part_hash_wait_seconds`). The tierstore service has a higher `cpu.weight` than job scopes. `recheck` cells are hashed by the same pool at lower priority. A paused receive is a slow consumer for the serving drive; the drive's read-ahead buffer and slot release (10.7) keep it from holding the spindle.

### 4.8 Memory, dirty pages and DONTNEED
- Parts stream into pooled 1 MiB cell buffers; in-flight memory is at most `ceiling + 32 MiB` per node. A part from a storage node is received over the network straight into these buffers (no second copy on the build node, which has no tier drives).
- **Dirty cap** `min(256 MiB, 2 x node ceiling)`: cells wait before `pwrite` when reached (`dirty_bytes`, `writeback_stall_seconds`).
- **DONTNEED is server-only and keyed to the `durable` bit.** The journal committer (section 8) sets `durable` on a cell after the batch `fdatasync`. Only the server issues `posix_fadvise(DONTNEED)`, only on cell-aligned ranges of `durable` cells, because DONTNEED on a dirty page does nothing (the mount cannot know a cell is durable). Policies are arms of T0 B and the section 17 benchmark: (a) never, (b) at commit, (c) when the pack-file page cache exceeds a cap, oldest durable groups first. Default (a) until data says otherwise. Races: DONTNEED against a splice in flight is safe (the pipe holds the old, correct pages); against a concurrent `pread` it is a re-read (performance); against a refetch after punch it is the same bytes; it can drop a neighbouring file's pages in the same cell (thrash on shared packs, never correctness).
- Parked FUSE replies hold no buffer; a pooled buffer is taken when the cell is ready.

### 4.9 Connections
One pooled HTTP client with a per-host pool of at least `ceiling / smallest part`, keep-alive, no per-request handshake. A client opens connections only to storage nodes it has a request for, taken in `rank(K)` order for the keys it reads (10.6), on the private network; the pool is shared by the node's mounts.

## 5. Serving

### 5.1 State page, reader counters and the eviction protocol
`open-pack` returns the **data fd** and a **state memfd** over the unix socket (SCM_RIGHTS). The data fd guarantees the right inode if the path is unlinked or recreated; the mount never reopens by path.

**State memfd.** The server creates the memfd, maps it read-write, writes the cell state bytes (and group touch epochs), then applies `F_SEAL_FUTURE_WRITE | F_SEAL_SHRINK | F_SEAL_GROW` and `F_SEAL_SEAL` before passing it. [K] `F_SEAL_FUTURE_WRITE` forbids new writable mappings and `write(2)` while the server's existing writable mapping stays valid. The mount maps it `PROT_READ` only and cannot mark a cell `present` or alter another mount's data. A conformance test asserts that a `PROT_WRITE` mapping from the passed descriptor fails (T-F7, T1).

**Counter memfd.** Each mount (holder) creates **one counter memfd for its whole life**, maps it read-write, and sends it to the server at handshake; the server maps it read-only. Inside it the mount allocates a **region per open pack**: one u16 reader counter per 16-cell group (256 groups x 2 B = 512 B for a 4 GiB pack), and tells the server `(region offset)` in `open-pack`. There is no slot table and no cap per pack, so the most shared packs (toolchains) are never refused. A new mount process always has a new memfd, so a counter is never reused across processes and cannot be underflowed by a late teardown of an old one. The mount creates the memfd with `MFD_ALLOW_SEALING` and seals it `F_SEAL_SHRINK` (never `F_SEAL_GROW`) before the handshake, and the server refuses a counter memfd without that seal: the server maps a file another process can resize, and a mapped page past the end of a shrunk file would fault (`SIGBUS`) in the evictor. The memfd grows (`ftruncate`) as the mount opens more packs; a region offset beyond the server's current mapping makes the server remap that holder's memfd at the new size before it acknowledges the `open-pack`, so the evictor never reads past its mapping.
- **The server never zeroes or drops a live holder's counters.** Liveness is a **lifeline**: at handshake the mount also sends one end of a `socketpair` that is held open for the process's whole life, separate from the multiplexed protocol connection (which may reconnect many times). The server polls the lifeline; only `POLLHUP` (the process exited or closed it) ends the holder: the server then unmaps that holder's counter memfd and drops its holds. A mount whose protocol connection flaps keeps its counters, its regions and its holds; it re-sends `open-pack` with the same holder id and gets the same regions back. A pidfd is not used because mounts run in other pid namespaces.

**Reader protocol (mount, no RPC), for each 16-cell group covering the range:**
1. `fetch_add(group counter, 1, SeqCst)`.
2. `load(state of every cell in the range, SeqCst)`: if any is not `present`, `fetch_sub`, then `wait` (or EIO for `bad`).
3. Read the bytes (pread, or splice into the pipe, 5.3).
4. **Post-read recheck:** reload the state of every covered cell (SeqCst); if any is not `present`, discard the bytes (empty the pipe or drop the buffer), `fetch_sub`, and go to `wait`. One extra load of the state bytes means even a forced counter loss cannot serve zeros.
5. Commit the reply; then `fetch_sub(group counter, 1, SeqCst)`.

**Evictor protocol (server), per cell, never per group range:**
1. For each candidate cell, **CAS `present -> evicting`**; remember exactly the cells won. A cell that is `claimed`, `absent`, `recheck` or `bad` is not touched, so an in-flight fetch's ownership is never overwritten, and a neighbouring cell going `absent -> claimed -> present` during the scan is simply not in the set.
2. For each group containing won cells, `load` and sum that group's counters across every holder's region for the pack (SeqCst); wait with backoff until the sum is 0.
3. **Timeout (`evict_wait`, 2 s):** if a group's sum has not reached 0, CAS the won cells of that group back `evicting -> present` and count `evict_timeouts_total`. Nothing has been journaled yet (wait first, journal second).
4. For cells whose group is clear: **append `PUNCH` records for the whole batch and fsync the journal once** (section 8), then `fallocate(PUNCH_HOLE | KEEP_SIZE)` over each contiguous run of won cells only, then CAS `evicting -> absent`.

Why this is correct: reader and evictor each store to one location then load the other's (the Dekker pattern); with SeqCst on both, either the reader sees `evicting` or the evictor sees the counter, never neither. Once a cell is `evicting` no new reader can start on it, so a sum of 0 observed in step 2 stays valid through the punch. The post-read recheck is a second, independent guard. T10a checks the protocol with an exhaustive interleaving model (loom) **including claim, publish and evict transitions on neighbouring cells and a reader that straddles groups**, so the proof is a test.

### 5.2 The FUSE library `tsfuse` (in this repository; Linux only)
**[D]** No vendored fuser. A crate `tsfuse` in this repository, designed for tierstore, serves a read-only tree and the put directory. The komira-ai/fuser fork is retired when `tsfuse` serves in production (T-R); **macOS does not block this (5.6).**

**Scope.** Linux only. Opcodes implemented:
- **Tree mount (read-only):** INIT, DESTROY, LOOKUP, FORGET and BATCH_FORGET (no-ops: inode numbers are record indexes in the mapped image), GETATTR, OPEN/OPENDIR/RELEASE/RELEASEDIR (5.3), READ, READDIR, READDIRPLUS, READLINK, STATFS, INTERRUPT. Everything else: `ENOSYS`.
- **Put directory mount:** the above plus CREATE, WRITE, FLUSH, FSYNC, SETATTR (size, times), MKDIR, UNLINK, RENAME. T-F9 fixes the list from the current put handler.
- Not implemented: xattrs, locks, bmap, ioctl, poll, notify store/delete/inval, passthrough, macOS/BSD.

**INIT flags to negotiate (fixed in T-F1).** Required: `ASYNC_READ` (without it the kernel serialises reads per file), `PARALLEL_DIROPS`, `MAX_PAGES` with an explicit `max_readahead`, `READDIRPLUS` and `READDIRPLUS_AUTO`, `CACHE_SYMLINKS`, `SPLICE_WRITE` (and `SPLICE_READ` for the put mount), `HAS_RESEND`, `NO_OPEN_SUPPORT`, `NO_OPENDIR_SUPPORT`. Never assumed: anything the kernel did not offer. INIT negotiates down to the kernel's minor.

**Shape**
1. **ABI module.** Hand-written `#[repr(C)]` structs for the opcodes above, parsed and encoded with `zerocopy`-style derives (size and alignment checked). The uapi header of the farm kernel is pinned in the repository; **T-F1 builds a farm probe that prints `sizeof` and `offsetof` of every struct** and a Rust test compares them.
2. **tokio-native dispatch with reply affinity.** Each reader owns a **cloned** `/dev/fuse` descriptor (`ioctl(FUSE_DEV_IOC_CLONE)`) driven by `AsyncFd`. [K] A reply is matched by `unique` against the **processing queue of the device it is written to**; clones share the connection and the pending queue but each has its own processing queue. **So a reply is written to the descriptor that read the request, not to any clone.** `Replier` carries its originating device (`Arc<DevFd>` for `/dev/fuse`; a handle to the owning queue's submit channel for io_uring, since a SINGLE_ISSUER ring cannot be submitted to from an arbitrary task) and completes by `write(2)` on that fd (or by sending to the ring task). `Replier` is `Send + 'static`; completion from any task is allowed, on the originating device.
   - The `unique -> cancel token` entry is **registered at read time**, before `Fs::handle` runs; `Replier::complete` is a single atomic take, so exactly one of the real reply and the `EINTR` reply is ever written. An INTERRUPT read by another clone between read and park finds the entry and is answered correctly.
   - A reader reads into a pooled buffer, parses in place, and calls `Fs::handle(req, replier)` returning `Handled::Done` or `Handled::Parked`. Metadata and ready reads complete inline with no `await`; a parked operation copies the few fields it needs and returns the buffer. A tree session sets a tiny `max_write` (8 KiB read buffers); the put session sizes to `max_write`.
   - **Clones do not remove the shared pending-queue lock** [K]: all clones poll one input queue, so a new request wakes every `AsyncFd` and N-1 readers see EAGAIN. Clones reduce contention on the processing queues only. The benchmark (V3 with N readers) is expected to show a knee, not linear scaling.
3. **Interrupts.** INTERRUPT cancels a parked operation through the token table and replies `EINTR`; an INTERRUPT that arrives before its request is answered `EAGAIN`. A SIGKILLed build frees its parked read.
4. **io_uring transport (kernel 6.14+).** Registers `FUSE_IO_URING_CMD_REGISTER` entries per queue and replies with `COMMIT_AND_FETCH`.
   - **`fuse.enable_uring` is set at boot** (kernel command line or module options in the hostkeeper node image), never at runtime. At startup `tsfuse` reads it; if 0 it uses `/dev/fuse`. Both transports share dispatch code behind a `Transport` trait.
   - **Hybrid, not alternative [K, uncertain]:** INIT, early requests, INTERRUPT and FORGET may still arrive on the `/dev/fuse` path in uring mode. The library keeps one `/dev/fuse` reader alive beside the rings and routes replies to those requests on it. T-F0 checks INTERRUPT delivery in uring mode and the INIT-then-register order; conformance item 4 runs on the uring transport.
   - **Parked reads hold ring entries.** A cold storm can occupy every entry of a queue and stall metadata behind it. Mitigations: depth per queue large enough for the expected parked count, small payload buffers for the tree session, Prepare making cold reads rare, `uring_entries_parked`. **The storm that must pass is `depth x queues x 1.5` parked reads**, and metadata p99 must not grow.
   - **Memory:** the kernel has one queue per CPU (88 here) and every queue registers entries each with a header and payload buffer: `queues x depth x (header + payload)`. At depth 64 and 256 KiB payload that is about 1.4 GiB per mount, over 10 GiB at 8 job mounts. **io_uring is the default for a mount only if the registered memory is at most 64 MiB at the depth that passes the storm test** (otherwise a shared node-wide mount, or `/dev/fuse`); T-F0 computes the per-mount cost.
   - **No splice on this transport:** one more copy than splice from the page cache; measured.
   - **Takeover is not available on io_uring until proven:** see item 5. A mount that must survive a process update uses `/dev/fuse`.
   - T0 E records `kernel.io_uring_disabled`, the seccomp profile of job sandboxes, memlock limits, and the FUSE request timeout (`fuse request_timeout`, 6.14+: a parked cold read can abort the whole connection if an administrator set it).
5. **HAS_RESEND takeover with every clone retained.** [K] `FUSE_NOTIFY_RESEND` requeues requests from the processing queues of devices that still exist; when a process dies, `fuse_dev_release` ends the in-flight requests of each clone it alone held with an error. **The supervisor (fd store) therefore holds the original descriptor and every cloned descriptor**; the new process receives all of them (SCM_RIGHTS), skips INIT, and sends RESEND; resent requests are answered under their original unique id on the descriptor that holds them. Conformance item 7 kills the server with N > 1 readers and several parked reads on different clones and asserts no EIO and exactly-once completion. io_uring: **the T-F2 spike answered no on Linux 7.0** (`docs/measurements/2026-11-uring-takeover.md`, read from the kernel's source): RESEND walks only the processing queues of `/dev/fuse` devices, a request held by a ring entry sits on the ring queue's own list, and no other process can commit it (the entry's buffers are the dead process's memory), so it stays unanswered until the connection is aborted. uring is takeover-incapable; a new kernel reopens the question.
6. **Mounting.** Direct `mount(2)` with `fd=`, `rootmode`, `user_id`, `group_id` with `CAP_SYS_ADMIN`; otherwise `fusermount3`. Options: `ro` (tree), `nosuid`, `nodev`, `default_permissions`, `allow_other`.

**Which fuser tests are ported, by file** (counted by `#[test]` in the fork; T-F1 recounts them against the fork's last tag; about 65 unit tests in total):

| Fork file (tests) | Disposition | Reason |
|---|---|---|
| `src/ll/argument.rs` (5) | **ported**: fixed struct, NUL-terminated name, rest bytes, short buffer, missing NUL | Every opcode parse depends on it |
| `src/ll/request.rs` (4) | **ported**: short header, short body, INIT parse; `mknod` dropped | MKNOD not kept; per-opcode encode/decode tests for each kept opcode are written new |
| `src/ll/reply.rs` (15) | **ported:** empty, error, data, entry, attr, open, write, statfs, create, directory (byte-exact). **Dropped:** lock, bmap, xattr_size, xattr_data | Opcodes not implemented |
| `src/reply.rs` (20) | **ported:** serialize and reply_* for the kept opcodes and **async_reply** (central for parked replies; extended: completion on the originating device). **Dropped:** reply_lock, reply_bmap, reply_xattr_* | Same |
| `src/ll/notify.rs` (6) | **ported:** `resend`. **Dropped:** inval_entry, inval_inode, store, delete, poll | Immutable tree |
| `src/session.rs` (9) | **ported (7):** from_fd handshake; refusal of an initialised connection; serve queued request without INIT; resent request marked and answered under its id; resend header-only; stopper ends every loop and leaves the device open (generalized to N clones); non-blocking device waits (`AsyncFd`). **Dropped (2):** passthrough stack depth; backing id | Passthrough deleted |
| `src/time.rs` (2) | **ported** | Timestamps |
| `src/mnt/mount_options.rs` (2) | **ported** to our option rendering | Smaller option set |
| `src/mnt/mod.rs` (2) | **dropped** | libfuse argument strings |
| `fuser-tests/` mount, users, unmount, mount_util, fusermount, fuse_conf, canonical_temp_dir | **ported to Rust conformance tests:** mount/unmount, kill-then-unmount, `allow_other`, fusermount3 path, mount table | Same behaviours against our library on a farm node |
| `fuser-tests/` libfuse, bsd_mount, macos_mount, experimental, simple | **dropped** | About fuser, BSD/macOS or the example fs |
| `tests/*.sh` pjdfs, xfstests, passthrough, BSD/macOS and Dockerfiles | **dropped as kept tests** | Shell (tests are Rust), passthrough, BSD/macOS. Read-only semantics re-expressed in the Rust conformance suite; pjdfstest may run once as a farm job for the benchmark report |

**Kernel conformance tests, on a farm node** (a test target with exec properties for `/dev/fuse`, `CAP_SYS_ADMIN`, kernel >= 6.14 and, for uring cases, `enable_uring=1`; provided by **T-ENV**, never on a developer machine):
1. INIT: record what the kernel offers; negotiated down; the flag list above.
2. LOOKUP/GETATTR/READDIR/READDIRPLUS equal an expected tree, including `.` and `..`, a directory larger than one buffer, a missing name.
3. READ: ranges inside, spanning, at and past EOF, zero length, larger than `max_read`, **a range containing a hole, a short file**; bytes compared by hash; writev, splice and uring paths. The `out.len` of a spliced reply equals the bytes actually spliced.
4. INTERRUPT on every transport: a blocked reader killed with SIGKILL gets `EINTR`; an early INTERRUPT gets `EAGAIN`; an INTERRUPT read by a different clone than the request's.
5. **Parked replies completed from another task with N > 1 clones, each on its originating device, out of order.**
6. Multi-reader: N cloned descriptors under concurrent load, no lost or duplicated replies; stopping ends all readers.
7. **Takeover with N > 1 readers**: kill mid-load with parked reads on several clones, resume with every retained descriptor, exactly-once, no EIO (`/dev/fuse`; on uring, per the T-F2 answer, it confirms that a request in flight on a ring entry is not resent).
8. Unmount while busy, `abort` via `/sys/fs/fuse/connections/<n>/abort`, mount table consistency.
9. io_uring: queues register; with `enable_uring=0` the transport falls back; the storm of `depth x queues x 1.5` parked reads does not stall metadata.
10. ABI layout probe equals our structs. 11. A `PROT_WRITE` mapping of the passed state memfd fails.

### 5.3 Serving path
**Metadata from the mapped image.** The mount `mmap`s the tree-list image read-only (`PROT_READ`, `MAP_PRIVATE`; `MADV_RANDOM` for records). LOOKUP binary-searches the sorted directory records, GETATTR reads the node record, READDIR/READDIRPLUS encodes straight from records. Entry and attribute validity are long (the tree is immutable). The image file is immutable, never punched or truncated. **Page-in on a cold page of the image is an inline stall on a reader thread:** at tree open the mount calls `MADV_WILLNEED` on the record sections and, for a hot mount, `mlock`s the directory and node sections (bounded: the image is small relative to the pack data), so a first touch does not block on the shared cache SSD during heavy writeback. **File data is not mmapped.**

**Opens.** A tree session negotiates `NO_OPEN_SUPPORT`/`NO_OPENDIR_SUPPORT` and answers the first OPEN per connection with `ENOSYS` (that first handle costs one round trip; the kernel then stops sending OPEN). **T-F0 confirms** that the kernel keeps the page cache across opens in this mode, including for that first handle; if not, the library returns `FOPEN_KEEP_CACHE` on OPEN. The benchmark reports both regimes. The put session handles OPEN/CREATE normally.

**Reads (`/dev/fuse` transport).**
1. Resolve the node to its span(s), map to pack and cell range, run the 5.1 reader protocol. If every cell is `present`: reply from the pack's data fd.
2. **Reply by splice:** `splice(data_fd -> pipe)` then `splice(pipe -> /dev/fuse)` with `SPLICE_WRITE`. **The out header length is set from the bytes actually spliced:** loop until the full length is in the pipe; only a read at EOF may be short; a short splice or a hole read with a wrong `oh.len` makes the kernel reject the write with EINVAL and leave the request unanswered. **Pipes are sized with `F_SETPIPE_SZ` to at least `max_read` plus the header** (the default 64 KiB pipe fails a 256 KiB splice). Small replies (starting threshold 16 KiB) use `pread` into a pooled buffer and `writev`. [K, uncertain] `FUSE_SPLICE_MOVE` normally fails on page-cache pages and falls back to a copy, so splice saves one copy, not all; V4 measures it.
3. **Double page caching.** After a span is served the second copy is dropped only by the server on `durable` cells (4.8). **All three choices (splice versus pread, KEEP_CACHE/NO_OPEN, DONTNEED policy) are measured.**
4. Not ready: `Handled::Parked`; completion later on the originating device (5.2). A fill that is lost (4.6) completes every parked read of the tree with EIO.
- **io_uring transport:** same logic; the payload is copied into the ring entry's buffer and committed.

D-state is reduced, not eliminated; `fuse_d_state_threads` stays an acceptance metric.

### 5.4 Mount to tierstore protocol and runtimes
- One long-lived multiplexed connection per mount, request ids, out-of-order completion, **the server spawns a task per request**.
- Messages: `open-pack(tree_ref, pack_idx, holder) -> (data fd, state memfd, region)`; `wait(pack, off, len) -> ok | eio(cell) | lost`; `ensure(pack, cell_runs, class, rank)`; `release(holder)`. The handshake sends the counter memfd and the lifeline socket once (5.1).
- **Epoch.** The server stamps every connection and reply with a random u64 epoch; the mount drops replies from a previous epoch. Handles are numbered per server, so every request but the handshake carries the epoch the mount was handshaken under, and the server refuses one of another epoch (a `wait` or `ensure` with `UnknownHandle`, an `open-pack` or `release` with `NotHandshaken`): a previous server's handle is never taken for one of this server's. On reconnect the mount re-handshakes with the same holder id (counter memfd and lifeline are unchanged), re-sends `open-pack`, and re-issues every parked `wait` idempotently. Backoff 50 ms doubling to 2 s with jitter.
- **Coalescing of waits.** A `wait` covers the remaining span up to the next 8 cells; concurrent reads join the pending `wait`.
- **Runtimes are separate.** `tsfuse` readers run on **dedicated threads** (one current-thread runtime or plain thread per reader), because ready reads run `pread`, splice or an image page fault inline and may block; the protocol client runs on its **own** runtime; they are joined by channels. A reader blocked in the kernel cannot stall the `wait` completions that unblock other reads. T10a has a test where every reader thread is blocked in a fake `pread` while `wait` completions still flow.

### 5.5 No global locks across I/O
`pinning` and `lifecycle` are removed; state is per pack. The pin fix of komira-ai/tierstore#343 stays as a bridge until this lands.

### 5.6 macOS: same engine, same cache, NFS front
macOS is not a blocker for anything in this document.
- **Front.** On macOS tierstore-fs is the NFSv3 server (the komira-ai/nfsserve fork) running **inside the tierstore process**. It does not use fuser, so retiring the fuser fork (T-R) is not blocked by macOS. `tsfuse` and the FUSE conformance suite are Linux-only.
- **Read path.** The NFS `read`, `lookup`, `getattr` and `readdir` handlers call the same engine (`ensure`/`wait`), the same cell cache and per-cell verification, the same L2 reader (the mapped list image) and the same storage-tier client (10.6) as the Linux mount. A Mac has no storage drives; it is a pure client of the storage tier's read path. The NFS handler is async, so a not-ready read awaits readiness the same way a parked FUSE read does.
- **What differs, behind an `EntryStore` trait.** (a) The NFS server is in-process, so the state page needs no memfd, no SCM_RIGHTS and no counter memfd: an entry is an in-process object with atomic cell states and an in-process reader count per group; the same CAS rules and the same evictor ordering apply (the Dekker argument holds within one process; the loom model covers both implementations via the shared trait). (b) Hole punching uses `fcntl(F_PUNCHHOLE)` on APFS [K, T-M1 confirms on the Macs]; if a volume cannot punch, eviction falls back to whole-entry eviction only. (c) Durability uses `F_FULLFSYNC`. (d) No splice, no io_uring, no `O_DIRECT`: reads are `pread`, with `F_NOCACHE` where a measurement says it helps. (e) The journal, `recheck` and boot-id rules of section 8 are shared code.
- **L2 and macOS.** Macs run the same tierstore binary, so the reader-first rollout (3.5) covers them; the CAS service's dual emission keeps old Mac binaries working until updated.
- **Tests.** The `EntryStore` conformance suite runs against both implementations on a Linux node (in-process) and on the Macs (T-M1, T-M2). Mac builds stay release-only; these tests are not per-PR.

## 6. Prefetch
Mount provider `Prepare` at job assignment runs on the same engine.
1. Read the tree list through the cache; mmap PACKS and CELLS.
2. Planner (a pure function of list and ranges table; T7): per pack, the needed cell set, ranked shared packs first, then tree order (6.1). Output `Vec<Ensure>` with ranks.
3. **Gap rule in cells (per drive):** a part reads one file on one drive, so reading through a gap costs gap bytes at the media rate against one positioning. Merge needed runs across a gap of up to `gap_cells = ceil(pos_ms x media_MBps / 1.048576 / 1000)` absent cells: about 3 at 12 ms and 190 MB/s for a cold HDD, and 0 for a hot NVMe copy. T0-F sets `pos_ms` and `media_MBps`. Gap cells that are fetched are real cells: verified, written and `present`, not discarded.
4. **Whole-pack rule, budget-aware.** `cov` = needed fraction of the pack's cells after merging: `cov >= 50%` fetch the whole pack; pack <= 64 MiB and `cov >= 20%` fetch the whole pack; otherwise merged runs only. If the node's queued backlog exceeds 2 s of budget throughput, a would-be-whole pack is downgraded to runs. `amplification` (bytes fetched / needed) must stay under 1.5 in T10b or the constants move. A later demand on missing cells of a `Partial` entry fills gaps; it never starts a second entry.
5. Prepare is cancellable: a released holder demotes its unstarted parts to `background`; they are dropped only if no other holder wants them.
6. Prepare never gates launch.
7. Prepare takes a hold on every pack of the tree, and that hold lasts until the job's mount session is released (one hold per tree per job), so packs the job has not opened yet stay held in the node cache (7).

This replaces the S3 hydrate queue, the FUSE `prefetch all` threads and the Prepare groups.

### 6.1 The order of Prepare's fetches [D]
**The order.** Prepare ranks the needed cells of a tree with no learned or recorded input:
1. **Shared packs first:** packs whose `pack_flags` bit 1 (`shared`) is set in the list's PACKS record. The CAS service sets the bit on a pack that at least `shared_min` trees named when it wrote the list (default 8): toolchains and shared libraries, which most jobs open first and which are most often already present on the node (then they cost nothing). A pack is never treated as shared on a guess; until the CAS service sets the bit (T-K1c), the order is plain tree order. Bit 1 was a reserved bit, and a reader ignores `pack_flags` bits it does not know (3.1).
2. **Then tree order:** the spans of files in directory-record order.
3. **The whole-pack rule** of step 4 above decides, per pack, whether the pack is fetched whole or as merged runs; ranks order the parts either way.

Ranks feed the queue of 4.5 and the first part of 4.4 unchanged. A demand read always outranks Prepare (4.5), so a job that reads in another order than Prepare's waits only for its own cells.

**Prior art.** Ordering a lazily fetched image by what a workload reads first is published practice, and both systems take the order from **build time**, not from runs: eStargz records the files a workload reads in an "optimize" run when the image is built, places them first in the layer and marks the end of that prioritized set with a landmark file, so a reader prefetches up to the landmark; Nydus writes a prefetch table into the image's bootstrap from build-time hints and prefetches it at mount. Neither learns at run time.

**Later option, not designed here: a publish-time order hint in the L2 list.** If T10b shows that the order of fetches matters (its time-to-first-read, `first_read_seconds`, and the first-touch hit rate, `first_touch_total{ready}`, measured with the order above), the next step is an order hint that the build system writes into the L2 tree list when it publishes a tree: an additional, optional section of the list (an additive format change, 3.1) giving a rank per pack or cell run, used by the planner in place of tree order. It needs no profile store, no write at unmount and no fetch at Prepare. Until T10b's numbers say so, nothing of it is built.

## 7. Eviction, holds and admission (the node cache)
- **Accounting by cells.** Use = (`present` + `claimed` + `recheck`) cells x cell size, charged at claim time and refunded on failure. The critical-mark reserve check applies at claim time per cell run.
- **Eviction order.** (1) Whole unheld entries by LRU. (2) If only held entries remain, **cell-group eviction** of held entries by the 5.1 protocol (candidate cells in 16-cell groups untouched longest by the touch epoch; CAS per cell), counted in `punched_bytes_total`.
- **Admission at job assignment.** The jobscheduler asks `reserve(estimated_held_bytes)`. **`held` = the planner's needed cells minus cells already `present`.** Refuse when `held_cells_charged + estimate > 0.9 x budget`; refusal reasons `budget`, `enospc`, `oversize` are metered. A pack larger than the budget is refused only if its needed set exceeds `budget - held`.
- **Holds** are refcounts per pack: mount sessions (via `open-pack`, released by lifeline hangup or `release`), Prepare, and the fill while parts are in flight. One lease per session. Holds are local to the node cache: the storage tier never evicts for capacity (10.5), so it needs no `touch`; a held pack is lost only to a DELETE or to loss beyond the redundancy (4.6).
- **Startup.** No eviction and no scrub until a 60 s grace window since process start, so mounts rebind their holds. The data file is `flock`ed by its owner; a process that cannot lock an entry reads and evicts nothing from it. A directory lock `packs-v2/.lock` makes exactly one process the evictor.
- Poisoning is per cell (4.6). The storage tier has no capacity eviction; its hot tier reclaims only clean copies (10.5).

## 8. Crash safety and journal
- **Order of a cell's durability:** verify in memory -> `pwrite` -> state `present` (servable now) -> later, in a batch: `fdatasync(data file)` -> set the `durable` bit -> append a journal record `(ADD, run start, count, crc)`. A journal record never precedes durable data.
- **Punches are journaled before they happen, in batches:** after the evictor has observed every counter sum at 0 (5.1), it appends a **batch** of `PUNCH` records (run start, count) and fsyncs the journal **once**, then punches. A record is durable before its punch. Replay applies ADD and PUNCH in order, so after a crash a punched cell is never resurrected as `present`. A crash between the record and the punch only leaks blocks that a later refetch overwrites.
- **Batching.** One committer per node commits every 1 s or at 32 MiB pending, with one `fdatasync` per dirty entry file; never one commit per part or per demand sub-range.
- **Snapshot.** The journal is folded into `<hash>.meta` (present bitmap, pack_root, size, last use, **`boot_id`**) at entry close, at eviction and every 64 records, by write-temp, fsync, rename and directory fsync.
- **Restart.** Load the snapshot, replay the journal; only committed cells are restored. The snapshot and journal carry `/proc/sys/kernel/random/boot_id`. **Same boot and a process crash** (SIGKILL, oomd kill, panic): the kernel and the page cache survived, journaled cells follow durable data, so they are restored `present` with no recheck. **Different boot, or no boot_id (power loss, kernel crash, unclean marker):** every restored cell is `recheck`: the mount cannot serve it; the server re-hashes it on first demand or by a low-priority sweep and flips it to `present` or `absent`. This covers devices that reorder flushes; the cost is bounded by the cells actually read.
- `fdatasync` cost through the loop device is measured in T0 B and is one reason for section 11. The entry path includes `pack_root`, so a changed pack never reuses a file.

## 9. S3-front large objects
- Objects over `whole_fill_limit` (1 MiB on the node) that miss in the S3 front are routed to the pack engine with `PackId = (key, ETag)` and `digest=none`; for a stored object the ETag is its content-derived generation (10.2).
- **No stale mix.** The generation is known before the first part: from `LOCATE` (10.6), or from the `PackId` when the S3-front index holds the ETag, so every part carries `If-Match` from the start. (Objects read from silo, whose first part went alone to learn the ETag, were deleted with silo, T11b.)
- **Integrity.** For a `digest=none` object the storage tier keeps a crc32c in every record header and a **per-cell crc32c table per block** (in the block trailer and the shard footer, 10.2, 10.4); the serving node verifies the crc of every whole cell it serves for such an object (hardware crc32c, negligible CPU) and returns an error on mismatch, which the client treats as a verification failure (4.6: the other hot replica, or degraded read of the cold stripe). The tables are in the node's memory, so no extra seek happens; packs with cell digests are verified by the client instead. A bad copy is quarantined and repaired (10.3, 10.4), never rewritten in place.
- Ranged S3 GETs are served from `present` cells of a `Partial` entry and wait on missing cells at `demand` class. A write-through PUT's generation is the hash of its bytes, so it names the same `PackId` the storage tier will report. What a key may name depends on its bucket's mode (10.9). In an immutable bucket, the CAS service's, keys are create-only and a different object needs a different key. In a mutable bucket a PUT to an existing key is an overwrite that creates a new generation and so a new `PackId`; a fill that began on the old generation keeps its pinned `If-Match` and ends as a lost location (4.6) if that generation is gone. Objects up to `whole_fill_limit` stay in the node's `obj/`. T12 owns routing.

## 10. The storage tier: hot NVMe and cold HDD on three storage nodes (replaces silo)

Where each part of the owner's brief is answered: (a) components (formerly roles) 10.1; (b) hot tier 10.3; (c) cold tier 10.4; (d) movement 10.5; (e) configuration 10.14, including the hot-only mode; (f) S3 semantics 10.9; (g) metadata, ordering and recovery 10.2, with the metadata store itself (schema, client, failover, backups, incarnation, fsck, upgrades) in 10.16; (h) capacity 10.15; (i) why not SeaweedFS, Ceph or silo 10.12, and what SeaweedFS 4.54 does, read in its source, 10.17; (j) migration 10.10 and 10.11. Units are defined in 10.2. Reading from build nodes is 10.6, the drive scheduler 10.7, membership and audit 10.8, risks 10.13, the reconciler 24.5.

### 10.1 Components and scope [D]
**A node runs components, not a role.** The tierstore daemon on any machine runs the set of components its configuration enables, one per configuration section (10.14). Each component has declared dependencies, startup validation refuses a set whose dependencies are not met, and **any set whose dependencies are met is valid**. No enum, configuration key or code path says "storage node", "build node" or "combined node": those are the names of three example configurations in `docs/examples/` (section 21), and the code asks only "is this component enabled", never "what kind of node is this".

| Component | Section | What it runs | Requires |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Hot store** | `[hot]` (with `[index]`, `[promote]`, `[gc]`, `[metadata]`, `[reconcile]`, `[health]`) | the hot tier on its NVMe or SSD (10.3), the key index (derived, 10.2) and the cache of the block table, the metadata client (`tierstore-meta`, 10.16), re-replication and hot scrub | `[node]` (id, cluster id, private address) and `[metadata]` (the metadata store's host list and credentials, 10.16) |
| **Cold store** | `[cold]` (with `[destage]`, `[rebuild]`, `[drive]`) | the cold tier on its hard drives with one `DriveSched` per drive (10.4, 10.7), destage, rebuild, scrub | hot store |
| **Peer service** | `[peer]` | the routes of 10.6, including `GET /store/v1/membership` (10.8) | hot store |
| **S3 front** | `[s3]` | the S3 subset of 10.9 and section 9 | `s3.backend = "store"`: hot store (the front is this store's door, and routes an immutable-bucket PUT to its writer node); `s3.backend = "cache"`: node cache and fetch engine (the front is a caching proxy of the storage cluster, section 9). Its keys are the S3 front's existing keys |
| **Node cache** | `[cache]` | the entry store, single flight and claims, holds, eviction and admission, the journal (sections 5 to 8; `tierstore-cache`), and the node-wide shared cache socket (5.4) | none |
| **Fetch engine** | `[fetch]` (with `[client]`) | membership bootstrap, `LOCATE`, the part queue, budget, verification (section 4; `tierstore-client`) | node cache; and one of: `[client]` (seeds or a membership file, 10.8), or the hot store in the same process (its membership document, 10.8) |
| **FUSE front** | `[fuse]` | `tsfuse` tree and put-directory mounts (5.2, 5.3) | fetch engine |
| **NFS front** | `[nfs]` | the NFSv3 server (5.6; macOS) | fetch engine |
| **Prepare provider** | `[prepare]` | the jobscheduler mount provider and Prepare (section 6): plans, takes holds and calls `ensure`; it mounts nothing itself | fetch engine (and so the node cache, where holds live) |


**Checks that are not set dependencies.** Three rules depend on the configuration's values or the platform, not on which sections are present, and are checked beside the table: the hot store needs the peer service when the membership document names more than one node; the FUSE front is refused on a host that is not Linux; the NFS front is refused on a host that is not macOS (the only platform it is built and tested for, 5.6).

A component runs if and only if its section is present (an empty table enables it with defaults, except `[metadata]`, whose host list and password file have no default; its timeouts do), and the daemon logs the set it runs at startup and exports it as `tierstore_components{component}`. **A node that runs the hot store is a storage node of the membership document** and is the only kind of node listed there (10.8); nothing else in the store distinguishes nodes. Whether a node holds durable data is therefore a fact of its configuration: a node without the hot store holds none, its cache can be lost and refilled, and nothing the store promises depends on it. Such a node reaches the storage cluster over the private network, never a drive directly, and never the metadata store: clients speak the S3 front and the peer protocol only.

**The examples.** `docs/examples/later-storage.toml` (hot store, cold store, peer service, S3 front with `backend = "store"`; the three storage nodes of LATER), `docs/examples/later-build.toml` (node cache, fetch engine, FUSE front, S3 front with `backend = "cache"`, Prepare provider; the 31 build nodes) and `docs/examples/now-combined.toml` (all of them on one machine except the NFS front; the six nodes of NOW). When one process runs both the fetch engine and the peer service, the client's transport to its own node id is an in-process call to the peer service's handler instead of a socket, so a part whose bytes are on that node is read without a network hop; this is a choice of transport by address, made because the membership document's node id equals `node.id`, not a branch on a kind of node. Its cache stays a cache: durability is still only the hot and cold stores' and the metadata store's.

**The CAS service** PUTs packs and lists to the S3 front of any storage node (an endpoint list, config); for its bucket, which is an immutable bucket (10.9), the front routes the PUT to the object's writer node (10.2). Build nodes read through the engine (sections 4 and 6), which asks the storage nodes for a location and then fetches parts straight from the node that holds the bytes (10.6).

**One daemon per node, plus the metadata store on three nodes that run the hot store.** The system is the tierstore daemon on every node and one small replicated database beside it: **the metadata store**, a dedicated Postgres cluster of three members, managed by Patroni with a three-member etcd, on three of the nodes that run the hot store (the three storage nodes on LATER; three of the six combined nodes on NOW; 10.16). Postgres, Patroni and etcd are packaged and updated by hostkeeper and are not components of the daemon; the hot store component requires `[metadata]` to reach them. The tierstore nodes do not elect a leader or implement consensus themselves: the quorum that orders block-level facts is Postgres's (with Patroni's fencing), and the data path needs none. Every ownership decision is a pure function of the key and the membership document (10.2, 10.8); every durable fact about bytes is in a replicated append-only segment, a sealed block or a shard footer on a node's disk, and every durable fact about *which* bytes are current, where their copies are and what is deleted is a row of the metadata store (10.2, 10.16). The few decisions that two nodes could make differently (the sealed length of a segment, a stripe's descriptor, a stripe's free, a delete against a relocate, the current version of a key of a mutable bucket) are single Postgres transactions, each a compare-and-set on one row. The operator, not a protocol, decides that a node is `out`, through verbs that write the metadata store (`tierstore store node out`, 10.8).

**Scope.** The store is a store kind (`store.kind = "tiered"`) for the farm's pack store, and a general-purpose S3 object store with the subset of 10.9: PUT, range GET, HEAD, DELETE and prefix LIST in two bucket modes, an immutable mode (create-only; the CAS service's fast path, with no metadata store on its PUT or GET path) and a mutable mode (ordinary overwrite, DELETE, `If-Match` and `If-None-Match`, linearized by the metadata store). Correctness never depends on a key being content-derived; the CAS service's keys are, and its bucket takes the fast path. A proxy with a hot tier in front of an S3-compatible cold store (configurations A and C of tierstore-design.md) keeps its `s3:` or `dir:` cold store, unchanged; its configuration check refuses `tiered` as a cold store by name.

### 10.2 Units, data model, ordering, index and recovery [D]
**Units.** Each mechanism has one unit, and the units are not mixed:

| Unit | Size | It is the unit of | It is never the unit of |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Object** (a pack, a list, a small object) or a **byte range** of it | any | read, fetch, LOCATE, `If-Match`, promotion, delete | placement or erasure coding |
| **Record** | one object of at most `hot.large_min` (4 MiB), plus a 64-byte header | append, replication batch, index entry, record id | a read beyond its own range |
| **Block** | at most `cold.block_bytes` (1 GiB) | storage, sealing, placement, erasure coding, destage, rebuild, scrub, GC, a row of the metadata store | read, fetch or promotion |
| **Cell** (client) | 1 MiB of pack bytes | verification on the build node (section 3) | anything in the store |

A cold read reads exactly the object's byte range from the one drive that holds it (the offset comes from the index); a degraded read decodes only that range from `k` shards (10.4); a promotion copies only the object (10.5). **A block moves whole only on destage, rebuild or a reclaim rewrite, never on a read.**

**Two kinds of truth [D].** The store keeps its facts in two places, and every fact has exactly one home:

| Fact | Home | Why there |
|---|---|---|
| The bytes of a record, its header, its trailer entry, a shard and its footer (which carries the stripe descriptor), a large file, a commit marker | the node's disk (**data-path records**: segment records, trailers, shard footers; the layouts of T-I2, otherwise as in revision 8) | bytes are written at disk speed and never wait for a database; a node can scan them without any index |
| Which blocks exist and their state, which nodes hold their copies, the sealed length of a segment and its epoch, the stripe descriptors and shards, exact live counts, tombstones, relocations, the current version of each key of a mutable bucket, membership, health facts, bucket definitions, the repair queue | **the metadata store**, Postgres (**metadata truth**, 10.16) | these are the decisions two nodes could make differently; one transaction decides them |
| The key index (`key -> entry`), the block table in memory and on disk | each node, as a **derived cache** of the two rows above | rebuildable from the node's own disk plus Postgres rows; never the authority |

The metadata store holds *no object bytes and no per-object row for an immutable bucket*: at 1.4 M keys today and 1e8 at the design point the key index stays on the nodes, and Postgres holds one row per block, stripe, shard and tombstone and one per live key of the mutable buckets only (10.16 bounds each).

**Object, key, generation, version.** An object is a key and a byte sequence. Its **generation** is the 128-bit blake3 of its bytes, computed while they stream in; it is the ETag, the `If-Match` value of 4.6 and the identity of "the same bytes". The optional PUT header `x-ts-expiry: <class>` (a small integer chosen by the CAS service, for example the week an object is expected to die) names an **expiry class**; it only groups objects physically (10.5) and never deletes anything. In a **mutable bucket** each key also has a **version**, a u64 assigned by the Postgres transaction that commits a write of the key: it orders the writes of one key, and is an internal order, not an S3 version id. An immutable bucket's keys have no version: a key has at most one live record until it is deleted. What a PUT of a key that already exists does depends on the bucket's mode (10.9): in an immutable bucket `If-None-Match: *` is `412`, the same generation is an idempotent `200` and a different generation is `409 KeyImmutable`; in a mutable bucket the write replaces the head, subject to its preconditions.

**Record id, buckets and their two modes [D].** The identity of one stored copy-set of one object version is its **record id**, `(block_id, offset)`: the block the record sits in and its offset within the block. A large hot file has a block id of its own, allocated at upload, and its record id is `(that id, 0)`. **Destage preserves offsets**, so a record id survives destage (the object's offset in the hot segment is its offset in the cold block, 10.5); only a **relocate** (pack assembly from large files, a GC rewrite) creates a new record id, and it is one Postgres transaction that adds a `relocations` row (below, 10.5). Bytes at a record id never change: a block is immutable once sealed and a segment's acknowledged prefix is never rewritten, so **any copy of a record id is current**, whichever node holds it.

A **bucket** is a row of the metadata store with a name and a **mode**, `immutable` or `mutable`, fixed when the operator creates it (`tierstore store bucket create <name> --mode immutable|mutable`; changing a bucket's mode means a new bucket and a copy). Nodes cache the bucket table, which changes rarely: a new bucket is pushed by the verb and polled. The two modes, in one paragraph (the S3 behaviour of each is 10.9):
- **Immutable** (the CAS service's bucket, and the fast path): a key holds at most one live record until it is deleted; PUT and GET do not touch the metadata store; the writer's all-node key check, the replication ack rule and the node indexes decide a create; DELETE is a metadata transaction that writes tombstones naming record ids. Correct for content-derived keys, or for any use with one writer per key; the one narrow case that is not linearizable (a create race across an epoch change) is stated and closed in 10.9.
- **Mutable** (the general rule: ordinary S3 semantics, nothing required of the keys): the current record of each key is the row `heads(bucket, key)`, and every write, conditional or not, is ordered by one Postgres transaction, so the last writer is the last committed transaction. The bytes are still appended to hot segments, replicated at the ack rule and read from any copy; only the *order* of writes to a key and its listing come from Postgres.

**Replica set and writer.** For a key `K` the storage nodes are ranked by rendezvous hashing: `score(n) = H(K || node_id)` (blake3 reduced to u64; equal weights), `rank(K)` = nodes by descending score. The key's **replica set** at PUT time is the first `hot.replicas` **live** nodes of the ranking whose hot device is `in` (10.8), taken as an unordered set, and its **writer** appends and replicates while the other members mirror. **`rank(K)` chooses, it never locates [D, 24.1]:** the replica set of the segment a key is written into is chosen when the segment opens and is **recorded** in the block table (the row of the metadata store, written lazily, 10.3); from then on the record's location is that row (and, after destage, its stripe and shard rows), which changes only by a committed transaction. A dead or added node changes no existing location; `rank(K)` only picks the candidates for new placements and tells a client which node to ask first. **The writer rule:** when the replica set is every live storage node (our cluster: `hot.replicas = 3` on three nodes), the writer is the first live node of `rank(K)`, so the three nodes share the writes evenly and each writes its own open segments; when the set is smaller than the cluster, the writer is one member chosen by a fixed rule of the set that makes every node writer of the same number of sets (with three nodes and pairs, each node writes exactly one pair; T-S1 defines it). With three nodes every probe is at most three nodes, so there is no probe-depth rule: any lookup can ask all of them (10.6). The key's **index nodes** are its replica set at PUT time: with `hot.replicas = 3`, every storage node indexes every key.

**Record.** A record is: header of 64 bytes (magic, flags, key length, data size, generation, crc32c of the data, **bucket id** (4 bytes), **`written_ms`**, crc32c of the header), key bytes, data, padding to 16 bytes. `written_ms` is the wall-clock time in milliseconds at append on the writing node; it is informational (the `Last-Modified` of an immutable-bucket object, and audit) and **is never used to order anything**: no decision of the store compares two `written_ms` values, so clock skew cannot change an outcome. Records are self-describing, so a segment can be scanned without any index. A second record kind, the **commit marker** `(bucket, key, version, record id)`, is appended to the writer's open segment after the transaction that commits a write of a mutable-bucket key (10.9); it carries no data, and exists so that `tierstore store metadata fsck` can rebuild the `heads` table from disk alone (10.16). **A marker has a lifetime.** A commit or delete marker is counted in its block's `live_count` and `live_bytes` while it is the newest marker of its key's head, and a block (segment or stripe) is not dropped or freed while it holds such a marker. The head row records where its marker lies (`heads.marker_block`, `marker_offset`, set by a small second transaction after the marker append, conditioned on the head's version, 10.16), and the transaction that supersedes the head (a put, a DELETE or a relocate) decrements the old marker's block as it does for the old data record. A relocate, a pack assembly or a GC rewrite of a mutable-bucket record appends a fresh marker `(bucket, key, version, new record id)`, durable on `hot.min_acks` copies, before the old block may be dropped or freed (10.5), and the old marker's location is tombstoned in the same transaction (10.16 transaction 4: a `tombstones` row for its `(marker_block, marker_offset)`, which the seal subtracts or the state rule decrements, like the marker superseded by a put). A delete marker stays live until a later put of the key, or until its block would be freed; the delete is then carried first by re-appending the marker. The fsck rule is: the highest marker version per key wins, and a head is rebuilt only if its record exists (10.16).

**Block.** A block is a sequence of records followed by a **trailer**, `block_bytes` long at most (default 1 GiB). Two kinds: a **segment block** (a sealed hot segment: small-object records in arrival order) and a **pack block** (assembled at destage from large objects, each record 4 KiB-aligned; 10.5). The trailer lists every record of the block sorted by (bucket, key) (bucket, key, generation, offset, length, crc32c, `written_ms`), then a per-cell crc32c table (one per 1 MiB cell of the block's bytes), then a footer (entry count, offsets, the block's blake3). The trailer is a deterministic function of the block's bytes, so two copies of the same bytes have the same trailer. A block is immutable once sealed. **No object straddles two blocks, except a chain:** an object larger than `block_bytes - cold.block_reserve` (1 MiB reserved for its header, alignment and a one-entry trailer) is stored as consecutive blocks each holding one slice of that object and nothing else (an object of 2.5 GiB is three blocks); a range read touches only the blocks the range covers. An object of at most that size always fits in one block alone, so "just under 1 GiB" never needs a chain.

**Ids [D].** `block_id` = (allocating node id: 8 bits, per-node counter: 56 bits); `stripe_id` likewise. **Ids stay node-allocated**: there is no central allocator, so a node with intact disk history opens a new segment while the metadata store is down (10.3; a node on a fresh device is the exception, below). **An id is never reused:** the counter is persisted with the replay mark, which lags the ids handed out by up to one flush interval, so **every start** (not only a fresh device or `metadata fsck`) sets the counter above the maximum id for its node id seen in the `NODE` file, the replay mark, the open and sealed segment files and `big/` names on its own NVMe, the `blk/*.idx` files and, when the store is reachable, the Postgres block and stripe rows, before its first allocation. **Only a node with intact disk history may allocate while the store is down:** its `NODE` file, its counter or replay mark and its segment files are present and its node incarnation is unchanged, so ids above its own disk maximum cannot collide with its own earlier ids. A node on a fresh or replaced hot device, or with a regenerated node incarnation, or whose `NODE` file or counter is missing, **allocates no id and opens no segment** until it has read the maximum block and stripe id for its node id from Postgres and from at least one peer (the peer's segment files and `blk/*.idx`); it then starts above that maximum and above its own disk maximum. While the store is down such a node answers reads but takes no new hot writes, and those go to other nodes. (The alternative, reserving ranges of N ids by writing the new high-water mark to the `NODE` file with `fsync` before handing any out, is not used.) A node on a fresh device, like `metadata fsck`, allocates above the maximum id seen in the metadata store and on disk across the nodes (24.15, 10.16), and only after it has read them. The node's `NODE` file also records its **node incarnation** (24.2), the **metadata incarnation** it runs under and the highest `txn_seq` it has seen (10.3, 10.16); both defend against a metadata store restored to an earlier point, the one way an id or a deletion could otherwise be forgotten.

**The index [D].** Each storage node keeps an **ordered map** `key -> entry` for every key of an **immutable bucket** whose replica set at PUT time included that node, plus a **tombstone entry** for every key whose record it has been told is deleted (below). An entry is: bucket id, generation (16 B), record id (block id 8, offset 4), length (4; a chain entry stores the total size and the first block), `written_ms` (8), flags, and, after a promotion, the promoted copy's location (10.5). **The index is derived only [D]: nothing in it is a fact that is not also on the node's disk or in the metadata store.** It is kept in an embedded ordered key-value store on the NVMe (`fjall`, an LSM-tree crate under a permissive licence), not a hand-rolled in-memory map with snapshots. Reasons: at 1e8 keys an in-memory `BTreeMap` costs about 180 to 220 bytes per key [I] (12 to 20 GB per node) and a consistent snapshot of a mutating map every few minutes is either a copy-on-write structure, a stop-the-world copy or a log mark we would have to build; the embedded store gives ordered range scans for LIST, point reads for `LOCATE` from its block cache, and continuous persistence. **The store is a cache of the bytes and of the tombstone rows, not the source of truth:** its own durability is not relied on (it runs with its journal flushed only at the replay mark below), and every fact in it is rebuildable from segments, trailers, `blk/*.idx` files, shard footers and the tombstone and relocation rows of the metadata store. T-S3 measures bytes per key on disk and in cache, point-read latency and bulk-load time at 1.4 M and 1e8 keys; `redb` (a copy-on-write B-tree, same licence class) is the alternative behind the same `Index` trait if `fjall` fails those measurements. A key is indexed on **`hot.replicas` nodes** (all three in our cluster), so any one node loss, or two, leaves every key indexed on a live node. **Objects do not move when they are destaged**: an object's record id is the same in the cold block, so destage changes the block's state (a Postgres transaction on its row), not the index (10.5). Only a GC rewrite or the assembly of a pack block from large files repoints an entry (a **relocate**, 10.5). The records of a **mutable** bucket are not entries of this index: a read finds the head in the metadata store (10.9) and then the record id in the block-table cache, and a node's `blk/*.idx` file and trailers still list them for rebuild and fsck. The **exact live counts** per block (`live_count`, `live_bytes`), which the free protocol of 10.5 reads, are columns of the block's row in the metadata store, decremented by the very transaction that writes a tombstone (below); a node keeps no counts of its own to be believed.

**Block table (a cache of Postgres rows) [D].** The block table is the rows `blocks` (one per block: `block_id`, kind, writer, replica set, epoch, state, sealed length and crc, blake3, live count and bytes, expiry class, stripe), `hot_copies`, `stripes` (`stripe_id`, `k` and `m` or replicas, expiry class, coordinator, state) and `shards` (`stripe_id`, shard index, block, node, drive, file, state), together the **stripe descriptor** as rows, in the metadata store (10.16). Every storage node caches the rows it needs in the same embedded store as the index, with each row's `row_version`, so that LOCATE and reads are answered from the node without a round trip (a bound per mode [E], from one base that 10.15 and 10.16 refer to: the 85% fill limit, 229.5 TB raw, which is about 214 k raw 1 GiB units including parity; at `4+4` that is about 107 k data-block rows (parity shards have no `blocks` row), about 27 k stripes and about 214 k shard rows, and at `4+2` about 143 k data-block rows, 36 k stripes and 214 k shard rows; at `cold.replicas = 2` one record per block, about 107 k; a few hundred thousand small rows at most). **The cache decides nothing.** A node refreshes a row when it misses, when a compare-and-set against it fails, and by a poll for changed rows every `metadata.tomb_poll` (2 s) (there is no push of block, stripe, shard or `hot_copies` rows; the pushes are `tomb-apply` for tombstones, the membership verb's epoch push and the bucket verb's push of a new bucket, the bucket table is also polled, and the membership epoch is polled every 5 s, 10.8); a stale row can only misdirect a read (bytes at a record id never change, so a copy that has moved answers `404` and the reader re-reads the row), never change a state, because every state change is a transaction conditioned on the row's version in Postgres. While the metadata store is unavailable a node keeps serving reads from its cache.

**Tombstones [D].** A DELETE of an immutable-bucket key is one Postgres transaction that inserts, for each live record id of the key, a **tombstone row** `(seq, block, offset, bucket, key, generation)` and decrements that block's `live_count` in the same transaction (10.9). **A tombstone names a record id, not a key and not a time**, so there is nothing to order: a record is live iff no tombstone names its record id, and a re-PUT of the same bytes after a delete is a new record with a new record id, live whatever happens to the old one. Nodes learn tombstones by **push** (`POST /store/v1/tomb-apply` from the deleting front, which acknowledges the DELETE once `N - min_acks + 1` nodes have applied it durably, below) and by **pull**: every `metadata.tomb_poll` (2 s) a node reads the tombstone rows after its **one cursor**, and a node returning from a stop reads from its cursor before it leaves `indexing`. **The cursor is gap-free by transaction visibility.** `seq` is handed out at INSERT, not at commit, so a transaction holding a lower `seq` can commit after one holding a higher, and a cursor that was only the highest `seq` applied would skip it for good. The cursor is therefore a horizon over transaction ids: every `tombstones` row carries `xid xid8 NOT NULL DEFAULT pg_current_xact_id()`, and a poll is one statement, `SELECT ... FROM tombstones WHERE xid >= :cursor AND xid < pg_snapshot_xmin(pg_current_snapshot()) ORDER BY seq`, after which the node sets its cursor to that same snapshot's `pg_snapshot_xmin`. Every transaction below the horizon has committed or aborted and its rows are visible, so every tombstone committed before the cursor has been read, and a cursor never passes a row whose transaction may still commit; a row withheld because its transaction is still open is read by a later poll (one long open transaction delays the pull, and `metadata.txn_timeout` bounds it). `seq` stays the row's identity and the order of the apply; the apply is idempotent per record id, so reading a row twice is harmless. There is one cursor, not one per origin; the same rule applies to every other table a node polls by cursor (the changed rows of the block table, below). **A cursor belongs to one metadata incarnation:** a point-in-time restore moves the database's xid counter back, so a saved horizon would sit ahead of the xids of everything written after the restore, and adopting a new incarnation therefore resets every cursor to 0 (10.16). **Before a node indexes a record by any route** (replication receipt, index replay, seal adoption, destage or GC repoint, rebuild), it checks whether a tombstone names the record id, in its `index/tomb-applied` file or in an in-memory set of tombstones applied since the last cursor advance; if one does, it indexes the record as a **tombstone entry**, not live (a tombstone pushed by `tomb-apply` to a replica that has not yet received the record, which was acknowledged on only `min_acks` copies, leaves no entry to convert, and the record arriving later by replication must not be indexed live). **Applying a tombstone** is idempotent per record id: the node turns every index entry whose record id is the named id, or an old id that reaches it through `relocations` (`old -> new`, chains included), into a tombstone entry (the key reads as deleted, and LIST never shows a tombstone entry). A DELETE after a relocate names the new id (it follows `relocations`), while a node that has not yet refreshed its block rows still holds its entry at the old id, so the apply resolves relocations in both directions: the node finds the old ids from its cached `relocations` rows, which it refreshes with the block and stripe rows (the lookup by new id is a node-side map), and where it has not yet cached the relocation row, `tomb-apply` and the pull fetch the `relocations` rows whose `new` is the tombstoned id from Postgres before acknowledging. **Invariant:** a tombstone entry exists on every node for every old id of a relocated, tombstoned record, and the `relocations` rows outlive the tombstone for this lookup (they are kept until the old block is freed). The repoint of a destage or a GC rewrite (10.5 step 7) never skips silently: before moving an entry from the old id to the new one, the node checks whether the new id is tombstoned (its `tomb-applied` file or the tombstone rows) and, if so, turns the entry into a tombstone entry; otherwise it repoints it. Only an entry that already is a tombstone entry, or whose key now holds a different record id (a replacement), is left alone; **the durable record:** a tombstone entry of the index is a cache only for rows still derivable from Postgres, and the one fact that is not (a tombstone applied by `tomb-apply` and acknowledged, which must survive a Postgres restored to before the DELETE) has its own storage. On `tomb-apply` the node appends `(seq, block, offset, bucket, key, generation)` to the fsynced file `index/tomb-applied` and acknowledges only after that fsync, and the `204` counts only such acknowledgements; the node trims the file when it drops its own tombstone entry (the block is freed). A tombstone that finds no entry to convert is kept in `index/tomb-applied` until the record's block is freed, not only until an entry is converted. This write is exempt from the rule that the index is flushed only at the replay mark, which holds only for entries that are derivable from Postgres rows. `metadata fsck` and invariant I10 read this file, not the in-memory index, and a node whose file is lost stays frozen and reported until fsck has run; a tombstone that names a record id that was **relocated** is followed through the `relocations` row `(old record id -> new record id)` to the new one, and a relocate skips any record whose id is already tombstoned (writing a tombstone for the new record id its already-written trailer lists), both under a row lock on the source block (`SELECT ... FOR UPDATE`) so that a relocate and a delete of one record serialize in Postgres. Blocks are immutable, so the trailer of a block still lists a deleted object; the tombstone row is what keeps it deleted when an index is rebuilt. **A tombstone is deleted exactly when what it names is gone:** a tombstone is deleted only in the transaction that sets `freed` on the block it names (the free of a stripe's blocks, 10.5, or the `dropped` to `freed` step of a hot block or large file, which waits on the same gate), and that transaction also deletes the relocation rows whose old block it is. A drop (`dropped`) deletes nothing. A node drops its own tombstone entry when its block-table row says the block is freed and the files are unlinked, and a node that reads a block row in `dropped` or `freed` removes every index entry of that block, live or tombstone; index replay and rebuild skip blocks whose row is `dropped` or `freed`. There is no drop rule, no per-origin cursor and no report from the nodes: the database holds the one copy.

**Recovery after a restart (g).** No object is looked up in an external database on the read path, and an index is always rebuildable from bytes the node or its peers hold plus rows the metadata store holds:
1. **Replay mark.** Every `index.flush_interval` (default 1 minute) a node flushes the embedded store and records, in the same flush, the **replay mark**: per open segment the committed length the index reflects, and the tombstone cursor (the horizon above), each together with the metadata incarnation it belongs to; a mark of an older incarnation is discarded, so the cursors restart at 0 (10.16).
2. **Replay.** For each open segment, scan records from the mark to the last record whose crc verifies; for each segment sealed since, read its trailer; for large files, `readdir` of `big/` (the file name carries key hash, generation, size and block id, so no file is opened); then read the **tombstone rows after the cursor** and the **relocation rows** since the mark from the metadata store and apply them. The record scan of one open segment is at most a few hundred MB of NVMe reads. The entries of `index/tomb-applied` are replayed as well: a node that crashes right after its `tomb-apply` acknowledgement keeps the delete, and a Postgres restored to before the DELETE does not bring the key back (fsck reads the file, 10.16; T-S3 tests it).
3. **No usable store (damaged or new NVMe).** Rebuild from the trailers: hot blocks from the node's NVMe; cold blocks from the sorted index files `blk/<block_id>.idx` that every index node keeps on NVMe (written at seal, replicated with the block), and if those are lost too, from the trailer at the end of the data shard of each cold block (one read per block, parallel across the 45 drives, minutes [E]); then read **every tombstone row and every relocation row** (a few hundred thousand at most plus the tombstones of the unfreed blocks) from the metadata store and apply them: an entry whose record id is tombstoned is dead, and an entry whose record id was relocated is the relocated one. There are no stamps to compare. If two trailer or `blk/*.idx` entries name one key and neither record id is tombstoned, the **lower record id is live** and the higher one is tombstoned through Postgres by the reconciler (24.5); until then every node serves the lower id, so that nodes agree. The rebuild is a bulk load in key order. A node on a fresh device first allocates its ids above the maximum seen (the ids paragraph) and the block-table cache is re-read from the metadata store. Peers need not stream anything: the node's own disk and the metadata store hold every input.
4. **A node the free gate stopped waiting for rebuilds before it leaves `indexing` (a durable-fact trigger, not a cursor comparison).** When a node starts or returns and the `health` table holds a `lost` row for it with no `returned` row it has already processed, or its `nodes.tomb_applied` or `incarnation_seen` predates that `lost` row's `since`, it first runs the holdings report of 24.4 (which inserts the rows that are legitimately pending for hot segments and large files), then rebuilds its index from the trailers and `blk/*.idx` of the blocks whose rows are not `dropped` or `freed`, treats a cold or pack block that still has no row as absent (it drops its entries), applies the tombstone rows still present with its cursor reset to 0, and only then reports `returned` and leaves `indexing`. This is correct however long the node was away, because `freed` rows of segments and large files are kept while such a node is `lost` (10.16) and the tombstones of freed blocks are gone with the blocks.
5. Until a node's index is built and its tombstone cursor is current it answers **`indexing`** (never a miss, 10.6). A node that restarts while the metadata store is unavailable cannot read its cursor's rows and stays `indexing`; the other index nodes of every key answer (a minority read, 10.9), and the node leaves `indexing` when the store returns.
Every index node applies the same records, relocations and tombstones, all of them naming record ids, so the index nodes of a key converge on the same entry, and an index entry is as durable as the record it points to.

**Tests this settles (section 14).** A tombstone applied to a node that lacks the record, then the record replicated to that node: `LOCATE` from that node answers deleted. A PUT on writer A, then a DELETE while A is down served by B, then an index rebuilt from trailers and the tombstone rows: the tombstone row names the record id, the rebuilt entry for that record id is dead, and the key stays deleted. A re-PUT of the same bytes after a DELETE, then a rebuild: the new record has a new record id that no tombstone names, so it is live, and the old record stays dead. A delete that races a relocate of the same record: both lock the source block, the second to run sees the first (the delete follows the relocation, or the relocate skips the tombstoned record and tombstones the new record id its already-written trailer lists), and the key never comes back.

### 10.3 The hot tier on NVMe [D]
**Purpose.** Absorb every write at NVMe speed, serve recent and re-read data fast, and hold data until it is sealed and destaged. It is **replicated only** (`hot.replicas = 3` with `min_acks = 2` in our cluster, decision Q2; there is no erasure coding on the hot tier: it is small, and the point is speed). It is deliberately small against the cold tier (10.15).

**Layout** (one xfs filesystem on the node's NVMe, `noatime`):
```
/mnt/nvme/ts-hot/
  NODE                     node id, node incarnation, cluster id, format version, metadata incarnation, highest txn_seq seen, Postgres system id, buckets record (id, name, mode)
  seg/<block_id>.seg       a WRITE segment: open, or sealed (dirty until destaged, then clean); a replica's copy has the same name and the same bytes
  seg/<block_id>.idx       sorted trailer index of a sealed segment
  big/<hh>/<khash>.<gen>.<size>.<block_id>.obj    a large object, whole file (key hash, generation, size, block id in the name)
  big/.<khash>.<nonce>.up              a large upload in progress
  cache/<boot>.<seq>.seg   a CACHE segment: promoted copies only (clean, single copy, never destaged, 10.5)
  blk/<block_id>.idx       index file of every block this node indexes, hot or cold (kept after destage)
  index/                   the embedded ordered store (key index, block-table cache, replay mark) and `tomb-applied`, the fsynced record of applied tombstones (10.2)
```
There is no tombstone log, seal record or descriptor file on the NVMe: those facts are rows of the metadata store (10.2). On LATER the same NVMe also carries the node's metadata member on **its own partition**, outside `ts-hot/` and outside this layout (10.16, below).

The `NODE` file is written at first start and rewritten atomically. The `buckets` record (id, name, mode of every bucket) is rewritten atomically when the node polls the bucket table, so `metadata fsck` can take the name and mode of a bucket id from the nodes (10.16). The node incarnation is a random 64-bit value that identifies this node's life (kept across restarts, regenerated when the node starts on a fresh hot device, 24.2); it is not the metadata incarnation, which is the value of `cluster.incarnation` the node runs under; the highest `txn_seq` is persisted at most every 10 s and **before any destructive action**, so a restored metadata store whose `txn_seq` is below it is detected (10.16).

**Write segments and cache segments are never mixed** [D]: `seg/` and `big/` hold new data, which must be destaged; `cache/` holds copies of objects whose durable copy is cold, which may be dropped at any time. The destager reads only `seg/` and `big/`, so a promoted copy can never be destaged twice.

**Small objects: Haystack-style append-only segments.** An object of at most `hot.large_min` (default 4 MiB) is appended as a record to the **open segment of its writer, replica set and expiry class**: there is no size class and the replica set is unordered, so on three nodes there are three open segments per expiry class in the cluster (with `hot.replicas = 3` one per writer node, each replicated to the other two; with pairs one per pair), at most 24 with `gc.classes_max = 8`, and 3 today (every object is class 0 until the CAS service sends the hint). Records are written with buffered `write` at 16-byte alignment (a 1 KiB object costs about 1.1 KiB, not a 4 KiB block) and made durable with `fdatasync`. A new segment file is preallocated to `hot.seal_bytes` if T0-S finds that an `fdatasync` that extends a file costs more than one into preallocated space on the U.2 device (it measures appended, `fallocate`d and pre-written files); the default is whichever is cheaper. **A segment opens without waiting for the metadata store**: its block id is node-allocated (10.2), it starts at epoch 1 under its writer, and its `blocks` and `hot_copies` rows are inserted lazily, batched every few seconds, by the writer's metadata client; if the store is down they are inserted when it returns, and the replicas know the segment from its replication stream in the meantime. **A hot copy whose row is missing is a pending insert, not an orphan; any holder may insert it** (10.16 transaction 1, 24.4).
- **Group commit.** One committer per open segment, leader-follower with no timer: while a batch is being made durable, arriving records queue; when it finishes, the next batch starts at once. A batch is at most `hot.batch_max` (4 MiB). The latency of a small PUT is one local `fdatasync` in parallel with one replica round trip, not a timer.
- **When a record becomes visible.** In an **immutable bucket** the index entry becomes visible after the ack rule is met (below), under the key lock; a second PUT of the same key waits on the first's outcome and then compares generations (`200` for identical, `409 KeyImmutable` for different, `412` for `If-None-Match: *`, 10.9). In a **mutable bucket** the record is durable on `hot.min_acks` copies but not visible until the metadata transaction that makes it the key's head commits (10.9); order between writers of one key is that transaction's, not a lock's.
- **Power-loss protection is a stop condition [D].** The group-commit latency, the ack rule, `hot.batch_max` and the metadata store's default `synchronous_commit = on` are sized on the assumption that an `fdatasync` on the U.2 NVMe costs tens of microseconds, which needs power-loss protection. **The owner states that the LATER NVMe drives have power-loss protection**; T0-S and the hardware audit (T-S20) verify it on every drive, and **a drive found without it is refused by the hardware audit: it is not silently retuned.** **NOW is the other case:** the SATA SSDs have no power-loss protection, so on NOW every `fdatasync` and `fsync` is honest (`hot.min_acks`, the segment and large-file commits, the metadata store's `fsync = on` and `synchronous_commit = on`), a small PUT waits for a flash program on two nodes (milliseconds [I]), and a metadata commit costs a few milliseconds too (10.16); the design accepts that latency for NOW and **nothing turns fsync off** on either profile.

**Replication protocol (the segment is the unit).**
1. The writer appends the batch locally and streams the **same bytes at the same offsets** to every other member of the segment's replica set: a message carries `(segment id, epoch, start offset, bytes, running crc32c of the segment through this batch)`.
2. A replica appends at that offset, `fdatasync`s and acknowledges with its committed length. It rejects a batch whose start offset is not its committed length, whose running crc does not match, or whose epoch is **below the highest epoch it has durably recorded for that segment** (a fence, below).
