3. **Ack rule [D].** A PUT is acknowledged to the client when the record is durable on **`hot.min_acks` copies** (default = `hot.replicas`; the writer's own `fdatasync` counts as one). Every acknowledged record is therefore on every copy of the default configuration. `min_acks` below `replicas` is allowed and documented as weaker (a record acknowledged on fewer copies is lost if the node that held it alone is lost before the segment is re-replicated). For a mutable bucket the acknowledgment of the PUT to the client additionally waits for the metadata transaction and the commit marker (10.9).
4. **Seal = fence + one compare-and-set on the segment epoch [D].** A segment's sealed length is decided once, by a compare-and-set of its row in the metadata store; a **fence** on the copies makes the old writer unable to gather an acknowledgment past what the seal will keep. There are no ballots and no acceptors. A segment opens at **epoch 1** under its writer.
   - *Fence.* The sealer (the writer for a size, age or early seal; a replica taking over from a writer that stopped answering) sends `POST /store/v1/fence(segment, epoch e+1)` to every copy. A copy **records the fence epoch durably if it is higher than the epoch it holds**, refuses any later batch under a lower epoch, and answers its committed length, its **verified** length (the crc-verified prefix, with its running crc) and the epoch it holds. A copy that already holds an epoch above the one asked for answers `fenced(epoch_held)` without lowering it; the sealer then retries with `e` = the highest epoch seen in the answers or in the row (whichever is higher), fencing at `e+1`, and counts a copy toward the quorum only if it recorded `e+1` or already held exactly `e+1`. With fences from at least `replicas - min_acks + 1` copies (1 copy at `min_acks = replicas`, the default; 2 of 3 at the cluster's configured `min_acks = 2`; in both any `min_acks` copies include a fenced one), the old writer can no longer gather an acknowledgment, so what remains acknowledged is on the fenced copies.
   - *Choose.* Among the `q` copies that answered (its own included), the sealer takes the **`r`-th longest** crc-verified length, `r = max(1, q - replicas + min_acks)`. At the default `min_acks = replicas` that is the shortest: every copy holds every acknowledged record, so none is cut, and every answering copy already holds the chosen bytes, so the seal never depends on a tail that only one copy has. Below the default, a record acknowledged on `min_acks` copies is kept whenever at least `r` of them answered; a copy shorter than the length fetches the difference in Converge. A copy whose verified length is below its committed length has damage inside acknowledged bytes and counts as not answering for Choose (it is repaired from a peer by the scrub path). The chosen length is never below the sealer's own last acknowledged offset; this is an assertion (a violation is a bug: the sealer does not commit and the segment stays fenced, and writes go to a new segment). The writer's last acknowledged offset is at most this length, so an early seal by the writer and a take-over by a replica choose by the same rule.
   - *Commit (one transaction).* `UPDATE blocks SET state = 'sealed', epoch = e+1, sealed_length = L, sealed_crc = C, record_count = ..., live_count = ... WHERE block_id = $s AND state = 'open' AND epoch <= e` (with the `e` of the Fence bullet; an insert if the row is not there yet, in the same statement). **A fence is recorded only on the copies (the durable fence epoch); the row stays `open` until this compare-and-set, and `fenced` is a condition of the copies, not a row state.** **Exactly one sealer's update changes the row**: the first compare-and-set to match wins, because the `state = 'open'` predicate makes a second sealer's update match zero rows whatever its `e`; a sealer that loses reads the committed row and converges to it. The live count is set from the trailer: the records within the sealed length minus the tombstones that already name the block.
   - *Converge.* Each copy makes its bytes equal to `[0, length)`: it truncates an unacknowledged tail beyond the length, or (a copy that did not answer and is shorter) fetches the missing bytes from an answering copy and verifies the running crc. A copy truncates or appends **only after reading a committed `sealed` row**; a fenced copy of a sealer that did not commit keeps its bytes. It appends the trailer (a deterministic function of the bytes) and checks the block's blake3 against the row's.
   - *Index.* Records within the sealed length that a copy had not indexed (written durably on every answering copy but not acknowledged, for example because the client's ack was lost) of an **immutable** bucket become visible; that is the same object a client retry would write, so it is allowed, and `hot_unacked_records_adopted_total` counts it (the property that follows, for a later DELETE or re-PUT of the key, is stated in 10.5 step 5). Records beyond the length were never acknowledged and never indexed. A record of a **mutable** bucket becomes the head only through a committed transaction, so an unacknowledged mutable record within the length with no head and no commit marker is garbage, tombstoned by the orphan sweep (10.9).
   **Two sealers under a partition** (the writer A seals early at its last acknowledged offset while the replica B, cut off from A, seals at its longer committed length) cannot both commit: the update of one row decides, and the loser converges to the winner's committed length. A side of the partition that cannot reach the primary of the metadata store (the minority) can fence but cannot commit; its segment stays fenced and its writes fail the ack rule (503) or go to a new segment, which is the documented behaviour of a minority. Destage, re-replication and trailer distribution act only on a committed seal.
   **When the metadata store is unavailable the fence still happens.** The old segment stops taking writes, the writer opens a new segment with a node-allocated id (a node with intact disk history; a node on a fresh device, with a regenerated node incarnation or with its `NODE` file or counter missing opens none until it has read the store and a peer, 10.2 ids), and **PUTs to immutable buckets continue**; the commit waits, a fenced segment is not destaged until its commit succeeds, and `hot_fenced_unsealed_blocks` counts them, per node, from the local fence epochs (the row stays `open`; headroom is stated in 10.13). **No wall-clock lease exists anywhere on the data path:** every decision is a fence on the copies or a compare-and-set in the database.
5. **Replica loss: seal and roll.** If a replica stops acknowledging for `hot.replica_stall` (default 5 s [I]), or the membership says it is `down` or `out`, the writer **seals the open segment early** (step 4, which raises the epoch) and opens a new segment with a replica set of live nodes, so writes continue at full replication at once, without copying a prefix first. The early-sealed block is under-replicated until **re-replication** (a `background` copy of its file to another live node, 10.7, planned by the reconciler, 24.5; part of milestone S1, 18) restores `hot.replicas` copies; `hot_under_replicated_blocks` counts it. With fewer than `hot.min_acks` live nodes, small PUTs answer 503 `Retry-After`.
6. **Seal triggers [D].** A segment is sealed when it reaches `hot.seal_bytes` (default = `cold.block_bytes - cold.block_reserve`; a record that does not fit seals the segment and opens the next), or by an early seal above, or by age: past `hot.seal_max_age` (24 h) **only if it holds at least `hot.seal_min_bytes`** (256 MiB), otherwise when it is older than `destage.max_age` (3 days). So a slow class does not produce a stream of small blocks (at today's 34 GB/day a pair fills 1 GiB in about 2 to 3 hours; a class at 1/8 of that reaches the floor in about 5 hours [E]). A sealed segment is never written again; the block's state moves to `sealed` (the file name does not change).

**Large objects: whole files.** An object over `hot.large_min` is a file `big/<hh>/<khash>.<gen>.<size>.<block_id>.obj`; the writer allocates its **block id at upload** and its record id is `(block id, 0)` (10.2). The writer streams the body into `.up` on its NVMe (`fallocate` when the length is known, 4 MiB writes) and, at the same time, to each other member of the replica set, which writes its own `.up`; the writer computes the generation while streaming and sends it in a trailer that every node checks against its own hash (a mismatch unlinks the `.up` and counts as an abandoned copy). Each node commits with `fsync`, a create-only check by the index under the key lock (an immutable bucket; a mutable bucket orders by its head transaction instead, 10.9), `renameat2(RENAME_NOREPLACE)` and a directory `fsync`. The PUT is acknowledged on `hot.min_acks` committed copies (and, for a mutable bucket, after its head transaction and commit marker). A replica that lags for `hot.put_copy_stall` (10 s [I]; time in the drive queue counts, a client pause does not) is abandoned and the writer re-sends the finished local file to another live node, so no giant buffer is held; if too few copies remain the PUT answers 503 and the `.up` files are removed when the writer's connection closes. A crash leaves only `.up` files, removed by the startup sweep. A large file has no seal: it is immutable from its rename, and its `blocks` row (kind `large`) is inserted lazily in state `sealed` with `live_count` 1, `record_count` 1 and `sealed_length` = size (10.16 transaction 1).

**Reads on the hot tier.** A record is read with `pread` at `(offset, length)` from the node's copy (the page cache is advised away on sealed segments after a read, `fadvise(DONTNEED)`, so the NVMe's pages do not crowd out the node's job; T0 B decides O_DIRECT); a large object with `sendfile` or `pread` ranges. Any copy can serve; the client picks one by advertised queue wait (10.6). Every record read verifies the record crc32c (a failure serves another copy and queues repair of that segment).

**Hot restart.** Section 10.2 recovery; a replica that was down comes back, reads the tombstone rows after its cursor and the relocation rows from the metadata store and applies them, staying `indexing` until it has done so; reads the block rows of the segments it mirrors, adopts any committed seal (truncating or catching up its copy), and catches up the open segments it still mirrors from their writers (a segment whose writer is gone and that has no committed seal is fenced and sealed by a replica, step 4). It needs no pull from any peer's log: no node holds a log that another needs.

**Hot scrub.** Sealed segments, large objects and cache segments are re-read in the background once per `hot.scrub_period` (7 days, NVMe is fast), verifying every record crc and the block's blake3; a bad copy is repaired from another copy (or, once destaged, from the cold tier), never rewritten in place; a bad cache segment is dropped.

**The NVMe is the node's single failure unit for hot data [D].** One NVMe per storage node holds that node's hot copies, its index and its block-table cache, and, **on LATER, the node's member of the metadata store on a partition of its own (10.16)**, so losing the device is also losing one of the three metadata members. Losing it is, for hot data, losing the node: its hot copies are re-replicated from the other copies (10.8), its index is rebuilt (10.2 step 3) and its block-table cache is re-read from the metadata store; and, for the metadata store, losing one member of three, which the other two keep serving (the synchronous standby and the primary or the other standby commit without it) until Patroni re-creates the member from the current primary (`pg_basebackup`, runbook in 24.12). On NOW the metadata member lives on the SATA SSD of one of three combined nodes, with the same consequence. With `hot.replicas = 3`, dirty data survives the loss of one NVMe plus a loss on a second node once its third copy has landed (a second NVMe also loses a second metadata member, so metadata commits stop until one returns, while immutable PUT and GET continue); before that (the replication lag of one batch) it is on two devices (10.13, Q2). Losing a second metadata member before the first is re-created stops metadata writes (503) while reads from the node caches and immutable-bucket PUT and GET continue (10.13).

**Failure domain.** `hot.failure_domain` is `node` or `drive` (a single-node install with several NVMe devices uses `drive`); replicas are placed on distinct domains. Configuration validation refuses `replicas` greater than the number of domains.

### 10.4 The cold tier on HDD [D]
**What it holds.** Only **sealed, immutable blocks** (10.2): sealed small-object segments and pack blocks assembled from large objects. Nothing small and nothing random is ever written to a cold drive: every write is a whole shard file of up to 1 GiB, written sequentially; every delete is the `unlink` of a whole shard file. This is the f4 / Windows Azure Storage shape (seal, then erasure-code lazily), chosen because it makes both the read and the write cost one sequential stream per drive and suits CMR and SMR drives alike (the drive type is a T0 fact).

**Redundancy is a configuration of the cold tier** (10.14): either `ec = { k, m }` with `block_bytes`, or `replicas = N`, with a failure domain `node` or `drive`.

**Stripe and shard (EC).** A **stripe** is `k` sealed data blocks of one expiry class plus `m` parity blocks. Block `i` of the stripe is **data shard `i`**; the shard file of a data shard **is the block's bytes, byte for byte**, so an object at `(block, offset)` is at file offset `offset` of one data shard. A stripe whose data blocks differ in length is encoded as if the shorter ones were extended with zeros up to the longest (parity length = the longest block rounded up to 4 KiB); the zeros are never stored. Reed-Solomon over GF(2^16) (`reed-solomon-simd`, MDS: any `k` of the `k+m` shards rebuild the rest), encoded in windows of 1 to 4 MiB per shard streamed from the `k` sources, so memory is `(k+m) x window`. The crate's decoder restores **data** shards only [V, checked before T-S8]: a missing parity shard is rebuilt by restoring the data windows (or reading them, when all data shards survive) and re-encoding, with no extra reads.

**Contiguous layout and "one read touches one disk".** In the healthy read path a read of an object of at most `block_bytes` is one sequential read of one data shard on one drive: records never straddle shards (they lie inside a block), and parity is not read. A block holds many records, so one drive's stream serves a whole pack. The only objects that cross a shard boundary are those larger than `block_bytes`, which are chains (10.2) laid out at block boundaries, so a range read still touches only the shards of the blocks it covers (a sequential read of a 3 GiB pack streams three shards, usually on three drives, in order). Striping is deliberately not used (HDFS-7285 stripes at 64 KiB to 1 MiB to make small files efficient and to parallelize large ones; here small objects are already packed by the hot tier, and contiguous blocks win on read cost and rebuild simplicity). The price is that one block is one spindle's bandwidth (about 190 MB/s [I]); promote-on-read (10.5) is the answer for a block that is hot.

**On-disk layout** (xfs on each HDD, `noatime,nodiratime,inode64`; keep xfs: extent allocation, extent-size hints, `fallocate`, cheap unlink of large files):
```
/mnt/hdd07/ts-cold/
  DRIVE                                     drive_id (UUID), node id, cluster id, format version, filesystem UUID
  s/<hh>/<stripe_id>.<idx>.shard            one shard of a stripe (idx 0..k+m-1); replicas mode: <block_id>.copy
  s/<hh>/.<stripe_id>.<idx>.<nonce>.up      a shard being written
  FAILED                                    present once the drive is failed (10.7); an operator removes it
```
- **Shard file:** data region at offset 0 (a data shard: the block's bytes including its trailer; a parity shard: parity bytes), padded to 4 KiB, then a **footer** of at least 4 KiB: format version, stripe id, shard index, `k`, `m`, the block ids and lengths of the `k` data blocks, the placement of every shard of the stripe at write time, a crc32c per 1 MiB cell of this shard, the blake3 of this shard, crc of the footer. The stripe's rows in Postgres (10.16) can be rebuilt by `metadata fsck` from any one footer of a stripe never converted, and from any parity footer of a converted one (10.5).
- **Writes are sequential and whole-file [D].** `fallocate` the exact length, `O_DIRECT` writes of 4 MiB at depth 2 from one write stream per drive (10.7), then footer, `fsync`, `renameat2(RENAME_NOREPLACE)` from the `.up` name, directory `fsync`. A crash leaves only a `.up` file, removed by the startup sweep. A 1 GiB `fallocate` with a 64 MiB extent-size hint keeps the file contiguous (T0-F C-5 measures extents per file).
- **Space accounting:** fill is the filesystem's used bytes over its capacity (`statfs`), so bytes that are not the tier's (any other file on the drive's filesystem) count against `fill_limit`; the tier's own share, the sum of allocated bytes of its shard files (`st_blocks`), is reported beside it. The marks (10.14): `cold.fill_limit` 85% (no new shard is placed on a drive above it) and a rebuild reserve (10.15).

**Placement: failure-domain aware, on 3 nodes x 15 drives.**
- Inputs: the `k+m` shards of a stripe, the node domains (3), the drives of each node (15), `max_shards_per_domain` (default `ceil((k+m) / domains)`).
- **Nodes:** the nodes are ordered by `H(stripe_id || node_id)`; shard `i` goes to node `order[i mod 3]`. With 4+4 that is 3 + 3 + 2 shards, with 4+2 it is 2 + 2 + 2; which node holds the lighter count rotates with the stripe id, and which shard indexes are parity rotates the same way (RAID-5 style), so data reads and fill spread over the nodes.
- **Drives within a node:** the node's `in` drives below `fill_limit` that hold no other shard of this stripe, by weighted rendezvous score `-free_bytes / ln(H(stripe_id || drive_id) / 2^64)`; the best-scoring drives take the shards. Weights are free bytes, so fuller drives are chosen less.
- **Invariants a placement must satisfy** (checked at write time and by a background **placement audit**, which repairs a violation by moving a shard like a rebuild does): the `k+m` shards are on distinct drives; no node holds more than `max_shards_per_domain`; and, **for the node domain, `m` is at least `max_shards_per_domain`** (so the loss of any node leaves at least `k` shards). A configuration that fails the last rule cannot survive its own declared failure domain and is **refused at startup** (a 10+4 code on 3 nodes puts 5, 5 and 4 shards on the nodes and fails the rule; that is SeaweedFS 4.54's default code, 10.17). A single-node install sets `failure_domain = "drive"` and the rule is `m >= 1`.
- The placement is **recorded** in the stripe's `stripes` and `shards` rows (10.16) and in every shard footer; it is never recomputed, so a membership change moves nothing by itself.

**Choice of code for 3 nodes** (n = k+m shards of a stripe on distinct drives; overhead = n/k; usable = 229.5 TB raw at the 85% fill limit divided by the overhead [E]):

| Scheme | Shards per node | Overhead | Logical capacity | Survives a node loss | Node loss plus one more drive | Reads to rebuild one shard |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `replicas = 2` | 1 copy on 2 of 3 nodes | 2.0x | 114.8 TB | yes | no (the second node's copy is the only one) | 1 |
| `replicas = 3` | 1 per node | 3.0x | 76.5 TB | yes | yes | 1 |
| `4+2` | 2 / 2 / 2 | 1.5x | 153.0 TB | yes, exactly (m = 2 = shards per node) | no | 4 |
| `6+3` | 3 / 3 / 3 | 1.5x | 153.0 TB | yes, exactly | no | 6 |
| **`4+4` (this cluster)** | 3 / 3 / 2 | 2.0x | 114.8 TB | yes, with one spare shard | **yes** (m = 4 >= 3 + 1) | 4 |
| `5+4` | 3 / 3 / 3 | 1.8x | 127.5 TB | yes, with one spare | yes | 5 |
| `10+4` (SeaweedFS default) | 5 / 5 / 4 | 1.4x | 163.9 TB | **no** (a node holds 5 > 4) | no | 10 |
| LRC (12,2,2), two-level codes | needs about 16 domains | 1.33x | 172.1 TB | **no** | no | 6 (local) |

- **No code is cheaper than 1.5x if a node must be survivable on 3 nodes** [E]: losing a node loses `s` shards, so `m >= s >= n/3` and `n/k >= 1.5`. This holds for every code (LRC and product codes only cut repair reads), so the real choice is 1.5x (a node loss uses all parity) against about 2x (a node loss plus a drive).
- **Recommended default for this cluster: `4+4`** (Q1; the tradeoff is stated in the decision table). Reasons: (1) after a node loss, `4+4` still has one spare shard, and **it can be rebuilt onto the two survivors at 4 + 4 shards, each node holding `m = 4`, so it survives the next node loss too**; `4+2` would have to put 3 shards on a survivor, which exceeds its `m`, so a lost node stays unrecoverable to full tolerance until a replacement node arrives. (2) Today under 2% of the cold capacity is in use (1.34 TB live of 115 TB logical), so the second spare costs idle disk and no capacity anyone needs; the code is recorded per stripe, so once logical use passes half of the guarded capacity (about 36 TB at `4+4`, 10.15) the operator switches the default to `4+2` for new stripes and a background re-encode (read `k`, write fresh parity, same shard layout) can convert old ones. (3) Repair reads are 4 shards, the same as `4+2`. (4) A node reboot, which hostkeeper rolls through one at a time, leaves `4+2` at zero parity for its duration and `4+4` at one. **For other installs** the schema default is `4+2` on 3 or more domains with `failure_domain = node`, and `replicas = 2` or `3` on 2 or 3 domains when the operator prefers no parity math.
- **Not offered by the schema as a recommendation:** codes wider than the domain count allows, EC with overwrite (every cold write is a whole new stripe), striped layout.

**Replication variant (`cold.replicas = N`).** There are no stripes: each sealed block is destaged alone as `N` whole copies `<block_id>.copy` on distinct domains by the same node and drive placement rule; a read uses one copy, a repair is a copy, `4+4`-style parity math is absent. Everything else (sealing, index, GC at block granularity, the scheduler) is shared.

**Degraded reads [D].** A read of a data shard is degraded when its drive is `stalled`, `failed`, `suspect` with a slow queue, or answers an error or a crc mismatch, or its node is `down`.
1. The node that owns the shard (if the node is up and only the drive is bad), or else the **reconstructor** (among the live nodes holding shards of the stripe, the one whose `k` shortest advertised drive queues are shortest, named by LOCATE, 10.6), serves the request. The requested range is rounded to 4 KiB.
2. It reads the same range from `k` other shards of the stripe (live data siblings first, parity as needed; a sibling shorter than the range contributes zeros), preferring shards on its own node and on drives with the shortest advertised queue, and decodes the missing range.
3. It verifies the result against the record's crc32c (small objects) or leaves cell digests to the client (packs, 3.3); on a mismatch it re-reads whole cells and checks each survivor's cell crc32c from the footers, excluding a corrupt survivor.
4. Cost: `k` reads of the range from `k` drives, latency bounded by the slowest of them, **admitted as `k` stream charges** on those drives' schedulers and charged `k` parts by the client (4.3); **hedging** (4.6) starts a degraded read in parallel for a `demand` read of at most 4 MiB that has no first byte by its p95 baseline, within the hedge budget and only with `k` free source slots. The whole shard is **not** rebuilt on a read; that is the rebuilder's job. A degraded read of a block queues its stripe for repair (10.8) if one of its shards is missing or bad.
- The degraded fraction is small and under our control: a single drive failure degrades 1/45 of the cold reads while its stripes rebuild; a node down (a planned reboot or an outage) degrades the reads of the data shards on that node, about 1/3 of cold reads, for the outage [E]. **Load with one node down:** each degraded read costs `k = 4` range reads, so the total drive-read volume is 2.0 times the healthy volume, carried by 30 of the 45 drives (3.0 times the healthy load per surviving drive), and the cold read capacity falls from about 8.5 GB/s to about 2.8 GB/s [E] (4.3); 17.2 item 5 measures it against a pass bar. No source publishes f4's or Backblaze's degraded-read share, so none is quoted.

**Section 24 governs when repair starts, its order and its commits** (the reconciler, 24.5: margin-triggered repair, the job queue, and the one transaction that commits each repair); this subsection keeps the rebuild mechanics and arithmetic.

**Rebuild of a lost drive (declustered, no hot spare) [D].** A drive is **lost** when it is `failed` for `rebuild.drive_delay` (default 10 minutes; a drive that returns clears it) or removed by the operator.
- Every stripe with a shard on it is queued, **fewest surviving shards first** (a stripe at exactly `k` survivors first). For each, the rebuilder reads `k` surviving shards **in full and sequentially** (a `background` stream on each source drive), decodes the missing shard in windows, and writes it as one sequential shard file to a drive chosen by the placement rule (same node preferred, never a drive of the stripe, never above `fill_limit`); then it commits the new location as one transaction on the stripe's `shards` row (node, drive, file; a compare-and-set on the row's version, refused when the stripe is `freeing` or `freed`, 10.5) and unlinks nothing (the lost drive is gone). The stripe's margin is restored as each shard commits.
- **Spare space is spread, not reserved on a drive:** the capacity guard keeps one node's raw plus one drive per remaining node free (10.5, 10.15), so a rebuild always has somewhere to write. Rebuild writes may exceed `cold.fill_limit` on a drive, up to `cold.rebuild_fill_ceiling` (92%), while any stripe is below its tolerance: weighted placement can push single drives past the limit when the free space is uneven, and a rebuild must not stop for that. New stripes from destage never exceed `fill_limit`.
- **Time [E, inputs: 190 MB/s per drive, 25 Gbit/s links, 1 GiB shards; every shard rebuilt costs `k = 4` shard reads and one shard write].** At the capacity guard (the most a drive holds unless the operator disables it) a drive holds about 142.8 TB / 45 = 3.2 TB, about 2,950 shards: reads 4 x 3.2 = 12.7 TB and writes 3.2 TB, spread over 44 drives, are about 0.36 TB per drive, **about 32 minutes at full spindle speed and about 2.1 hours at the default 25% share** (`rebuild.share`). A drive at `fill_limit` with the guard off (5.1 TB) is about 0.58 TB per surviving drive, about 51 minutes, and **3.4 hours at 25%**. The network carries the reads that cross nodes (about three quarters of them [I]), about 9.5 to 15 TB, 0.9 to 1.4 hours on one 25 Gbit/s link. At today's fill (under 2%) it is minutes.

**Rebuild of a lost node [D].** A node is **lost** when the operator marks it `out` (a held step) or, by default, after `rebuild.node_down_after` (default 24 hours) of being `down`; a reboot or an outage shorter than that rebuilds nothing, so a rolling update never starts a rebuild storm.
- Hot blocks: re-replicated to the surviving nodes (a file copy per block, 10.3).
- Cold shards: every stripe that had shards on the lost node is rebuilt as above onto the two survivors, **subject to the same placement invariant** (`<= m` shards per node, so 4 + 4 for `4+4`; a `4+2` stripe cannot be restored beyond four shards on two nodes, and the audit reports `cold_stripes_below_tolerance`). With a replacement node, the placement audit later moves shards back to restore 3 domains.
- **Time [E].** One decode per stripe restores every shard the stripe lost on the node (2 or 3 at `4+4`; the decoder restores data shards and parity is re-encoded from them, so no extra reads), so reads are `k` shards per affected stripe, not `k` per lost shard. At the capacity guard the cluster holds at most 142.8 TB raw, about a third of it on the lost node: **47.6 TB to write**, and reads of 4 of the 8 shards of every stripe, **71.4 TB**. Over the 30 surviving drives that is about 4 TB per drive: **about 5.8 hours at full spindle speed and about 1 day at a 25% share**. The network moves at most the 119 TB of reads and writes between the two survivors, about 5.3 hours over two 25 Gbit/s links, so the drives at their share are the bound. Afterwards the survivors hold 142.8 TB of 180 TB raw (79%), below `fill_limit`, with one drive's worth of free space each for the next drive failure. At today's fill it is minutes.
- **Margin 0 while a node is down [D].** With a node down, `4+4` stripes that had 3 shards on it have one spare; a drive failure in that window leaves them at margin 0, and a margin-0 rebuild reads exactly the four survivors, so one latent sector error loses the stripe. Therefore, while any node is `down`: (1) each surviving drive's scrubber first verifies the shards of stripes at margin 1 or less (chosen from the stripe rows the node caches, 10.16, so no extra I/O), so a latent error is found and repaired while the spare still exists; (2) a stripe at margin 0 is rebuilt at `prepare` class ahead of every other repair; (3) `cold_stripe_margin{margin="0"}` above zero raises an alert (the operations document carries the rule). `rebuild.node_down_after` (24 hours) still decides when the node's own shards are rebuilt.

**Scrubbing [D].** Each drive's scrubber, a `background` stream with its own bounded rate, re-reads every shard file sequentially once per `cold.scrub_period` (default 30 days; a full 6 TB drive is 8.7 hours of reading), verifying every cell crc32c against the footer and the shard blake3. A mismatch quarantines that shard (`.bad`), counts `cold_scrub_bad_total`, and queues the stripe for repair: the shard is **recomputed from the other shards and written to another drive** (never repaired in place). A weekly sample (`cold.deep_scrub_fraction`, default 1% of stripes per week) additionally decodes a parity-consistency check: it reads all `k+m` shards of the stripe and verifies that re-encoding the data shards gives the stored parity (it catches a parity shard whose crc matches but whose bytes are wrong, for instance a defective encoder).

### 10.5 Write path and movement: PUT, destage, reclaim, promote, delete and GC [D]
**Four movements, four rules.** **Destage** copies dirty data (sealed write segments and large files) to the cold tier, soon after sealing, as sequential writes; it never deletes anything. **Reclaim** (eviction of the hot tier) only drops clean bytes, by one unlink or one index-entry removal; it never copies and never waits on a hard drive. **Promotion** copies one object from cold into a cache segment; the cold block stays the durable copy. **GC** frees dead blocks whole and rewrites a block only when it is mostly dead. Un-destaged data is never reclaimable; if destage lags, writers get backpressure, and reads never stall on it.

**PUT, front to back.**
1. The CAS service sends one streaming `PUT /<bucket>/<key>` (Content-Length if known, else chunked; `x-ts-expiry` optional) to the S3 front of any storage node. The front reads the bucket's mode from its cache of the bucket table (10.16; a bucket that does not exist is `404 NoSuchBucket`; buckets are created by the operator verb, not by the API), computes `rank(K)`, the replica set and its writer (10.2), and forwards the stream to the writer (a front that is the writer keeps it).
2. **Size decides the path:** Content-Length at most `hot.large_min` (4 MiB) is a record in an open segment; larger is a whole file (10.3), which gets its own block id at upload (its record id is `(block id, 0)`). A chunked PUT is buffered up to `hot.large_min + 1` bytes first, so a body that ends inside the buffer takes the small path. Both paths append a record (or file) durable on `hot.min_acks` copies; what makes it visible differs by bucket mode (steps 3 and 4).
3. **Immutable bucket (the CAS service's bucket; Postgres is not on this path).** While the body arrives, the writer asks every live storage node for the key (10.2: `POST /store/v1/key`; the answers give the live record ids of the key and the tombstones the nodes applied, naming record ids). It runs the 10.3 protocol and acknowledges when the record or file is durable on `hot.min_acks` copies. **Outcomes:** `200` with `ETag` = generation when created, or when the key is live with the **same generation** (an idempotent retry, or two writers of one object, converge; no new record is written); `412` when `If-None-Match: *` is sent and the key is live, whatever its generation (ordinary S3; the PUT leaves nothing behind); `409 KeyImmutable` when the key is live with a different generation (an immutable bucket never overwrites) and for every PUT that carries `If-Match` (an immutable bucket accepts only creates); `503` with `Retry-After` when fewer than `hot.min_acks` copies could be made, when the key's answer is not definitive (a node `indexing`, or fewer than `N - hot.min_acks + 1` nodes answered, 10.2), or when hot dirty bytes are above `hot.dirty_slow` (below); `507` when hot fill is above `hot.dirty_reject` after reclaim, or no cold placement exists for a stripe and hot is full. A key that one node reports live and another node has tombstoned (the tombstone names the live record id) is dead.
4. **Mutable bucket (the general rule: ordinary S3 semantics, nothing assumed of the keys).** (1) The body is appended as a record (or a whole file) on the writer and durable on `hot.min_acks` copies; it is not visible yet. (2) **One Postgres transaction orders the write** (10.16): a conditional upsert of the row `heads(bucket, key)` with the precondition evaluated against the row itself (`If-None-Match: *` fails when a live head exists; `If-Match: <etag>` fails unless the live head's generation equals it; no header is unconditional), the new `version` from the cluster-wide sequence `ts.head_version` (the order of writes of one key, increasing across a delete and a re-create) and the new record id as head; the previous head's record id gets a `tombstones` row and its block's `live_count` is decremented in the same transaction (a hot block that thereby has no live record is marked `dropped` later by the reconciler, 10.16 transaction 7, not in this transaction); the operation id is recorded in `ops` (an ambiguous commit that is retried returns the recorded result). The last writer is the last committed transaction. (3) A **commit marker** record (bucket, key, version, record id, 10.2) is appended to the writer's open segment and made durable on `hot.min_acks` copies; it lets `metadata fsck` rebuild the heads from the disks (10.16). (4) `200` with `ETag` = generation. In a mutable bucket the ETag identifies the content, not the write: `If-Match` is a content precondition, so a write of the same bytes by another client between the client's read and its conditional PUT does not make it fail (it still gets a new `version` and a new record id). **A failed precondition** is `412`: the transaction commits only the tombstone of the record just appended (and the `ops` row) and returns the failure, so the appended bytes are dead at once (a record that has neither a head nor a marker, left by a writer that crashed before step 2 committed, is found by the orphan sweep, which tombstones it only under the age-and-transaction rule of 10.16 (never on the strength of `written_ms`), and transaction 6 refuses a record that already has a tombstone and makes the front re-append). While Postgres cannot be reached for the client retry window (`metadata.retry_window`, 10.16) the PUT is `503` with `Retry-After`; the appended record is then garbage of the same kind. `507` and the `hot.dirty_slow` `503` apply as in step 3. There is no key check on other nodes: the head decides.
5. **What create-only guarantees [D].** *The general rule* is the mutable bucket's: every write of a key, conditional or not, is ordered by one Postgres transaction, so `If-None-Match: *` and `If-Match` are linearizable whatever the keys are, whichever node a front routes to and whatever the nodes' membership views are. *The fast path* is the immutable bucket's, which keeps Postgres off the PUT path and therefore rests on the keys: when the storage nodes' membership views agree, create-only is linearizable (one writer per key, the key lock, the all-node check of step 3). When views differ (a node is `down` in one front's view and not in another's, for the seconds of an epoch change, 10.8), two fronts can route one key to two writers and both can create it. With content-derived keys (every object of the CAS service) both carry the same bytes; the duplicate is resolved by the same comparison (a tombstone for the higher record id, no counter and no alert), so its bytes are reclaimed and a later DELETE finds no second live record. Different bytes under one key in that window were both acknowledged and are **detected and repaired, not prevented**: the reconciler finds two live records of one key by the per-node index comparison of 24.5 (detection within the 24 h cycle at most, typically seconds through the targeted check), keeps the one with the **lower record id** (the lower `(block_id, offset)`; deterministic, so every node reaches the same answer) and tombstones the other through Postgres, `store_key_conflict_total` counts it and an alert is raised; until then `LOCATE` can return two generations, and no reader mixes bytes (every GET of a fill carries the fill's pinned generation, 4.6). So an immutable bucket is correct for content-derived keys, or for any user with one writer per key that retries every ambiguous PUT to acknowledgement; any other user takes a mutable bucket. **One property is stated here:** an unacknowledged PUT of an immutable bucket can become live when its segment seals (10.3, Index step), even after a later acknowledged DELETE or re-PUT of the same key, because DELETE and the create-only check see only indexed records. It is harmless for content-derived keys (same bytes; the duplicate record is resolved to the lower record id by the reconciler like any duplicate, 24.5, and the CAS service deletes only unreferenced packs and re-deletes). A user who needs DELETE to be final uses a mutable bucket or, after an ambiguous PUT failure, retries the PUT to an acknowledgement before any DELETE.
6. **After a delete:** a deleted key is absent in both modes and a later PUT creates it. In a mutable bucket the new head has the next version. In an immutable bucket the new record has a new record id, and the key check sees the tombstone of the old one, because a DELETE is applied on `N - hot.min_acks + 1` nodes before it is acknowledged and the check needs answers from the same number of nodes, so the two sets intersect (10.2). The CAS service's keys are derived from content, so a PUT of different bytes under a live key is a client defect, answered `409 KeyImmutable` (or `412` with `If-None-Match: *`) while the key is live.

**Hot dirty bytes** are the bytes of open and sealed write segments and of large files whose objects are not yet committed to the cold tier. They are the only hot bytes that must not be lost, and the only ones that count against backpressure (clean bytes are reclaimable, below).

**Destage: seal, then copy to cold (automatic, decoupled from reclaim) [D].**
- **Eligible:** a block whose seal is committed (the compare-and-set on its `blocks` row, 10.3; a segment that is fenced but not yet sealed because Postgres is unreachable waits), or large files, older than `destage.min_age` (default 1 hour). The destager reads `seg/` and `big/` only, never `cache/`.
- **When it runs:** whenever hot dirty bytes pass `hot.dirty_destage_start` (default 50% of the NVMe) it destages down to `hot.dirty_destage_stop` (35%); and independently any eligible bytes older than `destage.max_age` (default 3 days) are destaged, so the NVMe is a bounded-age window and the cold tier is the durable base. The work is a `background` stream rate-limited by `destage.bytes_per_hour`, raised to above `prepare` at `hot.dirty_urgent`. With `destage.min_age = 0` and `destage.max_age = 0` (a single-node install, 10.14) it destages as soon as a block seals.
- **Which first:** within an expiry class, oldest sealed first (least recently read first among equals). Stripes are formed from blocks of **one expiry class** (10.2) so that blocks that die together are frozen together (GC below).
- **Stripe formation.** A stripe needs `k` blocks of one expiry class, **from any replica set**: the coordinator streams blocks it does not hold from a peer's NVMe. Among eligible blocks it groups those of **similar length** (sorted by length, consecutive `k`), so parity, which is as long as the longest block, wastes little on short ones. Until `k` eligible blocks of a class exist the blocks wait on the hot tier; after `destage.partial_after` (default 7 days) or when hot dirty bytes pass `hot.dirty_urgent`, a stripe is made from `j < k` blocks, the missing data shards being virtual zeros (10.4). **A partial stripe costs `(j + m) / j` raw per logical byte** (`4+4` with one block is 5x; with two, 3x; with three, 2.33x), so it is a pressure valve, not a default; `cold_partial_stripes_total` counts them and GC compacts them later (below). With `replicas = N` there are no stripes and each block destages alone. **`cold_fill_efficiency`** (live data bytes over raw cold bytes used) shows the combined effect of short blocks, partial stripes and dead bytes; T0-F C-10 replays it.
- **Pack assembly.** Large objects are assembled into **pack blocks** inside the destage pipeline: the coordinator takes a window of the oldest `destage.pack_window` (64) eligible large files of one class, places them **first-fit decreasing** into blocks of at most `block_bytes - block_reserve` (so a 600 MiB and a 400 MiB pack share a block instead of each leaving 400 to 600 MiB of a block unused; T0-F C-6's pack size distribution checks the waste before `block_bytes` is frozen), 4 KiB-aligns each record, and **streams each block straight into its data shard and the encoder**, writing the trailer at the end; the NVMe files are the copies until the stripe commits, so nothing is written twice on NVMe. An object above `block_bytes - block_reserve` becomes a chain (10.2).
- **Procedure.** (1) choose the blocks and the coordinator (the node that holds most of them locally; the others stream from a peer's NVMe); (2) allocate `stripe_id` (node-allocated, 10.2) and compute the placement (10.4); (3) open one writer per shard (local, or `PUT /store/v1/cold/<drive>/shard` to the peer service with a generation trailer); (4) stream each block from NVMe once, teeing the bytes to its data shard and the encoder, and the parity windows to the parity writers, all as write streams, 4 MiB sequential, one write stream per drive; (5) every writer checks the crc32c table and blake3 of what it received against the coordinator's, `fsync`s, writes the footer, renames; (6) **the descriptor commit is the atomic point, and it is one Postgres transaction** (10.16): it inserts the `stripes` row (code, expiry class, the coordinator, state `committed`) and the `shards` rows (shard index, block, node, drive, file, state `ok`); for each data block it sets `blocks.state = 'cold'` and `stripe_id` conditioned on `state = 'sealed' AND stripe_id IS NULL`, **not on `row_version`** (streaming `k` blocks of up to 1 GiB takes minutes, and a delete or relocate that only lowers `live_count` in that window must not fail the commit or livelock the destage of actively deleted blocks; a block dropped meanwhile because its last record was deleted fails the condition and the whole transaction; the block's `live_count` is whatever Postgres holds at commit, and the stripe free below handles blocks that die later); it sets the `hot_copies` rows of these blocks to `clean`; and, for pack blocks, it inserts the new block rows and does the relocate of the records into them in the same transaction (`relocations` rows, and a compare-and-set of the head of a mutable-bucket key, below), skipping every record a tombstone already names. A pack of up to 1 GiB holds at most a few hundred large records [E], so the transaction is a few hundred rows. The transaction carries the operation id, so a commit whose acknowledgement was lost is retried and returns its recorded result instead of being applied twice; (7) once committed, each node learns it from its next refresh of the block and stripe rows it caches (at most `metadata.tomb_poll`, 2 s, later; the coordinator's own node at once), reading a new pack block's records from the trailer of its first data shard, and then moves each immutable-bucket index entry that is still at the old record id from its file to `(block, offset)`: before moving an entry it checks whether the new record id is tombstoned (its `tomb-applied` file or the tombstone rows) and, if so, turns the entry into a tombstone entry instead (10.2); only an entry that already is a tombstone entry, or whose key now holds a different record id (a replacement), is left alone.
- **Failure.** A writer that fails or stalls is replaced by another drive of the same node (the shard is re-sent from NVMe). If a node cannot be reached the stripe is placed on the other nodes if the invariant still holds (`4+4` places 4 + 4 on two nodes; **`4+2` cannot, so destage of `4+2` pauses during a node outage and the hot tier absorbs**); otherwise the stripe is abandoned (`.up` files unlinked) and retried later. A stripe whose shards are all durable but whose rows never committed (the coordinator died, or Postgres could not be reached for the retry window, 10.16) is an orphan, removed by a sweep after `destage.orphan_age` (1 day) and only while the metadata incarnation is unchanged and the node is not frozen (10.16); while Postgres is unreachable destage does not start new stripes. Nothing is ever acknowledged to a client from a cold write: the hot copies stay until commit.
- **Verification:** by default each writer's crc32c/blake3 comparison plus a read of the last cell of each shard (`destage.verify = "tail"`); `destage.verify = "full"` re-reads every shard (cost: the stripe's bytes once more); the scrubber (10.4) is the continuous check.
- **Converting replicated cold blocks to erasure coding** (milestone S1 to S2, or a change of `cold.ec`): a block destaged as `replicas = 2` copies becomes data shard `i` of a new stripe **in place**. The coordinator chooses the stripe's `k` blocks and the shard-to-node and shard-to-drive assignment **from the existing copy locations** (not from the stripe id's node order), so that the invariants of 10.4 hold (distinct drives, at most `max_shards_per_domain` per node); a block for which no such assignment exists is copied like a placement-audit move instead. For each chosen copy it creates the shard name as a **hard link** (`linkat`) to the copy file, so the bytes have both names; it reads the `k` data shards, writes only the `m` parity shards, and commits the stripe's rows with the re-encode, move or convert transaction (10.16 transaction 12; here the block rows' replica locations become `shards` rows and the `replicas` rows are removed in it). Only after the commit does it unlink the old `.copy` name and the block's other copy. A crash before the commit leaves both copies intact under their old names; the orphan sweep removes only shard-name links and parity files, never a `.copy` name, before the rows are committed; after a committed conversion it unlinks any `.copy` name whose block belongs to the committed stripe, so a crash between the commit and the unlink leaks nothing. A converted data shard keeps its copy footer (it names the block, not the stripe); the parity shards' footers carry the full descriptor, so `metadata fsck` can rebuild the stripe's rows from any parity footer, and the scrubber verifies a copy footer as such. No data block is re-copied unless the placement requires it.

**Relocate: a record moves, as one transaction [D].** A relocate gives a live record a new place and so a **new record id** (10.2): pack assembly of large files, the rewrite of a mostly dead stripe (below) and the compaction of a mostly dead hot segment are relocates; a destage of a segment into a stripe is not (it keeps every offset, so record ids survive it). The transaction (1) locks the source block rows with `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` and, for a mutable-bucket key, the key's `heads` row; (2) skips every record a tombstone already names, so a key deleted or overwritten meanwhile is not carried along as a live record, and **for each record it skips it inserts a `tombstones` row naming the new `(block_id, offset)`** that the already-written trailer lists (the pack assembly streams the records into the pack block, and a GC rewrite re-appends them into a hot segment, before this transaction runs, so their bytes and trailer entries exist; without the row an index rebuild from trailers would read the entry as live and the deleted key would come back once the old block is freed), copying bucket, key and generation and adding nothing to the new block's `live_count`, `live_bytes` or `relocations`; the row is deleted when the new block is freed; (3) inserts a `relocations(old record id, new record id)` row for each moved record, decrements the source block's `live_count` and counts the record in the new block; (4) for a mutable-bucket key, updates `heads` to the new record id as a compare-and-set on the head's version and old record id (a head that moved on is left alone), and appends a fresh commit marker `(bucket, key, version, new record id)`, durable on `hot.min_acks` copies, tombstoning the old marker's location in the same transaction (10.16 transaction 4, which is the one canonical body of a relocate), before the old block may be dropped or freed (its `dropped` and free conditions require that the head's marker no longer lies in the source block, 10.2); and (5) marks the source block `dropped` when it has no live record left and it is a hot segment that was compacted or rewritten away (a large file whose records moved into a pack stays a clean hot copy, `blocks.state = 'clean'`, until reclaim unlinks it, below). A DELETE or overwrite that names an old record id follows `relocations` (repeatedly, for a record relocated twice) to the live one, and a DELETE and a relocate of the same record serialize on the source block's row lock, so whichever commits second sees the first: a delete after a relocate tombstones the new record, and a relocate after a delete skips the dead record. The rows of `relocations` are deleted when `old_block` is freed (below). The nodes' derived indexes follow a committed relocate as in step 7 of the destage procedure (a GC rewrite repoints under the same rule, and a tombstone applies to the old ids of a relocated record, 10.2).

**Reclaim of hot space (eviction) [D].** Reclaim drops only clean bytes and never copies:
1. **Cache segments** (`cache/`), whole, least recently read first: one unlink, plus removal of the promoted location from the index entries they hold.
2. **Clean write segments and large files** (destaged, cold copy committed), whole file, least recently read first: one unlink (`hot.keep_clean = true` by default keeps them as a read cache until space is needed; `false` deletes at commit).
3. Nothing else: dirty bytes are never dropped.
A sealed write segment that is mostly dead before destage (`live < hot.gc_live_below`, 0.5) is compacted on NVMe (its live records re-appended to the open segment, each move a relocate, below), which is cheap there and keeps dead bytes off the cold tier; when the last live record has moved, the source block is `dropped` in that transaction.

**Headroom and backpressure.** Marks on dirty bytes as a fraction of the NVMe, in order: `hot.dirty_destage_start` 50% < `hot.dirty_urgent` 75% (destage priority rises above `prepare` and partial stripes are allowed) < `hot.dirty_slow` 85% (small and large PUTs answer 503 `Retry-After`, the CAS service backs off; `hot_put_throttled_total`) < `hot.dirty_reject` 90% (507). The reject mark also applies to the filesystem's total fill after reclaim: an xfs filled with 1 GiB files and small files risks allocation failures above about 90% [I]. The invariant the operator checks: **NVMe capacity x (reject - start) must hold the burst that arrives while destage is stalled**; for our hardware that is a 6.1 TB logical hot tier at `replicas = 3` (18.4 TB of NVMe hot partitions), 2.45 TB logical between the start and reject marks, which is about 72 days of today's 34 GB/day and about 3.6 days at 20x today's rate [E]. Destage pauses (never fails PUTs by itself) when cold cannot place a stripe (nodes down, no drive below `fill_limit`, the capacity guard below) or when the metadata store cannot commit (a stripe's rows are one transaction; a metadata outage also stops seals, repair and DELETE, 10.16, while PUT and GET of immutable buckets continue and the hot tier absorbs the burst within the headroom above); `destage_stalled_seconds` alarms.

**Capacity guard.** `cold.node_loss_reserve = true` (default for `failure_domain = node`): destage stops placing new stripes when the cold raw bytes in use would exceed `fill_limit` of the capacity of **all nodes but the largest, less `cold.reserve_drives` drives** (default: one drive per remaining node; 2 on our three nodes, 1 on the NOW profile of section 21): `0.85 x (2 x 90 TB - 2 x 6 TB)` = 142.8 TB raw, which is 71.4 TB logical at `4+4` and 95.2 TB at `4+2`. So a node loss can always be rebuilt onto the survivors (10.4) and, after that rebuild, each survivor still has a drive's worth of free space for the next drive failure. An operator who accepts the risk disables it.

**Promote on read: a cache copy, not a move [D].** A cold read is one stream from one drive, which is a fine cost for a cold object and a poor one for a hot one (a block is one spindle's 190 MB/s shared by 31 clients).
- **Policy.** Each storage node counts demand and prepare reads that it serves from the cold tier (not scrub, rebuild, GC or destage) in a count-min sketch per `promote.window` (1 hour). An object read **`promote.reads`** (default 2) times in the window, and no larger than `promote.max_object` (default 256 MiB), is promoted, **unless hot dirty bytes are above `hot.dirty_destage_start`**: under write pressure nothing is promoted, so a promoted copy is never the first thing reclaimed right after it was made. The build nodes' SSD caches absorb repeat reads by one node, so a cold object that is read twice by the storage tier is, in practice, wanted by several build nodes.
- **What is copied, and where.** The object's bytes (never its block) are copied by a `background` stream into a **cache segment** (`cache/`) on the NVMe of the node that served the cold read (the data shard's node: a local HDD-to-NVMe copy, no network), with **one copy**. The **cold block stays the authoritative durable copy and is never rewritten to remove a promoted object.** The promoting node sends `promoted(K, record id, generation, location)` to the key's index nodes, which add a second location `hot_clean = (node, cache segment, offset)` to the entry **only while its record id and generation still match** (a tombstone naming the record id that raced the promotion wins, and the copy is never used; a relocate keeps the hint, since the bytes are the same). Promotion is keyed by record id, so it serves both bucket modes: the bytes at a record id never change. The location is a cache hint, not a fact of Postgres: an index rebuild drops it; a node that restarts unlinks its cache segments instead of re-registering them, a cache segment's name carries the node's boot epoch so it is never reused, the index nodes drop every hint of a node whose boot epoch changed, and a hot read verifies the record's key and generation. LOCATE (10.6) returns it first, and falls back to the cold location if that node or file is gone. Dropping the copy is removing that location (and, when the whole cache segment is dropped, one unlink).
- **Cost on a small hot tier.** Promoted bytes are capped at `hot.promo_share` (default 20% of the NVMe), are never dirty, and are reclaimed whole-segment, least recently read first, with no rewrite; `promote.bytes_per_hour` (default 100 GiB) bounds the copy traffic. Promoting an object, not a 1 GiB block, keeps a hot 1 MiB object from costing 1 GiB of NVMe. A promoted copy is not a second durable copy and nothing relies on it.
- Today's read rate (0.5 MB/s cluster-wide) leaves this idle; it is built because the owner's design needs a path for a hot cold block, and its effect is measured in T0-F C-3b and T-B5.

**Deletes and garbage collection [D].** About 38% of pack bytes are already dead, and 14,795 of about 85.9 k packs are fully dead, because nothing deletes yet; deletes today are rare (0.035%) but the CAS service's planned GC will make delete volume a policy output. The tier is built for both.
- **DELETE** (`204` even when the key is absent; `If-Match` on a DELETE is supported in both bucket modes (`412` unless the live generation equals it; the per-mode behaviour is in the 10.9 DELETE row)). The two bucket modes differ as the PUT does, and both need Postgres: during a metadata failover a DELETE answers `503` with `Retry-After` (10.16).
  - **Mutable bucket:** one transaction marks the key's head deleted (a new `version` from the sequence `ts.head_version`, `deleted = true`), tombstones the old record id and decrements its block's `live_count`, and records the operation id; a delete marker (a commit marker with no record id, 10.2) is then appended and made durable on `hot.min_acks` copies, so `metadata fsck` does not resurrect the key from an older marker. `204` follows whether the key existed or not. A later GET or LIST reads the head, so a read after an acknowledged delete sees it (10.16 states the scope across a failover). A deleted head is kept as a row and **purged** by the sweeper only after the deleted-head retention (fixed at 7 days, not configurable) and once every record of the key is tombstoned and its block freed (10.16); versions come from the cluster-wide sequence, so a re-created key's version always exceeds every version remembered by a marker.
  - **Immutable bucket:** the deleting front asks `N - hot.min_acks + 1` nodes for the key's live record ids (normally one; a non-definitive answer is `503`, never a silent partial delete; it tombstones every live record id that any queried node reports, and nodes can report different ids for one key until the reconciler resolves a duplicate, 24.5, so the `204` does not claim the key is gone from a node that holds an unqueried duplicate, and the reconciler's next comparison tombstones any remaining live id of a key whose earlier id was tombstoned by this DELETE); one transaction inserts a `tombstones` row for each record id (following `relocations` to the live record, above), decrements each block's `live_count` and records the operation id; the front pushes the tombstones to every live storage node (`POST /store/v1/tomb-apply`; each node durably records the tombstone in its fsynced `index/tomb-applied` file (10.2) and turns its index entry into a tombstone entry; it never touches files, and `tomb-apply` is refused or recorded without effect on a frozen node) and answers `204` once `N - hot.min_acks + 1` nodes have applied them. Nodes also **pull** every new tombstone from Postgres by one gap-free cursor (10.2) every `metadata.tomb_poll` (2 s), and a node that returns from an outage pulls before it leaves `indexing`; a node that has not yet applied a tombstone may therefore serve the deleted object for at most `metadata.tomb_poll` after it is reachable again [D] (harmless to the CAS service, which deletes only unreferenced packs; a user who needs read-after-delete takes a mutable bucket).
  - **A hot block that has no live record left** (a large hot file, or a sealed segment not yet destaged, whose last record was just tombstoned) is marked `dropped` in the same transaction (10.16 transaction 5; a block emptied by a mutable overwrite is dropped by the reconciler, transaction 7), and every holder unlinks its copies into trash after it reads the committed row, only while it is not frozen (24.15 item 4, 10.16); this is the only unlink of a hot file; this is the hot-only block drop, a row, not a record in a log. A block that a descriptor commit made `cold` is never dropped: its stripe is freed by the transaction below. A drop keeps the block's `tombstones` and `relocations` rows and the block is freed through the same gate as a stripe block (the `freed` step of the pattern below); a node that reads a block row in `dropped` or `freed` removes every index entry of that block, whether live or tombstone. Blocks are never modified.
- **Free without writing: the stripe-free transaction.** A cold block is **dead** when its `live_count` in Postgres is 0. The count is exact: it is set at seal from the trailer (the records inside the sealed length, minus the tombstones that already name the block) and decremented by each transaction that tombstones or relocates a record of the block; it is never raised afterwards, so a count that reached 0 stays 0 and no node is asked anything. A stripe is freed when all its data blocks are dead, by unlinking its `k + m` shard files (no I/O of data). The steps:
  1. **Owner.** The reconciler (24.5), the one loop that runs on the node holding `reconciler_lease`, owns every free; there is no per-stripe owner. If that node dies another takes the lease (15 s).
  2. **Commit.** One transaction that checks the lease, the incarnation and `NOT frozen` (10.16) runs `UPDATE stripes SET state = 'freeing' WHERE stripe_id = $1 AND state = 'committed' AND row_version = $v AND NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM blocks WHERE stripe_id = $1 AND live_count > 0)`. No row updated means nothing is freed (a repair commit or a re-encode, move or convert commit (10.16 transaction 12) moved the row's version, or a count is above 0), and the owner looks again later: a free is never urgent (the capacity guard, not GC, protects writes). From the commit the stripe is dead; a `LOCATE` that still names it gets a 404 on the shard, which the engine treats as a lost location and re-asks (4.6).
  3. **Unlink.** Only after the commit, each holder (every node with shard files of the stripe, or hot copies or `blk/*.idx` of its blocks) learns of it from its next refresh of the stripe rows, moves those files into its trash (24.15 item 4: kept `trash.retention`, then purged) and records the move in `trash`; that row is its report. A holder that is down reports when it returns.
  4. **Freed.** When every holder has reported and every node that is neither `out` nor `lost` has a cursor above the highest `xid` of the tombstones that name one of the stripe's blocks (each node records its gap-free cursor of 10.2 in `nodes.tomb_applied`, and the owner reads `min(nodes.tomb_applied)` over the nodes neither `out` nor `lost`, counting a node's `tomb_applied` only when its `incarnation_seen` equals `cluster.incarnation`, so a node on an older incarnation, or one that has not yet re-read the tombstones after an incarnation change (10.16), blocks the free; the gate runs in the same transaction as the incarnation check; so a node that skipped a tombstone cannot let the free pass), the owner commits `state = 'freed'` and, in the same transaction, deletes the `tombstones` and `relocations` rows that name the stripe's blocks. Tombstones are therefore dropped exactly, by the free of the block they name; there is no drop rule and no per-origin log. A `lost` node is excluded from the gate because 24.4 already makes its return correct: on return, because the health table holds a `lost` row for it that it has not yet processed, it rebuilds its index from the trailers and `blk/*.idx` of the blocks whose rows are not `dropped` or `freed`, treats a block with no row as absent, applies the tombstone rows still present with its cursor reset to 0, and only then reports `returned` and leaves `indexing` (10.2 recovery step 4). A node that is down but not `lost`, in maintenance, or held by guard 1 or 2 of 24.3 holds the gate back, so the stall is bounded and visible: the metric `frees_blocked_by_node{node}` names the node holding the minimum cursor, and the alert `FreesBlocked` fires when frees have been blocked for 1 h, with the runbook line that the operator brings that node back or marks it `out`.
  5. **Free wins over repair.** A rebuild, a placement move or a re-encode commits its change to a stripe with the condition `stripe state = 'committed'` (and the stripe's version) in the transaction; once the free is committed that condition fails, the change is refused, and the executor unlinks the shard it wrote. The orphan sweep also removes shard files of freed stripes (10.16).
  6. **No revival.** A block's count never rises, so a dead block cannot be revived. A PUT of a key whose old record lies in a dead or dying block always writes a new record in a new block with a new record id; a relocate reads only from blocks of a `committed` stripe, writes only into new blocks, and skips what a tombstone names.
  A sealed hot block that is wholly dead is dropped by the same pattern in one transaction (`blocks.state = 'dropped'`, above) and unlinked on each copy after the commit; it is then freed as in steps 3 and 4: once every holder has reported its unlink and the gate of step 4 passes, the owner commits `dropped` to `freed` and deletes the `tombstones` and `relocations` rows naming the block in the same transaction.
- **Group by expiry class [D].** Open segments and pack assembly are per expiry class (10.2) and a stripe holds one class, so blocks that die together are frozen together and whole stripes become free. Until the CAS service sends the hint every object is class 0 and GC depends on the rewrite below. `gc.classes_max` (8) bounds the classes; extra values fall into class 0. Classes affect placement of bytes only, never retention.
- **Rewrite only when it pays.** A stripe with a live fraction below `gc.rewrite_below` (0.30) is **rewritten** only when the cold fill passes `gc.rewrite_fill` (60%): a `background` job reads the live records through the normal read path, re-appends them (same key and generation, same expiry class) into hot write segments with a relocate each (one transaction per batch, above), and when every live record has moved the old stripe's blocks have no live record and the stripe is freed by the transaction above. Rewrite cost is the live bytes: freeing a stripe at 30% live writes 0.43 bytes per byte freed [E], against about 2.3 bytes written per byte freed for a vacuum at a 30% garbage threshold that rewrites whole volumes (SeaweedFS's default, 10.12). Because capacity is under 2% used, rewriting is off in practice today and only whole dead stripes are freed; `gc.rewrite_bytes_per_hour` caps it and it never runs while destage is `urgent`.
- **Partial stripes** (made under pressure) are the first rewrite candidates.
- **Tombstones** are rows of the `tombstones` table, each naming a record id, and are deleted in the transaction that completes the free of the block they name (step 4 above, or the `freed` step of a dropped hot block), after every node that is neither `out` nor `lost` has applied them; there is no separate rule and no log to compact. A DELETE of a key whose record lies in a block that was freed meanwhile cannot happen: a key with a live record keeps its block's count above 0.
- **What is not claimed:** that GC never writes. Segments of small objects are garbage-collected like pack blocks (whole stripes, or rewrite), and the first rewrite pass is not measured; T0-F C-10 replays the measured death distribution against the grouping policy.

### 10.6 Read path and the peer protocol
**Lookup for a read of `(key, range)`, once per fill (not per part):**
1. **`LOCATE` [D].** The client computes `rank(K)` itself from the membership document (rendezvous hashing, 10.2): the **full ordering** of the storage nodes for the key, whose first `hot.replicas` live nodes are the key's replica nodes. It **contacts nodes only in `rank(K)` order** and never fans out: a `LOCATE` and a hit need the first of them, and only a definitive miss goes past the first `hot.replicas` (step 3). it sends a batched `POST /store/v1/locate` to the first node of `rank(K)` that is not `suspect` in its own view, and on a transport error or a stall (4.6) marks that node `suspect` and asks the next. **A co-located client** (a process that runs the fetch engine and the hot store, the NOW example): when its own node is among the key's first `hot.replicas` nodes of `rank(K)`, `LOCATE` is an in-process lookup of its own index, with no network call; when it is not, it asks the top-ranked node once, as any client does. **A key of a mutable bucket** (the request names the bucket, whose mode the front knows from its cache of the bucket table) is answered from its head: the answering node reads `heads(bucket, key)` from Postgres (a point read on the primary, 10.16), takes the head's record id, and answers from its cache of the block and shard rows, with the same answer kinds below; the co-located in-process shortcut applies to immutable buckets only, because the head is not in any node's index. For a key of an immutable bucket the answer comes from that node's index and its cache of the block rows (memory or NVMe). Either way the answer is made without touching **a hard drive**, and is one of:
   - **hot**: the generation and size, and the hot copies `[(node, file or segment, offset, length, load)]` **ordered by current load**, the first being the **recommended** copy (a `hot_clean` promoted copy is among them, 10.5); `load` is the queue wait the answering node last learned for that copy's NVMe (its own, or a peer's from `x-ts-load`, below);
   - **cold**: the generation and size, and for each block the object covers `(block id, data shard node, drive id, shard file, offset, length, the drive's load)`, plus a **reconstructor** node for a degraded read and the `k` source drives it would read, the least-loaded ones in the answering node's load table (10.4), and the stripe's current shard health;
   - **absent**, **`indexing`** (the node's index is not built yet), or **unreachable**; for a mutable bucket `absent` means that the head says the key does not exist or is deleted, and an unreachable metadata store is not `absent` but a `503` (step 3).
2. **Which node serves the bytes.** The client pins the generation and reads from the **recommended** copy: it sends parts by `GET /store/v1/hot/<file>` or `GET /store/v1/cold/<drive_id>/<shard>` with `Range`, `If-Match: <generation>`, `x-ts-class`, `x-ts-job` and `x-ts-rank`, **straight to the node that holds the bytes**; the part does not pass through the node that answered `LOCATE`. The next copies in the answer's order are the targets of a hedge (4.6) and of a retry, and a copy whose device enters a decrease state in the client's controller moves to the end. A co-located client reads its own copy when that copy's device is not the most loaded of the copies in its own load table (it is a storage node, so it has one), else the least-loaded peer's. With `hot.replicas = 3` on three nodes every node holds every hot object, so the answering node is usually also the recommended one and a hot read has no extra hop. A cold shard on a `stalled` or `failed` drive, a crc error, or a node the client holds `suspect` sends the part to the reconstructor (`GET /store/v1/degraded/<block_id>?range`), which serves it by the degraded read of 10.4.
3. **For an immutable bucket, a miss is definitive only when** `N - hot.min_acks + 1` storage nodes, none `indexing`, answered `absent`, where `N` is the number of storage nodes, asked **in `rank(K)` order**: an acknowledged object is durable and indexed on at least `min_acks` nodes, and any `N - min_acks + 1` nodes include one of them. Our cluster (`N = 3`, `min_acks = 2`): a hit at the first node asked is final, and a miss needs a second node's `absent`, so with one node down the other two decide. A hit needs only the key's first nodes; a miss walks further down `rank(K)`, because a key written while one of its first nodes was down sits on the next node in rank. On NOW (`N = 6`, `min_acks = 2`) a definitive miss therefore asks five nodes: this is the one path on which a client goes past the key's replica nodes, and a miss of a pack a tree names is rare (4.6). Anything less is **not a miss**: the S3 front answers 503 with `Retry-After` (never 404), the engine waits and retries (4.6), no negative entry is cached and nothing is repaired from it. So a pack whose index nodes are rebooting is delayed, never reported missing. **A mutable bucket has no definitive-miss question:** the head in Postgres decides whether the key exists, a deleted key (`deleted = true`) or one with no row is `404`, a live head names the record id, and the bytes are then read from any copy of that record id (the bytes at a record id never change, so any copy is current). A key whose head cannot be read, because Postgres is failing over or unreachable for `metadata.retry_window`, is `503` with `Retry-After`, never `404`; the retriable `503` is what the clients of section 23 expect (about 30 s at most).
4. **Negative cache.** A definitive miss of an immutable bucket is cached for 5 s **only for client GET and HEAD through the S3 front**, so 100 clients asking for a missing key cause one query. A local PUT of the key clears it. A mutable bucket has no negative cache: a head is always read from the primary. The engine filling a pack that a tree list names does not consult it (the tree is evidence that the pack exists); it single-flights one `LOCATE` per key.
5. **Corruption.** A record or cell that fails its crc or the client's digest is served from the other hot copy, or by degraded read of the cold stripe; the serving node is told (`x-ts-report: bitrot`) and queues repair of that segment or shard (10.3, 10.4).

**The peer service (every storage node).** HTTP/1.1 keep-alive on the **private network address only** (no public listener; TLS is not enforced and is an operator's choice on their own network; requests carry a cluster token read from a file, never an environment variable). Routes: `POST /store/v1/locate`; `GET /store/v1/hot/...` and `GET /store/v1/cold/<drive_id>/...` with the headers above (response headers: `ETag` = generation, `x-ts-queue-wait-ms`, `x-ts-service-ms`, `x-ts-drive-queue`, `x-ts-drive-state`; a queue over its bound returns `503` with `Retry-After`, a `stalled` or `indexing` drive `503` with `x-ts-drive-state`, never 404); `GET /store/v1/degraded/<block_id>`; internal routes between storage nodes: `PUT /store/v1/hot/replica` (segment batches carrying the segment id, its epoch, the start offset, the bytes and the running crc, which a replica rejects when the epoch is below the highest it has durably recorded for that segment, 10.3; large-file streams), `PUT /store/v1/cold/<drive_id>/shard` (a shard write with its generation trailer), `POST /store/v1/fence` (a segment and a higher epoch: the copy records the epoch durably, refuses later batches under the old one and answers its committed and crc-verified lengths, 10.3), `POST /store/v1/key` (the writer's all-node key check before an immutable-bucket PUT or DELETE, returning the key's live record ids and the tombstones the node applied, 10.2), `POST /store/v1/tomb-apply` (tombstones naming record ids, pushed by a deleting front and answered once durably applied, 10.5), `GET /store/v1/index-report` (the node's live keys of immutable buckets as `(bucket id, key hash, record id, generation)`, read by the reconciler's per-node index comparison, 24.5), `POST /store/v1/repair`, `GET /store/v1/ping` (10.7: per drive state, oldest in-flight call age, queue depths by class, streams, membership epoch); and, for clients as well as peers, `GET /store/v1/membership` (10.8: the membership document the node runs, rendered from the `nodes` and `drives` rows of Postgres at the epoch the node holds, 10.16, and kept as it was while Postgres is unreachable; the cluster token required, served whatever the node's index state). There are no routes for seals, descriptors, tombstone logs or index sync: block and stripe facts are rows in Postgres, which the nodes read and write through their metadata client. Every response of every route carries `x-ts-mem-epoch`, the epoch of the membership document the answering node runs, which is how a client follows the membership (10.8). It uses the same part engine on the client side: the `Upstream` trait gets a `StoreUpstream` implementation, so single flight, claims, verification, the controller and hedging are unchanged; only the transport, the `LOCATE` step and the choice among copies are new.

**Load among storage nodes [D].** Every internal request and response between storage nodes (replication batches, fences, key checks, tombstone pushes) carries `x-ts-load`: the sender's NVMe queue wait and depth, and per hard drive its state, queue depth by class, queue wait and the age of its oldest in-flight call. Each storage node keeps a **load table** of its peers from these, so it knows every node's load from traffic it already exchanges (`N^2` among storage nodes only: 9 pairs on three nodes). A storage node sends `GET /store/v1/ping` to a peer only when it has exchanged nothing with it for `peer.load_idle` (default 2 s), so an idle cluster still knows its peers' state; **clients never ping**. The load table orders the copies of a `LOCATE` answer and chooses a degraded read's sources.

**The S3 front** on each storage node and the peer service share the same index, cache of block and stripe rows, metadata client (10.16) and schedulers. For a mutable bucket the front reads the head from Postgres itself (a GET or HEAD) and orders every write by a transaction (steps 3 and 4 of the PUT, 10.5); for an immutable bucket it answers from the node indexes and never touches Postgres except to DELETE.

### 10.7 Per-drive I/O scheduler
Every HDD has a `DriveSched`, owned by the storage node that holds it and run on **dedicated blocking I/O threads per drive, `drive.streams x drive.depth` of them** (2 x 2 = 4 by default), never the tokio blocking pool, so the configured depth is real and a drive stall cannot starve the async runtime. Fifteen of them run on each storage node. The NVMe has a simpler `HotSched`: a bounded queue depth (`hot.queue_depth`, default 64 requests [I]), the class order below, a demand-first rule, and the same stall watchdog; it has no slices, streams or preemption, because NVMe seeks cost nothing. The rest of this section is the HDD scheduler; its first-byte and slice constants are for a 7.2k SAS drive and T0-F replaces them.

- **Streams.** A *stream* is one request's sequential run over one file (a part read, a shard write by destage or rebuild, a scrub, repair or promote read). The scheduler admits at most `drive.streams` streams at once (default **2**), of which at most `drive.write_streams` (default **1**) write. A stream issues `chunk` = 4 MiB `O_DIRECT` requests at depth `drive.depth` (2), the device's `max_sectors_kb`; the arm sees ascending addresses within a file.
