Whether a read stream copies to the socket from an `O_DIRECT` chunk or uses buffered `sendfile` with `DONTNEED` is a T0-F arm; the default is `O_DIRECT` (exact device request sizes, no page-cache pollution on a node whose SSD is the cache).
- **Classes and order.** Waiting requests sit in class queues; a free slot goes to `demand` > `write` (shard writes of destage and rebuild) > `prepare` > `background` (scrub, GC and promote reads, non-urgent rebuild reads); a rebuild of a stripe with no spare shard counts as `prepare`. Within a class: deficit round robin across job ids, then `(rank, arrival)`. Among equal candidates the scheduler prefers the stream whose file's first physical extent (read once with FIEMAP at open and cached) is next above the current head position, an elevator over at most a few waiting streams.
- **Writes are never starved [D].** While a write waits, at least one slice in every four on that drive goes to a write (`drive.write_min_share`, 25%), whatever reads wait. So a read-busy drive slows a destage or rebuild write, never stalls it, and the destage backpressure of 10.5 sees progress.
- **Slices.** An admitted stream keeps its slot for `slice_bytes` (default 32 MiB, about 180 ms at 190 MB/s, so a seek of about 12 ms costs under 7%); at the slice boundary it yields only if another stream of equal or higher class (or a write owed its share) waits.
- **Demand preemption.** A `demand` request that finds only lower-class streams admitted takes the next chunk boundary of the lowest-class stream (that stream is paused and resumes later, costing two seeks); preemptions are limited to `demand_preempt_max` per second per drive (default 4), so a demand storm cannot turn the drive into a seek machine; beyond that a demand waits for the slice boundary. A write's guaranteed share is not preempted.
- **Small lane.** A read of one small record (10.2) does not take a stream slot. On a drive with an idle I/O thread it runs at once on that thread; otherwise it runs at the next chunk boundary of any admitted stream, on that stream's thread, at most one small read per boundary, by class order; `drive_small_reads_total`.
- **Read-ahead and slot release (slow consumers) [D].** Each read stream has a buffer of two chunks (8 MiB): an I/O thread reads the next chunk only while the buffer has room, and the socket writer drains it. If the buffer is full and the socket has accepted no byte for `drive.slot_release_after` (default 1 s [I]), the stream gives up its slot: it leaves the admitted set and is **parked** at its current offset, keeping its buffered chunks, and the slot goes to the next waiter. Parked streams are a separate set per drive, bounded by `drive.parked_max` (16), and do not count toward `queue_max`, so stopped clients never make new requests see 503; beyond `parked_max` the oldest parked stream is ended with an error and its client retries. A parked stream whose socket drains rejoins the waiting queue of its class with its original arrival rank. Parked buffers per node are bounded by `cold.parked_buffer_max` (256 MiB); beyond it a parked stream drops its buffer and re-reads at readmission. `drive_slot_releases_total` counts. A client whose hash pool pauses receive (4.7) therefore holds buffered bytes, not the spindle.
- **Watchdog and liveness [D].** Every blocking call a drive's I/O threads make records its submit time (an injected clock in tests): chunk reads and writes, `fsync` of files and directories, `renameat2`, `open`, `fallocate`, FIEMAP, `unlink`, the startup `readdir`. A watchdog task checks every drive each second; any such call outstanding longer than `drive.stall_after` (default 10 s [I]; T0-F C-9 reads the kernel's SCSI command and error-handling timeouts to place it) puts the drive in **`stalled`**: every queued request fails at once with `503`, `x-ts-drive-state: stalled` and `Retry-After`; new requests are refused the same way; other admitted streams on the drive fail at their next chunk boundary; shard writes in flight are re-placed on another drive (10.5); `drive_stall_total` counts. A thread blocked in the kernel cannot be cancelled: it is counted in `drive_io_threads_stuck`, and the drive leaves `stalled` only when every stuck call has returned **and** a probe read of the `DRIVE` file's block completes within `stall_after`; it is then `up`. Three stalls within an hour make the drive `failed` (below).
- **Liveness is the scheduler's, not the process's.** `GET /store/v1/ping` (between storage nodes only) and the `x-ts-load` header (10.6) are answered from the scheduler's state: per drive its state, the age of its oldest in-flight call, queue depths and streams. A storage node treats a peer's drive whose oldest in-flight call is older than `stall_after` as `stalled` even before the owner's watchdog flips it.
- **I/O errors.** An EIO on a read of one shard file quarantines that shard (renamed `.bad`, repair of its stripe queued, 10.4); the drive stays `up`. `drive.eio_limit` errors (3) on distinct files within 10 minutes, or three stalls within an hour, make the drive **`failed`**: the node writes `FAILED`, serves nothing from it, and an operator clears it (no automatic retry loop on a dying disk). A write EIO re-places that shard and counts toward the same limit.
- **Backpressure.** Each class queue has a bound (`drive.queue_max`, default 8 requests). A read beyond it gets `503 Retry-After`. A drive whose write queue is full is not a placement target: the stripe coordinator picks another drive of the node (10.4); a write never waits unboundedly for a full queue. The client treats a 503 as a decrease signal **for that drive** (4.3) and a reason to prefer the other replica or a degraded read. **No number of clients can drive a spindle past `s*` streams.**
- **Instrumentation.** `x-ts-queue-wait-ms`, `x-ts-service-ms` and `x-ts-drive-state` on every response; per drive: state, streams active, queue depth by class, wait and service histograms, slice yields, preemptions, slot releases, small reads, stalls, stuck threads, bytes, util (from `/proc/diskstats`).
- **Constants [I until T0-S]:** `streams` 2, `write_streams` 1, `depth` 2, `chunk` 4 MiB, `slice_bytes` 32 MiB, `demand_preempt_max` 4/s, `queue_max` 8, `write_min_share` 25%, `slot_release_after` 1 s, `stall_after` 10 s. T0-S sweeps streams {1, 2, 3}, chunk {1, 2, 4, 8} MiB, slice {8, 16, 32, 64, 128} MiB, depth {1, 2, 4}, and runs the mixed, slow-consumer and churn arms of section 16.

### 10.8 Membership, states and audit
**Membership document.** The authority for membership is the metadata store (10.16). Its rows are the **epoch**; the **cluster settings that every node must agree on**, `hot.replicas`, `hot.min_acks` and the cold code for new stripes, which each node checks against its own configuration at startup and which take effect with the epoch; per storage node, that is per node that runs the hot store (10.1): id, peer address, S3 front address if it runs one, failure domain, and its hot device `in`, `drain` or `out` (table `nodes`); and per drive: id, node, mount path, weight = capacity, filesystem UUID, `in`, `drain`, `out` or `failed` (table `drives`). The file `membership.toml` is not the authority: it seeds `tierstore store init` only (the first start of a cluster, which writes `nodes`, `drives`, the cluster settings and epoch 1) and is not read again. Afterwards only an operator verb changes membership (`tierstore store node add|drain|out|in`, `tierstore store drive ...`, 24.11; a new node joins only through these verbs), and each verb writes the rows, the audit row and the epoch increment in one metadata transaction, behind the guards of 24.3 (ok-to-out, one node at a time, dry-run, refusal codes). A node learns the epoch from the metadata store (it polls every 5 s and the verb pushes the new epoch to every node it can reach) and from a peer response that carries a higher `x-ts-mem-epoch`; it then renders the document at that epoch from the rows. Views may differ for a few seconds, which the `rank(K)` order of `LOCATE` and the definitive-miss rule (10.6) make harmless. The document lists no other nodes and names no kind of node: a node is in it because it runs the hot store. A reader ignores keys it does not know, so the document grows additively. **The document is cached by every node: the node keeps the last document it rendered, on disk and in memory, so `GET /store/v1/membership` and every membership decision keep working while the metadata store is unreachable (10.16); a node that cannot reach the store keeps its last epoch and applies the next one when the store answers.** Drive identity is checked at startup: the path's `DRIVE` file and filesystem UUID must match the document, or the drive is not opened (a wrong-mount guard).

**Seed bootstrap: how a client finds the storage nodes [D].** A client is anything that uses `tierstore-client`: the daemon's fetch engine, the S3 front with `backend = "cache"`, a tool or a program linking the library (section 23). Its `[client]` section names **either** `seeds`, a list of storage-node peer addresses, **or** `membership`, the path of a membership file; validation refuses both and neither. (A process that also runs the hot store reads membership from the metadata store like every storage node and needs no `[client]` section.) A client never talks to the metadata store: it reads the document from a node.
1. **Bootstrap.** With seeds, the client sends `GET /store/v1/membership` with the cluster token (read from `client.token_file`, never an environment variable) to the seeds in a random order until one answers `200`; the body is the membership document as that node holds it, rendered from the rows at its epoch (the same text for the same epoch on every node), with `content-type: application/toml` and `x-ts-mem-epoch`. The route is served from the node's cached document, so it answers during a metadata outage. The client parses and validates it as a node does (10.14) and from then on uses the peer addresses it names; seeds are not used again while any node the document names answers. With a file, the client reads the file. A wrong token is `401` and is not retried on other seeds (the configuration is wrong, and every seed would say so); a seed that does not answer, answers `404` (a release without the route) or answers a document that does not validate is skipped. When no seed answers the client's constructor returns `MembershipUnavailable` naming every seed and its error, and a daemon keeps retrying with backoff (50 ms doubling to 10 s, jittered) while its fronts answer reads with `503` (S3) or park them (FUSE, NFS) as a non-definitive miss does (4.6), never as a miss.
2. **Following the epoch.** Every response of the peer service carries `x-ts-mem-epoch` (10.6). When a response carries an epoch above the client's, the client fetches `GET /store/v1/membership` from the node that sent it (one fetch at a time, whichever response triggered it) and adopts the document if its epoch is above the client's own; it never adopts a lower epoch, so a node that is still on an older epoch cannot move a client back. A client configured with a file re-reads the file instead. Between the response and the adoption the client keeps using its current view, which is the same few-seconds divergence nodes already tolerate.
3. **When every known node is gone** (every node the document names is `suspect` in the client's view, 10.8), a seeded client goes back to its seeds, so a cluster whose every address changed is found again from the configuration.

`membership_epoch` (gauge), `membership_fetch_total{outcome}` (ok, not_newer, invalid, not_found, unauthorized, unreachable) and `membership_bootstrap_seconds` (histogram) are exported by the client.

**States.** Section 24.2 extends this table (`new`, which is `nodes.admin_state = new`, then `missing`, `flapping`, `maintenance`, `lost`) and adds the `health` rows that decide `lost`; the rows below stay. `out` is the only cluster-wide state; the others are a viewing node's view, either reported by the owner or concluded by the viewer. `drain` and `out` are set only by the operator verbs above, which write them to the metadata store in one transaction with the audit row.

| State | Set by | Reads | Writes (shards, hot) and repair targets |
|---|---|---|---|
| `up` | default; owner after index, stall or probation ends | yes | below `cold.fill_limit` (hard drives); below the hot marks (NVMe) |
| `indexing` | owner, from start until its index or drive list is built | 503 `indexing`; never a miss | no |
| `stalled` | owner's watchdog, or a viewer from the ping's oldest in-flight age (10.7) | 503; the stripe is read degraded | no; shard writes in flight are re-placed |
| `suspect` | a storage node: 3 failed requests or pings (a ping only after `peer.load_idle` without traffic) to a peer, `up` again after 3 successes; **a client: its own requests** (3 transport errors or stalls in a row, 4.6), `up` again after one success, retried after 10 s with one request when it has no other candidate | last in preference; a client skips it for `LOCATE` while another node of `rank(K)` is not `suspect` | no |
| `down` | a storage node: a peer that answered nothing (no traffic, no ping) for 3 intervals (6 s); applies to all its drives. Clients have no `down`: a node they cannot reach is `suspect` | not asked; degraded read | no |
| `failed` | owner: `FAILED` written (10.7); cleared only by an operator | not asked | no; rebuild after `rebuild.drive_delay` (10.4) |
| `drain` | an operator verb (`store node drain`, `store drive drain`), written to the metadata store | yes | no new placements; its hot blocks are sealed and destaged, its shards moved or re-encoded elsewhere (section 21.3) |
| `out` | an operator verb (`store node out`, `store drive out`), written to the metadata store | not a candidate | no; its shards are rebuilt (10.4) |

**What happens in each case.**
- **A node reboots or is down for a while.** Reads of its hot copies go to the other replica, reads of its cold data shards are degraded (10.4), new PUTs go to the other nodes (the replica set is the first `hot.replicas` live nodes), new stripes are placed on the live nodes if the invariant holds (10.5). Nothing is rebuilt until `rebuild.node_down_after`, except that the reconciler (24.5) queues repair at once for any block or stripe whose margin has reached zero; the open segments it mirrored are seal-and-rolled (10.3).
- **A drive fails.** Its stripes read degraded and rebuild after `rebuild.drive_delay` (10.4); with `cold.replicas`, its blocks are re-copied from the other copy (milestone S1); nothing else changes.
- **An NVMe fails.** For hot data this is the loss of the node (10.3): the hot copies it held are re-replicated from the other copies (the reconciler reads `hot_copies` and queues the jobs); its key index is rebuilt from the cold trailers, the `blk/*.idx` files that survive on its hard drives and the tombstones in the metadata store (applied by record id, 10.5), its block table is read again from the metadata rows, and nothing is copied from a peer's index; its hard drives keep serving cold reads once the index is built. If the failed NVMe also held a metadata member (10.16), Patroni re-initialises that member from the primary onto its partition of the replacement device, and the cluster runs on two metadata members until it has caught up.
- **A drive or node is removed for good** (`out`): rebuild (10.4), re-replication of hot blocks (10.3).
- **Adding drives or nodes.** New stripes place on them by the weighted rule; existing shards do not move; a background **placement audit** (low rate, `background` class) moves shards only to repair an invariant violation (for example after a replacement node arrives following a node loss). A fourth storage node extends the rank list; `hot.replicas` and the code are config.
- **Corruption** is detected by the record or cell crc, the client's cell digest, or the scrubber; the copy or shard is quarantined and repaired from the other replica or by decode.
- **The metadata store is unavailable** (a failover, two of three members down, a frozen cluster): the nodes keep their last membership document, immutable-bucket PUT, GET, HEAD and LIST continue, repair copies continue, and every action that needs a commit waits or answers `503 Retry-After` (10.9, 10.16). Nothing destructive happens.

**Audit [D].** Redundancy is measured, not assumed.
- **Stripe margin** (no disk I/O): computed by SQL over the rows of `stripes`, `shards`, `drives`, `nodes` and `health` (the surviving shards of each stripe against the current drive and node states), `cold_stripe_margin{margin}` counts stripes by `surviving shards - k` (`< 0` is **unreadable**, `0` has no spare, and so on) and `cold_stripes_below_tolerance` counts stripes that would not survive the loss of a node; `hot_blocks_by_copies{copies}` counts hot blocks and large objects by live copy count, from `blocks` and `hot_copies`. These are the numbers the availability claims of 10.4 are read against. The reconciler (24.5) runs the same query to order its queue; the audit and the queue read one set of rows, so they cannot disagree.
- **Index audit.** Each node's key index is derived data. The audit compares it with the metadata store: for a sample of index entries it checks that no tombstone row names the entry's record id (a live entry under a tombstone is a **missed tombstone**; after an incarnation change the check runs from cursor 0, not from the node's cursor), and for the tombstones above the node's applied cursor and, in a mutable bucket, the `heads` rows, it checks that the record exists on the node where the metadata says it has a copy (a head or live count that names bytes the node does not hold is a **missing record**). It reports both counts per node as `metadata_audit_differences{kind="missed_tombstone"}` and `metadata_audit_differences{kind="missing_record"}` and repairs the first kind by applying the tombstone and the second by queueing a repair job. The audit reads rows and the node's own index and does no disk reads of data. This is the check that the index, which no protocol keeps in step, has not drifted from the metadata store. (`metadata_audit_differences` joins the table of section 12 with the task that registers it, T-S24, per 24.10, together with the audit's sampling and its two repairs.)
- **Live-key audit.** `tierstore store audit --keys <file>` takes a random sample of live keys that the CAS service supplies from its sweep (T-K1a), runs `LOCATE` and a one-cell read for each, and reports the histogram including **unreadable keys** and keys present in the index with no readable copy, as a report and as `store_audit_live_unreadable_ratio`.
- **Placement audit** (above) and the scrubbers' counters complete the picture.
- **Alerts** (in the operations document): any stripe or hot block below its tolerance for longer than `rebuild.drive_delay`; `cold_stripe_margin{margin="0"}` above zero while a node is down (10.4); `store_audit_live_unreadable_ratio` above zero; `destage_stalled_seconds` above an hour; `metadata_frozen` (a node or the cluster has frozen its destructive actions because of an incarnation or `txn_seq` mismatch or a set `frozen` flag, 10.16); `repair_stalled_on_metadata` (repair jobs exist and no metadata transaction has committed for 5 minutes, 24.5); `metadata_audit_differences > 0` for an hour.

### 10.9 The S3 API and how the CAS service uses it
The S3 front is a general-purpose object store with ordinary S3 semantics for the operations below. Its keys are content-derived for every CAS-service object, but no operation's correctness depends on that. A bucket has one of two **modes**, fixed when the operator creates it (`tierstore store bucket create <name> --mode immutable|mutable`; the bucket table is in the metadata store and cached by every node, and a bucket is not created by `CreateBucket`; changing a bucket's mode is a new bucket and a copy):

- **Immutable bucket** (the CAS service's bucket): a key holds at most one live record until it is deleted. The metadata store is not on the PUT or GET path, so PUT, GET, HEAD and LIST work during a metadata failover; only DELETE needs the store. It is correct for content-derived keys, or for any key that has one writer (10.5 states the create race honestly: two acknowledged records of one key with different bytes are found by the per-node index comparison of 24.5, the one with the lower record id, the lower `(block_id, offset)`, is kept, and `store_key_conflict_total`, already a row of the table of section 12, counts it; T-S25 increments it).
- **Mutable bucket**: ordinary overwrite semantics. The current version of every key is a row in `heads` (10.16), and every write is ordered by one metadata transaction, so the last writer is the last committed transaction. Correctness needs nothing of the keys. Every operation needs the metadata store.

| S3 operation | Immutable bucket | Mutable bucket |
|---|---|---|
| `PUT` object | Streaming PUT as in 10.5; the all-node check decides. Absent: create, `200` with `ETag` = generation. `If-None-Match: *` and the key live: `412` (whatever its generation). Unconditional and the key live with the same generation: `200`, idempotent, no new record. Unconditional and the key live with a different generation: `409 KeyImmutable` (an immutable bucket never overwrites). `If-Match` on PUT: `409 KeyImmutable` (an immutable bucket accepts only creates). `503` with `Retry-After` if too few copies could be made, the key's answer is not definitive or the store is slowing writers, `507` if full. Optional header `x-ts-expiry` (10.2). No metadata transaction. | Streaming PUT as in 10.5 (the bytes are durable on `hot.min_acks` copies, not visible), then one transaction that conditionally upserts `heads(bucket, key)`: unconditional replaces the head; `If-None-Match: *` fails if a live head exists; `If-Match: <etag>` fails unless the live head's generation equals it. The new head gets a `version` from the cluster-wide sequence `ts.head_version` (monotonic across a delete and a re-create), the previous record gets a tombstone and its block's live count falls in the same transaction, and a commit marker is appended and made durable on `min_acks` copies before `200` with `ETag`. A failed precondition is `412` and the appended record is tombstoned at once. `503` with `Retry-After` during a metadata failover or when too few copies could be made, `507` if full. `x-ts-expiry` accepted as for immutable buckets. |
| `GET` (whole object or `Range`) | One byte range (cell-aligned reads are the fast path; any range works), or the whole object as a stream. Served by 10.6 from `LOCATE` on one node's index. `404` only when the key's answer is definitive; `503` (never `404`) while it is not. Bounded staleness after DELETE: a node that has not yet applied a tombstone can serve the deleted object for at most `metadata.tomb_poll` (2 s) after it is reachable again. | The front reads the head row from the primary (one round trip, about 0.3 to 0.7 ms [I]), then the bytes from any copy of the head's record id (bytes at a record id never change, so any copy is current). Read-after-write and read-after-delete hold for every write the client saw acknowledged (10.16 states the scope across a failover): a committed head or tombstone never names a block without a `blocks` row (10.16 transactions 5 and 6 insert the row first), and a reader that misses the row refreshes its block-table cache once from the primary and answers `503` with `Retry-After`, never `404` or a misdirected read, if the row is still absent. `404` if the head is absent or deleted. `503` with `Retry-After` during a metadata failover. |
| `HEAD` | From the index of the first reachable node: size, `ETag` (generation), `Last-Modified` (`written_ms` of the record), no disk access. | From the head row: size, `ETag`, `Last-Modified` (the commit time). `503` during a metadata failover. |
| `DELETE` | Needs the metadata store. The front asks `N - min_acks + 1` nodes for the key's live record ids and tombstones every live record id any of them reports (an unresolved duplicate on an unqueried node is tombstoned by the reconciler's next comparison, 24.5), commits one transaction (tombstone rows naming the record ids, live-count decrements), pushes the tombstones to every live node and answers `204` when `N - min_acks + 1` nodes have applied them durably (10.5). `204` even when the key was absent. `If-Match` is supported: `412` unless the live record's generation equals it. `503` with `Retry-After` during a metadata failover. | One transaction marks the head deleted (a new `version` from the sequence, tombstone of the old record id, live-count decrement; the deleted head is purged after the deleted-head retention, fixed at 7 days, 10.16). `204` whether the key existed or not. `If-Match` is supported: `412` unless the live head's generation equals it. `503` with `Retry-After` during a metadata failover. |
| **`LIST`** (`ListObjectsV2` and V1: `prefix`, `delimiter`, `max-keys`, `start-after` or `marker`, `continuation-token`) | The front sends `list(prefix, after, limit)` to `N - min_acks + 1` live storage nodes, none `indexing` (two in our cluster; every node indexes every key there); each answers from its ordered index (10.2) in key order; the front merges the sorted streams, drops duplicates, applies tombstones, applies the delimiter roll-up (after emitting a common prefix it asks each node to **seek past** that prefix, so a rolled-up prefix of a million keys costs one seek, not a scan) and returns up to `max-keys` (at most 1,000) with the last key as the continuation token. With fewer reachable nodes it answers 503. **Weakly consistent:** a key whose PUT was acknowledged before the call began is listed (the PUT was acknowledged only after its index entries were published on the ack-rule copies); a key whose DELETE was acknowledged before the call is not (the delete was applied on `N - min_acks + 1` nodes and the list asks as many); concurrent changes may or may not be seen. Tombstone entries are never listed. The CAS service's janitor and adopt step use it. | From the metadata store: `heads` ordered by key, a prefix range scan, the delimiter roll-up by seeking past a common prefix, deleted heads skipped. **Strongly consistent:** a page reflects every write acknowledged before the call began, in one snapshot per page. Same parameters, same page limits. `503` with `Retry-After` during a metadata failover. |
| Conditional requests | `If-None-Match: *` and `If-Match` on PUT and DELETE as in the rows above. On GET and HEAD: `If-Match` (`412` on mismatch), `If-None-Match` (`304` on a match), `If-Modified-Since` and `If-Unmodified-Since` against `Last-Modified`, which is the record's `written_ms`. | The same, with `Last-Modified` the head's commit time. Preconditions on PUT and DELETE are evaluated against the head row inside the transaction, so two conditional writers cannot both win. |

**Which operations depend on the metadata store.** In an immutable bucket only `DELETE` does; PUT, GET, HEAD and LIST continue during a metadata failover, and a segment that cannot seal because the store is unreachable is fenced and a new one opened by a node with intact disk history (10.3, 10.2 ids), so ingest continues until hot headroom runs out. In a mutable bucket every operation does. During a failover (target at most 30 s, 10.16) each metadata-dependent operation answers `503` with `Retry-After`; it never answers `404` or a stale value.

**The client requirement (section 23).** Every client of the S3 front tolerates about 30 s of `503 Retry-After` on metadata-dependent operations: it treats `503` with `Retry-After` as retriable, retries for at least 60 s (a 30 s failover with margin) with jittered backoff that honours `Retry-After`, never treats `503` as absence, and makes its retries idempotent (a PUT of the same bytes, a DELETE; the store also deduplicates a retried metadata transaction by its operation id, 10.16). A timeout, a connection reset or a transport error on a metadata-dependent operation is retriable exactly like a `503`: it is retried idempotently with the same operation id inside the same 60 s window, and the final outcome is `ClientError::Unavailable`, never `NotFound`. A client's per-request read timeout should be at least `metadata.retry_window` plus a margin (60 s or more), or else it must follow this rule; the front keeps holding a request up to 45 s and then answers `503`, because that lets a failover be absorbed inside one request. `tierstore-client` does this itself; the CAS service's change to a 60 s retry window is its own task outside this repository.

**Out of scope, each answered `501 NotImplemented`.**
- **Multipart upload.** The CAS service streams one PUT of any size, and large objects already stream into whole files (10.3); multipart needs part storage, a completion commit and garbage collection of orphan parts, and can be added later as an additive feature if a user needs it.
- **Object versioning and `ListObjectVersions`.** They need retained old versions with their own lifecycle; the `version` in `heads` is an internal write order, not an S3 version id.
- **Lifecycle rules and expiration.** Deletion policy belongs to the client (the CAS service's garbage collection); `x-ts-expiry` only groups bytes physically.
- **ACLs, bucket policies and IAM.** The access model is the cluster token on a private network.
- **Object tagging, `CopyObject`, object lock and retention, server-side encryption, `PutBucket*` configuration APIs, presigned-URL issuance and date preconditions on PUT.** None is needed by a user of this store, and each can be added later without changing what is stored.

The `ObjectStore` conformance suite of the CAS service gains a tierstore target, and the suite is extended (T-S0) to run against both bucket modes. For an immutable bucket it proves create-only semantics, `412` on `If-None-Match: *` for an existing key, `409 KeyImmutable` on different bytes, idempotent PUT of identical bytes, range and HEAD behaviour, and that a LIST of a prefix returns exactly the live keys after PUTs and DELETEs (including across a node restart and with one node down). For a mutable bucket it proves overwrite, read-after-write and read-after-delete, conditional PUT and DELETE with `If-Match` and `If-None-Match: *`, `304` and `412` on conditional GET and HEAD, a LIST from `heads` that is exact after every acknowledged write, and the `503 Retry-After` answer (never `404`) during a scripted metadata outage.

**ETag.** The ETag is the 128-bit blake3 generation in hex, not an MD5; a client that verifies ETag as MD5 of the body is not supported (the CAS service does not). In a mutable bucket the ETag identifies the content, not the write. `If-Match` is a content precondition, so a write of the same bytes by another client between the client's read and its conditional PUT does not make it fail (it still gets a new `version` and a new record id); `version` is internal and is not exposed as an ETag. A client that needs write-level ordering puts a counter or nonce in the bytes.

### 10.10 Migration off silo (j)
**Status: not run.** Silo was removed from the farm before the store was built, so there was nothing left to migrate: the legacy upstream, its `store.legacy_upstream` and `store.legacy_hint_ttl` settings, the `[import]` settings, the `upload_part_size` cut of 4.4, the first part that went alone (section 9) and the `legacy_reads_total`, `import_bytes_total` and `legacy_mover_bytes_total` metrics are deleted (T11b, 10.11 step 5). The plan below is kept as the record of what was planned.

Silo stays up, read-only after the flip, for the window. On the LATER profile **nothing in the new store shares a drive with silo** (the storage nodes are new hardware). On the NOW profile (section 21) the tier's cold directories sit on silo's own drives, and the fill limit counts silo's bytes (10.4); silo uses about 9.3 TB of the 96 TB raw, so the tier has room, and silo's removal (10.11 step 4) returns its space. What is left is the copy volume and the rollback cost. Steps, each reversible until the last, with the cost of each reversal stated:
1. **Bring the store up dark** (T-S tasks, T0 on the storage nodes). The metadata store comes first, because the daemons read their membership and block rows from it: install and start the three Postgres members with Patroni and etcd (on NOW, three of the six combined nodes, each on its own partition of the SATA SSD; 10.16), check one failover by drill, and configure the off-box backups. Then format the drives (a held script per node) and run `tierstore store init` once, which reads `membership.toml` as its seed and writes the `nodes`, `drives` and cluster rows and membership epoch 1 into Postgres; the file is not read again. Then create the CAS service's bucket as an **immutable bucket** (`tierstore store bucket create <name> --mode immutable`; the mode is fixed at creation, 10.9), and start the storage daemons. Nothing writes to the store; T0 and T0-F-b measure it. The metadata restore drill (24.12, ending in `metadata fsck`) is run on the farm once in this step, before step 4, and the flip of step 4 does not start without it.
2. **Add the legacy upstream** to the engine: `store.legacy_upstream` is silo's S3 endpoint, **read-only**. A fill tries the store first (`LOCATE`, 10.6) and reads silo when the store has a **definitive miss**; a non-definitive miss never imports (a rebooting node never starts a burst of copies). The part-grid rule for silo-origin packs (`upload_part_size`, 4.4) applies on that path only. Cost per fill: one `LOCATE` round trip before the silo GET; the engine remembers "legacy" for `store.legacy_hint_ttl` (10 minutes) so later fills of the pack on that node go straight to silo. Metric `legacy_reads_total{outcome}`. `LOCATE` is answered from the node's index and does not touch Postgres, so a metadata failover does not delay a fill.
3. **Import the live packs (decision: both mechanisms, in this order).** (a) *Bulk mover:* the CAS service, which knows every live pack, drives a throttled, resumable mover over live packs not yet in the store: read from silo as one stream, PUT through the S3 front into the immutable bucket (the writer node computes the generation; Postgres is not on this path), verify against cell digests where an L2 list exists, else against size and silo's own checksum; `import.bytes_per_hour` (default 200 GiB) and one stream per storage node, backing off on the store's 503. Dead packs are never copied. The live set is 1.34 TB [M]; at the default rate the pass is about 7 hours, and it enters through the hot tier (on LATER 4.0 TB of NVMe at `hot.replicas = 3`, inside the 2.45 TB logical, 7.35 TB of NVMe, between the start and reject marks, 10.5; on NOW the hot partitions are far smaller and the import is bounded by them), then destages as sealed blocks; the seals and destages need the metadata store, and if it is unavailable the segments stay fenced or open in the hot tier and the pass continues (10.13 states the headroom). (b) *Lazy import:* a definitive miss served from silo sends an import request to the pack's writer node, which reads the whole pack from silo once and PUTs it; duplicates converge as `identical`. (c) *Age out:* the CAS service condemns and deletes packs as trees expire; DELETEs go to the store and to silo. A DELETE to the store needs Postgres and answers `503 Retry-After` during a metadata failover; the CAS service retries it (every client tolerates about 30 s of such answers, 10.9 and section 23).
4. **Flip the CAS service's writes** to the store's S3 front (config); every new pack is one streaming PUT to the store. **Rollback of this step is not free:** every tree published after the flip names packs that exist only in the store, so a rolled-back CAS service, which reads silo, finds none of them: each such tree is a miss (republish or rebuild), a cold start of every tree published since the flip. The runbook (T-S11) reads the scale from `store_puts_total` since the flip and sets the point after which rollback is not offered. No reverse mover is built.
5. **Catch-up pass** over live packs written to silo between the bulk pass and the flip, then **measure the end:** when `legacy_reads_total` has been 0 for 7 days and the mover reports nothing left, go to 10.11.

### 10.11 Retiring silo
**Status:** steps 1 to 4 were not needed (silo was removed from the farm before the store was built, 10.10); step 5 is done (T11b).

Held scripts (dry-run by default, never forcing):
1. Confirm preconditions: `legacy_reads_total` zero for 7 days; **the repair of milestone S1 is running** (hot re-replication, cold-copy re-replication after a drive or node loss, scrub of hot blocks and cold copies, and the margin and live-key audits; 18): `replicas = 2` without repair would leave a block with one copy forever after its first drive failure, so silo's data is not destroyed before the store can restore its own copies; a scrub pass has covered every cold copy at least once; the live-key audit (10.8) over the CAS service's whole live list reports no unreadable key and no key missing from the store; `hot_blocks_by_copies` and the margin histogram show nothing below tolerance; **the metadata store's backups are running** (the latest full backup and the WAL archive are current in the off-box repository, 10.16) **and the restore drill has passed** (a point-in-time restore to a scratch cluster followed by `metadata fsck` that reports no difference it cannot explain; 24.12). Silo's data is the last copy of anything the store lost, and a store whose block rows cannot be restored cannot say which of its blocks are whole.
2. Remove `store.legacy_upstream`; this is the **last reversible step** (silo's data is intact; re-adding the upstream restores the fallback).
3. Stop and disable the silo service on each of its nodes through hostkeeper, one node at a time under the one-live-update coordinator.
4. **Destructive, held script:** remove silo's data from each drive after a final listing is archived; remove the silo package and unit from the node image.
5. Delete the `upload_part_size` handling, the legacy upstream and the silo client (T11b).

### 10.12 Why not SeaweedFS, why not Ceph, and why not silo (i)
Evidence: the SeaweedFS source (read at `97f49571`, and re-read as release 4.54 for this revision, whose facts are in 10.17 and govern wherever this section differs from them), its wiki and releases page, the Ceph erasure-code documentation, and silo's measured layout on the farm; nothing was run against SeaweedFS or Ceph. Labels: SOURCE read in code, WIKI read in docs, [E] estimate, [I] inference.

**A correction.** Revision 5 rejected SeaweedFS because "its erasure coding runs only on sealed volumes and its garbage collection fights a store that is deleted from", and the layout comparison of earlier drafts implied our contiguous blocks were an advantage. Both are withdrawn. **SeaweedFS's erasure coding also keeps a needle on one shard in the common case**, the f4 behaviour this design has. The layout was stated in an earlier draft as 1 GB contiguous blocks; that is the legacy two-tier layout (1 GB large blocks, 1 MiB small blocks for the tail), which 4.54 still reads (`ec_encoder.go`: `DataShardsCount = 10`, `ParityShardsCount = 4`, `ErasureCodingLargeBlockSize = 1GB`; SOURCE). A volume encoded by 4.54 gets one uniform block per shard of the `.dat` size divided by the data-shard count, rounded up to 1 MiB, in one row, so shard `i` holds the `.dat` bytes `[i*block, (i+1)*block)` contiguously (10.17 point 2). Sealed-only encoding is also what this design does (it encodes sealed blocks), and deletes are rare today (0.035%). **The reasons to build our own are these five, and only these:**
1. **The 3-node geometry.** The default of the open-source code is 10+4, and `ec.encode` has no flag for another ratio; 14 shards over 3 servers are typically 5 + 5 + 4 (the balancer spreads evenly; it is not hard-coded, and a node can hold up to 6), so losing a server loses 5 shards or more against 4 parity, and `ec.encode` needs `-force` below four nodes (10.17 points 1 and 3). The 4.54 code does carry a per-volume ratio in the `.vif` (`EcShardConfig`), read on encode only when a `.vif` is already there, so a `4+4` layout on a fresh encode needs that file pre-seeded by hand, and no open-source command sets it. An earlier draft cited the wiki for "custom ratios are an Enterprise feature"; that claim was not verified against 4.54 and is withdrawn. Reasons 1 and 3 therefore rest on the default and on the absence of a supported way to choose the ratio; if the trial finds a supported way to run `4+4`, they weaken, and reasons 2, 4 and 5 stand with any ratio.
2. **Automatic destage and promote-on-read.** SeaweedFS encodes by a manual `ec.encode` shell command (or an admin or cron worker) on whole volumes chosen by `-fullPercent` and `-quietFor` (`shell/command_ec_encode.go`, SOURCE); EC volumes are read-only ("Update is not supported", WIKI); remote tiering is whole-volume `volume.tier.upload`, run by an operator, with cold reads as exact-byte ranged GETs, no local cache and no access-driven promotion (the way back is the manual `volume.tier.download`), and `volume.tier.move` moves volumes between local disk types or data centers (10.17 point 4). Here destage is automatic on seal, decoupled from reclaim, and promotion copies one object into a cache segment (10.5).
3. **Repair-read traffic.** Rebuilding a lost shard reads 10 shards at the default 10+4 and 4 at `4+4` or `4+2`: about 2.5 times less repair traffic on the drives and the network [E] (with a hand-seeded ratio of `4+4`, the same).
4. **No per-object metadata row for the common bucket.** The S3 gateway requires the filer, with one entry per object and its chunk list in leveldb2 (one filer) or an external database (SOURCE); 1.39 M objects are 1.39 M rows today, and an S3 PUT is cut into chunks of the filer's `-maxMB` (4 MiB by default, 8 MiB when that is unset) assigned to volumes independently (10.17 point 5; the hop count through filer, master lookup and volume server remains). Here the metadata store holds **block-level truth**: blocks, hot copies, stripes, shards, tombstones, health and the repair queue, a few hundred thousand rows for the whole tier [E: 10.16], and a row per object only in a mutable bucket (`heads`, 10.5). The per-key index of an immutable bucket is local to each node, derived from the blocks and rebuildable from them (10.2), and a pack is one record in one block.
5. **Integration.** One storage daemon per node and one configuration with tierstore's own cache, S3 front and fetch engine: `LOCATE` returns generation and location in one step, every response carries the drive's queue and state for the per-drive budget of 4.3, demand preemption reaches the drive (10.7), and on the immutable-bucket path create-only is decided by the index under a key lock and an all-node check rather than a separate lock service (SeaweedFS answers 503 when its lock service is unavailable, SOURCE, on the path that serves 74% of PUT requests today [M]). The metadata store sits beside the data path, not in it: an immutable-bucket PUT or GET does not wait for Postgres (10.5).

| Question | SeaweedFS (open-source) | This design |
|---|---|---|
| Erasure code | 10+4 by default; `ec.encode` has no ratio flag, a per-volume `.vif` ratio must be pre-seeded; one uniform block per shard (`.dat` / 10, 1 MiB rounded), the legacy two-tier 1 GB layout still read (SOURCE, 10.17) | `k+m` is configuration per stripe (10.14); a code that cannot survive its domain is refused (10.4) |
| Layout of an EC read | One shard per needle in the common case; a read fetches the whole needle, a range is cut from memory, no range-limited shard read (SOURCE, 10.17 point 7) | 1 GiB contiguous blocks, the same f4 behaviour, with range-limited healthy and degraded reads |
| When EC happens | Manual or scheduled `ec.encode` on whole volumes chosen by `-fullPercent` and `-quietFor`; `-force` below four nodes (SOURCE, 10.17) | Automatic on seal at about 1 GiB (10.5) |
| Repair reads per lost shard | 10 at the default 10+4 [E] | 4 [E] |
| Cold data that is hot again | EC volumes read-only (WIKI); whole-volume, operator-run tiering; no promotion on read, return by manual `volume.tier.download` (SOURCE, 10.17 point 4) | Object-granular promote into a cache segment (10.5) |
| Metadata | Filer entry per object in a database (SOURCE) | Block-level truth in a replicated Postgres (blocks, stripes, shards, tombstones: a few hundred thousand rows [E]); a row per object only in mutable buckets; the per-key index of an immutable bucket is local to the storage daemon and rebuildable from blocks (10.2, 10.16) |
| Deletes and GC | Tombstones in `.dat`; vacuum at a 0.3 garbage threshold rewrites volumes, about 2.3 bytes written per byte freed at steady eviction [E]; EC volumes decode back before vacuum (WIKI) | Whole stripes freed by unlink after one transaction shows no live record in them; expiry-class grouping; rewrite only below 30% live and above 60% fill (10.5) |
| Daemons | Masters, volume servers, filer and its store, S3 gateway | The storage daemon on every node; on three of the nodes also Postgres, Patroni and a three-member etcd (10.16); no database outside the storage tier |
| Create-only PUT | Under a distributed lock; 503 if the lock service is down (SOURCE) | Immutable bucket: the index under a key lock and an all-node check, with no metadata store on the path; mutable bucket: one conditional Postgres transaction (10.5) |
| LIST, S3 coverage | Range GET, HEAD, DELETE, LIST V1 and V2 exist | All implemented here too (10.9), with the out-of-scope features listed there |

**The two gaps that decide it (owner, revision 8), each with the number or the experiment that measures it.**
1. **Fresh outputs read by other build nodes.** A build output is written once and is then read by other build nodes within minutes (the next action's inputs on another node). Here every new object lands in the replicated NVMe hot tier (three copies on LATER, 10.3) and stays there until it is sealed and destaged (`destage.min_age` 1 hour at the earliest, 10.5), so those reads are NVMe reads served by the least-loaded of three nodes: about 4 to 5 ms to a verified first cell [E, section 2]. In SeaweedFS a new object is written into a volume on the disk type its collection names: HDD unless the operator assigns SSD volumes, and moved between local disk types only when an operator runs `volume.tier.move` (SOURCE, 10.17 point 4), not by being read; on HDD such a read pays a seek (about 12 ms [I]) under whatever load the drive carries. The experiment: 17.2 item 3 extended with a "fresh output" arm (an object written by one node and read by 30 others within 10 minutes; first-byte p50 and p99 from the hot tier here, from an HDD volume and from an SSD volume in the reference run of 17.2 item 6).
2. **Access-driven promote-on-read.** Here a cold object read `promote.reads` times within `promote.window` (2 in 1 hour) is copied back into an NVMe cache segment (10.5), so a cold object that becomes hot again is served from NVMe after its second read. SeaweedFS has no access-driven promotion: no read path downloads a tiered volume again, the only way back is the manual `volume.tier.download`, and a cold read is an exact-byte ranged GET with no local cache (SOURCE, 10.17 point 4); EC volumes are read-only (WIKI), so once encoded every read stays on the HDD shard. C-3b measures the effect on a skewed read mix (hit rate, promote bytes, NVMe cost).

**Durability versus capacity, stated plainly.** `ec = 4+4` costs 2.0x raw, the same as `cold.replicas = 2`; its gain over two replicas is **tolerance, not capacity**: any 4 of a stripe's 8 shards may be lost, so a stripe survives the loss of a whole node (3 shards) plus a drive elsewhere, where two replicas survive one loss. Capacity at equal tolerance would favour `4+2` (1.5x), which tolerates less (decision Q1).

**Where SeaweedFS is stronger, and what we take on.** It is production software with frequent releases, tooling (`volume.fix.replication`, `ec.rebuild`, balance, an admin UI) and Prometheus metrics; its Haystack small-object read is one seek with about 16 bytes per needle in memory (SOURCE); its S3 gateway, LIST and conditional PUT exist today. Its releases also show data-path bugs (a `volume.fsck -volumeId` purge of live chunks, fixed in 4.52); that is a fact about a fast-moving project, not a verdict. **Building our own costs more:** section 18 plans about 93 agent-days for the core T-S0 to T-S12 (a range of 62 to 112, the upper half more likely) and about 53 more for operations (T-S14 to T-S25), about 146 in all [E], and storage engines usually take two to three times a first estimate. The store keeps the engine's `Upstream` abstraction, so a build that slips can fall back to an S3-compatible store (SeaweedFS with replication `001` behind the same engine and the same S3 conformance suite) by configuration; T-S0 runs that conformance suite against a single-node `weed server -s3` first, because it costs hours. Since the owner's decision of 2026-11-18, SeaweedFS is trialled as the store and this fallback is the trial, not a contingency; 10.17 sets 4.54's behaviour against this design.

**Ceph RGW, the baseline that can express this shape today.** A replicated pool on the three NVMe devices and an EC `4+4` pool on the HDDs with a custom CRUSH rule (3 hosts, up to 3 OSDs per host), RGW storage classes with a lifecycle transition from the NVMe class to the HDD class, and RGW's LIST, range GET and conditional PUT: that is this design's tiering, built from existing parts [I: read in the Ceph documentation, not run]. It is rejected for these reasons, each a property of how Ceph lays bytes out, not of its maturity:
- **Striped EC layout.** An RGW object in an EC pool is striped across the `k` data OSDs in stripe units of 4 KiB to 4 MiB (the profile's `stripe_unit`), so a healthy read of one pack touches four drives and waits for the slowest; this design reads one drive per object (10.4). The small-object reads that dominate by count would also touch four drives each.
- **Small-object amplification.** BlueStore on HDD with a 4 KiB `min_alloc_size` and `4+4` stores each chunk of a small object on eight OSDs with their own allocation and metadata; 99% of our objects are under 64 KiB [M]. Here they are records in a segment, 16-byte aligned.
- **No whole-block seal-to-cold.** A lifecycle transition copies objects one by one into the EC pool; nothing seals a 1 GiB block and encodes it as a unit with offsets preserved, so a transition rewrites per-object metadata and the HDDs see per-object writes.
- **Operational weight.** Monitors, managers, OSDs per drive (45 plus 3) and RGW daemons, a CRUSH map and placement-group tuning for a three-node cluster, against one storage daemon per node and, on three nodes, a Postgres member with Patroni and etcd.
What is genuinely new here, and therefore must be built and tested: the per-stripe code recorded in every shard footer, seal-to-cold with record offsets preserved (so destage changes no index entry), and object-granular promotion. What is reused, not reinvented: Reed-Solomon (`reed-solomon-simd`), crc32c, blake3, the embedded ordered store (`fjall`), Postgres with Patroni and etcd for block-level truth, and the published designs of section References (Haystack, f4, Windows Azure Storage).

**Why not silo.** Silo was the farm's store when this was written (MinIO lineage); nothing measured showed a defect in it, so **staying on it was the null hypothesis**. The reasons to leave it are the hardware and the shape, not a bug:
1. **Hot and cold tiers with different redundancy are not silo's model.** It is one erasure set per pool; there is no replicated fast tier in front of an erasure-coded one inside the system, no seal, no promote.
2. **Its set is fixed at format time and cannot be drained node by node**; the new nodes cannot join the old set, and 45 drives would be several independent sets.
3. **A GET fans out over a set.** At 8+4 each 1 MiB erasure block is eight 128 KiB shard runs on eight drives, so a GET waits for the slowest of eight queues and each drive does small runs [M geometry; the measured 36% efficiency of one drive at 1 MiB scattered reads shows the cost]. The design reads one stream from one drive.
4. **Small objects are one erasure stripe each**, 1.39 M of them on hard drives, against records in a 1 GiB segment (10.3); silo also keeps about 10 small writes per second on every drive when idle [M].
5. We would keep maintaining a MinIO-lineage binary ourselves.
Against that (when this was written): silo worked, needed no new code and had no migration risk; it has since been removed from the farm (10.10). That is the case for the phased delivery of 10.13 and for the fallback above.

#### Why a metadata store, not a hand-rolled ledger or Raft masters
The store needs one place that decides facts several nodes could otherwise decide differently: which copy of a segment is the sealed one and how long it is, which shards make up a stripe, which blocks a node may unlink, which records a DELETE has killed, which repair jobs exist, and (for mutable buckets) which record is the current head of a key. Three ways to build that place were compared.

| | Hand-rolled ledger (single-decree ballots (Paxos, Lamport 2001; References) and durable acceptors on the storage nodes, majority-committed records, a logical clock, tombstone logs) | Raft masters (a replicated log and state machine, our own or a library, in dedicated or embedded processes) | Postgres under Patroni with a three-member etcd |
|---|---|---|---|
| Code we own | The protocols themselves: ballots, acceptors and their durable votes, record agreement, the clock rule, tombstone logs and cursors, the drop rule. The largest correctness surface of the design [I] | The state machine, its snapshots and compaction, membership changes of the log, client retry and idempotency, and either the Raft implementation or its integration | The schema, the client crate `tierstore-meta` (idempotent, failover-aware, incarnation check), the reconciler's transactions and `metadata fsck`; no consensus, replication or transaction code |
| Daemons we run | None beyond the storage daemon | Three more processes (or a log embedded in the storage daemon) | Postgres, Patroni and etcd on three nodes, packaged by hostkeeper (10.16) |
| Latency on the PUT path | None for an immutable PUT; a seal needs a round to a majority of nodes | None for an immutable PUT; a seal or commit needs a leader round and a log fsync | None for an immutable PUT or GET; a mutable-bucket PUT or DELETE and every seal commit one transaction, which waits for one synchronous standby (a whole synchronous-commit round trip of about 1 ms on LATER and a few ms on the SATA SSD of NOW, 10.16 [I, T0-S and the failover drill measure]) |
| What fails together | The votes live on the data NVMe, so a replaced or blank drive is a lost vote (the "amnesia" case), and the protocols must be right under every partition | A leader election stalls every commit; a bug in the state machine or the log damages all copies alike | The three members and the three etcd members share nodes with the storage daemons but not their partition or processes; a failover takes at most about 30 s [K, target, measured by drill], during which metadata-dependent operations answer `503`; two members lost stop metadata commits |
| Scale path | A new kind of fact is a new protocol; nothing is queryable | Our state machine does not shard or query; point-in-time recovery and operator views are ours to build | Vertical first; read replicas for operator queries; `heads` is the only table that grows with objects, about 17 GB at 1e8 live keys [I]; beyond that, partition `heads` by bucket or give the largest mutable buckets their own cluster; an upgrade path (10.16) and standard tooling for backup, point-in-time restore, inspection and audit queries |

**Verdict: Postgres under Patroni.** Three reasons, in order of weight.
1. **It removes the code most likely to lose data.** Consensus and replication are the hardest code to get right and the hardest to test, and the other two options are ours to write and keep right. Postgres's transactions, write-ahead log, synchronous replication and point-in-time recovery have decades of production use [I], and Patroni is a widely operated failover manager that refuses to promote a standby that was not synchronous. What remains ours is a schema, a client and the rules of using them, which the simulator (section 14) models with a linearizable store and tests under outage, ambiguous commit, failover and rollback.
2. **It makes the store general-purpose.** A mutable bucket's overwrite, conditional PUT, DELETE and LIST are ordinary transactions and range scans, and one commit order (the last committed transaction) replaces every ordering rule the other designs needed. Operator verbs, audits and runbooks read the same tables with SQL under a read-only role, and an unanticipated question is a query, not a new protocol.
3. **The cost lands where it can be borne.** The hot data path (append, replicate, ack at `hot.min_acks`) and the immutable-bucket PUT and GET never wait for Postgres, so the CAS service's traffic is unaffected by a failover; what a metadata outage stops is seal commits, destage, repair, DELETE and mutable buckets, and the hot tier has about 72 days of headroom at today's ingest on LATER [E] (10.13; NOW's is smaller and not claimed, section 20). Every client must retry `503` for at least 60 s (10.9, section 23); `tierstore-client` does, and the CAS service's change is T-K1b, which T-S11 (the cutover) waits for.

A shared Postgres was rejected: the CAS service's own database and the job scheduler's run on the cluster tierstore serves, so using either would make tierstore's metadata depend on the store that depends on it (a cycle), and one instance would share blast radius between unrelated services. The store has its own cluster (10.16). A metadata node outside the storage tier was rejected for the same reason: its loss or isolation must not be able to stop the tier's commits independently of the tier.

**The fallback, stated.** The client's public surface is a closed list of named transactions, the eleven of 10.16 (segment row insert, seal commit, descriptor commit, relocate, delete and tombstone, head upsert, stripe and block free, repair job lease, repair commit, membership verb, bucket create), not free SQL, so the store behind it can be replaced. If the drills fail the targets (a failover longer than 30 s, commit latency on the target drives that makes seals or mutable-bucket writes unusable, or the 1e8-key measurement of `heads` exceeding its bound), the fallback is Raft masters holding the same tables as a state machine, accepted only after the failing measurement is on record. It costs the consensus code this section argues against, which is why it is the fallback and not the plan. The failure that would not be answered by a fallback is Postgres operations being too heavy for the owner's operators; 10.13 names it, and T-S23 delivers the runbooks and drills that test it before production.

### 10.13 Risks named
- **A new correctness surface, bigger than revision 5's.** The replication protocol (fencing and the epoch compare-and-set that seals a segment, seal-and-roll, the ack rule), destage's descriptor commit and the stripe-free transaction, the node index and its recovery, tombstones and their apply on the nodes, relocate, rebuild, the degraded read, the reconciler and the metadata client are all new code that can lose, duplicate or resurrect data. f4's authors name journal-based delete tracking "the foremost source of production issues": the tombstone rows, which the nodes pull by one gap-free cursor and apply to their indexes, are the same shape, and the divergence bullet below is its risk here. Mitigations: write-once blocks, content-derived generations, one ordering rule (the last committed Postgres transaction orders records, relocates and tombstones), a crc on every record and cell, the stripe descriptor in every shard footer, deterministic simulation of every protocol under scripted crashes, symmetric and asymmetric partitions, lost acknowledgements, reorderings and a Postgres model with outage, ambiguous commit, failover and point-in-time rollback (section 14), and the audit and scrub counters (10.8, 10.4). The consensus code of revision 8 (ballots, acceptors, the clock, the tombstone logs) does not exist; the consensus that remains is Postgres's, under Patroni and etcd.
- **Estimate risk.** About 93 agent-days for the core (T-S0 to T-S12), range 62 to 112 with the upper half more likely, and about 53 more (40 to 70) for operations (T-S14 to T-S25), about 146 in all (section 18), are a planning figure, not a measurement; storage engines run two to three times a first estimate, and the line-rate calibration of 3 k lines per agent-day comes from plumbing PRs. **Phased delivery limits the exposure:** milestone S1 ships the metadata store and its deployment, the hot tier, the cold tier with `replicas = N` (no erasure coding), the S3 front with LIST, the repair that `replicas = N` needs (re-replication, scrub of copies, the margin and live-key audits) (there is no migration: silo was removed from the farm before the store was built, 10.10); S2 adds erasure coding, its rebuild and deep scrub; S3 adds promote and GC rewrite. At `replicas = 2` the replicated cold tier is 114.8 TB logical, 71.4 TB under the guard, against 1.34 TB live.
- **Three nodes are few.** With `4+2` a node down leaves zero parity; with `4+4` one spare; with `hot.replicas = 3` and `min_acks = 2`, un-destaged data is on two NVMe devices at acknowledgement and on three once the third copy lands, so it survives a node plus another NVMe; a record acknowledged on two and lost on both before the third copy lands (two simultaneous NVMe losses inside the replication lag) is the remaining window (Q2). The NVMe is a single device per node, so its loss is the node's loss for hot data (10.3). Rolling updates take one node down at a time under the coordinator, so the exposure is the length of a node's update; `rebuild.node_down_after` keeps an update from starting a rebuild. A minority partition cannot seal or acknowledge; it answers 503 (10.3).
- **The NVMe holds the index of 15 HDDs and, on three nodes, a Postgres member.** Its loss makes the node's healthy drives unserved until the index is rebuilt (24.6, R7); no block-level truth is lost with it, because the rows live in Postgres on the other members, and a lost member is re-created from the primary (24.12).
- **After a node loss `4+4` has no node-loss margin** until a fourth storage node exists. For the metadata store the same loss is gentler and the second one is not: with one node lost the remaining two members still commit (strict synchronous mode with one standby), and a second loss stops metadata commits until a member returns, the same outcome as the default of Q5 for two nodes lost (10.16).
- **Postgres operations are new work for the owner's operators.** Minor and yearly major upgrades, re-initialising a replica, a stuck replication slot or a failed archive filling the WAL partition, vacuum of the churning tables (`tombstones`, `ops`, `repair_queue`) and keeping backups restorable are the cost of the choice in 10.12. The defences are the packaged runbooks (24.12), a restore drill before production and every quarter, alerts on archive lag, replication lag, partition fill and table bloat, and drills of a killed primary, a power-cycle of two and of three nodes and a full WAL partition (T-S15). The exposure is a gap in practice, not in design, and the quarterly drill is what keeps it closed.
- **Rolling back the metadata store in time is the one way to make live data look dead.** A point-in-time restore, an old backup restored by mistake or a lagging standby promoted by a person gives Postgres an older picture than the disks hold: blocks that were freed would look live (harmless) and blocks written since would look unreferenced, so a sweep would delete live data, and the node-allocated ids written since could be handed out again. Defences: every node stores the metadata incarnation and the highest `txn_seq` it has seen and freezes every destructive action when Postgres shows a different incarnation or a lower `txn_seq`; restore is an operator verb that raises the incarnation; `metadata fsck` rebuilds the rows from the disks, allocates ids above the maximum on disk and in Postgres, and only then clears the freeze; the orphan sweep deletes only what has been unreferenced for `destage.orphan_age` (1 day) with no incarnation change in that time, and trash comes before delete (10.16, 24.15). The simulator's oracle checks this, with a negative case that must fail: with the freeze disabled it loses data (T-S15).
- **Patroni and etcd on a loaded NOW node.** On NOW the metadata members share nodes whose load average is high (131 on 88 CPUs); a Patroni or etcd heartbeat that starves is a failover that was not needed, or a primary demoted by its own watchdog. Defences: high `CPUWeight` for the three daemons and raised etcd heartbeat and election timeouts [I], etcd on the metadata partition so a busy data drive cannot delay its fsync, and the failover drill run on the loaded nodes, not on idle ones. The LATER profile has no such load. If the drill shows spurious failovers on NOW, the Postgres members move to the three least loaded nodes or wait for LATER (the rest of the store does not depend on it, T-S12).
- **A metadata outage stops some work and not other work.** While no primary is reachable (a failover of up to about 30 s [K], or two of three members down) seals cannot commit (open segments fence and the writers open new ones, so PUTs to immutable buckets continue; `hot_fenced_unsealed_blocks` counts the backlog), destage waits, repair jobs cannot commit their results (`repair_stalled_on_metadata` after 5 minutes), DELETE and every mutable-bucket operation answer `503 Retry-After`, and membership changes are refused. Immutable-bucket PUT, GET, HEAD and LOCATE continue from the nodes' indexes and hot segments. A long outage is paid in hot-tier space, because nothing destages: at today's ingest the hot tier has about 72 days of headroom on LATER [E: the room between the destage-start and reject marks, 2.45 TB logical, divided by the daily ingest of section 2, as in 10.15; NOW's is smaller and not claimed, section 20], so the outage that matters is one that lasts weeks, which is the case of the total loss of metadata, handled by restore or `metadata fsck`. Every client tolerates about 30 s of `503` (10.9, section 23); one that does not turns a failover into errors.
- **The node index and Postgres can disagree.** The index is derived and each node applies tombstones from Postgres by a sequence cursor, so a node that missed tombstones, applied one too early, or lost its cursor serves a deleted key or hides a live one. Bounds: a returning node pulls tombstones before it leaves `indexing`, and a running node pulls every `metadata.tomb_poll` (2 s); the audit (10.8, 24.10) compares each node's applied tombstones and live records with Postgres and counts differences, and a rebuild of the index from trailers, `blk/*.idx` and the tombstone rows is always available. In an immutable bucket a deleted object can be served for at most the poll interval after a node is reachable again; a mutable bucket reads its head from Postgres and does not depend on the index.
- **Drive facts unknown.** CMR or SMR, rpm and rate of the SAS drives, the NVMe's sustained write, `fdatasync` time, link speed and RAM are all T0 outputs; every constant marked [I] changes with them. The LATER NVMe drives are specified with power-loss protection (owner), and group commit, `hot.min_acks`, `batch_max` and Postgres's default `synchronous_commit = on` are sized on that; T0-S and the hardware audit verify it, and a drive found without it is refused by the audit rather than silently retuned. NOW (SATA SSD, no power-loss protection) keeps honest `fdatasync` and `fsync` everywhere and accepts millisecond commit latency; nothing turns fsync off. Sealed sequential writes and whole-file unlink are the safe choice on SMR as on CMR.
- **Degraded exposure.** A node down degrades about a third of cold reads for its duration and cuts cold read capacity to about 2.8 GB/s (4.3); the budget charges `k` per degraded part, the hedge (4.6) bounds the tail, the margin-0 rules of 10.4 apply, and the audit (10.8) shows the margin. There is no read drain: the data shards of a down node cannot be read anywhere else.
- **Index size and recovery time.** The index lives in an embedded ordered store on the NVMe (10.2), so its memory is the store's cache, not one entry per key in RAM. Recovery replays from the replay mark in seconds to minutes; a full rebuild from the cold trailers is read-heavy (10.2). T-S3 measures bytes per key, point-read latency and bulk load at 1e8 keys; if `fjall` fails them, `redb` replaces it behind the same trait.
- **Partial stripes and fragmentation.** Expiry classes multiply open segments and stripes waiting for `k` blocks; a partial stripe costs up to 5x at `4+4` (10.5). The design bounds it (one open segment per replica pair and class, the age-seal floor, stripes from any pair grouped by length, first-fit decreasing packing), and `cold_fill_efficiency`, `gc.classes_max`, `partial_after` and the counters show it.
- **GC and garbage are not measured.** 38% of pack bytes are dead today and no GC runs; the rewrite cost and the effect of expiry grouping are estimates until T0-F C-10 replays the death distribution (10.5).
- **Promote policy is unvalidated.** Thresholds are guesses; with 0.5 MB/s of cluster reads it will mostly idle (10.5).
- **Coupling with the CAS service's deletes.** The store with a cold tier never evicts for capacity, so a pack disappears only by a DELETE or by loss beyond the code's tolerance; a running job whose pack the CAS service deletes takes the lost path of 4.6, and the CAS service's 404 handling (3.5) is a requirement. A DELETE needs the metadata store, so the CAS service's garbage collection waits through a failover and must treat `503` as "retry", never as absence.
- **Hot spots.** A hot pack is one block on one spindle; defences are the 31 node caches, hedged degraded reads, the per-drive backoff of 4.3, slot release (10.7) and promote-on-read.

### 10.14 Configuration schema (e)
Configuration is files and flags, never environment variables; a flag overrides the file. Redundancy is two independent settings, `hot.replicas` and `cold.ec` or `cold.replicas`, with failure domains `node` or `drive`; leaving out the `[cold]` section removes the cold tier (below).

**Components are sections [D].** Each component of 10.1 is one section, present if and only if the component runs: `[hot]`, `[cold]`, `[peer]`, `[s3]`, `[cache]`, `[fetch]`, `[fuse]`, `[nfs]`, `[prepare]`. The other sections are settings of one of them (`[metadata]`, `[reconcile]`, `[health]`, `[index]`, `[promote]`, `[gc]` of the hot store; `[destage]`, `[rebuild]`, `[drive]` of the cold store; `[client]` of the fetch engine) or of the node (`[node]`, `[store]`), and a settings section whose component is absent is refused by name, so a typo cannot silently disable a component while its settings sit unused. There is no `role` key and no other key that names a kind of node. **The hot store requires `[metadata]`** (the dependency table of 10.1 lists it): every node that runs the hot store is a client of the metadata store (10.16), where block-level truth lives, and has no mode without it, so a single-node install and a hot-only install have a `[metadata]` section too (examples below). `[metadata]` holds only how to reach that Postgres; the Postgres, Patroni and etcd daemons are packages of hostkeeper with their own files (10.16, T-S23), not sections of this configuration. `[reconcile]` and `[health]` are settings sections of the hot store (their keys are in the key paragraph below): the reconciler of 24.5 runs on every node that runs the hot store and acts on the node that holds its lease, and the health facts of 24.2 are written by those nodes. The `[node]` key `membership` names the file that seeds `store init` (10.8): it is read only by `tierstore store init`, on the node where the cluster is created, when a cluster is first created; every later start ignores it, a node that did not run `store init` may omit it, and it is not the authority afterwards (membership is rows of the metadata store, changed by operator verbs). The dependency check is the table of 10.1, run once at startup, before any component starts; a refused set names each missing dependency. The three deployment examples are files, `docs/examples/later-storage.toml`, `docs/examples/later-build.toml` and `docs/examples/now-combined.toml`; the block below is `later-storage.toml` with every key commented, and T-S1's validation test parses every file of `docs/examples/` (rows of section 14).

```toml
# docs/examples/later-storage.toml: one of the three storage nodes of LATER (section 21.2);
# they differ only in node.id and addresses. Components: hot store, cold store, peer service, S3 front.
[node]
id = 1
membership = "/etc/tierstore/membership.toml"   # optional after `store init`: read only by `store init` on the node that creates the cluster, ignored on every later start (10.8)
token_file = "/etc/tierstore/cluster.token"

[peer]                              # the peer service (10.6); requires [hot]
listen = "192.0.2.11:7480"          # private network only (documentation address)

[s3]                                # the S3 front (10.9)
listen = "192.0.2.11:7481"
backend = "store"                   # "store": this store's door, requires [hot]; "cache": requires [cache] and [fetch]

[store]
kind = "tiered"
unavailable_wait = "10m"            # 4.6

[metadata]                          # the metadata store (10.16): a replicated Postgres on the three storage nodes; [hot] requires it
hosts = ["192.0.2.11:5432", "192.0.2.12:5432", "192.0.2.13:5432"]   # every member; tried in turn, the one that accepts writes is used
database = "tierstore"
user = "ts_rw"                      # data changes in schema ts only, no schema changes (10.16)
password_file = "/etc/tierstore/metadata.password"   # secret material, not readable by group or others
statement_timeout = "100ms"         # per statement of a hot-path transaction
txn_timeout = "2s"                  # per hot-path transaction
retry_window = "45s"                # how long one operation retries across a failover before it answers 503 Retry-After
tomb_poll = "2s"                    # how often nodes pull new tombstones and changed block and stripe rows; bounds immutable-bucket staleness after DELETE (10.9)

[hot]                               # the hot partition of the NVMe (the rest is the metadata partition, 10.16); replication only
dir = "/mnt/nvme/ts-hot"
replicas = 3                        # 1 .. number of failure domains (Q2: every node holds every hot object)
min_acks = 2                        # acknowledged on two durable copies; the third follows in the stream
failure_domain = "node"             # "node" | "drive"
large_min = "4MiB"                  # above: a whole file; at or below: a record in a segment
seal_bytes = "1023MiB"              # default = cold.block_bytes - cold.block_reserve
seal_max_age = "24h"                # age seal, only at or above seal_min_bytes
seal_min_bytes = "256MiB"           # below it, the age seal waits until destage.max_age
batch_max = "4MiB"
replica_stall = "5s"
put_copy_stall = "10s"
queue_depth = 64
dirty_destage_start = 0.50          # also: no promotion above this mark
dirty_destage_stop  = 0.35
dirty_urgent = 0.75
dirty_slow   = 0.85
dirty_reject = 0.90                 # also applies to total filesystem fill after reclaim
keep_clean = true                   # keep destaged copies as a read cache until space is needed
promo_share = 0.20                  # cache segments
gc_live_below = 0.5
scrub_period = "7d"
# on_full = "reject"                # "reject" | "evict"; only without a [cold] section (hot-only)

[cold]                              # 15 HDD per node
drives = ["/mnt/hdd01", "/mnt/hdd02", "...", "/mnt/hdd15"]
block_bytes = "1GiB"
block_reserve = "1MiB"              # header, alignment and trailer room; larger objects are chains
failure_domain = "node"
ec = { k = 4, m = 4 }               # exactly one of `ec` and `replicas` (Q1)
# replicas = 2
max_shards_per_domain = 3           # default ceil((k+m) / domains)
fill_limit = 0.85                   # no new stripe on a drive above it
rebuild_fill_ceiling = 0.92         # rebuild writes only, while a stripe is below tolerance
node_loss_reserve = true            # keep one node's raw free ...
reserve_drives = 2                  # ... plus this many drives (default: one per remaining node)
scrub_period = "30d"
deep_scrub_fraction = 0.01
parked_buffer_max = "256MiB"        # 10.7

[destage]
min_age = "1h"
max_age = "3d"                      # "0s" with min_age "0s": destage as soon as a block seals
partial_after = "7d"                # a stripe from fewer than k blocks (10.5)
bytes_per_hour = "2TiB"             # background rate; urgent destage is not capped
pack_window = 64                    # large files considered together for first-fit decreasing
verify = "tail"                     # "none" | "tail" | "full"
orphan_age = "1d"

[promote]
reads = 2
window = "1h"
max_object = "256MiB"
bytes_per_hour = "100GiB"

[gc]
rewrite_below = 0.30
rewrite_fill = 0.60
rewrite_bytes_per_hour = "50GiB"
classes_max = 8

[rebuild]
drive_delay = "10m"
node_down_after = "24h"
share = 0.25

[reconcile]                         # settings of the hot store: the reconciler (24.5)
interval = "10s"                    # desired versus actual redundancy is compared this often, and on events
lease = "15s"                       # the reconciler lease in the metadata store; the database clock judges it
lease_renew = "5s"
executor_wait = "30s"               # a job not leased by its first-ranked node within this is taken by the next rank (24.1)

[health]                            # settings of the hot store: the health facts (24.2)
report_after = "30s"                # a node unreachable for this long is reported unreachable by its peers (24.2)

[index]
engine = "fjall"                    # "fjall" | "redb" (10.2)
flush_interval = "1m"               # flush with the replay mark

[drive]                             # per-HDD scheduler, 10.7
streams = 2
write_streams = 1
depth = 2
chunk = "4MiB"
slice_bytes = "32MiB"
queue_max = 8
write_min_share = 0.25
slot_release_after = "1s"
parked_max = 16
demand_preempt_max = 4              # per second per drive
stall_after = "10s"
eio_limit = 3
```

`[metadata]` keys: `hosts` (required) is every member of the metadata cluster as `host:port`; the client tries them in turn and uses the one that accepts writes (10.16). `database` (default `tierstore`), `user` (default `ts_rw`) and `password_file` (required; the password is read from the file, never from the configuration or an environment variable) name the login.
