The three timeouts are the client's (10.16) and are tuning knobs, not site facts, so each has a default and `hosts` and `password_file` are the only required keys: `statement_timeout` (default 100 ms) and `txn_timeout` (default 2 s) bound the transactions of the hot path (background jobs use longer ones of their own), and `retry_window` (default 45 s) bounds how long one operation keeps reconnecting across a failover before its caller gets `MetadataUnavailable` and the front answers `503` with `Retry-After`; it is longer than the 30 s failover target so that a failover is absorbed by one retry window. `tomb_poll` (default 2 s) is how often a node pulls new tombstones from the metadata store (it is also the poll interval of the node's cache of block and stripe rows), and so the bound on how long an immutable-bucket node may serve a deleted object after it is reachable again (10.9). `[reconcile]` keys: `interval` is how often the reconciler compares desired and actual redundancy (it also runs on events), and `lease` and `lease_renew` are the reconciler lease of 24.5, judged by the database's clock, and `executor_wait` is how long the first-ranked node has to lease a job before the next rank takes it (24.1). `[health]` keys: `report_after` is how long a node may be unreachable before its peers report it (24.2). `[reconcile]` and `[health]` are settings sections of the hot store. The Postgres side (its members, Patroni, etcd, the partition, the backups) is not configured here.

```toml
# a small single-node install: one SSD for the hot tier, six HDD, no network redundancy
# components: hot store, cold store, S3 front (one node: no peer service is needed)
[node]
id = 1
membership = "/etc/tierstore/membership.toml"      # one node; the seed of `store init`
[s3]
listen = "127.0.0.1:7481"
backend = "store"
[store]
kind = "tiered"
[metadata]                          # a one-member Postgres on this machine, on its own partition of the SSD (no Patroni, no etcd)
hosts = ["127.0.0.1:5432"]
password_file = "/etc/tierstore/metadata.password"
[hot]
dir = "/srv/ts-hot"
replicas = 1                        # one domain; dirty data has one copy until destage
failure_domain = "drive"
seal_bytes = "255MiB"
seal_min_bytes = "0B"
dirty_destage_start = 0.30
dirty_destage_stop  = 0.20
[cold]
drives = ["/srv/hdd1", "/srv/hdd2", "/srv/hdd3", "/srv/hdd4", "/srv/hdd5", "/srv/hdd6"]
block_bytes = "256MiB"
failure_domain = "drive"
replicas = 2                        # each sealed block is destaged alone, as two copies on two drives
[destage]
min_age = "0s"                      # destage as soon as a block seals: the single-copy window is short
max_age = "0s"
```
(The single-node install uses cold replicas, not EC, because EC needs `k` sealed blocks to wait for: with a single hot copy the data would sit unprotected until they arrived. A node with two NVMe devices sets `hot.replicas = 2` and may use `ec = { k = 4, m = 2 }`.) **A single node also needs a metadata store [D].** The metadata store is not optional, because block-level truth (which blocks exist, where their copies are, the stripe descriptors, the tombstones, the mutable-bucket heads) is rows in it. A single node runs a **one-member Postgres** on its own small partition of the SSD (a few tens of GiB [I]): no Patroni, no etcd, no failover and no standby, so a restart or a crash of that Postgres is a short metadata outage (immutable-bucket PUT and GET continue as 10.16 describes; seals, destage, repair and DELETE wait) and the machine is a single point of failure for the metadata as it already is for the data. It is not run without backups: the install configures pgBackRest to an off-box target that is not tierstore (10.16), and a total loss of the machine is recovered by restoring the backup and running `tierstore store metadata fsck`, which rebuilds the rows from the facts on the surviving drives. `fsync`, `synchronous_commit` and `full_page_writes` stay on; there is no standby to be synchronous with, so a commit is durable on the one member's partition.

**Hot-only mode: no cold tier [D].** The same daemon and code, without destage: the configuration has no `[cold]` section (the cold store does not run, 10.1). The hot tier is then the durable tier, its redundancy is `hot.replicas`, and there is no dirty-versus-clean distinction. Segments still seal (so reclaim and GC work on whole files; `hot.seal_bytes` defaults to 1023 MiB and is bounded by the default block size of 1 GiB less the 1 MiB reserve, since `cold.block_bytes` and `cold.block_reserve` are not set) and the index, the fence-and-compare-and-set seals, the metadata store, tombstones, LIST and the S3 subset are unchanged. **The marks of 10.5 are measured on each node's total hot bytes** (open and sealed segments and large files, as a fraction of its NVMe) instead of dirty bytes. What happens when the NVMe fills is the one choice, `hot.on_full`:
- `"reject"`: never drop data. Above `dirty_slow` PUTs answer 503 `Retry-After` and above `dirty_reject` 507, exactly as a stalled destage would; GC (deletes, compaction of mostly dead segments) is the only way space returns. This is a store.
- `"evict"`: cache semantics. When any member of a replica set is above `dirty_destage_start` (members report their fill in `x-ts-load`, 10.6), the set's writer drops the set's least recently read **sealed** segments and large files, whole, until every member is below `dirty_destage_stop`; open segments are never dropped. A drop is a **row** of the metadata store, not a record that travels: the writer sets `blocks.state = 'dropped'` for the block id (a large file has its own block id) in one transaction, the same transaction from which the unlinks are scheduled, and it applies to the records physically in that block or file only, never to the key (so a copy of the key in another block is untouched). Every holder of a copy learns the state from its own poll of the `blocks` rows (a returning node reads them before it leaves `indexing`), unlinks the file and removes the index entries pointing into it, and the row becomes `freed` when every node not `out` has reported the unlink. A later read of a dropped key is a miss (404, a definitive miss), and a build node takes the lost path of 4.6. A drop is a destructive action, so a node that is frozen (10.16) or cannot reach the metadata store drops nothing; if writes then outrun the space already free so that open segments alone pass `dirty_reject`, PUTs answer 507. Because a dropped record is not a delete and its bytes simply vanish, `"evict"` is for immutable buckets only: the verb `tierstore store bucket create --mode mutable` is refused while `hot.on_full = "evict"` (a mutable bucket's head must never name bytes the store has dropped). This is a cache, and `store_evicted_bytes_total` counts it.
Validation refuses `hot.on_full` when the cold store runs, refuses a configuration without `[cold]` and without `hot.on_full`, and in hot-only mode refuses the settings sections of the cold store (`[destage]`, `[rebuild]`, `[drive]`, by the rule of 10.14 that a settings section needs its component) and the hot keys that only a cold tier gives meaning (`[promote]`, `hot.keep_clean`, `hot.promo_share`, `gc.rewrite_fill`). Hot-only mode still requires `[metadata]`.

```toml
# hot-only, three nodes, NVMe or SSD only: a replicated store that never drops data
# components: hot store, peer service, S3 front
[node]
id = 1
membership = "/etc/tierstore/membership.toml"      # three nodes; the seed of `store init`
token_file = "/etc/tierstore/cluster.token"
[peer]
listen = "192.0.2.11:7480"
[s3]
listen = "192.0.2.11:7481"
backend = "store"
[store]
kind = "tiered"
[metadata]                          # Postgres on the three nodes (10.16)
hosts = ["192.0.2.11:5432", "192.0.2.12:5432", "192.0.2.13:5432"]
password_file = "/etc/tierstore/metadata.password"
[hot]
dir = "/mnt/nvme/ts-hot"
replicas = 2                        # or 3: any two nodes may then be down for reads
failure_domain = "node"
on_full = "reject"                  # 507 when full; or "evict" for a cluster cache
# no [cold] section: the cold store does not run
```

```toml
# hot-only, one node, one disk: a local cache of an S3 bucket's packs, or a small store
# components: hot store, S3 front
[node]
id = 1
membership = "/etc/tierstore/membership.toml"      # one node; the seed of `store init`
[s3]
listen = "127.0.0.1:7481"
backend = "store"
[store]
kind = "tiered"
[metadata]                          # a one-member Postgres on this machine (see the single-node install above)
hosts = ["127.0.0.1:5432"]
password_file = "/etc/tierstore/metadata.password"
[hot]
dir = "/srv/ts"
replicas = 1
failure_domain = "drive"
on_full = "evict"                   # drop least recently read sealed segments; a dropped key is a miss
# no [cold] section: the cold store does not run
```

**Validation at startup (a configuration that cannot survive its own declared failure is refused, not warned about):**
1. `hot.replicas` is at most the number of hot failure domains; `hot.min_acks` is between 1 and `replicas`.
2. With the cold store: exactly one of `cold.ec` and `cold.replicas`. For EC: `k + m` is at most the number of cold drives, and with `failure_domain = "node"`: `max_shards_per_domain >= ceil((k + m) / domains)` and **`m >= max_shards_per_domain`**; with `"drive"`, `m >= 1`. For replicas: `replicas` is at most the number of domains. Without the cold store: `hot.on_full` set.
3. `hot.seal_bytes <= cold.block_bytes - cold.block_reserve` (their defaults, 1 GiB and 1 MiB, without the cold store); `hot.large_min <= cold.block_bytes / 4`; `hot.seal_min_bytes <= hot.seal_bytes`.
4. The marks are ordered: `dirty_destage_stop < dirty_destage_start < dirty_urgent < dirty_slow < dirty_reject <= 1`; `cold.fill_limit < cold.rebuild_fill_ceiling < 1`.
5. `destage.min_age <= destage.max_age`; `gc.rewrite_below` in (0, 1).
6. A proxy configuration (configurations A and C of tierstore-design.md) refuses `store.kind = "tiered"` as its cold store.
7. The code is recorded per stripe: changing `cold.ec` changes new stripes only; the running code of each stripe is in its stripe row and in its shard footers, and old stripes are converted only by the background re-encode (10.4).
8. **Components:** every dependency of the table of 10.1 is met (`[metadata]` among them whenever `[hot]` is present); no settings section is present without its component; the checks of 10.1 that are not set dependencies hold (`[peer]` when the hot store runs and the membership document names more than one node; `[fuse]` only on Linux; `[nfs]` only on macOS); `[client]` names exactly one of `seeds` and `membership`; at least one component runs. The node configuration refuses keys it does not know (a `role` key among them), unlike the membership seed file, which ignores them (10.8). The keys of the node cache, fetch engine, fronts, Prepare provider and profiles are in the build-node example of 21.2.
9. **The metadata store:** `metadata.hosts` is a non-empty list of `host:port` entries, each parseable, none repeated; `metadata.database` and `metadata.user` are non-empty; `metadata.password_file` exists, is readable by the daemon's user, is not empty and is not readable by group or others; the timeouts are ordered, `metadata.statement_timeout < metadata.txn_timeout < metadata.retry_window`, and `metadata.retry_window` is at least 30 s (the failover target of 10.16, so one retry window can absorb a failover); `metadata.tomb_poll` is positive and at most 60 s. For the reconciler: `reconcile.interval`, `reconcile.executor_wait` (at least `reconcile.interval`) and `health.report_after` are positive, `metadata.txn_timeout < reconcile.lease_renew` and `2 x reconcile.lease_renew < reconcile.lease` (a renewal that times out still leaves two more tries before the lease lapses). The check is static: validation does not contact the metadata store; the cluster id, schema version and incarnation are checked when the node connects, after validation (10.16). With `hot.on_full = "evict"`, no mutable bucket exists or may be created (see above).

### 10.15 Capacity for our hardware (h)
All figures [E] from the stated hardware and marks; decimal TB. **Each NVMe is split into two partitions:** a **256 GiB metadata partition** (0.275 TB) for the metadata store (10.16), and the rest, the **hot partition**, for the hot tier. Everything below that is about the hot tier uses the hot partition, 6.4 TB - 0.275 TB = 6.125 TB per device; the marks of 10.5 are fractions of the hot partition.

**Hot tier (3 x 6.4 TB U.2 NVMe, each 6.125 TB hot and 0.275 TB metadata).**

| Quantity | Value |
|---|---|
| Raw NVMe | 19.2 TB (17.5 TiB), of which 0.83 TB (3 x 256 GiB) is the metadata partitions and **18.4 TB (16.7 TiB) is hot raw** |
| Usable (logical) at `hot.replicas = 3` | 6.1 TB (`replicas = 2` would give 9.2 TB) |
| Destage start / urgent / slow / reject (of hot raw, dirty) | 9.2 / 13.8 / 15.6 / 16.5 TB raw: 3.1 / 4.6 / 5.2 / 5.5 TB logical at `replicas = 3` |
| Dirty room between start and reject | 2.45 TB logical: about 72 days of 34 GB/day, about 3.6 days at 20x |
| NVMe write rate from ingest | 3 x 34 GB/day = 102 GB/day cluster-wide, 34 GB/day per device (plus destage reads and promotion writes, and the metadata store's own writes, which are small, 10.16) |
| Promoted copies (cache segments, single copy, clean, reclaimable) | at most 20% of hot raw: 3.7 TB |
| Index | in the embedded store on each node's NVMe (10.2); derived only and rebuildable; 1.39 M keys today; T-S3 measures bytes per key and cache size at 1e8 keys |
| Metadata store | see the next table: a few GB today, bounded at about 50 GB [I], on its own 256 GiB partition |

At today's ingest destage is driven by age (`destage.max_age`), not by the marks: the marks and reclaim order are first exercised above about 1.0 TB/day (3.1 TB of logical dirty bytes within the 3-day window) [E]. The metadata partitions cost 0.83 TB of raw, which is 0.28 TB of logical hot capacity at `replicas = 3` (6.4 TB would be usable without them), and they come out of the dirty room too: 2.45 TB instead of 2.6 TB.

**Metadata store (three members, each a full copy on its own 256 GiB partition; one member's data is what is sized).** Estimates [I] from the schema of 10.16 and the rows it holds, to be replaced by measurement in T-S22 and the drills of T-S15; a row is counted with its indexes.

| Table or part | Rows | Size |
|---|---|---|
| `blocks`, `hot_copies`, `stripes`, `shards` | one `blocks` row per data block: at most about 107 k data blocks at `4+4` (214 k raw 1 GiB units at the 85% fill limit, 229.5 TB raw, half of them parity, which has no `blocks` row; the base of 10.2), about 6 k hot segments and files; about 27 k stripes at `4+4`; one `shards` row per shard, about 214 k at `4+4`, and one `hot_copies` row per hot copy (about 6 k hot blocks at 3 copies, up to about 18 k): about 370 k rows in all (107 k + 6 k + 27 k + 214 k + 18 k; about 2.5 k blocks today) | 50 to 150 MB (about 200 B per row) |
| `tombstones`, `relocations` | live until their block is freed (a tombstone is deleted when its block is freed): bounded by the deletes of the days between a delete and the GC that frees the block; sized for 1e7 rows | about 1 GB |
| `ops` | kept 24 h; at most about 1e6 transactions a day expected (immutable-bucket PUTs do not write it), sized for 1e7 | about 1.5 GB |
| `health`, `repair_queue`, `trash`, `audit`, `membership`, `nodes`, `drives`, `buckets`, `cluster`, `reconciler_lease`, `sync_probe` | a few thousand rows (`sync_probe` is one row, written at the rate of the ambiguous-commit retries); `audit` grows by one row per operator verb and destructive action, a few MB a year | under 100 MB |
| `heads` (mutable buckets only) | one row per live key: **about 170 bytes per row with its index; 1e8 keys is about 17 GB**, the bound 10.16 states for mutable buckets; zero rows when only an immutable bucket exists (the CAS service's) | 0 now, up to 17 GB at the bound |
| WAL and the archive spool | bounded by the WAL budget of 10.16 (`max_wal_size` and the retained-WAL cap that protects against a stuck replication slot) | up to about 20 GB |
| etcd (three members on the same partitions) | the Patroni keys only, a few KB | under 2 GB with its history and quota |

So the partition holds about 5 GB at today's scale, and about 45 to 50 GB with a full 1e8-key mutable bucket, in either case under a fifth of 256 GiB [I]; the headroom exists for the WAL of a stuck replica, the re-initialisation of a replica by `pg_basebackup` (which holds WAL on the primary while it runs), a major upgrade's `pg_upgrade --link` (which needs little extra space), and vacuum bloat of the churning tables (`tombstones`, `ops`), not for steady growth. The alert on this partition (`metadata_partition_fill`, 70%) joins the table of section 12 with the task that registers it. **On NOW** the same schema runs on three of the six combined nodes, each on a 64 GiB partition of its SATA SSD: at today's scale (a few GB) it fits several times over; a mutable bucket of 1e8 keys (45 to 50 GB with the other tables, WAL and etcd) would fit 64 GiB (68.7 GB) but would cross the 70% alert (about 48 GB) and leave no room for `pg_basebackup` or a stuck replica's WAL, so NOW keeps a mutable bucket below about 5e7 keys (8.5 GB of heads, about 35 GB in all, about 50% of the partition), and none is planned on NOW.

**Cold tier (45 x 6 TB SAS = 270 TB = 245.6 TiB raw).** `cold.fill_limit` 85% gives 229.5 TB raw (208.7 TiB).

| Code | Overhead | Logical capacity at the fill limit | **Logical capacity under the capacity guard** (`node_loss_reserve`: 0.85 x (2 x 90 - 2 x 6) = 142.8 TB raw) |
|---|---|---|---|
| `replicas = 2` | 2.0x | 114.8 TB | 71.4 TB |
| `replicas = 3` | 3.0x | 76.5 TB | 47.6 TB |
| `4+2` | 1.5x | 153.0 TB | 95.2 TB |
| **`4+4` (default)** | 2.0x | 114.8 TB | **71.4 TB** |
| `5+4` | 1.8x | 127.5 TB | 79.3 TB |

The guard keeps free, on the two nodes that would survive a node loss, the lost node's share plus one drive per survivor: after the node-loss rebuild of 10.4 the survivors are at about 79% of their raw, below `fill_limit`, with a drive's worth of space each for the next drive failure.

**Horizon at `4+4` under the guard (71.4 TB):** 1.34 TB is live today (1.9%); at 34 GB/day (which includes today's dead bytes) the guard is reached in about 5.7 years; at 20x today's rate (about 680 GB/day) in about 105 days; GC (10.5) lowers the rate of growth, and Q1's switch to `4+2`, which the operator makes once logical use passes half of the guarded capacity (about 36 TB), raises the ceiling to 95.2 TB. Fill is also checked against the measured live set and ingest by T0-F C-6 before the code is frozen: the capacity gap of silo (raw about 4.6x logical) is unexplained and does not enter this table.

**Rates.** Aggregate cold read bandwidth is about 45 x 190 MB/s = 8.5 GB/s of spindle against about 9.4 GB/s of network for three 25 Gbit/s storage nodes [I]; with one storage node down it is about 2.8 GB/s, because a third of the reads are degraded at `k = 4` reads each on 30 drives (4.3). Today's read load is 0.5 MB/s [M] and ingest 0.4 MB/s, so neither bound is near; they set the ceilings the controller of 4.3 works under. A stripe of `4+4` is 8 GiB of sequential writes spread over 8 drives, a few seconds at spindle rate.

### 10.16 The metadata store (Postgres) [D]
This subsection is the one place that defines the store that holds the block-level truth: what is in it, its schema, the transactions that change it, the client, the failure behaviour, the failover manager, the backups, the rollback defence, the repair tool and the upgrade procedure. The sections that use it (10.3 seal, 10.5 write path and movement, 10.8 membership, 10.9 the S3 API, 24 operations) say what they ask of it and point here for the how. The runbooks that operate it are listed in 24.12; the procedures are here.

**Purpose, and what is not in it.** Postgres holds the facts that more than one node must agree on and that must survive any one node: which segments and blocks exist, in what state, on which nodes; the stripe descriptors; the tombstones; the relocations; the heads (current versions) of keys in mutable buckets; the membership document and the health facts; the repair queue; the buckets; the audit trail. It holds **block-level truth only**. It never holds object bytes, and it is **never on the PUT or GET path of an immutable bucket** (10.9): those paths are decided by the nodes' own indexes, the replication stream and the all-node check, exactly as the data path of 10.3 to 10.6 describes, and they continue while Postgres is down. Every segment, trailer, `blk/*.idx` and shard footer on a node's disks is complete without it: a Postgres that is lost entirely can be rebuilt from the disks (`metadata fsck`, below). The node's `fjall` index (10.2) stays a derived, rebuildable local structure. The block table that each node kept in revision 7 and 8 as an authority is now a cache of Postgres rows that the node refreshes on a miss, on a failed compare-and-set and by a poll every `metadata.tomb_poll` (2 s).

What Postgres replaces is the consensus code this design would otherwise own: single-decree seal agreement, majority-committed descriptor and free records, the health ledger's voted facts, a cluster-wide logical clock and per-origin tombstone logs. A single transactional store gives all of them with one mechanism whose behaviour (isolation, crash recovery, point-in-time recovery, replication, tooling) is widely operated and documented [V]. The cost is a dependency with its own failure mode, stated in "What stops and what continues" below, and the operations of a database, stated in this subsection. 10.12 (why not) gives the reasons for not building the consensus ourselves.

**Deployment.** A dedicated Postgres cluster of **three members** on the storage tier: a primary, one synchronous standby and one more standby, never on a machine outside the storage tier. It is not the Postgres of the CAS service or of the job scheduler: the CAS service runs on tierstore (the store would depend on a service that depends on it, a cycle), and a shared instance would share its blast radius and its maintenance windows with services that have nothing to do with block truth.

| Profile | Members | Where | Storage |
|---|---|---|---|
| LATER | 3 | the three storage nodes | a 256 GiB partition of each node's NVMe (xfs, `noatime`) for Postgres data, WAL and etcd; the hot tier's usable space shrinks by about 0.28 TB per device (10.15) |
| NOW | 3 | three of the six combined nodes | a 64 GiB partition of the SATA SSD (21.1) |

Sizing of the partition [E from the row estimates below]: a few hundred thousand rows for blocks, stripes and shards, up to a few million tombstone and `ops` rows at the heaviest, and mutable-bucket heads at about 170 bytes per row with its index [I]: 1e8 live heads are about 17 GB [I] (that figure excludes the deleted heads of the retention window, bounded by the retention times the delete rate), so 256 GiB holds the metadata, WAL for a day of heavy writes, and etcd with room. Mutable buckets are meant for up to about 1e8 keys in all; T-S22 measures the bytes per head row, the point-read and LIST latency at 1e7 and 1e8 rows, the deleted-head count (`heads_deleted_rows`) and the vacuum behaviour, and this bound is stated again from that measurement. Moving the members from the three NOW nodes to the three LATER nodes is a Patroni member add and remove, one member at a time (T-S12, 21.3).

**What is in it: the schema.** Every table is in schema `ts`. Every row has `row_version bigint`, incremented by each update and used for compare-and-set. Row counts are estimates [I] at the LATER fill of 10.15 unless stated. Every table that a node polls by cursor (`tombstones`, `blocks`, `stripes`, `shards`, `hot_copies`) also has `xid xid8 NOT NULL DEFAULT pg_current_xact_id()`, indexed, which is what the gap-free poll of 10.2 reads (a column default applies to an insert only, so **a `BEFORE UPDATE` row trigger on `blocks`, `stripes`, `shards` and `hot_copies` sets `NEW.xid = pg_current_xact_id()` and `NEW.row_version = OLD.row_version + 1`**: no statement has to set them and none can forget, and the trigger's increment replaces the explicit `row_version = row_version + 1` that a statement of these tables would otherwise carry; `tombstones` rows are never updated; the trigger is part of the additive migration); every column added below is nullable or has a default, so the schema is additive.

| Table | Columns (besides `row_version`) | Written by, and when | Rows [I] |
|---|---|---|---|
| `cluster` | `id = 1`, `cluster_id uuid`, `incarnation bigint`, `frozen bool`, `frozen_reason text`, `schema_version int`, `pg_system_id text` | `store init` once (it also creates the sequences `ts.txn_seq` and `ts.head_version`); **read-only in steady state**: every write transaction takes `txn_seq = nextval('ts.txn_seq')` and returns it (a sequence is non-transactional and monotonic, takes no row lock and may have gaps, so no write serialises on this row); `store metadata restore`, `store metadata promote-lone` and `metadata fsck` completion set `incarnation`; fsck, a failed rollback check, `promote-lone` or the operator set `frozen` | 1 |
| `membership` | `epoch bigint`, `changed_at`, `changed_by`, `document jsonb` | `store init` (epoch 1) and each membership verb (10.8, 24.9); newest epoch is current; `GET /store/v1/membership` serves a document rendered from `nodes` and `drives` at that epoch | tens, kept in full |
| `nodes` | `node_id smallint pk`, `peer_addr`, `s3_addr`, `failure_domain`, `hot_state in\|drain\|out`, `admin_state in new\|active\|cordoned` (default `active`: `store node add` writes `new`; the owner's burn-in result moves it to `active` in a transaction with an `audit` row; `cordon` and its undo toggle `cordoned`, which means no new placements; `maintenance` lives only in `health` rows, 24.2), `pending_state in drain\|out`, `pending_effective_at timestamptz` (database clock, 24.9), `incarnation_seen`, `tomb_applied xid8` (the node's gap-free tombstone cursor, 10.2; reset to 0 when the node adopts a new metadata incarnation, 10.16) | `store init`; the node verbs (a delayed state change writes `pending_state` and `pending_effective_at`, 24.9); a node on start records the incarnation it runs, and advances its own `tomb_applied` after a poll (one small update per node every few seconds), which the free of 10.5 reads as `min(tomb_applied)` over the nodes not `out` | 3 to 6 |
| `drives` | `drive_id uuid pk`, `node_id`, `mount_path`, `fs_uuid`, `weight_bytes`, `state in\|drain\|out\|failed` | `store init`; the drive verbs; a node reporting a drive `failed` | 45 on LATER |
| `health` | `subject_kind node\|drive`, `subject_id`, `fact up\|missing\|maintenance\|flapping\|lost\|returned`, `since`, `until`, `flap_score`, `reporter` | nodes (`missing` and its closing `up`), the reconciler (`lost`, `flapping`, `returned`, maintenance end; 24.2, 24.3) and the operator verbs (`maintenance`, `lost`); one fact is one row written by one transaction, with no voter set | tens, history purged after 30 days |
| `buckets` | `bucket_id`, `name unique`, `mode immutable\|mutable`, `created_at` | `store bucket create` only; the mode is fixed at creation | a handful |
| `blocks` | `block_id pk`, `kind segment\|pack\|large\|cache-excluded`, `writer_node`, `replica_set smallint[]`, `epoch bigint`, `state open\|sealed\|cold\|clean\|dropped\|freed`, `sealed_length`, `sealed_crc`, `blake3`, `record_count`, `live_count`, `live_bytes`, `expiry_class`, `stripe_id null`, `degraded_since null` | lazy insert at segment open for an immutable bucket (batched every few seconds), or the first transaction that names a block with no row (5 and 6), or any holder (24.4); the seal compare-and-set; descriptor commit; relocate and tombstone transactions (live counts); the stripe and block free | data blocks only (parity has no row): about 107 thousand at `4+4` at the fill limit, plus the hot ones (the base of 10.2); `freed` rows purged after 7 days, **except** that the `dropped` and `freed` rows of kind `segment` and `large` are kept while any node is `lost`, or `out` and not yet rejoined fresh, and are purged only once every such node has reported its holdings (24.4) or rejoined fresh and adopted a new incarnation, because a returning copy with no row would otherwise be re-inserted as live (24.4) |
| `hot_copies` | `block_id`, `node_id`, `state` | the same transactions as `blocks`, and the reconciler's repair commits | 3 per hot block |
| `stripes` | `stripe_id pk`, `code k,m or replicas`, `expiry_class`, `coordinator_node`, `state committed\|freeing\|freed`, `layout_debt null`, `degraded_since null`, `move_to null` | descriptor commit (it sets `layout_debt` when the layout is degraded); stripe free; repair and move commits (24.5) | about 27 thousand at `4+4` (the base of 10.2) |
| `shards` | `stripe_id`, `shard_idx`, `block_id null` (null for parity), `node_id`, `drive_id`, `file`, `blake3`, `state ok\|missing\|bad\|rebuilding` | the stripe descriptor as rows: written by the descriptor commit (`blake3` is the blake3 of the shard, set at encode time and equal to the one the shard footer already carries, 10.4), changed by each repair commit | about 214 thousand at `4+4` (one per shard, parity included) |
| `tombstones` | `seq bigserial`, `xid xid8`, `block_id`, `offset`, `bucket_id`, `key`, `generation`, `created_at` | the delete and the mutable overwrite transactions; each names a **record id** `(block_id, offset)`; `seq` is assigned at INSERT, not at commit, so a node polls by the `xid` horizon of 10.2 (gap-free), never by the highest `seq`; deleted only in the transaction that sets `freed` on the block it names (a drop deletes nothing), which is exact: no drop rule and no per-origin cursor | thousands to a few million |
| `relocations` | `old_block`, `old_offset`, `new_block`, `new_offset` | the relocate transaction; kept until `old_block` is freed | proportional to GC and pack assembly |
| `heads` | `bucket_id`, `key bytea`, `version bigint`, `generation bytea`, `record_block`, `record_offset`, `size`, `deleted bool`, `committed_at`, `marker_block null`, `marker_offset null`; primary key `(bucket_id, key)`, and a partial index `(bucket_id, key) WHERE NOT deleted` for GET and LIST | mutable-bucket PUT and DELETE (`version` is `nextval('ts.head_version')`, never reset); the marker location is set by a small second transaction after the marker append, conditioned on `version` (transaction 6); the purge of deleted heads (below) | one per live key plus deleted heads younger than the deleted-head retention (fixed at 7 days; the bound is 1e8 live keys plus the deletes of the retention window: a sustained delete rate of R per day adds R x 7 rows of about 170 bytes), up to about 1e8 live keys |
| `repair_queue` | `job_id pk`, `kind`, `subject`, `priority P0..P4`, `margin`, `state queued\|leased\|done\|refused`, `lease_holder`, `lease_until`, `attempts`, `refusal_reason` | the reconciler (24.5) enqueues; executors lease and commit | zero when healthy; thousands during a rebuild |
| `reconciler_lease` | `id = 1`, `holder node_id`, `until timestamptz` | the node that holds or takes the reconciler's lease (24.5) | 1 |
| `ops` | `op_id uuid pk`, `kind`, `subject_digest bytea` (a digest of the target row ids), `result jsonb`, `committed_at` | every transaction of the client (idempotency); purged after 24 h | up to a few million |
| `audit` | `seq`, `at`, `actor`, `verb`, `subject`, `before`, `after` | every operator verb and every destructive action, in the transaction that does it | thousands per year |
| `sweep_candidates` | `record_block`, `record_offset`, `first_seen timestamptz` (database clock) | the orphan sweep of unreferenced mutable records (below) | small |
| `sync_probe` | `id smallint pk` (one row, `id = 1`), `touched_at timestamptz` | created by `store init` and by the migrate verb (additive); written only by a retry that finds an `op_id`, as one small update on the same connection under synchronous commit (the client's sync probe), and never read for any decision | 1 row |
| `trash` | `block_id` or `stripe_id`, `files`, `trashed_at`, `purge_after` | the free and orphan paths: trash before delete (24.15 item 4); purged by the sweeper after `purge_after` | small |

`blocks.epoch` is the segment epoch of the fence (10.3). **Live counts are exact, and meaningful only from the seal on.** The seal runs in one transaction: it locks or inserts the row, computes `live_count = record_count - (SELECT count(*) FROM tombstones WHERE block_id = $s AND offset < $L)` there and then does the compare-and-set, so the tombstones it subtracts are exactly those committed before it took the row lock. Every tombstone or relocate transaction (4, 5 and 6, transaction 6 also for the previous head's block and for the `412`-path tombstone) locks the block row `FOR UPDATE` and decrements only when the row's state is `sealed`, `cold` or `clean` and `live_count > 0`; in `open` it inserts only the `tombstones` row, and a missing row is a no-op for the count. The open-then-seal path applies to kind `segment` only: a kind-`large` row is inserted directly in state `sealed` with `record_count` 1, `live_count` 1 and `sealed_length` and `live_bytes` the file size (the file is immutable from its rename and holds one record, so the count is exact from the insert), and a delete of a large file decrements it like any sealed block. A commit or delete marker is a record of its block too: the transaction that supersedes it inserts a `tombstones` row for its `(marker_block, marker_offset)` (transaction 6 for an overwrite or delete, transaction 4 for a relocate), so this rule decrements it, or the seal subtracts it, exactly as for a data record. Every later transaction sees `sealed` and decrements, so in every ordering a tombstone is neither counted twice nor missed, and no count goes negative. Garbage mutable records, `412`-path tombstones and adopted unacknowledged records count as live from the seal until a tombstone names them. A stripe is freed, and a wholly dead hot block is dropped, by a transaction that tests the count (transaction 7 below), so no node needs to be asked whether anything still refers to the block.

**The transactions.** Each is one Postgres transaction under the client of the next heading, at the default isolation (`read committed`) with explicit row locks where stated. Each carries an operation id and **inserts it into `ops` as its first statement, before the incarnation check, any row lock and any side effect** (the client's replay rule below depends on it), checks `cluster.incarnation` and `NOT cluster.frozen` (a read of `cluster`: no write, no lock), and takes `txn_seq = nextval('ts.txn_seq')` and returns it; these three are not repeated below. **Every lease-guarded reconciler transaction** (7, 9 and every other commit of the reconciler: a refusal, a state change) begins with `SELECT 1 FROM ts.reconciler_lease WHERE id = 1 AND holder = $me AND until > clock_timestamp() FOR SHARE` and aborts if it returns no row; `now()` is the transaction's start time and would pass a holder paused after `BEGIN`, and the `FOR SHARE` lock makes a taker's `UPDATE` wait for the holder's commit, so a holder that passed the check cannot be overtaken (`idle_in_transaction_session_timeout`, set to `metadata.txn_timeout`, kills a stalled holder so the wait is bounded). "CAS" is `UPDATE ... WHERE id = $id AND row_version = $v`, and a transaction whose CAS touches no row reports the conflict to the caller, which reads the committed row and converges to it.

1. **Segment row, lazy insert.** `INSERT INTO blocks (block_id, kind, writer_node, replica_set, epoch, state, ...) VALUES (..., 1, 'open', ...) ON CONFLICT (block_id) DO NOTHING`, batched for every segment opened in the last few seconds, with the `hot_copies` rows. An open segment of an immutable bucket never waits for it: the writer appends and replicates under `(segment id, epoch 1)` whether or not the row exists yet (an immutable PUT does not touch Postgres, so it never waits for the row). The row is needed by the seal, which inserts it if it is still missing, and by a mutable write, whose head transaction inserts it first (5 and 6). **A hot copy whose row is missing is a pending insert, not an orphan; any holder may insert it** (`ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`, with the `hot_copies` rows, from the replica set and epoch of the segment header or replication stream, 24.4). **The open-then-seal path is for kind `segment` only. A kind-`large` row is never `open`:** wherever it is created (this transaction, transaction 5 or 6, the start of a destage, or a holder, 24.4) it is `INSERT INTO blocks (block_id, kind, writer_node, replica_set, epoch, state, sealed_length, record_count, live_count, live_bytes, blake3, ...) VALUES (..., 'large', ..., 1, 'sealed', size, 1, 1, size, generation, ...) ON CONFLICT (block_id) DO NOTHING`, with its `hot_copies` rows; the block id and size come from the `big/` file name and the rest from the front, which holds the record id.
2. **Seal compare-and-set.** After the fence of 10.3 has collected `replicas - min_acks + 1` fenced answers and chosen the length `L`: `UPDATE blocks SET state = 'sealed', epoch = $e1, sealed_length = $L, sealed_crc = $C, record_count = $n, live_count = $live, blake3 = $h WHERE block_id = $s AND state = 'open' AND epoch <= $e`, where `e1 = e + 1`; if the row does not exist the transaction inserts it in the sealed state instead. Exactly one sealer's update changes the row. A loser (zero rows) reads the committed row and converges its copy to it. A sealer that finds the row already `sealed` at another length truncates or fetches to that length, never the other way round. `live_count` is computed in this transaction as the live-count paragraph above states.
3. **Descriptor commit.** The coordinator of a destage has written the shards and their footers. One transaction: it checks that every source block is `sealed` (a kind-`large` row is `sealed` from its insert, so a large file can be a source) and has no `stripe_id`, as the condition `state = 'sealed' AND stripe_id IS NULL` and **not on `row_version`** (a delete or relocate that only lowers `live_count` while the stripe streams must not fail the commit; a block dropped meanwhile fails the condition and the whole transaction; the committed block's `live_count` is whatever Postgres holds at commit, and the stripe free of 10.5 handles blocks that die later); inserts the `stripes` row (`committed`) and its `shards` rows; sets `blocks.state = 'cold'` and `stripe_id` on the data blocks; sets the `hot_copies` rows of these blocks to `clean`; for a pack-assembly destage it also inserts the new pack block rows and runs the whole of transaction 4 inside the same commit (the `relocations` rows, the live counts, the compare-and-set of the heads of mutable-bucket keys with their new markers, and a `tombstones` row naming the new record id of every skipped record; transaction 4 is also callable on its own, for a GC rewrite); inserts nothing if a descriptor for these blocks already exists (the retry of an ambiguous commit finds its own `op_id`). Until it commits the shards are unreferenced files: if the coordinator dies they are found by the orphan sweep after `destage.orphan_age` (below) and trashed. The hot copies are reclaimable only after this transaction commits (10.5).
4. **Relocate (pack assembly and GC rewrite): the one canonical relocate body.** The new block's bytes and trailer are already written when it runs (the streamed pack of 10.5, or the records re-appended to a hot segment); (a) it locks the source block rows (`SELECT ... FOR UPDATE`) and, for a mutable-bucket key, the key's `heads` row; (b) it reads which of the records to be moved already have a tombstone and skips those; **for each record skipped because a tombstone names its old record id, or because a mutable head moved on, it inserts a `tombstones` row naming the new `(block_id, offset)` that the already-written trailer lists**, copying bucket, key and generation, and adding no `live_count` or `live_bytes` to the new block and no `relocations` row (in an `open` new block only the row is inserted, as in transaction 5; a new block's `live_count` is its trailer entries minus these tombstones, so a rebuild from trailers, fsck and the index audit read the entry as dead, and the row is deleted when the new block is freed); (c) for the rest it inserts `relocations (old_block, old_offset, new_block, new_offset)` and moves the records' `live_count` and `live_bytes` from the source block to the new one; (d) for a mutable-bucket key it does a compare-and-set of `heads` on `(version, old record id)` to the new record id and sets the new `marker_block` and `marker_offset`; the **fresh commit marker `(bucket, key, version, new record id)` is durable on `hot.min_acks` copies before this transaction commits**, and so before the old block may be dropped or freed; the transaction inserts a `tombstones` row for the old marker's `(marker_block, marker_offset)` and decrements its block under the state rule, and a head that moved on is left alone; (e) it runs the transaction-7 `dropped` statement for a hot source block that has no live record left and was compacted or rewritten away (a large file whose records moved into a pack stays `clean`, 10.5). The record id changes (`(block_id, offset)` is the identity of one stored copy-set, 10.2); the bytes and generation do not. A DELETE that names an old record id follows it through `relocations` to the new one; a relocate and a DELETE of the same record both lock the source block row, so they serialise and neither loses. Transaction 3 runs the whole of this body for a pack assembly, inside the descriptor commit.
5. **Delete (tombstone).** For an immutable bucket: the deleting front has asked `N - min_acks + 1` nodes for the key's live record ids (normally one); the transaction locks each named block row (`FOR UPDATE`), follows `relocations` for record ids that were moved, inserts one `tombstones` row per record id, and executes `UPDATE blocks SET live_count = live_count - 1, live_bytes = live_bytes - $size WHERE block_id = $b AND live_count > 0` (a record whose tombstone already exists is not counted twice: the insert has a unique key on `(block_id, offset)`), only for a block in state `sealed`, `cold` or `clean`; in `open` only the tombstone is inserted. A record in a block with no row yet gets the row first: before anything else the transaction runs the transaction-1 insert for it (a segment: `state 'open'`, epoch 1; a kind-`large` record: `state 'sealed'` with `record_count` 1, `live_count` 1 and size as `sealed_length` and `live_bytes`, so a tombstone that is the first transaction to name the file decrements it to 0 and drops it in the same transaction; writer and replica set as the front, which holds the record id, knows them, and the `hot_copies` rows, `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`). **When a decremented block is hot (`state IN ('sealed','clean')`) and its `live_count` reaches 0, the same transaction runs the transaction-7 statement** `UPDATE blocks SET state = 'dropped' WHERE block_id = $b AND state IN ('sealed','clean') AND live_count = 0`, and the unlinks are scheduled from that committed row; a drop deletes no `tombstones` or `relocations` row (they go with the `freed` step of transaction 7). The tombstones are then pushed to every live node (`POST /store/v1/tomb-apply`; a node applies each to the named id and to every old id that reaches it through `relocations`, 10.2); the `204` waits for `N - min_acks + 1` durable applications (10.9). For a mutable bucket the tombstone is part of transaction 6.
6. **Mutable PUT and DELETE: head upsert with preconditions.** After the record is durable on `min_acks` copies (not yet visible), one transaction first runs the transaction-1 insert for the new record's block and for any previous-head block whose row is missing (the `blocks` row `state 'open'`, epoch 1, writer and replica set as the writer, or the reader holding the record id, knows them, and the `hot_copies` rows, `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`), so that **a committed head or tombstone never names a block without a `blocks` row**; it then reads `heads (bucket_id, key)` `FOR UPDATE` (or inserts the row when absent) and evaluates the precondition against the row: no header, always; `If-None-Match: *`, fail if a live head exists; `If-Match: <etag>`, fail unless a live head exists whose `generation` equals it. On success: `version = nextval('ts.head_version')` (a cluster-wide sequence: the version rises across a delete and a re-create and exceeds every version a surviving marker remembers, so fsck can never resurrect an older write over a newer one), the head names the new record id with `deleted = false`; the previous head's record id gets a `tombstones` row and its block's `live_count` is decremented (under the state rule and the `AND live_count > 0` guard of transaction 5), in the same transaction. The transaction **refuses a record that already has a `tombstones` row** (the unique `(block_id, offset)` conflict aborts it): the front re-appends a fresh record and retries, and never acknowledges a PUT on a dead record. On a failed precondition the transaction inserts a tombstone for the just-appended record and commits that (the front answers `412`); a record that never reached either path (the front died) is found by `metadata fsck` or the orphan sweep as a record with no head and no commit marker (the sweep's rule is in "Sweeping unreferenced mutable records" below). A DELETE is the same transaction with `deleted = true`, a new `version` from the sequence and the old record id tombstoned; it answers `204` whether the key existed or not, and honours `If-Match`. After a successful commit the writer appends a **commit marker** record (bucket, key, version, record id) to its open segment and makes it durable on `min_acks` copies before answering `200` with the `ETag`; the marker is what lets `metadata fsck` rebuild the heads after a total loss. A small second transaction, **conditioned on the head's version `$v`**, then records the marker's location: it locks the head (`SELECT ... FOR UPDATE`, or `UPDATE heads SET marker_block = $b, marker_offset = $o WHERE bucket_id = $ AND key = $ AND version = $v RETURNING` the old marker) and reads the old marker under that same lock (the head upsert leaves `marker_block` and `marker_offset` naming the previous marker until a second transaction replaces it). If the head still has version `$v`, it tombstones the marker currently recorded on the head, if any (the previous marker, or none when the previous writer's own second transaction has not run yet), sets the head's marker to its own, and decrements the old marker's block under the state rule and the `AND live_count > 0` guard of transaction 5 (in an `open` segment only the row is inserted, and the seal then subtracts it, because its count takes the tombstones with `offset < L`). If the head has moved on (`version != $v`, or zero rows), it leaves the head alone, inserts a `tombstones` row for its **own** just-appended marker `(block, offset)` and decrements that block, because that marker is already superseded by a newer version. **The rule: the supersede of marker M(n-1) is done by whichever transaction installs M(n), keyed by version.** The unique `(block_id, offset)` insert (`ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`) makes it idempotent; a unique conflict here means "already superseded", is not an error, is not a replay signal and does not start the re-append path. A DELETE does the same for the head's superseded marker. Whatever the order of two writers' second transactions, only the newest marker stays live and is named by the head, and fsck rebuilds the head from it (T-S22 tests both orders). Writing the head's new `marker_block` and this decrement are one transaction, so a crash between the marker append and it leaves the old marker counted live, the safe direction. Marker tombstones are not pushed through `tomb-apply` (node indexes hold no marker entries), and fsck ignores a marker that has a `tombstones` row (10.2: a marker is live while it is the newest of its head). A mutable overwrite or delete only decrements the previous head's block; it never drops it, and the reconciler drops the block when its `live_count` reaches 0 (transaction 7). Last writer wins by commit order, and the order is Postgres's.
7. **Stripe and block free.** `UPDATE stripes SET state = 'freeing' WHERE stripe_id = $1 AND state = 'committed' AND NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM blocks WHERE stripe_id = $1 AND live_count > 0)`. If one row changes, the unlinks follow the commit, and `freed` (with the deletion of the stripe's block tombstones and relocation rows, one transaction) is committed after every holder reports its unlink and the gate of 10.5 step 4 passes; files go to `trash` first (24.15 item 4). A hot block that is wholly dead is dropped by `UPDATE blocks SET state = 'dropped' WHERE block_id = $1 AND state IN ('sealed', 'clean') AND live_count = 0`, which transaction 5 runs for a block its delete emptied (so the drop is in the delete transaction) and the reconciler runs for a block that reached 0 through a mutable overwrite or delete (transaction 6 only decrements; transaction 4 runs it for a compacted or rewritten hot source); the unlinks are scheduled from that committed row. **A drop leaves the block's `tombstones` and `relocations` rows in place.** A dropped hot block is freed through the same gate as a stripe block (step 4 of the free in 10.5): once every holder has reported its unlink and `min(nodes.tomb_applied)` over the nodes that are neither `out` nor `lost`, counted only for a node whose `incarnation_seen` equals `cluster.incarnation`, is at or above the highest `xid` of the tombstones naming the block, the owner commits `dropped` to `freed` and deletes those `tombstones` and `relocations` rows in the same transaction (a node that is `out` is cleaned at readmission, 24.4). The stripe and block free of this transaction begins with the lease check of the transaction preamble (the delete-path drop is guarded by the block row lock instead), and a `dropped` or free condition also requires that no live marker of a head lies in the block (10.2). This replaces the hot-only `block-drop` record of revision 8. A repair commit conditioned on the stripe (transaction 9) or a re-encode, move or convert commit (transaction 12) finds `freeing` and aborts: free wins over repair. A stripe set `freeing` by transaction 12 is freed without deleting the tombstones and relocation rows of its blocks, which now belong to the new stripe.
8. **Repair job lease.** `UPDATE repair_queue SET state = 'leased', lease_holder = $me, lease_until = now() + $lease, attempts = attempts + 1, row_version = row_version + 1 WHERE job_id = $j AND row_version = $v AND (state = 'queued' OR (state = 'leased' AND lease_until < now()))`. The lease is a convenience that stops two executors from copying the same data; correctness is in transaction 9, so a lease that expires while its holder is still working only wastes bandwidth.
9. **Repair commit.** After the executor has written a replacement copy or shard and its footer: one transaction conditioned on the job's `row_version` and on the stripe not being freed (`stripes.state = 'committed'`, `FOR SHARE`); it inserts or updates the `hot_copies` or `shards` row (`state = 'ok'`), sets the job `done`, and, when the reconciler's rule is a commit by the reconciler itself (a refusal, a state change), has its lease checked by the first statement of the transaction preamble (`FOR SHARE` on the lease row with `clock_timestamp()`), so the lease is enforced by the database's clock and not the nodes' clocks. A job whose version moved (the reconciler re-planned it) aborts and its data is orphan until swept.
10. **Membership verb.** One transaction: reads `membership` for the newest epoch `e`, applies the verb to `nodes` or `drives` (CAS on their `row_version`), inserts `membership (e + 1, ..., document)` where `e` is still the maximum (a concurrent verb loses, and is refused with `RACE`), and inserts the `audit` row. The guards of 24.9 (ok-to-out, one node at a time, dry-run, refusal codes) are evaluated before the transaction, and again inside it for the state they read.
12. **Re-encode, move or convert commit.** The coordinator calls it after it has written the new shards and their footers (a `4+2` to `4+4` re-encode, a move of a stripe's shards, `stripes.move_to`, or the in-place conversion of replicated blocks to a stripe, 10.5, 21.3 step 4). One transaction, with the lease check as the first statement of the preamble: (a) it is conditioned on the old stripe's `state = 'committed'` and its `row_version`, so a free wins (10.5 step 5); (b) it inserts the new `stripes` row (`committed`, the new code) and its `shards` rows (`ok`, each with its blake3); (c) `UPDATE blocks SET stripe_id = $new WHERE stripe_id = $old` as a compare-and-set on the blocks' version and state `cold` (for a replicas-to-EC conversion the same transaction removes the old stripes' copy rows); block ids, offsets, `live_count` and records stay untouched; (d) `UPDATE stripes SET state = 'freeing'` for the old stripe, deliberately skipping the `live_count > 0` test of transaction 7 because the blocks stay live (they now belong to the new stripe), and it clears `move_to` where it applies. It is idempotent on the `op_id`, and refused if any block has changed or has a tombstone-driven drop pending. The old shard unlinks and `freed` follow the transaction-7 gate and 10.5 step 4, but **must not delete the blocks' `tombstones` or `relocations` rows**, because the blocks live on.
11. **Bucket create.** `INSERT INTO buckets (name, mode) ...` (unique name, an existing name with the same mode is a no-op, with another mode is refused) and the `audit` row, in one transaction. The nodes poll the bucket table (it changes rarely) and the verb pushes the change; each node writes the table's ids, names and modes to the `buckets` record of its `NODE` file.

**Sweeping unreferenced mutable records.** A PUT in flight (record durable, head transaction pending or retrying inside `retry_window`, or head committed with its marker not yet appended) looks like garbage, so the sweep's safety rests on Postgres and not on `written_ms` (which orders nothing). The sweep first records the candidate (record id, `first_seen = now()` of the database) in `sweep_candidates`, and tombstones the record only when all of these hold: it is still not a head; no `heads` row and no commit marker names it; it has been a candidate for longer than `retry_window + txn_timeout` plus a margin (45 s + 2 s plus the margin), measured by the database clock; and the incarnation is unchanged, as for shards. The check and the tombstone insert run in one transaction that locks the key's `heads` row `FOR UPDATE`, the serialisation transaction 6 uses, and transaction 6 refuses a record that is already tombstoned. Records of a still-open or just-sealed segment that are not yet swept count as live in `live_count` at the seal, and their block frees only after the sweep tombstones them.

**Heads: versions and purge.** `heads.version` comes from the sequence `ts.head_version`, which `metadata restore` never resets and which fsck sets above the maximum marker version seen, so no write's version ever falls below a remembered one. A deleted head stays as a `deleted = true` row. The sweeper (the job class of the `ops` and `trash` purges) deletes it in a transaction conditioned on its `row_version` and on `committed_at < now() - interval '7 days'` (the deleted-head retention, fixed at 7 days and not configurable), and only once every record of the key is tombstoned and its block freed (the `tombstones` lifecycle); the old data record id was tombstoned at delete time, but the delete marker is still a record of its block and counted in its `live_count`, so the purge transaction also inserts a `tombstones` row for the head's delete marker `(marker_block, marker_offset)`, decrements that block under the state rule and the `live_count > 0` guard of transaction 5, and drops the marker's block when its `live_count` reaches 0 (the transaction-5 and transaction-7 statements; a dropped block is then freed through the gate of 10.5). Without it a purged delete marker would keep its block live forever. The retention exceeds the `ops` retention (24 h) plus `txn_timeout` and the client retry window, which 7 days cover. A PUT that finds no head inserts a version from the sequence, as for a new key. GET and LIST read heads with `deleted = false`, through the partial index, so deleted rows cost no scan.

**The client (`tierstore-meta`, T-S22).** One crate, on `tokio-postgres` (MIT or Apache-2.0), used by the storage node's components and by the operator verbs. It does the following for every caller.
- *Failover-aware.* It connects to the configured `metadata.hosts` list with `target_session_attrs = read-write`. On a connection error, a `read-only` error (a demoted primary) or a timeout it reconnects to the next host with jittered backoff (100 ms doubling to 2 s), for up to `metadata.retry_window` (45 s, longer than the 30 s failover target, which the failover manager below can reach, or pass by a few seconds, when the primary's node also held the etcd leader). Then the caller gets `MetadataUnavailable`, and the S3 or peer front answers `503` with `Retry-After`. It never turns an unavailable store into an answer of absence, and it never caches a negative answer.
- *Timeouts.* A per-statement `metadata.statement_timeout` (100 ms) and a per-transaction `metadata.txn_timeout` (2 s) for hot-path transactions (the heads, the seal, the delete); background jobs (fsck, the reconciler's scan, purges) use longer ones that they name. `statement_timeout` and `txn_timeout` bound the statements and the wait for each reply, **but never the `COMMIT` of a hot-path transaction**: `COMMIT` runs with the statement timeout disabled for that statement (`SET LOCAL statement_timeout = 0` just before it), and the client never sends a cancel request or terminates the backend while a `COMMIT` is in flight. When its own deadline passes it abandons its wait and treats the outcome as ambiguous, because with `synchronous_commit = on` a cancelled wait returns "transaction has already committed locally" without replication, so the commit is visible and a failover can lose it. The 100 ms default is a value for NOW to be **sized by measurement**: since `COMMIT` is exempt it bounds only the other statements, T-S22 measures the NOW p99 statement time at load, and the default is raised (for example to 250 ms) if it fails; the ordering rule of validation item 9 (`statement_timeout < txn_timeout < retry_window`) is kept.
- *Idempotent.* Every transaction carries an operation id (`op_id`, 128 bits: the node id (8 bits), a random 64-bit boot nonce drawn fresh at every process start and never persisted, and a counter in the remaining bits that starts at 0 per boot; an `op_id` is never derived from a persisted counter alone, so a fresh device or a restored `NODE` file cannot reuse one) and inserts it into `ops` with its `kind`, a digest of its subject (the target row ids) and the result in the same transaction. **The `INSERT INTO ops (op_id, kind, subject_digest)` is the first statement of every transaction**, before the incarnation check, the row locks and any side effect. An ambiguous commit (the connection is lost during `COMMIT`) is retried with the same `op_id`; if the original exists or is still in flight (its old backend may still be waiting on the standby when the retry arrives on a new connection), the retry's insert waits for the original's outcome and then fails with unique violation `23505` on the `ops` primary key. The client treats exactly that error, from that statement, as the **replay signal** (not a lookup that finds no row): it aborts its own transaction, re-reads `ops` for the `op_id`, and returns the recorded result instead of applying twice, **only if its `kind` and subject digest match** (a mismatch is an error, counted in `metadata_op_id_mismatch_total`, never a replay), and only after the sync probe below. A unique violation from any later statement (`tombstones`, `heads`, `blocks`) is never a replay signal and keeps its own meaning (the refuse-and-re-append path of transaction 6). Before it returns the recorded result it must make the original commit replicated: it does a trivial synchronous write on the same connection (an update of the one-row table `ts.sync_probe`), and a later synchronous commit waits for all earlier WAL, so once it returns the original commit is on the standby too; if that write fails or waits out `retry_window`, the retry returns `MetadataUnavailable` and no recorded result. A retry whose `ops` insert succeeds knows that no original committed and applies the operation as before. Every update of a row is a compare-and-set on `(id, row_version)`, so every transaction is keyed by id and version, and a stale actor loses cleanly.
- *Incarnation.* Every transaction reads `cluster` (no write, no lock), checks `cluster.incarnation = <the node's incarnation> AND NOT cluster.frozen` and gets `txn_seq` from `nextval`; a mismatch is reported as `Frozen` and the node freezes its destructive actions (below).
- *Reads.* Reads of mutable heads always go to the primary, so a read after an acknowledged write or delete sees it: read-after-write and read-after-delete hold for every write the client saw acknowledged. A read may also observe a head whose commit is locally flushed but not yet acknowledged by the synchronous standby; if the primary fails in that window, the promoted standby lacks that head and the value disappears. That applies only to writes that were never acknowledged to any client, so no acknowledged commit is lost; the design does not claim linearizability across a failover for unacknowledged writes. A point read is one round trip, about 0.3 to 0.7 ms [I, T-S22 measures]. Nodes read their caches of `blocks`, `buckets` and the membership epoch as stated for each: block rows are refreshed on a miss, on a failed compare-and-set and by the poll of 10.2, and the membership epoch is polled every 5 s (10.8).
- *Metrics.* `metadata_txn_seconds{kind}`, `metadata_txn_total{kind,result}`, `metadata_unavailable_total`, `metadata_ambiguous_retries_total`, `metadata_op_id_mismatch_total` and `heads_deleted_rows` are registered by T-S22, `hot_fenced_unsealed_blocks` is registered by T-S4 (a per-node gauge of the local segment files whose durable fence epoch is above the epoch last known for their row and that have not yet seen a committed `sealed` row; operators sum it across nodes, and it is not computed from Postgres), and `metadata_replica_lag_bytes`, `metadata_wal_free_bytes`, `metadata_backup_age_seconds` and `metadata_partition_fill` by T-S23 (the Postgres-side exporters); they join the table of section 12 with the task that registers them; the test that holds the registry to that table both ways fails until they do.

Every client of the S3 front and of the peer protocol has to ride out a failover: it treats `503` with `Retry-After` as retriable, retries for at least 60 s (covering the 30 s target with margin) with jittered backoff that honours `Retry-After`, never reads a `503` as absence, and retries idempotently (a PUT of the same bytes, a DELETE); a timeout, connection reset or transport error on a metadata-dependent operation is retried like a `503` (same operation id, same 60 s window, final outcome `ClientError::Unavailable`, never `NotFound`), and a per-request read timeout is at least `metadata.retry_window` plus a margin (60 s or more) or the client follows that rule. `tierstore-client` does this itself (section 23); the CAS service's change is its own task outside this repository (10.9).

**What stops and what continues during a metadata outage.** An outage is a failover (about 30 s or less), the loss of two of the three members (until one returns or the operator acts: Q5's default for two nodes lost), or a freeze.

| Operation | During the outage | Why |
|---|---|---|
| PUT to an immutable bucket | **continues** | the all-node check, the replication stream and group commit need no Postgres; a segment whose seal cannot commit is fenced and the writer opens a new one (node-allocated id), counted by `hot_fenced_unsealed_blocks` |
| GET, HEAD, LOCATE of immutable and cold data | **continues** | the node indexes, the cached `blocks` and `shards` rows and the footers answer; the cached tombstones are as fresh as the last poll |
| Hot replication, group commit, drive scheduler, scrub reads, repair copies that only add data | **continue** | no metadata is needed to add a copy; its commit waits |
| Seal commit, destage start, descriptor commit | stop | they are transactions; segments fenced on their copies and still `open` in the row are not destaged until the seal commits; hot headroom is about 72 days at today's ingest on LATER (10.15; NOW's is smaller and not claimed, section 20) |
| Relocate, GC rewrite, stripe and block free, orphan sweep, trash purge | stop | transactions; the data stays |
| Repair commits and new leases | stop (queued jobs wait; copies already made are kept) | alert `repair_stalled_on_metadata` after 5 minutes without a commit |
| DELETE (any bucket) | `503 Retry-After` | it needs the tombstone transaction |
| Mutable-bucket PUT, GET, HEAD, DELETE, LIST | `503 Retry-After` | the head is in Postgres |
| Membership verbs, bucket create, `store ... get` that reads Postgres | refused or stale | the verb is a transaction; `get` of the cache says how old it is; nodes keep their last membership document |
| Reconciler | pauses | no lease can be renewed; nothing is decided without Postgres |

**The failover manager: Patroni with a three-member etcd [D].** Patroni (its `synchronous_mode: true`, `synchronous_mode_strict: true`, `synchronous_node_count: 1`) runs on each of the three members, with a **three-member etcd** on the same three nodes as its distributed configuration store (DCS). The third member is an asynchronous standby, which Patroni makes synchronous if the synchronous standby fails. Settings: `ttl: 20`, `loop_wait: 5`, `retry_timeout: 5` (the rule `loop_wait + 2 x retry_timeout <= ttl` holds: 5 + 2 x 5 = 15 <= 20) and `failsafe_mode: true` (the primary keeps running while it can still reach all the members it knows of, so the loss of the node that hosts the etcd leader, or an etcd re-election, does not demote a healthy primary) [K; T-S23 confirms every name and default, `failsafe_mode` included, against the pinned release]. **Member sets.** The steady-state set is exactly three Postgres members and three etcd members; a steady-state set that is not exactly three is refused when the configuration is rendered. The member-add and member-remove verbs of 21.3 step 5 render the transient sets (up to six Postgres and six etcd members while the LATER members catch up), and refuse a second etcd member joining while one is still joining, a removal that leaves no synchronous standby or no etcd majority, and any set of more than six; 21.3, T-S12 and T-S23 cite this rule. **Patroni's watchdog** (`/dev/watchdog`, the `softdog` module where there is no hardware one) is enabled, so a primary that has lost its leader lease is demoted or reset before a new primary can be promoted.

*Failover time.* The worst case is `election + ttl + loop_wait + promotion and crash recovery + reconnect`. The case that matters most is the power loss of the primary's node, which also hosts an etcd member and possibly the etcd leader: a new etcd leader needs an election first (on NOW up to about 5 s, twice the 2.5 s election timeout, because it is randomised) and then refreshes the remaining TTL of the existing leader-key lease; the leader key then expires at most `ttl` after the last renewal, a standby looks only at its next loop (up to `loop_wait`; 20 + 5 = 25 s with the settings above), then comes the leader race, the promotion and crash recovery, then the clients' reconnect (backoff up to 2 s). Detection is therefore about 25 s without an etcd re-election and about 27 to 30 s with one (the election overlaps the lease in part), and promotion and reconnect land at or above 30 s. **30 s is therefore the target and the drill's reference figure, reported and reviewed and never a pass criterion (24.13), not a derived bound** [I]: the failover drill of T-S23 and T-S15 measures it, for a primary's node power-cycled with the etcd leader on it and without, and reports p50 and max against the target. `retry_timeout: 5` is at least the 5 s worst-case etcd election on NOW, so a standby's node that holds the etcd leader can die without the healthy primary's DCS update outlasting it (with `failsafe_mode` as the second guard): the drill that kills a non-primary node holding the etcd leader asserts no primary change and no `503` beyond one retry (T-S23, T-S15). The trade-off on NOW at a load average of 131 is that a `ttl` of 20 s is short for starved Patroni and etcd processes (the spurious-failover risk of 10.13), and the drill, not the arithmetic, is the arbiter. A planned switchover takes a few seconds. The client's `retry_window` of 45 s and the clients' 60 s of retries cover the target with margin. The loss of the synchronous standby stalls commits for up to one more Patroni loop (`loop_wait`) before the third member is made synchronous, and on NOW etcd's election timeout (heartbeat and election timeout to be pinned, about 250 ms and 2.5 s) is measured under build load; both are measured by T-S23 and T-S15, and the target stays [I] until then.

*Reasons for this choice.*
- **Why not our own lease manager.** A lease needs a store that survives the failure of the primary. Using Postgres itself for the lease fails at the moment the primary dies; any other quorum store is a consensus implementation we would own, which is exactly the code this design deletes. Patroni gives fencing (the DCS lease plus the watchdog), refuses to promote a standby that was not synchronous (so no acknowledged commit is lost), planned switchover, rolling restarts and `pg_rewind`, and is operated by many sites [V].
- **Why not manual promotion.** No human is on call at the hour of a primary's failure, and the owner's target is 30 s or less.
- **What it costs.** Two more daemons per metadata node (Patroni and etcd), packaged and updated by hostkeeper (T-S23). etcd needs low fsync latency, so it shares the metadata partition with the WAL. On NOW the nodes run at a load average of 131 on 88 CPUs [M], so Patroni, etcd and Postgres run with a high `CPUWeight` and etcd's heartbeat and election timeouts are raised (250 ms and 2.5 s) [I; the drill measures]; Patroni and etcd starvation on NOW is a named risk (10.13).

**Durability settings.** `synchronous_commit = on`, `fsync = on` and `full_page_writes = on` on both profiles; nothing turns fsync off. Strict synchronous mode means that a commit is on two members' storage before it is acknowledged; it also means that metadata writes stop (the `503` row above) when two of the three members are down. That is the same outcome as Q5's default for two nodes lost, and it is the price of never acknowledging a commit that a failover could lose (the scope of that guarantee is the writer's acknowledgement: a head read before its commit reached the standby is not an acknowledged write, as the Reads bullet above says).
- *LATER.* The NVMe drives have **power-loss protection** (the owner's statement; T0-S and the hardware audit verify it, and a drive found without PLP is refused by the audit, not silently retuned). With PLP a `fdatasync` is a write to the drive's protected cache, so Postgres's default `synchronous_commit = on`, the group commit of the hot tier, `hot.min_acks` and `batch_max` are sized assuming it; a single WAL flush costs tens of microseconds [I, T0-S measures], and a whole synchronous-commit round trip with the synchronous standby (the flush on both nodes and the network between them) about 1 ms [I, T0-S and the failover drill measure]; these are two different figures, and the commit figure is the one the other sections quote.
- *NOW.* The SATA SSDs have no PLP: every flush is honest (`fdatasync` and `fsync` reach the medium) and a single WAL flush costs milliseconds, so a whole synchronous-commit round trip with the synchronous standby is a few ms [I, T0-S and the failover drill measure]. NOW accepts the latency; the metadata transactions of the hot path are one per seal, delete or mutable write, never one per immutable PUT.

**Backups and point-in-time restore.** pgBackRest to an **off-box repository that does not depend on tierstore** (an S3-compatible bucket or a file server elsewhere, configured by the operator): a weekly full backup, a daily differential, continuous WAL archiving with `archive_timeout = 60s`, retention 30 days. A point-in-time restore is possible to any second of the last 30 days, and a total loss of the cluster costs at most about one minute of WAL (the archive timeout) [I]. The backup's age and the archive's lag are metrics and alerts (`metadata_backup_age_seconds`, a page past 36 hours). A **restore drill** runs once on the farm before production and then quarterly; each drill restores to a scratch cluster, runs `metadata fsck` against real node disks (or a copy of their facts), and ends with the check that a node refuses to start against the restored database until the incarnation is raised (next paragraph). A restore is a destructive event for every fact written since the restore point, which is why it is guarded by the incarnation and fsck.

**Incarnation, rollback detection and freeze.** A restore, or a standby promoted while it lagged, makes Postgres older than the disks: it may not know of segments sealed, stripes committed or tombstones created since, and a node that trusted it could unlink live data, free a stripe that is committed or reuse an id. The defence has three parts.
1. `cluster.incarnation` is **the primary defence**. It is set by `store init` and raised by every path that can roll Postgres back: `tierstore store metadata restore` (after a point-in-time restore), `tierstore store metadata promote-lone` (the manual promotion of the lone member of R6, and of any asynchronous or lagging member, whether by `pg_ctl promote` or `patronictl failover --force`; the verb raises the incarnation and sets `frozen` in the same step, and the nodes stay frozen until `metadata fsck` runs) and `metadata fsck` when it completes. **Adopting a new metadata incarnation is a cursor reset.** On adoption a node sets its tombstone cursor and the poll cursors of `blocks`, `stripes`, `shards` and `hot_copies` to 0 (the cursors are xid horizons, and a point-in-time restore moves the xid counter back, so a saved cursor would sit ahead of the tombstones and rows written after the restore and they would never be pulled), re-reads every tombstone row and every changed row (the apply is idempotent per record id, so the re-read is safe), and stays `indexing` until that first poll completes; it then writes `nodes.incarnation_seen` = the new incarnation and `tomb_applied` = 0, and advances `tomb_applied` only from the re-read onward. The cursors are persisted in the replay mark together with the incarnation they belong to, and a mark of an older incarnation is discarded, which gives the reset even when the node restarts before adopting. The stripe-free gate of 10.5 counts a node's `tomb_applied` only when that node's `incarnation_seen` equals `cluster.incarnation`, in the same transaction as the incarnation check, so a node on an older incarnation, or one that has not re-read, blocks the free. Each node's `NODE` file holds the incarnation it runs, the highest `txn_seq` it has seen (persisted at most every 10 s and before any destructive action) and the Postgres system identifier.
2. A node **freezes** when: Postgres's incarnation differs from its own; Postgres's `txn_seq` is below the highest the node saw (a **best-effort, probabilistic backstop**: it sees a rollback only while the live sequence is still below the remembered value, which is persisted at most every 10 s, and traffic from other nodes can hide a short rollback; the sequence is not in commit order and has gaps, and only this high-water comparison depends on it, so `metadata restore` never resets it); the Postgres system identifier in `cluster` differs from the one in its `NODE` file (a structural backstop: a different cluster was restored in place; a timeline change alone does not freeze, because a normal Patroni failover to a synchronous standby loses nothing); or `cluster.frozen` is set. A frozen node does no destructive action (unlink, stripe free, orphan sweep, trash purge, block drop, tombstone deletion, id reuse) and refuses metadata writes. It continues what only adds data or reads: reads, immutable-bucket PUTs and repair copies. The alert is `metadata_frozen`.
3. The orphan sweeper deletes nothing while frozen, and it deletes an unreferenced shard or segment only after it has been unreferenced in Postgres for `destage.orphan_age` (1 day) **and** the incarnation has not changed in that time; trash before delete still applies. So a restore that forgets a descriptor does not turn its shards into garbage until fsck has run and the incarnation has changed. **The orphan sweep never quarantines or deletes a `seg/` or `big/` file that has no row.** Only a file whose row is `freed` or `dropped`, or a shard or stripe with no row, is an orphan; a segment or large file with no row is inserted (24.4) instead.

Ids are node-allocated (an 8-bit node id and a 56-bit counter, never reused; no central allocator, which is why a new segment can open while Postgres is down). Every start, and fsck, allocate above the maximum id seen in Postgres and on disk for that node id (10.2).

**`tierstore store metadata fsck`.** An operator verb, also run by the restore runbook (24.12) and the quarterly drill. *Inputs:* every node's on-disk facts: the `NODE` files, segment files and trailers (and the open or fenced segment files that have no trailer, below), `blk/*.idx`, shard footers (which carry the stripe descriptor), large files, commit markers, the bucket ids in record headers, trailers, markers and node indexes, and the tombstones recorded in each node's `index/tomb-applied` file (not the in-memory index). *Outputs:* it rebuilds or corrects the Postgres rows: blocks, hot copies, stripes and shards from the footers and trailers; live counts from trailers minus tombstones; mutable heads from commit markers (the highest marker version per key wins, and a head is rebuilt only if its record exists; a delete marker with the highest version leaves the key deleted); the id allocators (blocks, stripes, nodes and **buckets**) and each node's counter above the maximum seen; the sequences `ts.txn_seq` (above every node's remembered value) and `ts.head_version` (above the maximum marker version seen). It reports every difference. **Open and fenced segments.** For a segment with no trailer (open or fenced on its copies) fsck scans each copy's records and commit markers up to its last crc-verified record, as the node-start replay does (10.2), and reads markers from every copy, since each copy may end at a different length.
