It inserts a missing `blocks` row (`ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`) in state `open` at the highest fence epoch recorded on any copy, with the replica set from the segment header, as in 24.4, and raises the `epoch` of an existing row to the highest fence epoch held on the copies (a restored row says `open`, epoch 1, while the copies hold epoch 3). It leaves the sealing to the normal seal path of 10.3, which takes the length by the fence rule, and never truncates. A head is rebuilt from a marker only if its record exists on some copy and its block has a row. **Bucket ids:** fsck reports every bucket id found on disk that has no `buckets` row, and the ids found per node; it takes the name and mode from the nodes' `buckets` record in `NODE` and falls back to the operator only when no node has it. The operator binds each reported id with `store bucket create <name> --mode immutable|mutable --id <n>` (refused if the id is already bound; a bare `store bucket create` always allocates above `max(buckets.bucket_id)`), and `--apply` refuses to complete while an id seen on disk is unbound unless the operator names the ids to skip (records under a skipped id are left untouched and nothing is deleted). **Rows with no files:** a block, stripe or shard row whose files exist on no node and not in trash, and that no commit marker or applied tombstone names, is classified *gone*; after the operator has reviewed the dry-run, `--apply` sets the stripe to `freed` and its blocks to `freed` or `dropped` and deletes the relocation and tombstone rows that name them. Gone rows are never rebuilt, never queued as repair and not counted as `cell_lost` or in `lost-report`; the report ends with one alert, `fsck_rows_without_files{count,bytes}`, and no P0 jobs. A row with some files (a shard or copy on any node or in trash) stays a normal repair item, and a block with fewer than `k` shards found is not freed but reported `lost` and goes through the normal reconciler path. **Mutable buckets:** fsck recomputes the `live_count` and `live_bytes` of a block as the number of its records that are the rebuilt head of a key or are named by a delete marker; every other record of a mutable-bucket block is headless, stays in place and is handed to the age-gated sweep, which tombstones and decrements it only after `destage.orphan_age` has passed under the new incarnation. For immutable buckets, live counts stay trailers minus applied tombstones, taken only over blocks that still exist. *It never* deletes or unlinks a file, drops a row it cannot explain (the exception is the *gone* rule above), or edits a segment: the only deletes in its transaction are of rows that name nothing on any disk. When it completes it raises the incarnation and clears `frozen`, and the nodes adopt the new incarnation (a cursor reset: they re-read every tombstone and changed row, see the incarnation paragraph above). **Dry-run is the default**: it prints the report and the plan and changes nothing; `--apply` runs it, and it refuses to run while any node is unreachable unless told which nodes' facts to skip (and then the nodes it skipped stay frozen). **A floor on the nodes read:** `--apply` completes only if facts were read from at least `max(N - hot.min_acks + 1, hot.min_acks)` nodes (2 of 3 at `min_acks = 2`, 3 of 3 at `min_acks = replicas`), so that every acknowledged delete (on `N - min_acks + 1` nodes' `tomb-applied` files) and every acknowledged mutable commit marker (on `min_acks` copies) is on at least one node that was read. Below the floor fsck runs only as a dry-run report: it does not raise the incarnation and does not clear `frozen`. With any node skipped, a row with no files is unclassified, never *gone*, and no row that a skipped node could hold is deleted; it is left for a later run. The audit (24.10) also compares the nodes' applied tombstones with the `tombstones` table, so a derived index that has diverged is found without a restore.

**Access.** Three roles: `ts_rw` (DML on `ts.*` only, no DDL; used by the node and the verbs), `ts_ro` (read-only, for operators' SQL views), and `ts_owner` (used only by `store metadata migrate`). Password files, not environment variables; `pg_hba.conf` admits only the storage nodes' private addresses with `scram-sha-256`. Configuration is passed as flags and the `[metadata]` section of 10.14 (`hosts`, `database`, `user`, `password_file`, `statement_timeout`, `txn_timeout`, `retry_window`, `tomb_poll`).

**Schema changes.** Additive only: a new table, a new nullable column or one with a default, a new index built concurrently; never a rename, a type change, a dropped column or a new required column. They run by the explicit verb `tierstore store metadata migrate`, never on start (24.15 item 10), under `ts_owner`, and raise `cluster.schema_version`. A node that finds a `schema_version` newer than its own continues in a read-only compatible mode (it uses only the columns it knows) and reports it; one that finds an older version refuses to start and says which verb to run. So the previous release runs on the new schema during a roll, and the new release runs on the old schema until the verb is run (T-S22 tests both directions).

**Hostkeeper and planned work.** hostkeeper's update hook for a node that holds the Postgres primary first runs a Patroni switchover (`ok-to-stop` refuses to proceed otherwise), so a planned roll never fails the primary over by timeout; the hook for a standby checks the cluster is healthy and the other standby is synchronous, restarts it, and waits for it to catch up before the roll proceeds to the next node (one node at a time, 24.15). A hostkeeper host is never rolled while an etcd member on it would leave etcd without a quorum.

**Minor upgrades.** Rolling: the standbys first (asynchronous standby, then the synchronous one, each waiting for catch-up), then a Patroni switchover to an upgraded member, then the old primary. No outage beyond the switchover (a few seconds of `503`, inside the clients' retry). (No runbook of 24.12: it is a step of the routine roll, 24.15 item 10; R15 repeats it.)

**The yearly major upgrade (runbook R15, 24.12).** Planned a window ahead; target at most 15 minutes of metadata-dependent unavailability [I; the practice run measures]. Immutable-bucket PUT and GET continue throughout (the outage table above).
1. Build the new major's packages and the pgBackRest stanza for it with hostkeeper; practise the whole runbook first on a copy: restore the latest backup to a scratch cluster, upgrade it with `pg_upgrade --link`, run T-S22's conformance and the fsck dry run against it.
2. Take a full backup and check it restores.
3. Announce the window: from here the metadata-dependent operations answer `503 Retry-After`, as in an outage.
4. Wait until the asynchronous standby's replay LSN equals the primary's flush LSN (no writes are arriving, so this is exact), then stop it and keep it, untouched, on the old major: it is the rollback point until step 6 passes.
5. Stop the Patroni cluster, run `pg_upgrade --link` on the primary, start it on the new major, run `ANALYZE`.
6. Re-create the synchronous standby from the new primary (`pg_basebackup`), run the conformance and the failover drill checks against it, and re-open the window. Until this passes the old-major standby is the rollback: stop the new cluster, then run `tierstore store metadata promote-lone` on the old-major standby (it raises the incarnation and sets `frozen`), then `tierstore store metadata fsck --apply` (R14). Any commit acknowledged after the standby was stopped (none, if the window was open first) is recovered by fsck from the disks. A bare `pg_ctl promote` or `patronictl failover` is never used for the rollback.
7. Re-create the third member from the new primary, and retire the old-major standby only after one more full backup of the new cluster has succeeded.

**Tests.** T-S22 (the client and schema: idempotent retry after an ambiguous commit, CAS conflict, incarnation mismatch, both-direction schema compatibility, the failover reconnect against a fake that drops the primary), T-S23 (the failover drill, which records the failover time against the 30 s target, switchover, restore and both upgrade runbooks on the farm), T-S24 (fsck, incarnation, rollback detection and freeze, including the negative case that with the freeze disabled the simulator loses data), T-S14 and T-S15 (the simulator's Postgres model and the drills, 14 and 24.13).

### 10.17 Versus SeaweedFS 4.54

**Status of this comparison (owner, 2026-11-18).** SeaweedFS is trialled as the store now, and the implementation of this document's own store is paused. This document stays as the complete design of our own store, on paper, so that it exists if the owner resumes it; section 18 says which tasks are paused. This section sets what SeaweedFS 4.54 does, read in its source, against what this design does, point by point, and what each difference costs on our three-node hardware (3 storage nodes, each one NVMe and fifteen hard drives). 10.12 keeps the earlier reasoning and is reconciled with this section.

Evidence: the SeaweedFS 4.54 source, read for this revision (SeaweedFS 4.54 source, `weed/util/version/constants.go:11-13`: `MAJOR_VERSION = int32(4)`, `MINOR_VERSION = int32(54)`, `VERSION_NUMBER = fmt.Sprintf("%d.%02d", ...)`, which gives 4.54). Nothing was run against SeaweedFS for this section. Each statement below is what the code does where it was read; where a claim of an earlier draft was refuted or not found, the text says what the code does instead.

**1. The erasure-coding layout is 10+4 by default, and `ec.encode` does not expose the ratio.**
- *SeaweedFS 4.54.* The compile-time default is 10 data and 4 parity shards, 14 in all (SeaweedFS 4.54 source, `weed/storage/erasure_coding/ec_encoder.go:21-22`: `DataShardsCount = 10`, `ParityShardsCount = 4`; `weed/storage/erasure_coding/ec_context.go:60-67`, `NewDefaultECContext`; `ec_encoder.go:70,75`, the fallback for a nil or unset context). The code also has an `ECContext` with `DataShards` and `ParityShards`, so non-default ratios exist; this is a default, not a fixed layout. The shell command `ec.encode` has no flag for the data or parity count (SeaweedFS 4.54 source, `weed/shell/command_ec_encode.go:101-114`: the flags are volume id, volume ids, collection, full percent, quiet for, parallelism, batch size, force, shard replica placement, source disk type, disk type, rebalance and verbose), and neither has the `VolumeEcShardsGenerate` request (`weed/pb/volume_server.proto:458-461`: volume id and collection only). The volume server encodes with the default unless a `.vif` next to the volume already carries an `EcShardConfig` with `data_shards` and `parity_shards`; then it uses those when both are above zero and their sum is at most `MaxShardCount`, and otherwise logs a warning and falls back to the default (`weed/server/volume_grpc_erasure_coding.go:68-77`; `weed/pb/volume_server.proto:632-642`, "null = use default 10+4"). The `.vif` is written at the end of an encode and records the layout used (`volume_grpc_erasure_coding.go:~151-159`), so a custom ratio on a fresh encode needs that `.vif` pre-seeded by hand; no shell command of the open-source tree sets it. A rebuild takes the ratio from the `.vif` and fails closed on an unreadable `.vif` or impossible counts (`weed/storage/erasure_coding/ec_encoder.go:132-160`, `ResolveRebuildECContext`). How a ratio is selected beyond this was not traced.
- *This design.* `k+m` is configuration per stripe, `ec = 4+4` on our cluster (decision Q1, 10.4, 10.14), and a code that cannot survive its failure domain is refused at startup (10.4).
- *Cost on our hardware.* To run `4+4` on SeaweedFS 4.54 an operator must pre-seed a `.vif` for each volume by hand before its encode, with no supported command; the default 10+4 is what an unmodified `ec.encode` produces. The rebuild read of a lost shard is 10 shards at 10+4 against 4 at `4+4` (10.12 reason 3 [E]).

**2. A 4.54 EC shard is one contiguous block: the uniform block is the volume's `.dat` divided by the data-shard count, rounded up to 1 MiB.**
- *SeaweedFS 4.54.* A new encode (`WriteEcFiles`) stores the `.dat` size and sets the block size to `UniformBlockSize(size, dataShards)`: the `.dat` size divided by the data-shard count, rounded up to a whole multiple of 1 MiB (`ErasureCodingSmallBlockSize`), at least 1 MiB (SeaweedFS 4.54 source, `weed/storage/erasure_coding/ec_encoder.go:113-120`, `perShard := (datFileSize + int64(dataShards) - 1) / int64(dataShards)`, then `blocks * ErasureCodingSmallBlockSize`; the caller at `:87-88`). The divisor is the context's data-shard count: 10 by default, and the `.vif` ratio for a custom one. That one value is passed as both the large and the small block size, so the encode is exactly one row (`encodeDatFile`, `:623-640`): there are no separate small blocks and no tail rows. The tail is zero-padded: `encodeDataOneBatch` reads each data shard's range in 256 KiB batches and zero-fills what is past the end of the file (`:576-580`), so all shards have equal length. Data shard `i` therefore holds `.dat` bytes `[i*block, (i+1)*block)` contiguously. The block size and the `.dat` size are persisted to the `.vif` (`EcShardConfig.BlockSize` and `DatFileSize`). Legacy volumes stay supported: a block size of 0 in the `.vif` means the older two-tier layout (1 GB large blocks, 1 MiB small blocks for the tail), located by `LocateData` in `ec_locate.go`, and a nonzero block size must be a multiple of 1 MiB (`ValidateBlockSize`, `ec_encoder.go:91-106`) or it is rejected as corrupt. The 1 GB and 1 MiB constants (`ec_encoder.go:26-27`) remain for the legacy layout and for rebuild buffer sizing. A needle whose bytes straddle a block boundary spans two shards [I, from the layout above].
- *This design.* The block is a fixed 1 GiB, sealed from a hot segment with its records packed in arrival order and their offsets preserved (10.3, 10.4); one block is one shard file on one drive, `k` of them make a stripe, and the index entry does not change when a block is destaged (10.5).
- *Cost on our hardware.* Both layouts keep one needle on one shard in the common case, so a healthy read touches one drive; 10.12's earlier statement that SeaweedFS's EC "already uses 1 GB contiguous blocks" is corrected here: for a volume encoded by 4.54 the shard size follows the volume (for a volume of `S` bytes at 10+4, about `S/10` rounded up to 1 MiB), not 1 GB. The difference that matters is not contiguity but granularity: a SeaweedFS volume is encoded whole, at its own size, by an operator command (point 4), where this design encodes each sealed 1 GiB block automatically.

**3. Placement of 14 shards on three servers: a server loss is unrecoverable, and `ec.encode` needs `-force` below four nodes.**
- *SeaweedFS 4.54.* No code hard-codes 5/5/4. The balancer spreads shards evenly, `ceil(14/3) = 5` per node, so 5, 5 and 4 is the ideal and typical outcome on three servers; the within-rack node spread caps data shards (10 gives `ceil(10/3) = 4` per node) and parity shards (4 gives `ceil(4/3) = 2` per node) independently, so a node can hold up to 6 in a worst case (SeaweedFS 4.54 source, `weed/storage/erasure_coding/ecbalancer/balancer.go:685-720, 804-826`). The arithmetic holds in either case: a node holding 5 or more of 14 shards leaves at most 9, below the 10 needed, so losing it loses more than the 4 parity shards and the volume cannot be recovered. `ec.encode` counts all data nodes in the topology; below 4 (`ParityShardsCount`) and without `-force` it logs "skip erasure coding with %d nodes, less than recommended %d nodes" and returns without an error, so it does nothing and reports success; with `-force` it proceeds (`weed/shell/command_ec_encode.go:109,143-152`). The threshold is exactly `nodeCount < 4`. The shell help text says only that with fewer than 4 servers you can survive 4 corrupted shard files, not the loss of a server (`command_ec_encode.go:41-48`). The encode fails on a placement violation only when `-shardReplicaPlacement` limits are set (`weed/ec/ec_encode.go:840-943`, `verifyEcShardsBeforeDelete`); with none, nothing in the encode path fails because one server holds more than 4 shards.
- *This design.* `4+4` places 3 + 3 + 2 shards on the three nodes, failure domain node, and the placement rule `m >= max_shards_per_domain` (4 >= 3) refuses a configuration that cannot survive its own domain (10.4); the loss of a node plus a drive elsewhere is survived (decision Q1).
- *Cost on our hardware.* With three servers, 10+4 needs `-force` to start, then places 5/5/4: any one server lost takes 5 or more of 14 shards and the volume is gone, where the owner's target is to survive a node plus a drive. Run on SeaweedFS 4.54 as is, the cold tier has no tolerance of its own failure domain; the way to a layout that survives it is a hand-seeded ratio (point 1).

**4. Tiering is whole-volume, operator-triggered, and has no local cache and no promotion.**
- *SeaweedFS 4.54.* Remote tiering moves a volume's whole `.dat` to a remote backend: `volume.tier.upload` calls the volume-server gRPC `VolumeTierMoveDatToRemote` (SeaweedFS 4.54 source, `weed/shell/command_volume_tier_upload.go:228`; help text "upload the dat file of a volume to a remote tier", line 52). It runs when an operator or a scheduled job runs the command; it can pick volumes by `-fullPercent` and `-quietFor`, but nothing in the volume server's read path starts it. `volume.tier.move` is not a remote-tier command: it moves volumes between local disk types (for example hdd to ssd) or data centers (`weed/shell/command_volume_tier_move.go:47-52`). With `-keepLocalDatFile` the upload leaves the local `.dat` (`command_volume_tier_upload.go:96`). A volume loaded from a remote file (`HasRemoteFile()`) refuses writes and appends but accepts deletes: on load it sets `noWriteCanDelete = true` and `noWriteOrDelete = false` (`weed/storage/volume_loading.go:172-174`), so `IsReadOnly()` is true; the delete path skips appending a tombstone to the `.dat` for a remote volume (`weed/storage/volume_write.go:451,505`) and only updates the needle map. A cold read is one `GetObject` per `ReadAt` with the exact range `bytes=off-(off+len(p)-1)` (`weed/storage/backend/s3_backend/s3_backend.go:224-231`), and there is no data cache in `weed/storage/backend`: the only cache is the S3 session and client cache (`s3_sessions.go:68-72`). There is no access-driven promotion: no read-path code downloads a remote volume again; the way back is the manual `volume.tier.download` (`VolumeTierMoveDatFromRemote`, `command_volume_tier_download.go:343`).
- *This design.* Destage is automatic on seal, decoupled from reclaim (10.5); a cold object read `promote.reads` times in `promote.window` (2 in 1 hour) is copied into an NVMe cache segment that destage never reads, so a cold object that becomes hot again is served from NVMe (10.5); tiering is per 1 GiB block, not per volume (10.5).
- *Cost on our hardware.* Tiering and EC placement are decisions the operator takes volume by volume, with volumes encoded or moved whole. After the move, every read of a cold range pays a drive seek or a remote GET and no read of it makes the next one cheaper, so a pack that 31 build nodes read again is read from the cold medium each time [I]. The two gaps that decide 10.12 (fresh outputs read by other build nodes, promote-on-read) are not closed by 4.54's own tiering; the reference run of 17.2 item 6 is the experiment that measures them on a trial.

**5. S3 writes are chunked at the filer's `-maxMB`, 4 MiB by default.**
- *SeaweedFS 4.54.* The S3 gateway (`weed s3`) does not send data through the filer's HTTP auto-chunk path; `putToFiler` cuts chunks itself with `operation.UploadReaderInChunks` and `ChunkSize = s3a.uploadChunkSize()`, and uploads them directly to volume servers, assigning volumes through the filer's `AssignVolume` gRPC (SeaweedFS 4.54 source, `weed/s3api/s3api_object_handlers_put.go:705,768`). `uploadChunkSize()` follows the filer's `-maxMB`: `weed s3` reads `resp.MaxMb` from the filer's `GetFilerConfiguration` once at startup (`weed/command/s3.go:314,329`), and the filer flag defaults to 4 (`weed/command/filer.go:136`), so the chunk is 4 MiB by default. Caveats: a `MaxMB` of 0 or less falls back to 8 MiB (`defaultUploadChunkSizeMB = 8`, `s3api_object_handlers_put.go:523,533-534`); values are clamped to 2047 MiB (`:525,536`); small inline saving is disabled on the S3 path (`SaveSmallInline` is false), so even a small object is a chunk (`:764-768`); and there is no per-request `maxMB` override on the S3 path (that query parameter exists only on the filer's HTTP handler, `weed/server/filer_server_handlers_write_autochunk.go:177-190`, which also clamps at 2047 MiB).
- *This design.* A pack is one streaming PUT and one record in one block; an object is one record, not a list of chunks (10.3, 10.9).
- *Cost on our hardware.* At the 4 MiB default a 1 GiB pack is 256 chunks [E], each a separate needle assigned to a volume by its own request, with the object's chunk list in a filer entry; a whole-pack read then follows the chunk list instead of one sequential extent, and 4 MiB is the unit of a needle's CRC and of an EC read (points 6 and 7). The chunk size is a filer setting read once at S3 startup, so changing it takes a gateway restart [I].

**6. A ranged read verifies the CRC of the whole needle first, once per needle.**
- *SeaweedFS 4.54.* On the paged read path (`Volume.readNeedleDataInto`, SeaweedFS 4.54 source, `weed/storage/volume_read.go:197`) a partial range first calls `verifiedNeedleBlocks`, which streams the entire needle data from the data file, compares the whole-needle CRC, and records per-block CRCs (blocks of 64 KiB to 1 MiB, at most about 64 per needle; `rangeCheckBlockSize`, `:44`, `:462`, `:511-518`) before writing any byte of the range (`:202-204`, `:225-233`). This happens only the first time: the per-block CRCs are kept in a process-wide LRU of 64 K entries keyed by volume load generation, needle id, append time and checksum (`:33`, `:227`), concurrent first reads share one pass, and later ranges of the same needle read only the block-aligned span of the range and check each block against the cached CRC (`rangeBlockChecker`, `:292-333`). The entries are dropped on a volume reload or compaction (`:243`). A full-needle read goes straight to the streaming path with the full CRC check (`:199-200`), and the non-streaming path for small needles reads the whole needle with `ReadData`, which also verifies the CRC (`:95,117`).
- *This design.* A cold read reads exactly the range from one drive and verifies the cells it covers against the per-cell crc32c table of the block trailer and shard footer, and the client verifies each 1 MiB cell against the L2 list's digest (2, 10.2, 10.4); no read verifies bytes it did not return.
- *Cost on our hardware.* The first range request for each large pack on a 4.54 volume reads the whole pack from the drive, which for a build node asking for one 16 to 64 MiB part of a 1 GiB `.so` is a read of the whole file's data on the server first [E]; with 31 build nodes and many packs that first pass is spent once per needle and then amortised by the cache, but a cache of 64 K entries is small against the number of packs and is lost on a volume reload [I].

**7. An EC read has no range support: the whole needle is read, and a range is cut from memory.**
- *SeaweedFS 4.54.* On the volume server an EC read locates and fetches every interval of the whole needle (header, data and trailer, `GetActualSize`) across shards, reconstructing from other shards per interval when a shard is unavailable (`readOneEcShardInterval`, SeaweedFS 4.54 source, `weed/storage/store_ec.go:670`, falling back to `recoverOneRemoteEcShardInterval`, `:1465`; `LocateData` for the full needle in `weed/storage/erasure_coding/ec_volume.go:1040`). `ReadEcShardNeedle` takes no offset, size or read option (`store_ec.go:550`); `readEcShardIntervals` sums every interval and allocates the whole buffer (`:615-632`). The ranged streaming path (`ReadVolumeNeedleDataInto`) is only for local normal volumes: `shouldAttemptStreamWrite` returns false for an EC volume (`weed/server/volume_server_handlers_read.go:254, 377-379`, EC branch at `:260-263`). An HTTP `Range` header on an EC needle is still honoured, but only after the whole needle is in memory: `writeResponseContent` slices a `bytes.NewReader(n.Data)` through `ProcessRangeRequest` (`:363-366`, `:510`), so the 206 response works and the read saved nothing. Interval reads are bounded-parallel (`ecIntervalReadConcurrency = 8`, `store_ec.go:~600`) but still cover the full needle.
- *This design.* A healthy cold read reads the byte range from one drive; a degraded read decodes only that range from `k` shards, charged `k` stream slots (10.4, 4.3).
- *Cost on our hardware.* A 4.54 EC volume server reads, and on a failed shard reconstructs, the whole needle for every ranged request: for the fetch parts of 16 to 64 MiB that the build nodes send (4.0), the drive does the work of the whole pack, up to about 1 GiB, per part [E]. When a shard is lost within parity, the reconstruction work is per whole needle, where this design's is per range.

**What we would take from the trial, and what stays ours.** The trial settles by measurement what the source reading cannot: first-byte and throughput of 4.54 on our drives, its S3 front's behaviour under the CAS service's PUT mix, and whether a hand-seeded `4+4` volume layout is workable. This design's data model, metadata store, operations and tasks are not changed by it, and none of the client-side design depends on which store runs behind `LOCATE` and the S3 front: the client crates, the cache and the fetch engine speak the peer protocol and the S3 API of 10.9 to either.

## 11. The cache device: a real partition, not a loop on an image
**Decision [D]: a dedicated xfs partition of the node SSD (noatime), no loop device. Required, not gated:** T0 measured that the loop device turns `O_DIRECT` cache writes into page-cache writes (945+ MB/s that have not reached the SSD) and stalls four buffered writers for more than 20 s [M]. The other reasons stand:
1. Each `fdatasync` through xfs-on-loop-on-image costs two journals and two flushes.
2. Two page caches and two dirty accountings double memory pressure (`losetup --direct-io=on` was the considered stopgap; with `O_DIRECT` already defeated by the loop it is not relied on).
3. Cell-group eviction frees space only if discards propagate through the loop to the image; on a partition `PUNCH_HOLE` frees directly and `fstrim` works.
4. A fixed 185 GiB image takes the space whether or not the cache is full.
5. One fewer layer between FUSE reads and the SSD.

The device is the build node's local SSD that holds the cache (on the farm's six existing nodes the root SATA SSD; their SMR drives are never a cache): a new partition of budget + 15% (185 GiB -> 220 GiB). The 31 build nodes of the target farm have their own cache SSDs, whose size and type T0 records. **If the root filesystem is xfs it cannot be shrunk; T0 records its type.** The plan is free unallocated SSD space if it exists, else a node image re-provision through hostkeeper in a maintenance window; never an in-place live repartition by tierstore.

**T0 B** measures the partition (it is no longer a gate): `fdatasync` p99, sustained write at 1 MiB and mixed-load read p99, against the loop numbers above. **T10b depends on T13 so constants are frozen on the shipped device.** The partition is mounted at a new path read from `cache.root`; the old image is not converted and is removed after the soak.

**Metrics added by revision 8, not yet in the table above.** The table is the registry of `tierstore-metrics` (its test holds the two equal both ways), so each name below joins the table in the pull request that registers it, by the task that implements it: `tierstore_components{component}` (gauge: which components a node runs, 10.1; the daemon, T-L); `membership_epoch` (gauge), `membership_fetch_total{outcome}` (counter: ok, not_newer, invalid, not_found, unauthorized, unreachable) and `membership_bootstrap_seconds` (histogram) of the client's membership (10.8; T-SB); `hedges_suppressed_total{reason}` (counter: budget, decrease, ceiling, no_alternative), `locate_local_total` and `peer_load_pings_total` (counters) of the hedge budget and the no-ping rules (4.6, 10.6; T3, T-S6); `first_touch_total{ready}` (counter: each first read of a cell in a job session, `ready = yes` if it was `present`) and `first_read_seconds` (histogram: from the provider's `Prepare` to the job's first served READ), which decide whether Prepare's order matters (6.1, 17.3; T-F8 and the Prepare provider); and the operations metrics of 24.10 (T-S22, T-S24, T-S25).

The metadata store and the reconciler add their own metrics: the client's transaction count, latency, retry and unavailability counters, the incarnation, the `txn_seq`, the freeze state and the count of fenced-unsealed blocks (`hot_fenced_unsealed_blocks`) of 10.16, and the reconciler's lease, queue, throttle and refusal metrics of 24.5, and the gauge `frees_blocked_by_node{node}` (the node holding the minimum `tomb_applied` while a free is held back) with the alert `FreesBlocked` of 10.5 (T-S25). They join this table in the pull requests that register them, by T-S22 (the client and schema), T-S23 (the deployment: Patroni, etcd and backup state) and T-S25 (the reconciler). Two names already in the table change with named owners: T-S4 (fence and one compare-and-set seal) changes `hot_seal_rounds_total{outcome}` to count the rounds of the seal of 10.3 (its outcomes are committed, lost the compare-and-set and too few fences, not prepare rounds or ballots), rewriting the `SealRound` variants in `crates/tierstore-metrics/src/labels.rs` and the help text in `groups/store.rs`; and T-S3 removes `cluster_stamp_wall_skew_seconds`, which has no source once the cluster stamp is gone, from `groups/store.rs` along with its row in the table. Both edits keep `crates/tierstore-metrics/tests/section12.rs` green in the same pull request. The index audit's `metadata_audit_differences{kind}`, `fsck_rows_without_files` and `metadata_frozen` are registered by T-S24, `hot_fenced_unsealed_blocks` by T-S4, and `frees_blocked_by_node` and the reconciler's stall gauge (`repair_stalled_on_metadata`) by T-S25, which also owns the alerts on `metadata_frozen` and `repair_stalled_on_metadata`.

`request_to_start_seconds` (tierstore's `tierstore_request_to_start_seconds`, T14) is one sample per run, on the build node's clock: from the first `Prepare` of the run that the mount provider takes (the scheduler's agent sends it the moment it has the job) to the first lookup or listing the mount serves inside one of the run's trees (the agent and the overlay's mount touch only the roots, so that is the container runtime making the job's root or the command itself), which the mount reports on the fill socket off the request's path. `{drive}` is the drive id (45 hard drives and 3 NVMe devices), never a key. Trace each demand read end to end: handler, wait sent, budget acquired, `LOCATE`, device chosen, GET start, device queue wait, first byte, cell received, hash done, write, reply.

## 13. Deleted code and migration of the cache
**Deleted**
- FUSE passthrough (`treefs/backing.rs`, `core/passthrough.rs`, `Handle::Backed` and `Spilled`, `max_backing_ids`, backing table, negotiation in `fuse.rs::init`).
- The per-blob path: `Plan::Blob`, `Origin::Span`, `fill_blob`, the `fill <sha> <size> from <key> @ <off>` request, `blob_path` in `cache_file`, per-file span copies.
- `pin_once`, `pinning`, `lifecycle`, `loaded.pinned`, touch/pin/unpin/pins_live traffic.
- All three prefetch mechanisms: `local_cache/prefetch.rs`, `fs-prefetch` threads, Prepare groups and `GROUP_MAX_BYTES`.
- Extents machinery: `local_cache/extents/*`, per-range flights, `extent_save`, `extents.writing`, `move_whole`'s re-hash, `fetch_parts` gap reading.
- The v1 list reader (only refusal remains) and, after the dual-emission release, the CAS service's v1 list writer.
- The komira-ai/fuser fork dependency and the blocking mount client (T-R, T11a).
- The erasure-set budget model and the `upstream.data_shards`/`upstream.drives` topology flags (T11a); with silo, the legacy upstream and the `upload_part_size` handling (T11b; no silo client was ever written as an upstream).
- Config: `max_concurrent_fills`, `fill_socket_concurrency`, `prefetch_concurrency`, `trees.fill_concurrency`, `fill_chunk`, `trees.fill_queue`, `trees.fill_rate`; `trees.coalesce_gap` and `trees.whole_pack_percent` replaced by section 6.

**Kept:** the reserve and critical-mark check (at claim time), staging files and create-only link for non-pack objects, lock-generation `owns()` checks, the S3-front index, tree image serving (komira-ai/tierstore#341, komira-ai/tierstore#342), tree store placements and the provider Prepare entry point, the NFS front on macOS.

**Cache layout migration.** It is a cache; there is no conversion. The new binary writes `packs-v2/`, does not read `extents-v1/` or `origin=span` entries, and deletes them in a low-priority `tierstore gc legacy`. **Rollback:** the old binary ignores `packs-v2/`; during the overlap `packs-v2` is capped at 50% of the budget and the rollback procedure includes `tierstore gc packs-v2 --purge` (a held script) before the old binary starts. List-format rollback is in 3.5; store rollback is in 10.10.

## 14. Test plan
Behaviour tests only: an injected clock, a fake upstream with scripted per-part and per-cell completion order and corruption, a fake drive and a fake storage node with scripted service times, hangs, errors and crashes, a model of the metadata store (a linearizable Postgres with scripted outage, ambiguous commit, failover and point-in-time rollback, 10.16), scripted interleavings and message loss between nodes, and progress barriers. No test asserts timing: every "after X seconds" below is a step of the injected clock.

| Test | Proves | Defect it catches |
|---|---|---|
| N readers on spans of one pack: one `PackFill`; each cell claimed once | Single flight | Duplicate fills |
| Demand sub-range plus overlapping grid part: skips claimed cells; GETs <= parts + sub-ranges; bytes_fetched == bytes_needed | Claim state | Cell fetched twice |
| Reverse-order part completion: a read on the last cell is served after only that cell lands | Independent per-cell verification | Frontier stalls |
| A 1 GiB file span: a read at the end waits only for its cells | Per-cell digests | Whole-file wait |
| Hedge and original both deliver a cell: one publishes, the loser's bytes never written | Write-once cells | TOCTOU rewrite |
| Two lists with the same pack bytes give one PackId; a changed pack a new path | `pack_root` identity | Path collision |
| Ramp and grid: a first touch mid-pack ramps to the next grid boundary and every later part is a whole grid part; at offset 0 the 5th piece (4 MiB, after 4+8+16+32) is trimmed at the 64 MiB boundary; no runt after the ramp | 4.4 | Runt parts, boundary crossing |
| Budget: floor, share, ceiling and bytes in flight equal the table of 4.3 for (D, N, s*, P) and are recomputed when P changes; share is at least one part at every P | 4.3 | Contradictory budget |
| v1 list or missing L2 sidecar: refused, `ListVersionUnsupported`, no bytes served, no code path parses v1 | No legacy reader | Serving unparsed data |
| Backfill: `l2-backfill` writes an L2 sidecar under the unchanged tree id, reads each pack once, and a second run writes nothing | 3.5 | Identity change; repeated reads |
| Dual emission: the CAS service writes v1 and L2 under one unchanged tree id; old reader ignores the sidecar; new reader ignores v1; id equals the pre-change id for the same tree | 3.5 | Action-cache invalidation |
| The CAS service on `ListVersionUnsupported` uses its miss path and the job continues | 3.5 | Partial outage |
| L2 round trip: each cell digest equals independent sha256; deterministic; sorted directories | Format | Wrong digests |
| Crash between `pwrite` and journal append: cell absent after restart; no journal record visible without its data | Commit ordering | Trusting zeros |
| Crash after a batched PUNCH fsync and before/after the punch: replay never marks a punched cell `present`; one fsync per batch | Section 8 | Zeros after punch + crash |
| Process crash, same boot: restored cells `present`, no recheck; different boot or no boot_id: every restored cell `recheck`, not served by the mount, corrupted cell refetched | Section 8 | Needless rehash; silent bad serve |
| Journal batching: at most one `fdatasync` per dirty entry per interval; `durable` set only after it | Batching | Per-part commit; DONTNEED on dirty pages |
| Loom: reader/evictor protocol with per-cell CAS, neighbouring cells going `absent -> claimed -> present` during the scan, a reader straddling groups, post-read recheck: no reader returns bytes of a cell whose punch has begun; the evictor never changes a `claimed` cell | 5.1 | Zeros served; lost claim |
| Mount's state mapping is read-only: a `PROT_WRITE` mmap of the passed state memfd and a `write(2)` fail | 5.1 | Cross-mount corruption |
| Mount killed (lifeline hangup): counters dropped, eviction proceeds; mount whose protocol connection flaps keeps counters, regions and holds and is never zeroed while its lifeline is open | 5.1 | Wedged eviction; punch under a live reader |
| A mount grows its counter memfd past the server's mapping: the server remaps before acknowledging `open-pack`, and the evictor reads the new region's counters | 5.1 | Evictor reading past its mapping; a missed reader |
| Reader with a forced loss of its counter: post-read recheck discards the bytes | 5.1 | Zeros after forced clear |
| Live reader stuck beyond `evict_wait`: won cells restored `present`, nothing journaled, `evict_timeouts_total` increments | Evictor timeout | Evictor deadlock |
| More than 128 holders on one pack are all served | 5.1 | EBUSY on shared packs |
| Eviction removes an unheld entry's path while a mount holds the data fd: reads return that entry's bytes | fd, not path | Unlink race |
