**T-I2: the freeze delta.** Every change to an item T-I froze (komira-ai/tierstore#352), and whether it is additive. The store's formats and routes were merged as drafts that no release has shipped (the merged code and tests still implement the ballot and stamp design and are retired below): nothing below has shipped on a wire or a disk, so there is no previous release to run against new data, and from the first release on every format changes additively only (B8; T-S21 runs the mixed-version tests).
- **Metadata store (revision 9).**
  - `tierstore-format`: the record's 8-byte `stamp` field becomes `written_ms` (wall-clock milliseconds at append; informational: `Last-Modified` of immutable-bucket objects and audit; never used to order anything), a new record kind, the **commit marker** (bucket, key, version, record id), exists for mutable buckets, a tombstone names a record id `(block_id, offset)`, and the `NODE` file gains the node incarnation, the metadata incarnation and the highest `txn_seq` the node has seen. Merged in T-I (komira-ai/tierstore#352) but unreleased, and removed by T-I2 in the same PR together with their tests: the cluster stamp (`hlc.rs`, `stamp.rs`), the ballot and acceptor records, the seal and descriptor state files (`seal.rs`; `seals/`, `stripes/*.desc`), and the descriptor state-file code of `descriptor.rs`, which is **edited, not deleted, like `free.rs`**: it keeps the stripe-descriptor types the shard footer carries (`StripeDescriptor`, `Code`, `PlacedShard`, `ShardLoc`) and loses only the majority-committed descriptor records, the tombstone log with its per-origin cursors (`tomb.rs`, `tomb/`), the ledger fact record, and the majority commit of the stripe free. In `free.rs` the stripe-free record keeps its fields, minus the stamp and the owner, and the `.free` file stops being a majority-committed file: a free is the transaction of 10.5 on the stripe row. Both the stamp removal and the new `written_ms` field are in T-I2, so `free.rs` is edited, not split. The shard footer (which carries the stripe descriptor, and the blake3 of its shard already, 10.4), the stripe-free layout (minus the stamp and the owner) and the pack-block layout are unchanged. The other changes to what T-I froze are listed here, and each is **not additive but unreleased**, so the additive rule (B8) applies from the first release: (1) the 4-byte **bucket id** in the 64-byte record header, taking a field's place (the 8-byte stamp becomes `written_ms`, above); (2) the trailer's entries gain the bucket and are sorted by (bucket, key) (bucket, key, generation, offset, length, crc32c, `written_ms`); (3) the bucket id in the entries of `blk/*.idx` and `seg/*.idx`, which follow the trailer's entries; (4) the **block id** in the large-file name, `<khash>.<gen>.<size>.<block_id>.obj`; (5) the Postgres system id and the `buckets` record in the `NODE` file, beside the incarnations and `txn_seq` above.
  - `tierstore-store-api::proto`: new routes `FENCE = "/store/v1/fence"` (POST: segment, new epoch; the answer carries the copy's committed and crc-verified lengths) `TOMB_APPLY = "/store/v1/tomb-apply"` (POST: tombstones, each naming a record id, applied durably before the answer) and `INDEX_REPORT = "/store/v1/index-report"` (GET with `?bucket=&hash_prefix=<bits>&digest=1`: the node's live keys of immutable buckets as `(bucket id, key hash, record id, generation)`, as per-range digests or the entries of one range, for the reconciler's comparison of 24.5; an older node answers `404` and the reconciler skips it; T-S6 implements the route and does not introduce it on its own); an optional `bucket` field is added to `LocateRequest` and to the key-check (`POST /store/v1/key`) and `tomb-apply` bodies, which 10.6 and 10.5 name, and when the field is absent the key is treated as an immutable-bucket key, so old and new messages stay compatible; the routes `SEAL`, `DESCRIPTOR`, `TOMB` and `INDEX_SYNC` are **removed**, and their `SEAL`, `DESCRIPTOR`, `TOMB` and `INDEX_SYNC` constants, handlers on the fake node and tests are deleted by T-I2; the replica batch carries `(segment id, epoch, start offset, bytes, running crc32c)` and the headers `x-ts-stamp` (`header::STAMP`) and `x-ts-ballot` (`header::BALLOT`) are removed (`x-ts-ledger-epoch` was only designed in revision 8 and never merged); `x-ts-segment`, `x-ts-offset` and `x-ts-running-crc` stay and an epoch header (`header::EPOCH`, `x-ts-epoch`) is added. `x-ts-mem-epoch` stays. `HotStore::seal(ballot)` becomes `seal(fence)`, `Index::put_if_absent(stamp)` and `delete(stamp)` lose the stamp and `delete` names a record id. The additive tests of T-I2 for these items: a route that an older node does not serve answers `404` (`INDEX_REPORT`, `FENCE`, `TOMB_APPLY`), and a message without the new optional `bucket` field, or with fields a reader does not know, is accepted and read as an immutable-bucket key.
  - `Meta` (new, in `tierstore-store-api`): the metadata store's operations: a transaction closure keyed by operation id, compare-and-set on `(id, row_version)`, the incarnation and `frozen` check, `txn_seq` in every result, and typed reads of the rows of 10.16; with an in-tree model (T-S22) the other tasks run against. `View` is built from the `nodes`, `drives` and `health` rows (the membership document is rendered from them) and gains the per-component states of 24.2; it no longer has the ledger voters.
  - These are **not additive at the Rust source level** (the crates are not published and their only users are this repository's tasks: T-S1, T-S2, T-S3, T-S4, T-S6, T-S7, T10a) and additive on every wire and disk that has shipped, which is none. A node of an older release answers the new routes `404`, which no client sends.
  - **Merged code and tests this revision retires.** Each is rewritten or deleted by the task named, in the same pull request that removes the thing it tests; none is dropped to make a gate green (T7). (a) The `tierstore-format` tests of `hlc.rs`, `stamp.rs`, `tomb.rs` (per-origin log and cursors) and `seal.rs`, the ballot, descriptor and majority-commit cases of `seal_free.rs`, and the matching fuzz cases are deleted by T-I2 (not by T-S2), in the same PR as the files. The stripe-free and descriptor-footer cases stay and are edited by T-I2 for the dropped stamp and owner, and `tests/descriptor.rs` keeps its footer and stripe-code cases and loses the state-file cases. T-S2 deletes nothing; it only adds the `written_ms`, commit-marker, record-id tombstone and `NODE` incarnation round-trip cases (the T-S2 row's tests). (b) The `tierstore-harness` seal model (T10a, merged as komira-ai/tierstore#365): rewritten to the fence plus compare-and-set seal by a T10a follow-up that lands with T-S4 (the two-sealers and fence rows of section 14 replace it). (c) The fake storage node's seal, descriptor, tomb and index-sync handlers and their `proto`, ballot, stamp and index-sync tests are deleted by T-I2 (above). The T10a follow-up that lands with T-S6 only rewrites the harness fake node and models to serve `fence` and `tomb-apply`, and removes the stamp from `Index::put_if_absent` and `delete`. (d) The merged `tierstore-index` crate (T-S3, komira-ai/tierstore#367) and its tests implement the stamp-ordered liveness rule, the per-origin tombstone log and drop and `index-sync`; the T-S3 rework rewrites them to the record-id tombstone, `tomb-apply` and the Postgres-derived rebuild, in the same pull request that removes each thing it tests.
- Operations (section 24): the `shards` rows gain a blake3 per shard (the shard footer already carries it, 10.4; the column is part of the schema of T-S22), and `View` gains the per-component states of 24.2.
- `tierstore-store-api::view`: `Role`, `Role::stores` and `NodeView::role` are **removed** (decision 1 of revision 8: the document lists only nodes that run the hot store, so there is nothing to tell apart); `View::live_nodes` filters on the node's state and hot admin state only; `NodeView` gains `peer` (a `host:port` string) and an optional `s3` of the same form; the docs of `View::nodes` ("storage and combined; build nodes are not listed") and of `ClusterSettings::cold_code` (`cold.mode = "none"`) are reworded to "nodes that run the hot store" and "no cold store". `Placement::rank(key, view)` and every other reader of `View` (T-S4, T-S7) recompile unchanged. **Not additive at the Rust source level**; the crate is not published and its only users are this repository's tasks (T-S1, T-S6, T-S12, T10a), so no released binary or other repository sees it. The membership file seeds `store init` only and its reader ignores unknown keys, so a document that still has a `role` key parses.
- `tierstore-store-api::proto`: a new route `MEMBERSHIP = "/store/v1/membership"` (GET; body the membership document as `application/toml`, rendered from the Postgres rows at the current epoch; `401` on a wrong token). The header `x-ts-mem-epoch` was frozen as "piggybacked on peer responses" and carried by `Envelope` on the typed answers, but a GET's `PeerBody` had no field for it; T-I2 states it is on every response of every route, the read routes included, and adds `PeerBody::mem_epoch`. **Additive on the wire**: no route, header or message changes meaning; a node of an older release answers the new route `404`, which a client treats as "try the next seed" (10.8). **At the Rust source level** `PeerBody` (all fields public, built by struct literal in the fake and in `tests/types.rs`, with a hand-written `Debug` that prints the new field) and `PeerClient` change: `PeerClient` gains `membership(node)` for following the epoch from a known node; bootstrap from a seed, which has an address and no node id yet, is a free function of `tierstore-client`, `fetch_membership(addr, token)`, returning the document's bytes and epoch. A wrong token is `PeerError::Protocol { status: 401 }` and a missing route `PeerError::NotFound`, so no new error variant is needed. Doc-only changes: `PeerClient`'s comment drops "a `combined` node", and the module doc of `proto` ("Build nodes use the read routes (`locate`, hot, cold and degraded GET, `ping`); storage nodes also use the internal routes") becomes "clients use `locate`, the hot, cold and degraded GETs and the membership route; nodes that run the hot store also use the internal routes and `ping`, which clients never send".
- `tierstore-store-api::proto`, load in `LOCATE` (10.6): `HotCopy` gains `load` (the copy's device queue wait in milliseconds as the answering node knows it, `Option<u32>`) and `LocateAnswer::Hot`'s copies are ordered by load with the first recommended (a meaning added to an existing order; promoted copies are no longer forced first); `ColdExtent` gains `drive_load` (`Option<u32>`) and its `sources` are chosen by the load table. A new header `x-ts-load` (`header::LOAD`) carries a node's load on internal routes, with a `NodeLoad` message type. Doc-only: `HotCopy::promoted` ("listed first") and `LocateAnswer::Hot::copies` ("a promoted copy first") become "ordered by load"; `tierstore-fetch-api`'s `ids.rs` rank doc ("profile order, else tree order") becomes "shared packs first, then tree order"; `PeerClient`'s doc stops quoting the section 14 row "View states on scripted pings". **Additive on the wire** (new optional JSON fields with defaults, a new header that older nodes ignore; an older node's answer has no load and its copies keep the old order, which a client reads as unranked). **At the Rust source level** `HotCopy` and `ColdExtent` are all-public structs built by struct literal, so the fake and the tests change with them.
- `tierstore-fetch-api::list`: `PACK_SHARED = 1 << 1`, a new meaning of a bit that was reserved zero, and the doc of `PackRecord::pack_flags` names both bits; additive (readers ignore unknown bits and do not mask them, 3.1). The two copies of `CELL_SHIFT = 20` (the state page's in `tierstore-fetch-api` and the block trailer's in `tierstore-format`) are unchanged.
- Unchanged: the shard-footer, stripe-free (minus the stamp and the owner) and pack-block layouts of `tierstore-format` (the segment, trailer, `blk/*.idx` and large-file-name changes are the ones listed under T-I2 above), the state page layout, the mount protocol, `tsfuse`, the provider protocol (`provider.proto`), and every trait of `tierstore-fetch-api`.
Tests of T-I2: `tests/view.rs` rewritten (the role-name JSON cases and `only_storage_and_combined_roles_store_data` removed; live nodes are those up and not `out`; a membership document with an unknown `role` key parses), `proto` (the membership route on the fake node: `200` with the document and the epoch header, `401` on a wrong token; `mem_epoch` on a GET body; the fence route records the epoch durably and a batch under a lower epoch is refused; `tomb-apply` applies by record id and is idempotent; the removed routes are absent from the route table), `locate` (an answer with load fields decodes in a reader that ignores them, and an answer without them decodes with their defaults), and `meta` (the model: an ambiguous commit applies once, a stale `row_version` is refused, a wrong incarnation is refused).

**Revisions 8 and 9 and the tasks that were under way.** The decisions of revision 8 and of revision 9 change these tasks of this table; the others are unaffected (their interfaces, crates aside, are unchanged). Three store tasks were under way when the store was paused; T-S3 has since merged in its revision 8 form (komira-ai/tierstore#367), and T-S1 and T-S5 are paused with the rest. Each entry says what revision 9 would change when its task is resumed:
- **T-S1** (spec and configuration, paused; what revision 9 would change when resumed): revision 8's changes stand (no `role` key and no `Role`; components as sections with the dependency check and validation item 8 of 10.14; `[client]`, `[cache]`, `[fetch]`, `[fuse]`, `[nfs]`, `[prepare]` sections; the membership document without roles and with addresses; the examples are files in `docs/examples/`). Revision 9 adds a `[metadata]` section (`hosts`, a list of `host:port`; `database`; `user`; `password_file`; `statement_timeout`; `txn_timeout`; `retry_window`; `tomb_poll`) with its validation (at least one host, the timeouts ordered, the password file readable and not world-readable), drops every voter-set key (ledger voters, recorded voters), and treats `membership.toml` as the seed of `store init` only: after `store init` the document is rendered from Postgres. As ever, configuration is flags and files, not environment variables.
- **T-S3** (index, merged in revision 8 form as komira-ai/tierstore#367; the revision 9 rework is paused with the store): the merged crate `tierstore-index` implements revision 8's design: the liveness rule by cluster stamp, replay from the replay mark, the per-origin tombstone log with its drop, recovery from trailers and `blk/*.idx`, `index-sync` and the LIST merge, on `fjall` or `redb`. If the store is resumed, revision 9 reworks it, as a follow-up to the merged crate: the design changes. The index is derived state only and is rebuilt from trailers, `blk/*.idx`, shard footers and the tombstones in Postgres; the block table is a cache of Postgres rows, not an authority; the hybrid logical clock, `index-sync`, the per-origin tombstone logs, the pull cursors per origin and the tomb-drop reports are dropped; a tombstone names a record id and is applied by record id, pushed by `tomb-apply` and pulled by one gap-free cursor. The merged `hlc.rs`, `stamp.rs`, `tomb.rs` and `seal.rs` of `tierstore-format`, the descriptor state-file code and the four routes are retired by T-I2 (`free.rs` is edited in place by T-I2, not retired: the stripe-free record loses its stamp and owner and its majority commit, and keeps its other fields), not by the T-S3 rework, which builds on the result. The 1e8-key bytes-per-key and bulk-load measurement stays. On resuming, the rework waits for the formats of T-S2 as amended by T-I2.
- **T-S5** (HDD drive scheduler, paused; what revision 9 would change when resumed): unchanged in substance, since the scheduler never touches metadata. The only change is that the `x-ts-load` header on internal traffic no longer travels beside the cluster stamp (there is no stamp), which affects a constant and a test fixture and nothing in the scheduler.
- **Every client-side task below** places its code in the crate T-L creates; T-L's first PR (about a day) lands first, and a task already part-way through moves its modules into the crate unchanged.
- **T1, T1b, T8** (entry store, journal, eviction): code lands in `tierstore-cache` with documented public items, not in the daemon crate.
- **T2, T4** (hash pool, budget): code lands in `tierstore-client`.
- **T3** (fetch engine): split between `tierstore-client` (parts, retries, hedges, `LOCATE`, generation) and `tierstore-cache` (single flight, claims, `Engine`). No client pings: the client reads the recommended copy of the `LOCATE` answer, contacts nodes only in `rank(K)` order, keeps a client-local `suspect` from request outcomes, hedges at the p95 within a 5% budget, and a co-located client looks up its own index in-process (4.6, 10.6). It treats `503` with `Retry-After` as retriable and never as absence (section 23).
- **T5, T6** (mount protocol server and client): they are the shared cache's server and client in `tierstore-cache`; the protocol is unchanged.
- **T7** (planner): in `tierstore-tree`; ranks shared packs first, then tree order (6.1); no profile input.
- **T9** (metrics): the new metrics of section 12 and, from revision 9, the metadata metrics registered by the tasks named in its row; per-crate registry handles.
- **T10a** (fake harness): the fake storage node serves the membership route (T-I2); it gains the model of the metadata store; the library-mode suite (T-L) uses the fakes.
- **T-FMT** (L2 format): readers ignore unknown `pack_flags` bits; bit 1 is `shared`; readers accept only `cell_shift = 20`.
- Not started and changed by revision 8 (T-S6 and T-S12 are paused store tasks; the others are client-side and continue): T4 (controller signals from part responses and `LOCATE` answers, no pings), T-S6 (membership route, `StoreUpstream` in `tierstore-client`, load-ordered `LOCATE`, `x-ts-load` and the storage-node load table, pings between storage nodes only), T-S12 (step 6 of 21.3 is a configuration change, not a role change), T-F8 and T-M1 (adapters on `tierstore-tree`), T10b (the report of 17.3), T12 (on `tierstore-cache`).
- Not started and paused, and what revision 9 would change in them when the store is resumed: T-S0 (conformance for both bucket modes), T-S2 (`written_ms`, commit marker, `NODE` file; no ballot, ledger, tombstone-log or descriptor-majority formats), T-S4 (fence and one compare-and-set seal, epoch check, lazy segment rows), T-S6 (the routes `fence` and `tomb-apply` instead of `seal`, `descriptor`, `tomb` and `index-sync`; the mutable-bucket front; `503` windows), T-S7 and T-S10 (descriptor commit, stripe free and relocate as transactions), T-S8 (the in-place conversion of replicated blocks commits through the re-encode, move or convert transaction, 10.16 transaction 12, so it depends on the T-S22 interface), T-S9a and T-S9b (state from Postgres tables and job leases; ordering from the reconciler), T-S12 (the Postgres member move and the re-encoding drain through transaction 12), T-S14 (the Postgres model), T-S15 (the metadata drills), T-S17 to T-S21 (state from Postgres; database roles; the migrate verb; pgBackRest replaces the off-box ledger export), T-K1b (the client requirement of 60 s of retries on `503`).
- **Section 24 (operations and repair):** T-S9a and T-S9b keep the copy, decode, rebuild and scrub mechanics and are executors of the reconciler (T-S25). T-S13 and T-S16 are deleted and replaced by T-S22 (rows and client) and T-S25 (the reconciler and the operator verbs); new in revision 9: T-S22 to T-S25. T-S6 adds the operator API's transport; T-S12's drain steps go through the membership validator (24.9).
- Unaffected: T0, T0-F-a, T-F0 to T-F7, T-F9, T-ENV, T-B1 to T-B4, T-J1, T-K1a, T-S5, T13, T14, T-R, T11a, T11b (T-S11 is not needed and T11b is done, 10.10). (T-B5 reads through `tierstore-client`, which is where `StoreUpstream` lives in any case. T0-S gains the power-loss-protection and metadata-partition items above and is otherwise unchanged.)

**Estimate for the storage tier [E] (if the store is resumed).** The T-S tasks of the core (T-S0 to T-S12, T-S9 split into T-S9a and T-S9b) sum to about 93 agent-days of code and tests (revision 9 removes about 5 from T-S2, T-S3 and T-S4, where ballots, the stamp, the tombstone logs and `index-sync` were, and adds 1 to T-S6 for the mutable-bucket front and 1 to T-S0), with a range of **62 to 112, the upper half more likely**; the low end is the line-rate calibration of revision 5 (about 3 k lines per agent-day on plumbing PRs), the high end applies the usual two-to-three-times for crash-correct storage code. The operations tasks (T-S14 to T-S25, with T-S13 and T-S16 deleted) sum to about 53 agent-days (range 40 to 70): the 45 of revision 8 less the 15 of T-S13 and T-S16, plus 21 for T-S22 to T-S25 and 3 more for the simulator's Postgres model and the metadata drills (T-S14 +2, T-S15 +1), less 1 in T-S20 (no off-box ledger export). **The change from revision 8 is therefore about +6 agent-days on a total of about 146 (93 + 53), which is inside the error of the estimate: protocol code was removed and integration and operations code added** [I]. The CAS service tasks and benchmarks come on top (T-K1a, T-K1b, T-B5: about 10; the client tolerance of T-K1b is a small change inside it). Revision 5's estimate for its smaller cold tier was 25 to 40.

**Milestones (phased delivery, 10.13; the plan if the store is resumed, none is in progress).** **S1** = T-S0 to T-S7 and **T-S9a**, with **T-S22, T-S23 and T-S24** (every seal, destage and delete commits through the metadata store, so the schema, the client, the cluster and the freeze are part of the first milestone) and the copy-repair half of **T-S25** (loop, lease, queue and copy executors): hot tier, cold tier with `replicas = N`, S3 front with LIST and both bucket modes, the repair `replicas = N` needs (re-replication of hot blocks and cold copies, scrub, margin and live-key audits); there is no migration, because silo was removed from the farm before the store was built (10.10). **On the NOW profile** S1 runs with `cold.replicas = 2` on the SMR drives (34.6 TB logical under the guard is about 26 TB at 2.0x, far above the 1.34 TB live) and S2 converts those blocks in place to `4+2` (10.5); the Postgres members run on three of the six combined nodes. **S2** = T-S8 and T-S9b with the decode half of T-S25: erasure coding (including the in-place conversion of S1's replicated blocks), rebuild by decode, deep scrub, the margin-0 rules. **S3** = T-S10: promote and GC rewrite; **T-S12** (the NOW-to-LATER migration, which includes moving the Postgres members) is needed when the storage nodes arrive and depends only on S2 and T-S23 (stripe freeing of fully dead stripes ships with T-S7's free transaction).

**Order if the store is resumed; today only the client-side tasks run. Day 0 (no dependency):** T-I (every trait and format, the store's included: their rules are in section 10; merged as komira-ai/tierstore#352) and its delta T-I2, T0, T0-F-a, T-F0, T-B1, T-ENV, T-FMT Q1. **After T-I:** T-L's first PR (the three crates, about a day) ahead of the client-side tasks, then T-SB (on T-I2 and T-L), T-K1c (on T-FMT); T-S1 would start after T-I2. **Right after T-I (about a day), in parallel against fakes:** T-FMT format, T-F1, T-F3, T-J1, T1, T1b, T2, T3, T4 (on starting constants), T5, T7, T8, T10a, T14, T-M1, and the store's T-S0, T-S1, T-S2, T-S5. **Right after T-I2:** T-S22 (its trait and model first, so that the tasks that commit to the metadata store can run on it, then the schema and the real client). **Then:** T-S3 (on T-S2 and the T-S22 model), T-S4 (on T-S2, the T-S3 interface and the T-S22 model), T-F2, T-F4..T-F9 (T-F5 and T-F6 on T-F0, T-F7 on T-ENV), T6 (on T5), T-K1a (on T-FMT), T-S23 and T-S24 (on T-S22; T-S23 also needs a three-node farm cluster), T-S14 growing alongside. **Then:** T-S6 (on T-S3, T-S4, T-S5, T3, T-S22), T-S7 (on T-S2, T-S5, T-S22 and T-S6's interface), T0-S (first half as soon as the nodes exist; second half on T-S4, T-S5, T-S7, T-S8, T-S9b), T-K1b (on T-S6). **Then:** T-S25 (on T-S7, T-S22; the decode executors on T-S8), T-S9a (on T-S7 and the T-S25 interface), T-S8 (on T-S7), T-S9b (on T-S8, T-S9a), T-S10 (on T-S7, T-S6), T-S12 (on T-S8, T-S9b, T-S23), T-S15 (on T-S23, T-S25 and the LATER cluster), T13, T-B2/T-B3/T-B5, T10b, T-B4, T11a (on T10b), T11b (done), T-R.

Longest chains (if the store is resumed): T-I -> T-S2 -> T-S3 -> T-S4 -> T-S6 -> T-S7 -> T-S9a (store, milestone S1: about 48 serial agent-days, the 53 planned with T-S11 less its 5 [E], and about 4 to 6 weeks more for S2 and S3 [E]; T-S22, T-S23, T-S24 and the copy half of T-S25 run beside this chain on the model and need the farm cluster only for their drills, so they do not lengthen it) and T-I -> T-F1 -> T-F2 -> T-F8 -> T10b -> T-R (FUSE). The task graph has no cycle: T-S6 needs T-S3, T-S4, T-S5 and T-S22 and not the CAS service; the CAS service's write side (T-K1b) follows T-S6; T-S9a needs only the interface of T-S25, and T-S25 needs T-S7 and not T-S9a.

**Which tasks start now (the client side), and which store tasks would wait for this revision's formats if resumed.** Start now (client side): T0, T-F0, T-B1, T-ENV, T-FMT Q1, T-K1a, T0-F-a (silo and object-size facts, which the trial also uses), and the client-side tasks (T-F*, T1*, T2 to T9), none of which depend on the store's formats. T0-S is paused with the store (its first half, the drive, NVMe `fdatasync` and power-loss-protection facts, would start when the storage nodes exist and the store is resumed). If the store is resumed, T-I freezes the store's record (`written_ms`), commit marker, tombstone, block, footer and stripe-free formats and the replication messages (segment epoch, fence) as written here, and T-I2 adds the `Meta` trait; then T-S0, T-S1, T-S2, T-S5 and T-S22 in parallel, and the rest in the order above.

## 19. Critique resolution

**The owner's decisions of revision 9 (2026-11-18)**, and where each is applied.

| Decision | Applied as | Where |
|---|---|---|
| Block-level truth in a dedicated Postgres cluster across the storage nodes | Three members (primary, one synchronous standby, one more standby) on the storage nodes, never on a machine outside the storage tier; NOW on three of the six combined nodes, on a 64 GiB partition of the SATA SSD; LATER on the three storage nodes, on a 256 GiB partition of the NVMe; point-in-time backups to an off-box target that is not tierstore; not the CAS service's or the jobscheduler's Postgres (the CAS service runs on tierstore, so that would be a cycle, and a shared instance shares blast radius) | 10.16, 21, 24 |
| The LATER NVMe drives have power-loss protection | Group commit, `hot.min_acks`, `batch_max` and Postgres's default `synchronous_commit = on` are sized assuming it on LATER; T0-S and the hardware audit verify it and a drive without it is refused, not silently retuned; NOW (SATA SSD, no PLP) keeps honest `fdatasync` and `fsync` everywhere and accepts millisecond commit latency; nothing turns fsync off | 10.3, 10.16, 21, T0-S |
| Every client tolerates about 30 s of `503` with `Retry-After` during a metadata failover | Stated for every client of the S3 front or the peer protocol: retry for at least 60 s with jittered backoff, never read `503` as absence, retry idempotently; `tierstore-client` does it itself; the CAS service's change is its own task outside this repository | 10.9, 23 |
| The store is general-purpose | Keys are content-derived for every CAS-service object but correctness never depends on it; create-only (`412`) stays a capability; ordinary S3 semantics are specified for mutable buckets (overwrite, DELETE, prefix LIST, `If-Match`, `If-None-Match`); the immutable bucket is the CAS service's fast path, with its create race stated | 10.2, 10.9, 20 |
| Failover manager: Patroni with a three-member etcd | On the same three nodes, failover target at most 30 s, measured by drill; no lease manager of our own | 10.16, 22, T-S23 |

**The owner's decisions of revision 8**, and where each is applied.

| Decision | Applied as | Where |
|---|---|---|
| No roles in code | Components with sections and declared dependencies; any set whose dependencies are met is valid; `Role` removed from the frozen view; the deployment shapes are files in `docs/examples/` | 10.1, 10.8, 10.14, 21, T-I2 |
| The client as libraries, the daemon as a thin host | `tierstore-client`, `tierstore-cache`, `tierstore-tree` with versioned APIs, API tests and a library-mode suite; the daemon's fronts and Prepare use only them, checked by a layering lint; the client-side tasks mapped onto the crates | 23, 18 (T-L) |
| Learned access profiles (first asked for, then dropped by the owner as too ambitious for now) | Prepare's order is shared packs first, then tree order, under the whole-pack rule; eStargz and Nydus cited as prior art; a publish-time order hint in the L2 list named as a later option, gated on T10b's report | 6.1, 17.3, 18 (T-K1c) |
| Seed bootstrap of membership | Seeds or a file; `GET /store/v1/membership` with the cluster token; the epoch followed from `x-ts-mem-epoch`; an additive route of the peer protocol | 10.6, 10.8, 18 (T-SB, T-I2) |
| Explain the 1 MiB cell | Digest table, first byte, FUSE read size, fetch grid; `cell_shift` per pack | 2 |
| Two layers of reads, explained | FUSE reads of at most 1 MiB from the local cache; fetch parts of 16 to 64 MiB or whole packs from a storage drive into it | 4.0 |
| No client pings; load from the storage nodes; hedging; only `rank(K)` nodes; co-located `LOCATE` | Clients learn liveness from their own requests and the epoch from response headers; storage nodes share load on their own traffic (`x-ts-load`) and ping only when idle; `LOCATE` answers copies ordered by load with a recommended one; hedges at the p95 within a 5% budget; a client contacts nodes only in `rank(K)` order (past the key's replica nodes only to decide a miss) (a definitive miss walks further down the ranking); a co-located client looks up its own index | 4.3, 4.6, 4.9, 10.6, 10.7, 10.8, 14, 18 (T3, T4, T-S6, T-I2) |
| Operations, repair and runbooks | Section 24 (decisions D1 to D9), with every blocker of its adversarial review resolved (24.14) and the prioritized additions of 24.15; the reconciler of 24.5 on the metadata store; T-S17 to T-S25 | 24, 10.2, 10.4, 10.8, 10.16, 18 |
| SeaweedFS's deciding gaps; `4+4` against two replicas | Fresh outputs from replicated NVMe here against HDD volumes or age-moved SSD volumes there; promote-on-read here, none for read-only EC volumes there; `4+4` is 2x raw like two replicas and buys tolerance, not capacity | 10.12, Q1 |

**The critique of revision 6** (an adversarial recheck of the storage tier; verdict: ready with named fixes) and the owner's rulings made after it. Each finding was resolved in revision 7 as below.

| Finding | Disposition | Where |
|---|---|---|
| F1 Tombstone ordering used a per-node counter: records and tombstones of one key written by different nodes were incomparable, and a re-PUT of identical bytes after a delete had the same `(key, generation)` as the deleted record | **Fixed by identity, not by order.** A tombstone names a record id, the pair `(block_id, offset)` of one stored copy-set, and kills exactly that record; a re-PUT of identical bytes after a delete is a new record with a new id, so it is live and the old tombstone cannot touch it. No clock is compared anywhere: a mutable bucket orders the writes of one key by the Postgres transaction that commits them, and an immutable bucket has at most one live record per key, with the all-node check and the `N - min_acks + 1` intersection of DELETE and re-PUT making an acknowledged delete visible to the next create. Both failure cases (the re-PUT after a delete, the delete racing a relocate) are simulation tests. | 10.2, 10.16, 14 |
| F2 Two sealers under a partition (the writer's early seal and a replica's take-over) had no tie-break; create-only was overstated during a view divergence | **Fixed.** A seal is a fence followed by one compare-and-set on the segment's row in Postgres: the sealer fences every copy at the next epoch (a copy refuses batches of the older epoch and reports its verified length), picks the length by the same rule as before, and commits it with `UPDATE ... WHERE state = 'open' AND epoch <= e`; exactly one sealer's update wins and a loser reads the committed row and converges to it. Destage, re-replication and trailers act only on a sealed row; the simulation includes asymmetric partitions with a lost acknowledgement. Create-only is stated as linearizable when views agree, with divergence detected by `store_key_conflict_total` and resolved by the reconciler (10.5). | 10.3, 10.5, 10.16, 14 |
| F3 Silo would be retired before the store could re-replicate a cold copy or a hot block | **Fixed.** T-S9 is split; T-S9a (hot and cold-copy re-replication, scrub of copies, margin and live-key audits) is in milestone S1, and silo's destructive step is gated on it; decode rebuild stays in S2 (T-S9b). | 10.11, 18 |
| F4 Degraded-read load was not in the budget | **Fixed.** A degraded part is `k` charges at the reconstructor and at the client; the reconstructor is chosen by queue; capacity with a node down is stated (about 2.8 GB/s); 17.2 item 5 has a pass bar. No read drain before maintenance (decided: the shards cannot be read elsewhere). | 4.3, 4.6, 10.4, 17.2 |
| F5 Rebuild arithmetic counted reads only and used a fill the guard forbids; survivors sat at `fill_limit` after a node-loss rebuild; margin 0 while a node is down | **Fixed.** Arithmetic counts writes and one decode per stripe, at the guard; the guard keeps one drive per survivor free; rebuild writes may reach `rebuild_fill_ceiling` (92%); while a node is down, scrub verifies margin-1 stripes first, margin-0 stripes rebuild first, and margin 0 alerts. | 10.4, 10.5, 10.15 |
| F6 Freeing a stripe had no owner or protocol | **Fixed.** Live counts are exact rows in Postgres (set at seal, decremented by each tombstone's transaction); a stripe is freed by one conditional transaction that succeeds only while no block of it has a live record and the stripe is still `committed`; shards are unlinked only after that commit; a re-PUT always writes a new hot record, so it can never be lost to a free in flight. | 10.5, 10.16 |
| F7 Open segments per ordered pair and size class fragment the cold tier; partial stripes; objects just under a block | **Fixed.** One open segment per unordered replica pair and expiry class; age seal only above a 256 MiB floor; stripes from any pair, grouped by length; first-fit decreasing pack assembly; `block_reserve` makes the chain boundary exact; `cold_fill_efficiency` metric and C-10 replay. | 10.2, 10.3, 10.5, 16 |
| F8 The index was hand-rolled persistence over an unspecified in-memory map | **Fixed** by decision: an embedded ordered store (`fjall`, `redb` as the measured alternative), treated as a rebuildable cache with a replay mark; LIST seeks past rolled-up prefixes. | 10.2, 10.9, 18 |
| F9 Ceph RGW baseline missing; the SeaweedFS ratio fact; the decoder API | **Fixed.** Ceph RGW is a row of 10.12 with its reasons; the SeaweedFS ratio fact was re-read (default 10+4, per-volume shard counts in the current source, a per-volume ratio that must be pre-seeded; the Enterprise claim is withdrawn, 10.17) and no longer carries the decision; the decoder restores data shards only, and parity rebuild re-encodes. | 10.4, 10.12, 18 |
| F10 Reclaim order could evict a just-promoted copy; reject at 95%; `fdatasync` on appended files and PLP; NVMe as the failure unit | **Fixed.** No promotion above the destage-start mark; slow at 85%, reject at 90% (also of total fill); preallocation decided by T0-S; no PLP is a stop condition for the owner; NVMe loss is node loss for hot data, stated. | 10.3, 10.5, 10.13, 16 |
| F11 S1 replicated blocks would be re-copied to become EC | **Fixed.** In-place conversion: one copy is renamed as the data shard, only parity is written. | 10.5, 18 |
| Owner: tierstore owns the movement; destage decoupled from eviction; promoted copies separate | **Applied.** Destage copies dirty data; reclaim only drops clean data and never waits on a drive; cache segments are never destaged; the cold block stays authoritative. | 10.3, 10.5 |
| Owner: units explicit | **Applied.** Block for storage, placement, EC and destage; object or byte range for read, fetch and promotion. | 10.2 |
| Owner (after revision 7 was first proposed): cold code `4+4` and three hot replicas decided | **Applied** as decisions with their consequences (3x NVMe write fan-out, 6.1 TB usable hot (after the metadata partition, revision 9), the sizing of 10.5 and 10.15, hot reads without a hop, the `N - min_acks + 1` miss rule). | Decisions, 10.2, 10.3, 10.6, 10.15 |
| Owner: one codebase for both deployments, cite published data or name the experiment, an implementation-ready breakdown | **Applied.** Section 21 (NOW and LATER profiles with full configurations, the migration by membership epochs), section 22 (evidence map), section 18 (tests and start point per task, T-S12). | 18, 21, 22 |
| Owner: a mode without a cold tier | **Applied.** No cold store (revision 7: `cold.mode = "none"`; revision 8: no `[cold]` section) with `hot.on_full = "evict" \| "reject"` and two example configurations. | 10.14 |
| T0 first results (drive rate, sha256 speed, loop device) | **Applied.** In the measured facts; sha256 via `ring`; the cache move is required. | facts, 4.7, 11, Q3 |
| Revision 5 claimed SeaweedFS's EC model was a reason to reject it | **Corrected.** SeaweedFS's EC keeps a needle on one shard in the common case (4.54 encodes one uniform block per shard; the 1 GB layout is the legacy one); the remaining reasons are listed in 10.12, and 10.17 has the 4.54 facts. | 10.12, 10.17 |

**The evidence on SeaweedFS and on building (critiques before revision 6).**

| Finding | Disposition | Where |
|---|---|---|
| "Deletes constantly" is the wrong framing (147 DELETEs against 418,823 PUTs); the design's rejection rested on it | **Accepted.** Revision 5's argument is dropped; the reasons are those of 10.12. | 10.12 |
| "Refuted" overstates it; delete rate after GC is unknown | **Accepted.** GC volume is treated as unknown and sized for; the 38% dead bytes and 14,795 dead packs are the stated input. | Measured facts, 10.5, 10.13 |
| The capacity gap (silo raw about 4.6x logical) and long fill horizons are soft; 20x growth already happened once | **Accepted.** The new store does not depend on the gap; the horizon is given at today's rate and at 20x; C-6 explains the gap before the import volume is trusted. | 10.15, 16 C-6 |
| The workload counters do not measure the whole-pack load; the 74% create-only rejections are a load on the lock path | **Accepted.** Create-only is decided by the index under a key lock, no lock service; a measured pack-load run is in T0-S (C-3). | 10.5, 16 |
| "Loss is a miss" is a convenient framing for a store that holds the CAS data | **Accepted.** The tier is durable: no capacity eviction with a cold tier, redundancy per tier, audit of margin and unreadable keys; a pack lost beyond the code's tolerance is still a miss the CAS service republishes. | 4.6, 10.8 |
| Chunk fan-out is a configurable default; the dominant cost is hops and filer lookups; vacuum 2.3x is only at steady eviction; repair is scheduled | **Accepted and stated** in 10.12. | 10.12 |
| The filer store is the largest SeaweedFS operational unknown; the create-only lock couples availability to the lock service | **Accepted** and stated in 10.12. | 10.12 |
| Estimates are optimistic; segment reclaim is the same garbage problem; LIST is not 2 agent-days; perpetual ownership; rollback is not free; eviction coupling is data loss | **Accepted.** Estimate 62 to 112 agent-days for the core (about 146 in all with operations, section 18) with phased delivery; GC is designed and not claimed free; LIST is designed with an ordered index and merged fan-out; ownership and rollback are stated; capacity eviction does not exist with a cold tier. | 10.5, 10.9, 10.13, 18 |
| Run two cheap reversible experiments before committing | **Kept as an option:** the conformance run and the two-node reference run are T-S0 and 17.2 item 6, a reference and a fallback, not gates (the owner has decided to build). | 17.2, 18 |

**Revision 5's cold-tier findings (revision 4 critique, N-C1 to N-C11): disposition.** The cold tier of revision 5 (copies on the build nodes' SMR drives, HRW placement, per-file generations, eviction) is replaced, so most of its mechanisms are superseded rather than resolved; the lessons carry over as follows.

| Finding of revision 5 | Disposition (revisions 6 and 7) |
|---|---|
| N-C1 generation per PUT breaks create-only and failover | **Carried:** generation = blake3 of the bytes; create-only by the index under a key lock with the all-node check in an immutable bucket; conflict rule (lower record id wins, 10.2, 10.5); a mutable bucket takes its precondition from the `heads` row instead (10.2). |
| N-C2 hung drive not detected; stuck thread not cancellable | **Carried** unchanged for HDDs: watchdog, `stalled`, probe before `up`, `failed` after three stalls (10.7). |
| N-C3 repair only on full reads; copy count decays unmeasured | **Replaced:** repair is rebuild by decode and scrub, margin and live-key audits (10.4, 10.8). |
| N-C4 node-wide reaction to one drive; slow consumer; third copy | **Carried:** per-drive permits plus a storage-node level (4.3); slot release (10.7); hot-spot defences (10.13). |
| N-C5 SMR churn; write class; fill marks | **Reduced:** writes are whole sequential shards and unlinks; the churn arm runs only if the drives are SMR; the write share is kept (10.7, 16 C-5b). |
| N-C6 tier eviction can fail a running job | **Removed by construction:** the store never evicts for capacity (10.5); the lost-mid-job path remains for DELETE and catastrophic loss (4.6). |
| N-C7 probe depth, hint, size not in name, `down` undefined | **Simplified:** three nodes, `LOCATE` in `rank(K)` order (revision 8: nodes in `rank(K)` order, load-ordered copies), index kept on `hot.replicas` nodes, definitive-miss rule `N - min_acks + 1` (10.6); states table kept (10.8). |
| N-C8 small objects have no policy | **Replaced:** hot-tier segments with group commit, sealed at 1 GiB, packed into cold blocks (10.3, 10.4). |
| N-C9 task graph cycle; T11 waits for silo | **Carried:** T-K1a and T-K1b split, T11a and T11b split, no cycle (18). |
| N-C10 migration capacity, rollback cost, destage race | **Changed:** no shared drives so no capacity inequality; rollback cost and import convergence stated (10.10). |
| N-C11 deletes without tombstones; space accounting; negative cache; threads versus depth; one EIO fails a drive | **Changed:** tombstones are Postgres rows naming a record id, applied to the node indexes by push and by a single sequence cursor, and deleted in the transaction that frees their block (10.2, 10.16); space accounting by allocated bytes kept (10.4); negative cache and threads-versus-depth kept; EIO quarantines a shard (10.7). |

**Critique of revision 3: resolved in revision 4 and unchanged here.** F1 reply affinity and read-time cancel registration (5.2); F2 per-cell CAS eviction (5.1); F3 sealed state memfd, per-holder counter memfd, lifeline, post-read recheck (5.1); F4 takeover with every clone retained (5.2); F5 `durable` bit and server-only DONTNEED (4.8, 8); F6 id-preserving sidecar, dual emission first, node-by-node readers (3.5); F7 per-drive budget quantities and grid-relative ramp (4.3, 4.4); F8 macOS on the same engine (5.6); F9 io_uring memory, hybrid transport, environment (5.2); F10 INIT flags, splice length, pipe size, image page-in (5.2, 5.3); F11 separate runtimes (5.4); F12 T-ENV and dependencies (18); F13 boot id and batched punches (8); N2, N2b, N5, N11, S5, M1 with them.

## 20. Limits of this design
- **Nothing here was run.** [I], [E] and [K] items have named checks: T0-S and T0-F (drive behaviour, CMR or SMR, scheduler constants, network, encode speed, object sizes, GC replay), T-F0 (kernel behaviour), T-FMT Q1 (tree id).
- **The owner's decisions** are at the top: the hardware and the tier shapes, Q1 the cold code (`4+4`, decided), Q2 hot replicas (3, decided), Q3 per-cell digests (default yes, sha256), Q4 the old-tree trust gap.
- **The CAS service's decisions (T-K1a):** its 404 handling (the miss path), which the lost path of 4.6 relies on, and whether it sends the expiry-class hint.
- **Three storage nodes are a small cluster.** The tolerance is one node at a time (one spare shard at `4+4`, none at `4+2`); a second fault during a node outage or during the destage window loses data that is not yet protected twice. A minority partition can neither commit a seal nor acknowledge a write that needs metadata. The design states the windows (10.13) and measures the margin (10.8); it does not claim more.
- **The metadata store is a dependency with its own limits (10.16).**
  - **A metadata outage stops DELETE, seals, destage, repair and every operation on a mutable bucket**, each answering `503` with `Retry-After` where a client is waiting; immutable-bucket PUT and GET continue, a writer opens new segments (their ids are node-allocated) and fenced-unsealed segments wait. Hot headroom for a long outage is about 72 days at today's ingest [E] on LATER; the figure for NOW is smaller and is not claimed here.
  - **A failover is up to about 30 s of `503`, a target [I] that the drills of T-S23 and T-S15 measure**; every client must ride it out (section 23).
  - **Two of the three metadata members down stops metadata commits**: strict synchronous replication refuses to commit on one member, which is the same outcome as losing two nodes of the storage tier. One member down loses nothing and stops nothing after a failover. A total loss of all three members is a restore from the off-box backup with at most about a minute of write-ahead log lost, followed by `metadata fsck`.
  - **A restore to an older point is the one hazard the design defends rather than prevents**: the incarnation, the rollback check and the freeze of destructive actions (10.16) stop nodes from deleting data a restored database no longer knows about, and `metadata fsck` rebuilds the rows from the disks; until fsck has run, destructive work is frozen.
  - **Mutable buckets are bounded by the `heads` table**: one row per live key plus deleted heads not yet purged (bounded by the purge delay times the delete rate), about 170 bytes with its index [I], so about 17 GB at 1e8 live keys [I]; they are meant for up to about 1e8 keys in all, a bound the load test of T-S22 measures, and every operation on them costs a round trip to the primary (about 0.3 to 0.7 ms [I]) and fails during a failover. They are not a substitute for an object store's scale-out namespace.
  - **An immutable bucket is correct only for content-derived keys or one writer per key** (10.5). With any other key scheme and two writers racing across an epoch change, both can be acknowledged; the reconciler keeps the record with the lower id, tombstones the other and counts and alerts it (`store_key_conflict_total`), which loses an acknowledged write. Such a user takes a mutable bucket.
  - **After a DELETE in an immutable bucket a node that has not yet applied the tombstone can serve the object for up to `metadata.tomb_poll` (2 s) after it is reachable again** [D]; the CAS service deletes only unreferenced packs, so this is harmless to it.
  - **Metadata commit latency on NOW is a few milliseconds per commit round trip (10.16)**: the SATA SSDs have no power-loss protection and every commit is a real flash program on two nodes; nothing turns `fsync` off. On LATER the sizing assumes power-loss protection, which is the owner's statement about the drives; T0-S and the hardware audit check it, and a drive found without it is refused.
- **The store is general-purpose within a stated subset.** Multipart upload, object versioning, lifecycle rules, ACLs and policies, tagging, CopyObject, object lock, server-side encryption, bucket configuration APIs, presigned-URL issuance and date preconditions on PUT answer `501 NotImplemented`; each has its reason in 10.9. Buckets are created by an operator verb and their mode is fixed at creation.
- **Create-only is linearizable only while the storage nodes' views agree**; a divergence is detected, counted and resolved by the reconciler, not prevented (10.5). With content-derived keys it converges.
- **Durability of hot data is `hot.replicas` copies until destage**; with `min_acks` below `replicas` it is weaker, as documented (10.3).
- **GC is designed and not measured.** Rewrite costs, the effect of expiry grouping and the promote thresholds are estimates until T0-F C-10 and C-3b.
- **The estimate is about 93 agent-days for the core (T-S0 to T-S12), 62 to 112 with the upper half more likely, and about 53 more for operations (T-S14 to T-S25), about 146 in all (section 18),** and revision 9 adds about 5 to the total (revision 8 planned about 95 for the core), inside the error of the estimate (the protocol code of ballots, ledger facts, tombstone logs and the stamp is removed; the metadata client, the reconciler, fsck and the Postgres operations are added) [I], with a calendar of about 7 to 10 weeks to S1 and 4 to 6 more for S2 and S3 [E]. The fallback to an S3-compatible store (10.12) is kept available by the `Upstream` abstraction.
- **io_uring FUSE** (parked entries, memory, hybrid delivery, takeover) is decided by T-F0, T-F5 and the section 17 rules; `/dev/fuse` is the default until they pass. `fuse.enable_uring` is set at boot by the hostkeeper image.
- **Kernel facts tagged [K]** (reply matching per device, resend with clones, `F_SEAL_FUTURE_WRITE`, uring INTERRUPT delivery, `F_PUNCHHOLE` on APFS) come from source knowledge; each has a T-F0, T-F7 or T-M1 check. The farm kernel version is reported, not verified.
- The CAS service's hashing CPU doubles (cell digests; T-FMT measured 325 MB/s per core for cells against 319 MB/s for whole files). The id-preserving backfill reads packs once, about 0.9 core-hours of hashing per TiB.
- The three Postgres members and their etcd share three combined nodes with builds (load average 131 on 88 CPUs was measured on today's nodes [M]); the CPU weights of 10.16 are the defence and the failover drill on the farm measures whether it holds [I].
- The 60 ms cold first-byte figure is an idle-farm target; the loaded bound of 10.7 is a hypothesis for T10b.
- The first-part-small recipe, the P probe arm, striping across hot replicas, the write share, slot release, object-granular promote, expiry-class grouping and the per-stripe code in every shard footer are this design's own, with no external source; T0-S and T10b validate them. The seal's fence is an epoch check on the replicas with one compare-and-set in the metadata store; the quorum, the failover and the fencing of a deposed primary are Postgres, Patroni and etcd, operated as shipped and not reimplemented here. The new code is the `tierstore-meta` client, the reconciler and `metadata fsck`, with simulation tests against a model of the store that includes outage, ambiguous commit, failover with and without the loss of an asynchronous tail, and point-in-time rollback.
- **The cold read capacity with a node down (about 2.8 GB/s) and the rebuild times are estimates** from vendor drive rates; T0-S and 17.2 measure them.
- **Whether Prepare's order matters is not known** until T10b's report (17.3); the shared-pack threshold is this design's guess.
- **The hedge budget (5% of parts, at the p95) is taken from published practice**, not measured here; C-3 and T10b check that hedges cut the tail without raising drive load beyond the budget.
- **Operations and repair (section 24) are designed, not run:** the timers, shares and the 14 to 20 hour node rebuild are estimates until the simulator, C-11 and the drills of 24.13 measure them; vendor defaults cited as comparators were read from their documentation and are not re-checked here.
- **The build system does not use the libraries yet:** its own rules admit no dependency on code of ours (section 23); the design does not depend on it.
- **The crate split moves code, not behaviour:** the client-side tasks keep their tests, and the API tests of section 23 freeze each crate's surface from 1.0; a later breaking change is a new major version.

## 21. Deployment profiles [D]
One codebase, one binary and one configuration schema (10.14) serve both deployments. A profile is only configuration (which components each machine runs, and their settings) and a membership document; nothing in the code branches on which profile runs, and the names "storage node", "build node" and "combined node" below name example files in `docs/examples/`, not anything the code knows (10.1). The store's formats are the same in both, and the code of every stripe is in its rows and in every shard footer, so the migration from NOW to LATER moves bytes and changes configuration and membership, never code or formats.

### 21.1 NOW: six combined nodes on today's hardware
**Hardware [M]:** six nodes, each with 2 x 8 TB drive-managed SMR hard drives (ST8000DM004, xfs, which held silo until it was removed from the farm) and one SATA SSD of about 1 TB (root filesystem today; the node cache moves to a partition of it, section 11). There is no NVMe. Each node runs builds **and** stores packs: it runs every component of 10.1 except the NFS front, the configuration `docs/examples/now-combined.toml`. Three of the six nodes also run a member of the metadata cluster (below).

**Hot tier: a partition of the SATA SSD, `hot.replicas = 2`, `min_acks = 2`.** The SSD is the only fast device. The hot tier gets its own xfs partition beside the cache partition (sized by T0 from the SSD's free space; this profile assumes 300 GiB [I]), so the two never share free space or a filesystem journal. Why two replicas, not three as on LATER:
- the SSD also holds the root filesystem and the build cache, so every extra copy is write load on the device the jobs read from; three copies of 34 GB/day is about 100 GB/day of SATA writes spread over six SSDs, which is affordable, but buys little here;
- with six failure domains, two copies already survive any one node, and the short destage window below bounds the time data spends on two devices;
- the consumer SATA SSDs have no power-loss protection [I, T0 records it], so each group commit is a real flash program (milliseconds [I]); at about 2.5 small PUTs per second that is acceptable, and fewer copies keep the tail short. The power-loss-protection requirement of 10.3 (the owner's statement about the LATER NVMe, checked by T0-S and the hardware audit) applies to LATER only; on NOW the SSDs have none, every `fdatasync` and every metadata `fsync` is honest, nothing is turned off, and the rate is low enough that T0's `fdatasync` latency sets `batch_max` and nothing else.
`LOCATE` asks the first live node of `rank(K)` (10.6); a hot read crosses the network five times in six, at SATA SSD speed, which is not the bottleneck: the co-located build cache absorbs repeats.

**Cold tier: `ec = 4+2` on the 12 SMR drives, failure domain node, one shard per node, `max_shards_per_domain = 2`.** Twelve drives on six nodes allow a code of 6 shards with one per node. `4+2` at one shard per node survives the loss of **any two nodes**, or of a node and a drive, at 1.5x raw; after a node is lost for good its stripes are rebuilt onto the five survivors with one survivor holding two shards (allowed, since `m = 2 >= 2`), so the cluster again survives a further node. Against the alternatives:
- `replicas = 2` costs 2.0x and survives only one node;
- `8+4` at two shards per node is also 1.5x and survives two nodes, but a stripe waits for 8 sealed blocks (more partial stripes at 34 GB/day, 10.5), a repair reads 8 shards instead of 4, and every stripe touches every drive;
- `4+4` would cost 2.0x for a tolerance six domains do not need.
`4+2` also keeps repair reads at 4, the same as LATER. On SMR drives the tier's write pattern, whole sequential shard files and whole-file unlinks, is the pattern drive-managed SMR handles well, and one healthy read is one stream on one drive [M: T0 measured 119 MB/s sequential and 36% of that at 1 MiB scattered on these drives]. C-5b (steady-state churn) runs in full on NOW because the drives are SMR.

**Metadata store: three Postgres members on three of the six combined nodes (nodes 1, 2 and 3 in the example), on a 64 GiB partition of the SATA SSD.** The members are one primary, one synchronous standby and one more standby, with Patroni and a three-member etcd on the same three nodes (10.16); the other three combined nodes run only the `[metadata]` client. The partition is its own xfs filesystem (`noatime`) beside the cache and hot partitions, so Postgres's write-ahead log, its data and etcd's log never share free space or a journal with the build cache; it holds Postgres data, WAL and etcd and is generous for the rows of 10.16 (a few hundred thousand block and stripe rows plus tombstones; mutable-bucket heads at about 170 bytes each [I]). What differs from LATER:
- **Honest fsync, millisecond commits.** The SATA SSDs have no power-loss protection, so `synchronous_commit = on` with a synchronous standby costs two real flash programs per commit, milliseconds [I, T-S23's drill measures it]. The metadata rate is far below the data rate (a commit per seal, destage, repair, tombstone batch and mutable-bucket write; immutable PUT and GET take none), so this is acceptable; `fsync`, `full_page_writes` and `synchronous_commit` stay `on`.
- **CPU weights.** The nodes carry builds (a load average of 131 on 88 CPUs was measured on today's nodes [M]), so the Postgres, Patroni and etcd units run with high `CPUWeight` and etcd's heartbeat and election timeouts are raised to 250 ms and 2.5 s (10.16) [I]; a starved etcd would cost a spurious failover, and the failover drill on the farm (T-S23, T-S15) is where it is measured under build load.
- **Failure domains.** Three members on three different nodes: one node lost leaves two members and a failover of up to about 30 s; two nodes lost stops metadata commits (the same outcome as two nodes lost from the data tier, where `4+2` has no spare shard left); a combined node without a member holds no metadata state beyond its caches.
- **Backups** go to an off-box repository that does not depend on tierstore, configured by the operator (10.16); the restore drill runs on the farm once before production and quarterly.

**The drives' filesystems.** The tier's cold directories are `ts-cold/` on the SMR drives' xfs filesystems, which held silo when this was measured; silo has since been removed from the farm, so the figures below, which count its bytes, are a lower bound. Fill is measured on the filesystem (10.4), so any other bytes count: `0.85 x (96 TB - one node's 16 TB - one drive's 8 TB)` is 61.2 TB of filesystem use under the guard (`reserve_drives = 1`), of which silo holds about 9.3 TB [M], leaving about 51.9 TB raw, **about 34.6 TB logical at `4+2`**, against 1.34 TB live [E]. Silo's own I/O when this was measured (about 10 small writes per second per drive [M]), which the drive scheduler could see only through its service times, is gone with silo; the per-drive budget of 4.3 backs off on slow service times as on any slow drive.

**Configuration (NOW, node 1; the six nodes differ only in `node.id` and addresses):**
```toml
# docs/examples/now-combined.toml: components: hot store, cold store, peer service, S3 front,
# node cache, fetch engine, FUSE front, Prepare provider
[node]
id = 1
membership = "/etc/tierstore/membership.toml"      # six nodes, failure domain = node; read only by `store init` on the node that creates the cluster, ignored on every later start (10.8)
token_file = "/etc/tierstore/cluster.token"

[peer]
listen = "192.0.2.21:7480"

[s3]
listen = "192.0.2.21:7481"
backend = "store"

[cache]
root = "/var/lib/tierstore-cache"     # the SSD cache partition (section 11)

[fetch]                               # finds the storage nodes through this process's own membership file

[fuse]

[prepare]

[store]
kind = "tiered"

[metadata]                            # the metadata store (10.16): Postgres on three of the six nodes, each on a 64 GiB partition of its SATA SSD
hosts = ["192.0.2.21:5432", "192.0.2.22:5432", "192.0.2.23:5432"]   # the same list on all six nodes
database = "tierstore"
user = "ts_rw"
password_file = "/etc/tierstore/metadata.password"
statement_timeout = "100ms"
txn_timeout = "2s"
retry_window = "45s"
tomb_poll = "2s"

[hot]                                 # SATA SSD partition
dir = "/var/lib/ts-hot"
replicas = 2
min_acks = 2
failure_domain = "node"
seal_bytes = "1023MiB"
seal_min_bytes = "256MiB"
dirty_destage_start = 0.40
dirty_destage_stop  = 0.25
dirty_urgent = 0.60
dirty_slow   = 0.80
dirty_reject = 0.90
promo_share = 0.10                    # the build cache beside it absorbs most repeat reads

[cold]
drives = ["/mnt/hdd1", "/mnt/hdd2"]   # the SMR drives' xfs; the tier writes under ts-cold/
block_bytes = "1GiB"
failure_domain = "node"
ec = { k = 4, m = 2 }
max_shards_per_domain = 2             # one per node at write time; two allowed after a node-loss rebuild
fill_limit = 0.85
node_loss_reserve = true
reserve_drives = 1

[destage]
min_age = "1h"
max_age = "1d"                        # short: the hot tier is two consumer SSD copies
partial_after = "3d"

[rebuild]
node_down_after = "24h"
share = 0.25

[reconcile]                           # settings of the hot store: the reconciler (24.5)
interval = "10s"
lease = "15s"
lease_renew = "5s"
executor_wait = "30s"                 # a job not leased by its first-ranked node within this is taken by the next rank (24.1)

[health]                            # settings of the hot store: the health facts (24.2)
report_after = "30s"                # a node unreachable for this long is reported unreachable by its peers (24.2)

[drive]                               # SMR: one stream until T0-F says otherwise
streams = 1
write_streams = 1
```
Until milestone S2 ships erasure coding, NOW runs this profile with `cold.replicas = 2` in place of `ec` (about 26 TB logical under the guard against 1.34 TB live), and S2 converts those blocks in place (10.5). Validation passes: `4+2` on six node domains with at most two shards per node satisfies `m >= max_shards_per_domain`; `hot.replicas = 2` is at most six domains; the marks are ordered; `metadata.hosts` lists three addresses of distinct nodes and `metadata.retry_window` exceeds the failover target. The Postgres, Patroni, etcd and pgBackRest settings are those of 10.16, shipped as hostkeeper packages (T-S23); they are not keys of this file. Nodes 4 to 6 carry the same `[metadata]`, `[reconcile]` and `[health]` sections and no member.

### 21.2 LATER: three storage nodes and 31 build nodes
**Hardware [D]:** 3 storage nodes of 1 x 6.4 TB U.2 NVMe and 15 x 6 TB SAS HDD; 31 build nodes with local SSD caches. **Hot tier: the NVMe, `hot.replicas = 3`, `min_acks = 2`** (Q2: every storage node holds every hot object; a hot read is served by the least-loaded node without a hop). **Cold tier: `ec = 4+4` at 3/3/2, failure domain node, `reserve_drives = 2`** (Q1). **Metadata store: the three Postgres members run on the three storage nodes, on a 256 GiB partition of each node's NVMe** (xfs, `noatime`; Postgres data, write-ahead log and etcd), with Patroni and the three-member etcd on the same nodes (10.16); the hot tier's usable space shrinks by about 0.28 TB per device for it. The NVMe drives have power-loss protection (the owner's statement; T0-S and the hardware audit verify it and refuse a drive without it), so group commit, `hot.min_acks`, `batch_max` and Postgres's default `synchronous_commit = on` are sized on that assumption and a commit is a fast flash program on two nodes. The members are on a node set that holds no builds, so no CPU weights are needed beyond the units' defaults. The storage node's configuration is the first example of 10.14 (`docs/examples/later-storage.toml`). A build node runs the node cache, the fetch engine, the FUSE front, the S3 front as a caching proxy and the Prepare provider, and no store component; its configuration, with every key of those sections commented, is below. Capacity is in 10.15. A build node carries no `[metadata]` section: it never talks to Postgres; it is a client of the storage nodes and rides out a metadata failover by the client requirement of section 23.

```toml
# docs/examples/later-build.toml: one of the 31 build nodes of LATER; they differ only in addresses.
# Components: node cache, fetch engine, FUSE front, S3 front (caching proxy), Prepare provider.
# No [node] section: a node that runs no store component has no node id.
[client]                              # how tierstore-client finds the storage nodes (10.8)
seeds = ["192.0.2.11:7480", "192.0.2.12:7480", "192.0.2.13:7480"]   # or: membership = "<file>"
token_file = "/etc/tierstore/cluster.token"

[cache]                               # tierstore-cache (sections 5, 7, 8, 11)
root = "/var/lib/tierstore-cache"     # the SSD cache partition (section 11)
budget = "185GiB"                     # section 7
shared_socket = "/run/tierstore/cache.sock"   # the default; FUSE mount processes and other processes attach here (5.4, 23)

[fetch]                               # the fetch engine (section 4); its constants are frozen by T10b

[fuse]                                # tsfuse (5.2); the transport follows fuse.enable_uring, read at startup

[s3]
listen = "127.0.0.1:7481"
backend = "cache"                     # a caching proxy of the storage cluster (section 9)

[prepare]                             # the jobscheduler mount provider (section 6)
socket = "/run/tierstore/provider.sock"
```

### 21.3 Migration from NOW to LATER, without a rewrite
Each step is a membership or configuration change of the same binary; each is reversible until the last.
1. **Bring up the storage nodes dark:** format their drives and the 256 GiB metadata partition of each NVMe (a held script per node), T0-S (which also checks power-loss protection), start the daemons with the LATER storage configuration (its `[metadata]` section lists the three current members), the three nodes added with `store node add` (24.9, R5), which writes their `nodes` and `drives` rows in a new membership epoch (state `new`, then `in`); they need no `membership.toml`, and the file is not edited; the combined nodes stay `in`. They hold no blocks yet, so their indexes are empty and their `LOCATE` answers fall through to the nodes that hold the data (a client walks `rank(K)` past a node that answers `indexing`); each applies the tombstones from Postgres before it leaves `indexing`, and its index fills as the copies of steps 2 to 4 arrive.
2. **Move new writes:** a new membership epoch marks every combined node's hot device and drives **`drain`**: they are no longer placement targets for hot replica sets or new stripes, but they keep serving reads. Replica sets are computed over nodes whose hot device is `in`, so from this epoch every PUT goes to the three storage nodes with `hot.replicas = 3`, and every new stripe is `4+4` on them. (The cluster-wide hot settings, `replicas = 3` and `min_acks = 2`, take effect in the same epoch.)
3. **Drain the hot tier of the combined nodes:** each seals its open segments (the fence and the compare-and-set of 10.3 and 10.16, run over the copies of each segment) and destages every dirty block onto the storage nodes' drives as `4+4` stripes; clean and cached copies are dropped. Nothing is copied to the storage nodes' NVMe: those blocks are cold.
4. **Drain the cold tier of the combined nodes by re-encoding:** for each `4+2` stripe with shards on `drain` drives, a coordinator on a storage node reads its four data shards (one sequential stream per SMR drive), writes four data and four parity shards at 3/3/2 on the storage nodes, commits the new stripe and shard rows in one transaction (the re-encode, move or convert transaction, 10.16 transaction 12, which points the blocks at the new stripe; block ids and offsets are unchanged, so no record id changes and no index entry moves), and then unlinks the six old shards. It is a `background` job at `rebuild.share`, verified like a destage. At today's volume (a few TB of cold data) it is hours [E].
5. **Move the three Postgres members to the storage nodes** (T-S12), with no metadata outage beyond one switchover:
   1. On each storage node, Patroni and etcd are started from the same packages and the new Postgres member is added to the running cluster (Patroni member add: it takes a base backup from the primary and streams; it is an asynchronous standby while it catches up). The cluster now has six Postgres members and the strict synchronous standby is unchanged (the transient sets of the member verbs, 10.16). etcd members are added one at a time (never more than one joining at once, so the quorum is never lost), and each new member is waited on until healthy.
   2. When the three new members are caught up and healthy (replication lag at zero, checked by the verb), one planned **switchover** moves the primary to a member on a storage node, and the synchronous standby follows to another. This is the only moment of unavailability: a few seconds of `503` with `Retry-After`, inside what every client must tolerate (section 23; the CAS service's part is T-K1b, which lands before the cutover); immutable-bucket PUT and GET continue throughout. The configuration of every node (`metadata.hosts`) is changed in a rolling restart to list the new members first; the old list keeps working until the old members are removed, because the client follows the primary by `target_session_attrs=read-write`.
   3. The three old members are removed one at a time (Patroni member remove, then the matching etcd member removal), with a check after each that a synchronous standby exists and that a majority of etcd is healthy; the combined nodes' metadata partitions are then freed. The final cluster is three members on the three storage nodes.
   4. A fresh backup, a verification restore into a scratch cluster and `metadata fsck` in dry-run on it close the step, because it changes where every row lives. The pgBackRest stanza follows the primary; the off-box repository is unchanged.
6. **Remove the store components:** when no data remains on `drain` devices (`cold_stripes{...}` and `hot_dirty_bytes` on the combined nodes at zero), a final epoch marks the combined nodes' hot devices and drives `out` and drops them from the document, and each combined node's configuration (the NOW example's name) loses `[node]`, `[store]`, `[hot]`, `[cold]`, `[peer]`, `[metadata]`, `[reconcile]`, `[health]` and their settings sections (`[destage]`, `[rebuild]`, `[drive]` and any `[index]`, `[promote]`, `[gc]`), gains `[client]` with the storage nodes as seeds, and switches `s3.backend` to `"cache"`: it is now `docs/examples/later-build.toml`, one of the 31 build nodes. Their SMR drives are then freed.
**Rollback:** until step 6, marking the combined nodes `in` again restores them as placement targets; stripes already moved stay valid where they are, because a stripe's code and placement are in its rows and its shard footers; until step 5.3 the old Postgres members are still members, and a switchover back restores the old primary. **Tests:** the migration is a scripted simulation in section 14 (the membership epochs above on fake nodes, and the metadata model of the simulator with a member set that changes by add, switchover and remove: reads succeed at every step, nothing is placed on `drain` devices, every stripe ends `4+4` on the storage nodes, no index entry or record id changes during the re-encode, no acknowledged metadata commit is lost across the move, and the only metadata unavailability is the one switchover).

## 22. Evidence: what published data settles, and what our experiments decide
The problems this design solves are largely solved elsewhere; where the published data is clear this design follows it, and where it is not, the experiment that decides it is named.

| Choice | Settled by | Or decided by |
|---|---|---|
| Small objects appended to large append-only files with an in-memory or embedded index; one disk operation per small read | Haystack (OSDI 2010) | C-5 (group-commit batch sizes), C-9 (index) |
| Seal at about 1 GB, then erasure-code lazily; hot copies dropped after the code is durable | f4 (OSDI 2014), Windows Azure Storage (SOSP 2011; seal at about 1 GB per the LRC paper) | C-5 destage rate, C-10 fill efficiency |
| Contiguous data blocks, so a healthy read touches one drive; parity read only on failure | f4; HDFS-7285 and HDFS-8030 (striping exists for small files and parallel bandwidth, which the hot tier's packing already provides) | C-2 (one drive streaming whole packs), C-3 |
| Range-granular degraded read; hedged reconstruction when a read is slow | f4 (reconstruct only the requested range); Windows Azure Storage / LRC (parallel reconstruction of slow reads) | C-11 and the 17.2 one-node-down pass bar |
| Code width bounded by failure domains; no code below 1.5x survives a node on three nodes | LRC (ATC 2012: local groups need many domains); Ceph EC profiles (failure domain as a profile property); arithmetic of 10.4 | none needed |
| Per-shard checksums, scrub, recompute-and-rewrite repair, prioritized re-replication | GFS (SOSP 2003), Backblaze Vaults, Ceph scrub | C-11 scrub rate and foreground cost |
| Redundancy chosen per tier by configuration, declustered repair with spread spare space | Colossus (2021 post: client-chosen encodings, custodians), f4 rebuilders, Backblaze | C-11 rebuild rate at `rebuild.share` |
| Ranged GETs of 8 to 16 MiB and many concurrent requests to saturate a storage service | Durner, Leis, Neumann (VLDB 2023), AWS S3 guidelines | C-1, C-4 (part size P and first-part size on our drives) |
| One or two sequential streams per spindle, long slices, prefetch depth matched to seek cost | Li, Shen, Papathanasiou (EuroSys 2007, competitive prefetching); Iyer and Druschel (SOSP 2001, anticipatory scheduling); Schindler et al. (FAST 2002); Ruemmler and Wilkes (1994) | C-2, C-2b, C-8 (streams, chunk, slice, write share, slot release) |
| HDD efficiency by request size on our drives | T0 [M]: 119 MB/s sequential, about 43 MB/s (36%) at 1 MiB scattered on an ST8000DM004 | T0-S on the SAS drives |
| Order of the writes of one key, and a tombstone that kills exactly one stored record | the commit order of one replicated database (a conditional upsert on one row is the textbook compare-and-set); a record id `(block_id, offset)` as identity instead of a clock | the simulation tests of section 14 (a re-PUT after a delete, a delete racing a relocate) |
| One agreed sealed length under partitions | fencing by epoch (a replica refuses batches of an older epoch; the idea of fencing tokens and of epoch-numbered logs as in Raft and Chain Replication) plus one compare-and-set on a row of the replicated metadata store | the partition simulation of section 14 |
| Synchronous replication: a commit acknowledged by two nodes is not lost when the primary fails | [K] PostgreSQL documentation (`synchronous_commit`, `synchronous_standby_names`: a transaction waits for the standby's flush) and Patroni's synchronous mode (strict mode refuses to commit without a synchronous standby, and promotes only a standby that was synchronous) | T-S15 drill "kill the primary": every transaction acknowledged before the kill is present on the new primary, and with the standby also lost commits stop (503) instead of being acknowledged on one copy; the simulator's Postgres model checks the same with and without an asynchronous tail |
| Failover by a distributed lease, with a watchdog, within about 30 s | [K] Patroni documentation (leader key in the etcd store with `ttl`, `loop_wait`, `retry_timeout`; watchdog demotes or resets a primary that cannot renew) and etcd documentation (low `fsync` latency; election and heartbeat timeouts); the values `ttl: 20`, `loop_wait: 5`, `retry_timeout: 5` and `failsafe_mode: true` are read from the documentation and T-S23 confirms them against the pinned release | T-S23 and T-S15 drills on the farm and, later, on the storage nodes: time from killing the primary (and from a node power-off, and from a partition of the primary) to the first committed transaction on the new primary, measured under build load on NOW; the target of at most 30 s is [I] until then |
| Point-in-time recovery to any second of the last 30 days, off-box | [K] pgBackRest documentation (full, differential and incremental backups, continuous WAL archiving, `archive_timeout`, point-in-time restore) | the restore drill of T-S23, once before production and then quarterly: restore to a chosen second in a scratch cluster, measure the restore time and the WAL lost, run `metadata fsck`, and check that a node refuses to run against the restored database until the incarnation is raised (T-S24) |
| Metadata rows and the `heads` table at 1e8 keys | arithmetic of 10.16 (about 170 bytes per row with its index [I]); vendor and community sizing of Postgres at that scale is not for this workload | the load test of T-S22: 1e8 head rows, point-read and upsert latency on the primary and a prefix LIST, on the NOW partition and on the LATER one |
| sha256 cost on build nodes without SHA-NI | T0 [M]: about 290 MB/s per core with `ring`, 150 MB/s with `sha2` | T-FMT (blake3 cells, if ever) |
| Loop-device cache | T0 [M]: `O_DIRECT` absorbed by the page cache, buffered writers stalled more than 20 s | T0 B on the partition |
| Hot-tier group-commit latency, PLP, preallocation | not published for our devices; the owner states that the LATER NVMe drives have power-loss protection | T0-S NVMe items and the hardware audit verify it (a drive without it is refused); NOW measures honest `fdatasync` and metadata commit latency on the SATA SSD |
| Promotion thresholds, expiry-class grouping, GC rewrite threshold | no published data for this workload | C-3b, C-10 |
| Embedded index store at 1e8 keys (a derived cache, rebuildable from trailers, `blk/*.idx` and the tombstones in Postgres) | vendor benchmarks only | C-9, T-S3 |
| SMR behaviour under sealed-shard churn | drive-managed SMR behaviour is vendor-specific | C-5b (runs in full on NOW) |
| FUSE transport (`/dev/fuse` clones, io_uring) | kernel documentation; our baselines (2,769 and 28,199 opens/s) | 17.1 |
| Prefetch order for a lazily fetched tree | eStargz (prioritized files and a landmark, recorded by an optimize run at image build time) and Nydus (a prefetch table in the bootstrap, from build-time hints): both decide the order at build time, not from runs | 17.3 decides whether a publish-time hint (6.1) is worth building |
| Hedged requests at about the p95 cut tail latency for about 5% extra load | Dean and Barroso, "The Tail at Scale", CACM 2013 | C-3 (hedge to the other replica against a loaded drive), T10b |
