| 1 MiB cells | arithmetic of section 2 (digest size, hash rate [M], drive rate [I], FUSE read size [K]) | T-F0 (`max_pages`), T10b first byte |

## 23. The client as libraries, the daemon as a thin host [D]
**Why.** The read path of sections 2 to 9 is useful outside the daemon: a tool that reads one file of a tree, a benchmark that measures the storage tier without FUSE, a scheduler that prefetches in its own process, later a Python or TypeScript program. So the client is three layered crates, each usable alone, and the daemon is one program built on them with nothing of its own on the read path.

| Crate | What it is | What it is not | Depends on |
|---|---|---|---|
| **`tierstore-client`** | Membership (seeds or file, epoch following; 10.8); batched `LOCATE` with single flight per key (10.6); ranged part fetch: the part grid, ramp and first piece (4.4), the queue with classes, DRR and ranks (4.5), stall detection, retries, hedges, the pinned generation and `If-Match` (4.6); per-cell verification with the hash pool (3.3, 4.7); the budget (4.3); the definitive-miss rule and the lost outcome (4.6, 10.6). Its output is verified cells delivered into the caller's buffers. | no cache, no files on disk, no state page, no daemon, no FUSE; it does not dedupe two callers' fetches of one pack (that is the cache's single flight). | `tierstore-fetch-api`, `tierstore-store-api` (protocol types), `tierstore-format` |
| **`tierstore-cache`** | The local cache: the entry store (sparse files, cell states, the sealed state memfd on Linux and the in-process store on macOS; 2, 5.1, 5.6), single flight and claims (`PackFill`, `ensure`, `wait`; 4.1, 4.2), holds, eviction and admission (7), the journal and restart (8). In-process by default; **shareable across processes** on Linux through the existing mechanism of 5.1 and 5.4: one owning process holds the `flock`s and serves `open-pack`, `wait`, `ensure` and `release` on a unix socket (the server side, T5), and any other process attaches as a holder (the client side, T6), maps the state page read-only and reads pack bytes from the passed data fd with the reader protocol of 5.1. | no FUSE, no tree format, no network code of its own: claimed cells are fetched by `tierstore-client` | `tierstore-client`, `tierstore-fetch-api` |
| **`tierstore-tree`** | Open an L2 tree list (3; from the cache, or from bytes the client fetched) and read by path: `lookup`, `read_dir`, `stat`, `read_link`, `read(file, offset, buf)`, from the cache (an in-process or attached `tierstore-cache`) or **straight from the client** (verified cells into the caller's buffer, no entry on disk); the mapped-image serving of 5.3 (binary search of sorted directory records, WILLNEED, optional `mlock`); the planner of section 6 (T7, a pure function). | no FUSE and no NFS: the fronts are adapters on it | `tierstore-client`; `tierstore-cache` behind the default cargo feature `cache`, so a tool can link the tree reader with the client alone |

**`tierstore-meta` is not a client library.** The crate that talks to Postgres (the schema, the migrate verb, the failover-aware idempotent client, the incarnation check, the bucket table and the `heads` access; 10.16, T-S22) is a **store-side** crate: only the storage nodes' components (the seal, destage, repair, the reconciler, the front's DELETE and mutable-bucket paths, `metadata fsck`) link it. A build node, the FUSE front, Prepare, a tool reading a tree and the three crates above never link it and never hold database credentials; they reach metadata only through the S3 front and the peer protocol of the storage nodes. It has no public API promise (it changes with the schema, additively, by the migrate verb), and the layering check of T-L also fails when a client-side crate or a front depends on it.

**The client requirement during a metadata failover.** The metadata store fails over in up to about 30 s (10.16), and during it every operation that needs it answers `503` with `Retry-After`: DELETE, every operation on a mutable bucket, and any other path that commits (immutable-bucket PUT and GET do not need it and continue). Every client of the S3 front or of the peer protocol therefore must:
1. treat `503` with `Retry-After` as retriable, and keep retrying for at least 60 s (twice the failover target) with jittered backoff that honours `Retry-After`;
2. never read a `503` as absence: it is not `404`, not a miss and not a lost object;
3. retry idempotently: a PUT of the same bytes and a DELETE are safe to repeat (the front records an operation id, 10.16, and an immutable-bucket create of the same generation answers `200`), and a conditional request is repeated with the same condition.
**`tierstore-client` implements it itself** (module `retry`): its retry window is a constructor option with a default of 60 s, its backoff is jittered and starts from `Retry-After`, and a request that is still refused when the window ends returns `ClientError::Unavailable` (a named variant that carries the last `Retry-After`; never `NotFound` or `Lost`); a timeout, a connection reset or a transport error on a metadata-dependent operation is retried exactly like a `503`, idempotently with the same operation id inside the same window, and ends the same way; a per-request read timeout should be at least `metadata.retry_window` plus a margin (60 s or more) or follow that rule. The library-mode suite below tests it against the fake storage node. The other clients are bound by the same text: the CAS service (the first client; its change is its own task outside this repository, and it deletes only unreferenced packs, so a `503` on DELETE is retried later), the jobscheduler and any tool or program that speaks the S3 front, and a Python or TypeScript binding later; a client built without the library follows the three rules above or is not supported.

**The daemon (`tierstore`) is a host.** It reads the configuration (10.14), starts the enabled components of 10.1 and wires them: the FUSE front (`TreeFs`, an adapter of `tierstore-tree` onto `tsfuse::Fs`: opcodes, parked replies, NO_OPEN, splice; each mount runs in a mount process of the same binary that attaches to the node-wide shared cache through `cache.shared_socket`, as 5.1 and 5.4 describe), the NFS front (the same adapter onto the NFS handlers), a node-wide shared `tierstore-cache` with its socket, the S3 front (section 9's routing of large objects into the cache's engine), the Prepare provider (the jobscheduler provider protocol onto `tierstore-tree`'s planner and the cache's `ensure`), and, when enabled, the store components of section 10, unchanged by this section. **Every read the fronts and Prepare make goes through the public API of the three crates**; the daemon has no private fetch path, no second cache and no second tree reader. A layering check in `cargo xtask` (T-L) fails when a front or the Prepare provider of the daemon imports `tierstore-store-api`'s peer protocol, a non-public module of the three crates, or the entry store directly, and its negative case is a fixture module that does each of the three and must be refused.

**Who uses the crates.**
- **The daemon:** all three, as above.
- **jobscheduler, Prepare in-process:** a jobscheduler agent on a build node links `tierstore-tree` and attaches to the node-wide shared cache (`tierstore-cache`, client side), and runs the planner and `ensure` in its own process at job assignment, without the provider round trip; the daemon's Prepare provider is the same library code behind the provider socket, for agents that do not link it. Both take the same holds (6, item 7).
- **The build system (the farm's build executor):** reading an action's input or output tree without a mount, through `tierstore-tree` with the client alone. Its own rules admit no dependency on code of ours today, so it adopts the crates only after those rules name them as allowed; until then it reads through the mount, and nothing in this design depends on it linking them.
- **Tools:** `tierstore verify` (re-derives per-file sha256 from cells, 3.4), `tierstore store audit` (10.8), and a `tierstore tree cat <tree> <path>` verb that reads one file through the client with no daemon.
- **Benchmarks:** T-B5 and T0-S read through `tierstore-client` directly, so the storage tier is measured without FUSE or the cache in the way; the FUSE benchmarks (17.1) keep their harness.
- **Later, Python and TypeScript bindings** over `tierstore-tree` and `tierstore-client` (not designed here; the stable API below is what makes them possible).

**A stable, documented, versioned API.** Each crate has its own version (not the workspace's): 0.x from T-L, while the tasks of section 18 fill it, and 1.0.0 when T10b has frozen the constants and T-F8 serves through it; from 1.0.0, within a major version its public API changes additively only (a new item, a new optional field behind a constructor, never a removed, renamed or retyped item), and a breaking change is a new major version with a note in this document. Every public item is documented (`#![deny(missing_docs)]`), each crate's README shows the use alone (client only; cache with a client; tree straight from the client), and errors are `thiserror` enums with context (`ClientError`, `CacheError`, `TreeError`; `MembershipUnavailable`, `ListVersionUnsupported`, `Unavailable`, `Lost` and `NotFound` are named variants, never strings). Each crate carries an **API test** (`tests/api_v1.rs`) that names every public item of its major version with its full signature, so a removal, rename or retype fails to compile; a PR that adds an item adds it to the test. The traits of `tierstore-fetch-api` (`Entry`, `EntryStore`, `Engine`, `Budget`, `Verifier`, `Upstream`, `ListL2`) stay the interfaces the crates implement and the fakes stand in for; the crates' own APIs are the concrete types callers use. The interface crates of T-I (`tierstore-fetch-api`, `tierstore-store-api`, `tierstore-format`) are not part of this promise: they change only through a freeze delta of section 18 (such as T-I2), with its wire and format changes additive (B8 of AGENTS.md).

**Library-mode test suite (T-L).** Behaviour tests that run the crates with no daemon, against the fake storage node of `tierstore-store-api` on a loopback listener at port 0: the client alone fetches and verifies a pack and reads a `digest=none` object; the client bootstraps from seeds (one seed down, one answering `404`, a wrong token) and follows a higher epoch; a DELETE that the fake answers `503` with `Retry-After` for 30 s (on a fake clock, not real time) succeeds when the window allows it, is retried with the same operation, and returns `Unavailable` (never `NotFound`) when the fake keeps refusing past the window; the cache in-process fills, holds, evicts and restarts with the client; a second process attaches to a shared cache, reads through the state page and survives the owner's restart (the epoch and reconnect rules of 5.4); the tree reads every file of a test tree by path from the cache and straight from the client, with byte-for-byte equal results; the planner ranks a test tree shared packs first, then in tree order. These are rows of section 14.

**Mapping of the client-side tasks onto the crates.** The tasks keep their scope and tests (section 18); this is where their code lives.

| Task | Crate and module |
|---|---|
| T1 entry store (Linux) | `tierstore-cache` (`entry::linux`) |
| T-M1 in-process entry store; NFS handlers | `tierstore-cache` (`entry::inproc`); the NFS adapter in the daemon on `tierstore-tree` |
| T1b journal and snapshot | `tierstore-cache` (`journal`) |
| T2 hash pool | `tierstore-client` (`verify`) |
| T3 fetch engine | split: the part queue, grid and ramp, demand sub-ranges, stall retries, hedges, pinned generation and `If-Match`, the `LOCATE` step and the lost outcome in `tierstore-client` (`fetch`); single flight, claims and the `Engine` implementation (`ensure`, `wait`) in `tierstore-cache` (`fill`) |
| T4 budget | `tierstore-client` (`budget`) |
| T5 server side of the mount protocol | `tierstore-cache` (`share::server`); the daemon only binds the socket |
| T6 mount client | `tierstore-cache` (`share::client`), used by mount processes and by any process attaching to the shared cache |
| T7 Prepare planner | `tierstore-tree` (`plan`) |
| T8 eviction and admission | `tierstore-cache` (`evict`, `admit`) |
| T9 metrics | each crate registers its metrics on a registry handle its constructor takes (no global registry); the daemon exports them |
| T-F8 tree `Fs` on `tsfuse` | split: path lookup, image serving and the read path with the post-read recheck in `tierstore-tree` (`Tree`); the `tsfuse::Fs` adapter (`TreeFs`) in the daemon |
| T12 S3-front integration | the daemon, on `tierstore-cache` |
| T-S6 `StoreUpstream` (the client side of the peer protocol) | `tierstore-client` (`store`); the peer service stays with the store components |
| T-SB seed bootstrap | `tierstore-client` (`membership`); the route in the peer service (T-S6) |
| T-S22 metadata schema and client | `tierstore-meta`, a store-side crate linked by the storage components only (not a client library) |
| The `503` retry rule (section 23, 10.9) | `tierstore-client` (`retry`) |

## 24. Operations and repair [D]
This section is the operations and repair design: who decides that hardware is gone, what is repaired in which order, how a returning node or drive is reconciled, how capacity is added, what the operator runs, and the runbooks. It was written after a study of Ceph, Swift, Dynamo and Cassandra, HDFS, GFS, f4, Backblaze, MinIO and SeaweedFS and an adversarial failure review; every blocker the review named is resolved in the text below and listed in 24.14. All block-level state it speaks of (the health rows, the block, stripe and shard rows, the repair queue, the membership) lives in the replicated metadata store, a dedicated Postgres cluster on the storage nodes (10.16); this section says what the operations do with it, and 10.16 says how it is run. Labels as elsewhere: [D] decided here, [E] arithmetic, [I] inference that a named test or measurement settles. Constants are [I] until T0-S and the simulator (24.13) tune them.

**Decisions.**
- **D1. The block is the placement group.** The unit of placement, repair, audit and rebalance is the recorded block (a hot segment or large object, a cold stripe). No placement groups are layered over keys, and `rank(K)` is a routing hint and tie-break, never a correctness input (24.1).
- **D2. Health facts are rows of the metadata store.** Obligation-changing facts (lost, returned, maintenance, flapping) are rows of the `health` table, and a fact is one transaction; the database orders facts, so no voter set, no per-fact agreement and no consensus code of ours exists (24.2).
- **D3. Two clocks and a damper.** Short detection only reroutes reads; a long, explicit `lost` decision, recorded as a row, moves a node's repair from "margin-driven" to "rebuild everything"; flap damping is a preference with a floor (24.3). Durations of decisions are measured by the database clock inside transactions, and by monotonic clocks on nodes.
- **D4. A reconciler** compares desired redundancy with the actual rows, keeps a persistent repair queue ordered by remaining margin, and has executors that lease jobs and commit each result as one conditional transaction, with **one throttle budget shared with scrub** (24.5).
- **D5. Degraded placement is a recorded exception with a debt counter** (`layout_debt`), reverted by a mover or by adopting the returning node's copy (24.4, 24.5).
- **D6. Adding capacity never moves existing data** unless an operator applies a rebalance (24.8).
- **D7. Ten audited invariants**, each with a metric, a check and an alert (24.10).
- **D8. Operator verbs are generated from the operator API**, destructive ones dry-run by default and refuse unsafe states (24.11).
- **D9. A deterministic cluster simulator is the primary test**, with a model of the metadata store in it; farm drills confirm what it cannot (24.13).

**The three-node fact (stated once, applies everywhere).** The metadata store has three members on three storage nodes (primary, one synchronous standby, one more standby, 10.16), and a commit is acknowledged only when it is on the primary and the synchronous standby. While one node is down, **every seal, every stripe commit (every repair commit), every health fact and every mutable-bucket write still commits, on the two surviving members**, provided the survivors are the primary and a standby that is (or is made, within seconds, by Patroni) synchronous. Two things follow. First, when the node that is down held the primary, or the synchronous standby, commits pause for a failover or a synchronous-standby change, at most about 30 seconds as a target [I, drill 13 measures], and the front answers `503` with `Retry-After` meanwhile (every client tolerates this, section 23). Second, **with two nodes down, or with one node down and one survivor unable to commit (a stalled disk under its Postgres member), nothing commits**: this is Q5's default outcome for two nodes lost, no worse. The metric `repair_stalled_on_metadata` (jobs exist and no transaction has committed for 5 minutes) pages. After a node is marked `out`, the two remaining nodes read every `4+4` stripe at margin 0, **and one more node loss stops every metadata commit as well as leaving every stripe at margin 0**. Rebuilding onto two nodes is an interim state: **a fourth storage node is the fix** for sustained operation after a node loss (the Postgres member of the lost node is rebuilt on the replacement or on the fourth node, R12).

### 24.1 Placement: the block, not the key
- **Where key K lives:** in the index, `K -> (record id, length, generation)` where a record id is `(block id, offset)` (10.2), on every node that indexes K (immutable buckets); in a mutable bucket the current record id of K is the row `heads(bucket, K)` in the metadata store, and the node index holds nothing authoritative for it.
- **Where block B lives:** the rows of the metadata store: hot, `blocks` and `hot_copies` (the replica set recorded when the segment opened, changed only by a committed re-replication transaction); cold, the `stripes` and `shards` rows, which are the stripe descriptor (each shard's footer carries it too, 10.2). Reads always use the recorded location. A node's local block table is a **cache** of these rows, refreshed on a miss, on a failed compare-and-set and by a poll every `metadata.tomb_poll` (2 s), never by a push and never an authority.
- **What rendezvous still does:** choose among eligible live candidates at decision time, never locate existing data: (1) which node a client asks first for `LOCATE` (10.6); (2) the targets of new segments, new shards and repair writes (weighted by free bytes over `in`, not-`flapping`, below-fill-limit candidates, 10.4); (3) the preferred executor of a repair job without a fixed target: `rank(job subject)` among live nodes, the next rank taking it when the first has not leased it within `reconcile.executor_wait` (30 s), which gives every job a deterministic owner without an election. The reconciler's own single holder is chosen by the lease row (24.5), not by rank.
- **Why no placement groups.** A placement group exists to bound the number of independent decisions; the 1 GiB block already does: at the capacity guard (142.8 TB raw, a case of its own, smaller than the 85% fill limit that 10.2 sizes the tables on) LATER's cold tier is about 133,000 shard files, about 16,600 `4+4` stripes, about 2,950 shards per drive [E], the same order as the per-device group counts Ceph recommends, and the hot tier has at most tens to hundreds of segments.
- **The versioned map.** One document carries a version: the **membership document** (operator intent: nodes, drives, their states and domains; epoch `M`, 10.8), rendered from the `nodes` and `drives` rows at the epoch of the newest `membership` row. Runtime facts (24.2) are rows of `health` and carry no epoch of their own: a fact never changes the map, it is read when a decision is made. Peer messages carry the membership epoch (`x-ts-mem-epoch`); a receiver that sees a higher epoch pulls the document from the metadata store (or from the peer when the store is unreachable) before it acts. **Epoch refusals are by content, not by number:** a placement (a new segment, shard or repair target) planned under an older document is refused (`409 epoch-behind` with the diff) only when the difference changes the eligibility of that item's target or sources; an unrelated change (another node's state, another drive's `lost`) never restarts in-flight work. Reads are never refused for epoch skew.
- **Recorded exceptions** are fields of the stripe and block rows: `layout_debt` (the stripe sits in a degraded layout, 24.5), `move_to` (a pending move) and `degraded_since`. The recorded location always serves reads.
- **Audit instead of group digests.** No per-group digest comparison between nodes exists: the rows have one copy, in the metadata store, and the question "do the rows agree with the bytes on disk" is answered by the audit of 24.10 (invariants I9 and I10), whose full form is `metadata fsck` in dry-run mode. Bytes are the scrubber's (24.7).

### 24.2 States and health rows
**Granularity.** State is per component: a node's daemon, its hot device, and each of its drives. A drive's identity is a UUID written into its `DRIVE` file at format time and mounted by UUID, so renames and slot moves are recognised. A node's identity is `node.id` plus a **node incarnation**: a random 64-bit value written into the `NODE` file, kept across restarts and regenerated when the node starts on a fresh hot device (T-S2 adds it to the identity file; the `nodes` row records the incarnation last seen, `incarnation_seen`). The node incarnation is a different thing from the **metadata incarnation** of 10.16 (one number for the whole store, raised by a restore); this section says "incarnation" for the first and "metadata incarnation" for the second.

**States added to 10.8.**

| State | Meaning | Decided by | Reads | New placements | Repair effect |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `new` (`nodes.admin_state = new`) | in the membership document, no data, not yet verified | owner (format check, then an 8 GiB write-and-verify burn) | no | no | none; becomes `up` |
| `missing` (drive) | absent at boot or gone from the bus | owner | not asked | no | lost after `rebuild.drive_missing_after` (1 h); a returning drive clears it |
| `flapping` | the damper tripped (24.3) | each viewer, deterministically from recorded transitions; a `health` row after 1 h | last in preference | not preferred, with a floor (24.3) | its returned-processing is held |
| `maintenance` | the operator said "away on purpose until T" | `health` row | as `down` | no | the lost budget does not run inside the window; at T the node is `down` from T |
| `lost` | repair obligations for every block on this component are active | `health` row | not asked | no | every block listing it is queued for full rebuild |

`indexing` (10.8) keeps its meaning for client traffic only. Everything a node needs in order to take part in sealing, replication and repair is served in `indexing`, independent of the index rebuild: `fence`, replication batches, hot restart convergence, `tomb-apply` (queued until the index can take it), executor work on shards. So a node that restarts takes part at once and a power event does not wait for index rebuilds before any seal can commit (the seal's commit is a Postgres transaction, 10.3).

**Health rows.** The `health` table holds one row per fact: `(subject_kind node|drive, subject_id, fact up|missing|maintenance|flapping|lost|returned, since, until, flap_score, reporter, row_version)`. A subject is a node (with its incarnation) or a drive UUID. A subject's state is its open row (`until` null) of the most severe fact, and every change is **one transaction** that closes a row, opens another and writes an `audit` row; `since` and `until` come from the database clock (`now()` inside the transaction), so durations of a fact are never computed from two nodes' clocks; the lease checks of 24.5 are the exception to `now()`: they use `clock_timestamp()` inside the transaction, because `now()` is the transaction's start time. Updating a row is a compare-and-set on `(subject, row_version)`, so two writers of the same fact cannot both succeed and a retry of an ambiguous commit finds its `op_id` (10.16). Facts about different subjects commute. The table is small (tens of rows a year plus the transitions of the trailing 48 h, 24.3). There is no ledger, no ledger epoch, no voter set and no single-decree agreement: the metadata store orders the facts, and its own replication (10.16) is the only agreement protocol in the system.

- **Who writes which row.**
  - A node writes `missing` for a peer component its own view has held unavailable without interruption for `health.report_after` (30 s), naming itself as `reporter`, and closes the row (`up`) when it sees the component serve again. The reconciler (24.5) acts on a `missing` row only when its own view agrees or a second reporter (not the subject) has written it, so a node that is partitioned from its peers but can reach Postgres cannot declare the others gone alone.
  - The **reconciler** (24.5) writes `lost`, `flapping` (after the damper has held for 1 h), `returned`, `maintenance-end` and the clearing of `flapping`, because these follow timers and guards (24.3) it evaluates in one place under its lease. A `lost` row is refused by the transaction itself unless the subject's open row is `missing` (or `failed` for a drive) with `since` older than the timer, evaluated by the database clock inside the transaction, and unless the guards of 24.3 hold at that moment.
  - The operator verbs write `maintenance` and `lost` (`store node out`, `store drive fail`) in the same way, with the `--reason` in the `audit` row.
- **A partitioned minority.** A node that cannot reach the metadata store's primary commits nothing, so it writes no fact; and it cannot acknowledge `min_acks = 2` hot writes if it is on the small side of a partition (the replicas it needs are on the other side), so it acknowledges no write and fences itself by refusing to roll; when it heals it reads the rows about itself and rejoins by 24.4. A deleted-key and stale-read statement for such a node is in 24.4.
- **If the store cannot commit** (two members away, or a failover in progress), no fact is written and nothing is declared: no `lost`, no `returned`, no new maintenance window. Detection still reroutes reads, hot and cold data keep serving, and the margin-triggered repair that needs only data copies (24.5) continues until its commit; the fallback is that the operator waits for the store (R11, R16). The health rows are an optimisation of decisions, never a safety dependency of data: a seal or a stripe commit is its own transaction against its own rows.

### 24.3 Timers, damping and guards
**Detection (per viewer; reroutes only).** As 10.8: storage nodes judge peers from their own traffic and `x-ts-load`, and ping only an idle peer (10.6); `down` after 3 silent intervals (6 s); a client judges only from its own requests. `stalled` past `stall_lost_after` (30 min) becomes `failed`.

**Decision (health rows).**

| Timer | Default | Effect |
|---|---|---|
| `rebuild.drive_delay` | 10 min | a `failed` drive becomes `lost` |
| `rebuild.drive_missing_after` | 1 h | a `missing` drive becomes `lost` |
| `rebuild.node_down_after` | 24 h within a trailing 48 h, cumulative | a node becomes `lost`, if the guards pass |
| `maintenance.max` | 24 h | cap on a maintenance window |

A drive is lost after minutes and a node after a day because a `failed` drive is a definite fact (the owner wrote `FAILED`) and its declustered rebuild takes about 2 hours, while a node that is down is most often a crash and reboot, and a node's loss costs 47.6 TB of writes and 71.4 TB of reads at the guard (10.4). **This timer no longer gates repair of exposed data: margin-triggered repair (24.5) starts at once for every block below its threshold, whatever the timer says.** The `lost` row decides only whether the rest of the node's blocks, those still at a safe margin, are rebuilt too.

**Clock rule [D].** Every duration that decides anything is measured by exactly one of two clocks. **Inside a transaction:** the database's clock (`now()` of the metadata primary): `since` and `until` of health rows, the reconciler lease, job leases, `purge_after` of trash, `orphan_age`. **On a node:** the monotonic clock (detection intervals, the damper's windows, hedging, local timeouts). Wall-clock time of a node is used only for `written_ms` (informational, 10.2) and logs; no ordering, no lease and no timer depends on it, and a node's skew against the database is measured at every transaction and alerted (24.15, clocks). Time-of-day jumps on a node therefore change nothing; a failover of the metadata primary moves the database clock to the new primary's clock, so a duration spanning a failover is bounded by the two primaries' skew (alerted at `clock.max_skew`, 1 s) [I].

**Unavailability budget.** A node is eligible for `lost` when its **cumulative** unavailable time in the trailing 48 h reaches `node_down_after`, or it has been continuously unavailable that long. The history it is computed from is the `missing` and `maintenance` rows of the node (opened and closed by transactions, so their `since` and `until` are on the database clock), kept at least 48 h after closing; a viewer's restart or a power event therefore does not reset it. The node event history on each storage node's NVMe (what `store node get` prints with the reasons) is a local diary and is not an input of the decision. Time inside a declared maintenance window does not count. A point-in-time restore of the store (R14) loses the rows written after the restore point, so budgets restart from the restored rows; this only delays a `lost` row, never exposed data (margin-triggered repair does not read it).

**Flap damper (a preference with a floor).** Per component a score `s`: +1 per unannounced down transition (a clean shutdown announces itself), halving every hour; **downs of two or more nodes within one minute count once** (a correlated event, such as power, is not flapping). `s >= 2` sets `flapping`: last in read preference, not preferred as a hot replica or shard target, returned-processing held. It clears when `s < 1` and the node has been `up` for its probation (15 min after an unannounced restart, 60 s after an announced one; **probation is skipped after a correlated event**). The score is computed by each viewer from the transitions it saw (deterministically from the recorded transition history, so viewers agree), and the `flap_score` column records the reconciler's value for display. **Floor:** if excluding `flapping` or probation nodes would leave fewer eligible nodes than `hot.min_acks` (hot) or than the stripe invariant needs (cold), the exclusion is ignored for that decision; the damper never makes writes fail. `flapping` for 1 h raises a ticket and writes a `flapping` row; **`flapping` for 6 h pages `NodeDownNeedsDecision`**, because on three nodes there is no automatic exit (R4). A writer does not seal-and-roll on a `replica_stall` of a replica that has been `suspect` more than twice in the last hour; it waits `put_copy_stall` instead, so a flapping replica does not fragment segments every few minutes.

**Guards against self-made storms.**
1. At most one node is auto-declared `lost` at a time; a second needs the operator.
2. `lost` for a node is written only if `ok-to-out` holds: the survivors can hold the rebuilt shards **below `rebuild.auto_ceiling` (default 0.85), leaving room for one further drive's rebuild**, the resulting layout satisfies `m >= max_shards_per_domain`, no stripe is already unreadable, and the metadata store keeps two healthy members after the node's Postgres member (if it holds one) is moved or dropped (R6, R12). Otherwise nothing is automatic and the page says why.
3. Auto declarations pause while any survivor drive is `failed` and not yet rebuilt.
4. **Burst guard:** if more than 2 drives, or 20% of one node's drives, fail within 10 minutes (an HBA, expander or backplane fault), their `lost` rows are held, the node is treated as `suspect` as a whole, and the operator decides (R9). Drive-level declarations never become a node loss through the side door.
5. `rebuild.node_down_after = 0` means operator-only.

Alerts while a node is down: at +0 `NodeDown` (info, with the count of stripes at margin <= 1); +10 min ticket; +1 h `NodeDownLong` page; +4 h `NodeDownNeedsDecision` page naming the two decisions (wait for return, or R6).

### 24.4 A returning node or drive
Cold shards are immutable and deterministic (a data shard is the block's bytes; parity is a fixed function of the stripe), so a shard whose blake3 matches its `shards` row is valid forever; the only staleness is **stripe membership** (a shard of a stripe freed while the component was away must never come back). Hot segments are fenced by epoch, committed length, running crc and the sealed row (10.3); large objects are immutable files named by generation.

On start, and when a drive reappears, the owner sends a **holdings report** while `indexing`: it lists every hot segment, large object and cold shard it holds and compares each with the rows of the metadata store (`blocks`, `hot_copies`, `stripes`, `shards`), which the primary serves and a caching peer can serve for reads of rows that never change:
- **Known and consistent:** adopted. Every shard file carries its **last-verified time**, persisted in its footer's companion record; a shard verified by scrub within `cold.scrub_period` before the component left, whose size and footer match its row, **stays verified**; any other returning shard gets a cheap footer and size check at once and joins the normal scrub order, not a front-of-queue full read. So after a reboot the verified margin (the series the alerts read) returns within minutes, not after a 14-day scrub.
- **Known but behind** (a hot copy shorter than its sealed length; tombstones not yet applied to its index): converged by the hot restart rules (10.3), and the index pulls the tombstones after its cursor (10.9) before the node leaves `indexing`.
- **Layout debt it can repay by adoption:** for a stripe rebuilt into a degraded layout while this component was away, whose original shard here verifies, one transaction points the `shards` row back at this component (conditioned on the row version and on the stripe being `committed`) and the rebuilt copy is deleted after that commit. Nothing is copied back. Only a stripe already at its full layout without this shard treats it as **surplus**, kept as a spare for `surplus_grace` (7 days) and then deleted.
- **Hot segment or large file with no row: not an orphan, unless its id was freed.** The `dropped` and `freed` rows of kind segment and large are kept while any node is `lost` or `out` and not yet rejoined fresh (10.16), so a copy with no row at all is a pending insert and never a freed block returning; a node that returns after the purge (every such node had already reported its holdings or rejoined fresh) holds no copy of a freed block. It is never quarantined and keeps being served from its own index and the replication stream. The holder inserts the row itself (`INSERT INTO blocks ... ON CONFLICT (block_id) DO NOTHING` plus its `hot_copies` row, as in 10.16 transaction 1), taking the replica set and epoch from the segment header or replication stream and the highest fence it recorded (or `open` at epoch 1 if none), and, for a `big/` file, kind `large` in state `sealed` with its size, one record and `live_count` 1. The insert runs once the store is reachable and before the node leaves `indexing`; the file then follows the normal seal path (10.3 step 4); a large file's row is inserted as `sealed` (no seal step).
- **Unknown** (a stripe id or shard with no row; a block row in state `freed` or `dropped`; a cold or pack block id with no row): quarantined under `orphan/`, never served or counted, deleted after `surplus_grace` and only when the sweeper's conditions hold (the metadata store reachable, not frozen, the row unreferenced for `destage.orphan_age`, 10.16).

A `lost` row deletes nothing from the stripe and shard rows; they change only when a rebuild commits, so a node declared lost that returns brings back every stripe not yet rebuilt at no copy cost, and the queue shrinks as shards are adopted.

**Retention [D].** There is no age limit on what a returning component may bring back. Cold shards and hot segments are adopted by their rows **however long the component was away**: the rows (and the `freed` and `dropped` states, which stay as long as the unlinks are outstanding and, for segments and large files, while any node is `lost` or `out` and not yet rejoined fresh) define validity, and a tombstone is deleted from the store only in the transaction that frees its block, so a copy the store no longer knows is exactly an orphan and is quarantined. A node that came back from a long absence pulls every tombstone after its cursor; a node the gate stopped waiting for (the `health` table holds a `lost` row for it with no `returned` row it has processed, or its `tomb_applied` or `incarnation_seen` predates that row's `since`) rebuilds its index from the trailers and `blk/*.idx` of the blocks whose rows are not `dropped` or `freed`, treats a block with no row as absent, applies the tombstone rows still present with its cursor reset to 0, and only then reports `returned` and leaves `indexing` (10.2 recovery step 4); the rows of freed segments and large files are kept while such a node exists (10.16), so a returning copy of a freed block is quarantined and never re-inserted. The two protections instead of an age limit are the node incarnation (a restored or replaced device is a new node incarnation that must rejoin fresh, R7) and the metadata incarnation with its rollback detection (10.16): a component can never bring back state into a store that has lost the rows describing it, because every destructive action freezes until `metadata fsck` has reconciled the rows with the disks (24.5, R14). `store node rejoin-fresh` discards a node's hot segments and local index on the operator's order, while its cold shards are still adopted.

**Reads while partitioned.** A node that cannot reach the metadata store serves, from its index, immutable-bucket keys it holds: the bytes at a record id never change, so a hit is never stale bytes; a key deleted on the other side may still be served by that node until it can pull the tombstone, bounded by the length of the partition plus `metadata.tomb_poll` (2 s) [D] (a general user who needs read-after-delete uses a mutable bucket). Reads of mutable-bucket keys need the head row and answer `503 Retry-After` (the bytes would be correct, the head is not known); this is the stated per-bucket-mode answer to F-MINORITY-READ. Such a node acknowledges no write that needs the store (every mutable write, every immutable DELETE, every seal) and, if it is on the small side of a hot-replication partition, none that needs `min_acks` copies either.

### 24.5 The reconciler
**Shape.** One loop, run by every storage node and **active on the node that holds the lease** `reconciler_lease` (one row: `holder`, `until`). A node takes or renews the lease by a transaction `UPDATE ... SET holder = me, until = now() + 15 s WHERE until < now() OR holder = me`, every 5 s (`reconcile.lease_renew`, `reconcile.lease`; the lease is **enforced by the database clock, not by node clocks**); candidates try with jitter and prefer the lowest rank, and a node that loses the row stops at once. Every destructive or committing transaction of the reconciler begins with `SELECT 1 FROM ts.reconciler_lease WHERE id = 1 AND holder = $me AND until > clock_timestamp() FOR SHARE` **inside the transaction** and aborts if it returns no row (10.16): `clock_timestamp()` answers a holder paused after `BEGIN` (`now()` would be the transaction's start time), and the `FOR SHARE` lock makes the taker's `UPDATE` wait for the holder's commit, so a holder that passed the check cannot be overtaken, and one that was paused (a stall, a stopped VM, a partition) and wakes after the lease passed cannot commit anything, whatever it believes. The wait is bounded by `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout`, which kills a stalled holder. The lease take itself is one statement and keeps `now()`. A new holder loses no work: the queue is rows (below), and its first pass recomputes everything.

Every `reconcile.interval` (10 s) and on events (a health row, a membership epoch change, a block sealed, a drive state change) the holder compares, for every block and stripe, the **desired** redundancy (the configured code and replica count and the placement invariant, from the membership document) with the **actual** (rows of `hot_copies`, `shards`, `health`, `nodes` and `drives`: a copy on a component that is `down`, `failed`, `missing`, `stalled` or `lost` counts as gone for margin), and maintains the table `repair_queue`: `(job_id, kind, subject, priority P0..P4, margin, state queued|leased|done|refused, lease_holder, lease_until, attempts, refusal_reason, row_version)`. The queue is a **cache of a computation**: it is a pure function of the other rows, so a store restored from a backup, or a queue the holder discards, is recomputed in one pass and nothing is lost; a stale `leased` job whose `lease_until` passed is re-queued with `attempts + 1`. The pass reads rows indexed by state (not every row of every table) and is event-driven for changes; its cost at LATER's counts at the capacity guard (about 133,000 shard rows; a case of its own, 142.8 TB raw, not the 85% fill limit of 10.2) is a few tens of milliseconds of database time [I, T-S25 measures]. A degraded read only enqueues its stripe. Repair has its own thread pool and the `background` class of 10.7.

**Executors and commits.** Executors run on the nodes: a node holding a source or the target of a job (or the preferred executor of 24.1) **leases** a queued job by CAS on `(job_id, row_version)`, setting `lease_holder` and `lease_until` (job lease 5 min, extended while progress is reported), copies or decodes the data into a temporary file, and commits the result as **one transaction** conditioned on the job's row version, on the lease still being its own, and on the stripe not being `freeing` or `freed` (**a free wins over a repair**: the repair finds the stripe gone and deletes its temporary file). Two executors on one job (a lease lost to a stall) both finish and the conditional transaction lets one commit. The executor's copies need no lease holder and no reachable store beyond the lease itself; the commit does.

**What enters the queue [D].** An item enters **as soon as its margin is at or below its threshold, independent of the `lost` timer** (the X1 fix):
- a hot block with fewer than `hot.min_acks` live copies, or a cold stripe at margin <= 1 (counting shards on `down`, `failed`, `missing` or `stalled` components as gone), is queued **immediately** and repaired onto live components, recording `layout_debt` when the target layout is degraded;
- a hot block below `hot.replicas` but at or above `min_acks`, or a cold stripe at margin >= 2 below its full layout, is queued only when its components are `lost` or `out` (or when a `lost` row exists for them), because a reboot returns those shards at no cost.
So on NOW (two hot replicas) a crash queues every dirty block the node held at once, and on LATER a node down puts its 3-shard stripes (margin 1) in the queue at once, onto the two survivors at 4/4 with `layout_debt`; a return then repays the debt by adoption (24.4).

**Create-race conflicts of immutable buckets [D].** A record has a stable identity, its **record id** `(block_id, offset)`, and carries its key, bucket id and generation in its header. The holder detects a create-race conflict (10.5) by the **periodic per-node index comparison**, the same comparison as layer 3 of the audit of 24.10 (the dry-run scan). It is **digest-based and incremental**, so that it moves kilobytes and not the index (at 1e8 keys a pass that moved every live key of every node would be several GB per node and could not run every 10 s). The `index-report` route of 10.6 has a request shape, `GET /store/v1/index-report?bucket=&hash_prefix=<bits>&digest=1`: with `digest=1` a node answers, for each of 2^16 key-hash ranges (the first 16 bits of the key hash) of the live immutable-bucket records of its own index, an order-independent digest (a wrapping sum of the blake3 of `(key hash, record id, generation)`, which the node keeps up to date incrementally as it indexes and tombstones, so a request reads no index entry) and a count; without `digest`, it answers the full entries `(bucket id, key hash, record id, generation)` of the one range `hash_prefix` names. The holder runs a **rolling slice per pass**: every `reconcile.interval` it asks every node for the digests of the next 1/8640 of the 2^16 ranges (eight ranges), so the full key space is covered once per **24 h cycle** (a constant of the reconciler, not a setting); it compares the digests across nodes, and asks for the entries of a range from every node only when the digests differ in a way the previous pass did not already explain (with `hot.replicas` equal to the number of nodes every node indexes every key and equal ranges have equal digests; with a smaller replica set the nodes' ranges differ by construction, and the holder remembers the digest tuple of a range it has already resolved, asking again only when a digest changes). It groups the entries by key. It also runs an **immediate targeted check** of a key's range when the writer's all-node key check (10.2) sees a second live record id for the key, or when `hot_unacked_records_adopted_total` increments. **Cost:** one pass moves about 8 ranges x 16 bytes x the number of nodes, and a differing range about 1.5 thousand entries of 40 bytes at 1e8 keys (60 KB); the index read load is zero for a digest and one range scan for a differing range. **Detection latency** of a create-race conflict is at most the 24 h cycle, and typically seconds through the targeted check. Two live record ids of one key with different generations (different bytes) are a **conflict**; equal generations (the content-derived case) are a **duplicate**, resolved the same way. "Lower record id" means the lower `(block_id, offset)`. For a conflict or a duplicate the holder runs one lease-checked transaction that inserts a `tombstones` row for the record with the higher `(block_id, offset)` (the unique-conflict rule of 10.9 applies), decrements its block's `live_count` (so the free protocol reclaims the bytes) and writes an `audit` row; a conflict also increments `store_key_conflict_total` and raises the alert, a duplicate neither. Every node then applies the tombstone to its index through the normal tombstone path (10.2). T-S25 implements the comparison and counts the metric (already a row of the table of section 12); T-S6 supplies the `index-report` route.

**Priorities (by remaining margin).**

| Class | Items | Share |
|---|---|---|
| P0 | cold margin <= 0; hot block with one live copy | may take `rebuild.p0_share` (75%) of drive time; preempts scrub and every lower class |
| P1 | cold margin 1; hot block below `min_acks` but above one copy | `rebuild.share` (25%) |
| P2 | cold margin >= 2 below the full layout; hot block below `replicas` (only when `lost`/`out`) | `rebuild.share`, after P1 |
| P3 | placement-invariant violations, `layout_debt` repayment by move, surplus cleanup | `rebuild.share / 2` |
| P4 | drain, evacuation, rebalance | `rebalance.share` (10%) |

Within a class: longest exposure first (`degraded_since` on the block or stripe row), then block id. A new P0 item interrupts lower work at the next cell boundary.

**One throttle, shared with scrub.** One budget per drive and one per node link, shared by rebuild, evacuation, rebalance **and scrub**, applied to what demand leaves (demand keeps its preemption and the write share of 10.7). Shares are fractions of the drive's sequential rate measured by T0-S (an implausible measurement keeps the defaults and raises an alert). A decode reading `k` shards charges `k` read streams to its sources and one write stream to its target; at most one background stream per source drive, at most `rebuild.max_items` (4) items per node. Every knob lives in the configuration; a runtime override through the operator API is listed with its expiry in `store repair status`. `store repair pause --class P3,P4 --for 2h` pauses classes; P0 is paused only with `--include-p0`. A target above `cold.rebuild_fill_ceiling` (92%) is refused; an item with no target shows `blocked: capacity` and pages `RepairBlockedCapacity`.

**The X2 cap, and the other policies of the loop.**
- **The flap cap never leaves zero targets (X2).** The damper prefers non-`flapping` targets with a floor (24.3). When every candidate target of a job is `flapping` (or in probation) the reconciler takes the least-flapping candidate (lowest score, then rendezvous order) instead of none, raises the ticket `repair_targets_flapping` and records the choice in the job; a job is never left with no target because of the damper.
- **Adopt-on-return** (24.4): when a component returns, the reconciler closes the `missing` row, takes jobs whose subject has an adoptable original shard off the queue and leaves their repayment to the transaction of 24.4; the queue shrinks without copies.
- **Degraded placement and its debt counter:** `layout_debt` is set on the stripe row by the commit that places a stripe in a degraded layout; `layout_debt_stripes` counts them and P3 repays them on return (24.4).
- **The capacity invariant** (24.15 item 3) is evaluated each pass; when it would be broken the reconciler publishes the destage hold that the hot marks of 10.5 act on.
- **The freeze before the orphan sweep (10.16).** The orphan sweep, trash purge, unlink of a freed block and every other destructive action runs only when the node is not frozen (the metadata incarnation and `txn_seq` check of every transaction); while frozen the reconciler still enqueues and executors still **copy** (copies only add data), but a commit that changes rows waits and the job shows the reason `frozen`.
- **Refusal reasons from a closed set** (24.15 item 2), shown by `store repair status` and `store repair explain`; every job that is not progressing has exactly one in `refusal_reason`.
- **`repair_stalled_on_metadata`:** the alert (page) when jobs exist and no transaction of the reconciler or an executor has committed for 5 minutes; it names whether the store is unreachable, in failover, frozen or refusing (a statement timeout storm). It joins the table of section 12 with the task that registers it (T-S25).

**Per-item commit protocol (crash-safe, never deleting the only copy).**
1. Read `k` source shards in windows; verify every cell against its source's footer crc32c; a bad cell excludes that source and another survivor is tried.
2. Decode the missing shard(s); write `repair/<stripe>.<shard>.<row version>.tmp` on the target; `fsync` file and directory.
3. Verify: blake3 of the rebuilt shard equals the per-shard blake3 the stripe's rows record (the `shards` row's `blake3` column and the shard footer's existing blake3, both set at encode time; data shards also match the block's blake3 in the sealed row).
4. Rename to the final name, write the footer, `fsync`.
5. **Commit in one transaction** (10.16): `UPDATE shards SET node_id, drive_id, file, state = 'ok' ... WHERE stripe_id = $s AND shard_idx = $i AND row_version = $v`, together with `stripes.state = 'committed'` checked in the same statement, the job row set to `done`, the reconciler's or the executor's lease checked, the `op_id` recorded and `txn_seq` returned; refused if the stripe was freed or the row was already repaired. An ambiguous commit is retried with the same `op_id`.
6. Only after the commit is the old location removed from the rows and anything deleted (by the unlink path, to `trash/` first, 24.15 item 4). The function that deletes a shard or copy takes a `Redundancy` witness that only a check "another verified copy, or the committed replacement, exists" can produce.
At start the daemon removes every `*.tmp` under `repair/`; a crash at steps 1 to 4 leaves only a temporary file, a crash at 5 leaves either no commit (the job is re-queued) or a commit (the retry finds its `op_id`), and after 5 the item is done.

**Salvage [D].** A `failed` drive that still reads in part is the last source for a stripe at margin <= 0. `store drive salvage D` copies its readable cells read-only, verified by cell crc, into repair items of such stripes, and the reconciler uses a `failed` drive as a last-resort source for P0 items only. `store drive safe-to-destroy D` refuses while any stripe at margin <= 0, or with `cell_lost`, depends on D.

**Latent errors at margin 0.** A margin-0 rebuild reads exactly `k` survivors; a latent sector error in a needed cell loses that cell: the stripe is marked `cell_lost` for that range, an alert fires and `store lost-report` names the keys. A pack the CAS service can republish becomes a miss (4.6); nothing is ever served wrong. The defence is the margin-first scrub (24.7).

**Writes during repair.** Hot: one node down on LATER leaves two live nodes, every PUT needs both (`min_acks = 2`), and a second failure makes small PUTs `503 Retry-After`: hot writes never go below `min_acks`, with no sloppy quorum and no hint queue. A seal, a destage and a mutable-bucket write each need the metadata store; while it is in failover they wait or answer `503`, and the open segments are fenced and rolled to new segments so that immutable-bucket PUTs continue (10.3). Cold: new stripes go to live nodes if the invariant holds (4/4 on two nodes is valid at `4+4`) and carry `layout_debt`; `layout_debt_stripes` counts them, and P3 repays them on return (adoption first, a plain copy only for shards that are not there). **Blocks below `hot.replicas` copies are destaged promptly** while any node is down (they are the least protected data), and no other stripe creation is held back. Placement during an outage never targets a `down`, `suspect`, `flapping`, `lost` or `new` component and never passes the fill limit (subject to the damper's floor, 24.3).

### 24.6 Hot-tier specifics
Each hot block's `blocks` row holds `degraded_since`, and its `hot_copies` rows are the verified full copies; the wanted count is derived from `hot.replicas` and the membership document (24.5), not stored; `hot_blocks_by_copies` and `hot_under_replicated_blocks` are computed from them. With one of three nodes down a sealed block stays at two copies until the node returns: **expected, not an alert**, while `copies >= min_acks`. After a return only blocks whose durable home is still hot are caught up (sealed and not yet destaged, and large objects not yet destaged), fewest copies first, by a background whole-file copy verified per record and by blake3, at most 2 per node pair, inside the shared link budget. A stale replica (committed length below the sealed length, epoch below the fence, diverging running crc) is converged and never read. A segment that stays `fenced` without a sealed row because the store was unreachable is sealed when it returns (the CAS of 10.3), counted by `hot_fenced_unsealed_blocks`, and is not destaged before.

**A dead NVMe** is, for hot data, the node's loss (10.3), and it also holds the node's index and its caches of the block, stripe and bucket rows (the rows themselves are in the metadata store, and a Postgres member on that node is lost with it, R12). The node's cold shards are intact but unserved until its index is rebuilt (degraded reads meanwhile, the load of a node down). The node returns as a **new node incarnation**; its caches are pulled from the store, its index is rebuilt from the trailers and `blk/*.idx` of the blocks that have rows and the store's tombstones, and its holdings are adopted (24.4); before it allocates a block id it learns the highest id its node id ever used from the store and from disk and starts above it (24.15 item 1). How long the index rebuild takes is measured by T-S3; a periodic index snapshot on one of the node's HDDs is allowed if that measurement asks for it, and it is a cache only: the rows decide validity, never a snapshot (R7).

### 24.7 Cold-tier specifics
**Dead drive.** `failed` -> 10 min -> `lost` row -> its stripes queued by margin -> declustered rebuild (10.4): about 2.1 hours at the 25% share at the capacity guard [E, 10.4]. Exposure while rebuilding is margin `m - 1` (3 at `4+4`); `exposure > 4 h` at margin <= 1 pages. There is no hot spare and no identity to keep: a replacement drive is a `new` drive that adds capacity.

**Dead node.** Its 3-shard stripes are repaired at once (margin 1, 24.5); the rest follow its `lost` row (24 h cumulative, or `store node out`). Rebuilding everything onto two nodes reads 71.4 TB and writes 47.6 TB over 30 drives: at the 25% share (47.5 MB/s per drive) the reads bound it at about 14 hours, about 14 to 20 hours at the default share and about 5 hours at the P0 share [E]; C-11 measures it. Replacement hardware (`store node replace`, R6) joins as the same node id in a new node incarnation; debt stripes are repaid to it by moves, and stripes rebuilt as 4/4 are valid and moved back to 3/3/2 only as P4 work.

**Scrub.** Every shard is read once per `cold.scrub_period`: 14 days on CMR drives (LATER), 28 on SMR (NOW); at 3.2 TB per drive that is about 2.6 MB/s, about 1.4% of 190 MB/s [E], inside the shared budget. While any component is `down`, stripes at margin <= 1 are scrubbed first (above ordinary scrub, below P0). No new ordinary scrub starts while a P0 to P2 item exists. Scrub runs only below a load gate (demand under 50% of the drive over 1 minute) and an optional hour window. A shard failing verification is quarantined (`.bad`) and its stripe queued; more than `scrub.error_burst` (20) bad shards on one drive in a period marks the drive `failed`. SMART pending and reallocated sector growth sets the drive `suspect-media`, which starts an **evacuation** (one read per shard) rather than a decode. Scrub also compares each footer it reads with the node's `shards` row and counts differences in `metadata_audit_differences` (24.10).

**Margin-0 alerts.** `cold_stripe_margin{margin="0"} > 0` and `hot_blocks_by_copies{copies="1"} > 0` older than `rebuild.drive_delay` page, with the stripe count and the P0 ETA while a node is down.

### 24.8 Capacity changes (no data moves by default)
- **Adding a storage node (3 to 4):** a membership verb (24.9); the node is `new`, burns in, becomes `up`. New hot segments may place on it (replica sets of 3 of 4); new stripes use the weighted rule over four nodes (`4+4` as 2/2/2/2). Existing hot blocks and stripes do not move. The new node's placement weight is capped at 1.5 times the median free fraction until its fill reaches the median, and at **1.0 times while a node-loss rebuild runs**. Adding it changes no existing agreement: a seal is one compare-and-set on its row, a stripe commit one conditional transaction, and the metadata store's three members do not change (a fourth node hosts a Postgres member only when the operator moves one there, R12, T-S12).
- **Optional rebalance:** `store rebalance plan` (a dry run listing moves) and `store rebalance apply`: whole-shard copies, never a decode, one move per stripe per step, only for stripes at full margin, from nodes above mean fill + 5% to nodes below, at `rebalance.share`, paused while any P0 to P3 item exists; the old shard is deleted after the move's transaction commits.
- **Adding drives to a node:** the same, with the cap per drive; `store rebalance --node N` balances within a node on request.
- **Removing hardware:** `drain` creates P4 evacuation items (copies; a decode only for an unreadable shard); `store node ok-to-stop` and `store drive safe-to-destroy` say yes only when every block and stripe with a copy there has full margin without it (and, for a node, when its Postgres member, if any, is not needed to keep the store's two healthy members).

### 24.9 Changing the membership
`membership.toml` seeds `store init` only (the first start of a cluster: it writes the `nodes` and `drives` rows, the cluster settings and epoch 1); it is not read again. Afterwards **a membership change is an operator verb that writes the metadata store** (`store node add|drain|out|in|cordon|replace`, `store drive ...`, `store bucket ...` for buckets, 24.11; `store node add` registers a new node row in state `new` through the validator, after the hardware-audit check of 24.15 item 9, and its drives' rows in the same epoch), in **one transaction** with its `audit` row and the increment of the epoch (a new `membership` row whose document is rendered from the `nodes` and `drives` rows). The validator is the code of the verbs, run inside the transaction, before any node acts on the change; a failed validation changes nothing and says why. It checks: a compare-and-set on the previous epoch (the verb reads epoch `M` and the insert is conditioned on `M` still being newest); at most one node or drive state change per epoch; `in` to `out` directly is refused (through `drain` and `safe-to-destroy`, or `store node out` with its `ok-to-out` result); duplicate node ids and drive UUIDs; a capacity within 2x of the probed device; the resulting layout satisfies `m >= max_shards_per_domain`; and a node that holds a Postgres member is not drained or put `out` unless the store keeps two healthy members without it. **`in` to `drain` or `out` takes effect after `membership.apply_delay` (10 minutes)**: the transaction records the pending state and its effective time (`nodes.pending_state`, `pending_effective_at`, database clock), `store status` shows it as pending with the command that cancels it, and consumers treat the node as `in` until the effective time; a revert inside the delay is a transaction that clears the pending state and cancels every consequence.

Nodes learn the epoch by polling the store every 5 s, by a push from the verb to every node it can reach, and from `x-ts-mem-epoch` on peer messages; the document they hold is a rendered copy. **While the store is unreachable a node keeps its last document** and acts on it; no membership change can be made in that state, which is the right answer (R6 waits for the store). Clients are unchanged: seeds or a membership file, `GET /store/v1/membership` and `x-ts-mem-epoch` (10.8); the route serves the document rendered from the rows.

### 24.10 Invariants and the audit
| Id | Invariant | Measured by |
|---|---|---|
| I1 | Every acknowledged write is readable from at least `min_acks` hot copies until destaged, then from at least `k` verified cold shards; an acknowledged mutable write is the head row | live-key audit and canary reads; `hot_blocks_by_copies`, `cold_stripe_margin_verified` |
| I2 | A stripe's shards are on distinct drives, no node holds more than `max_shards_per_domain`, and `m >= max_shards_per_domain` | placement audit; `placement_violations` |
| I3 | No stale, surplus or freed copy is served or counted | `stale_served_total` (must stay 0); orphan counters |
| I4 | A committed row never changes except by a conditional transaction naming its version (a sealed block's length and crc never change; a committed stripe changes only by repair or move) | `commit_conflicts_total` |
| I5 | The only copy of anything is never deleted | `last_copy_delete_refused_total`; the `Redundancy` witness (24.5) |
| I6 | No placement targets an ineligible component or uses an ineligible map | `placement_epoch_rejects_total` |
| I7 | Every item below its threshold is queued and progresses | `repair_oldest_exposure_seconds{class}`, `repair_stuck_items` (no progress for 15 min), `repair_stalled_on_metadata` |
| I8 | Repair and scrub stay within their shared budget while reads meet their targets | `repair_share_actual`, demand wait histograms |
| I9 | **The rows of the metadata store agree with the footers on disk:** every block, hot copy, stripe and shard row matches a trailer, an index file or a shard footer, and every file with a footer has a row | `metadata_audit_differences{kind}`; **`store metadata fsck` in dry-run mode is the audit** |
| I10 | **The tombstones applied in every node index equal the tombstones of the store** (for the immutable buckets' live records: no record is live in an index that a tombstone row names, and no applied tombstone lacks its row) | `metadata_audit_differences{kind="missed_tombstone"}`, and `{kind="missing_record"}` for a head or live count that names bytes a node does not hold (10.8); the dry-run fsck compares them |

`metadata_audit_differences` is a new metric, named here, that joins the table of section 12 with the task that registers it (T-S24); it replaces the group digest and ledger series of the earlier invariants I9 and I10.

Audit layers: (1) continuous margins and copy counts from the rows and states, in a `nominal` and a `verified` series; (2) scrub, which also compares each footer with its row (24.7); (3) **the dry-run fsck**: `store metadata fsck` without `--apply` scans every node's on-disk facts and compares them with the rows (10.16), writes nothing, and prints every difference; the daemon runs its per-node part continuously as the scrub's comparison, and the full fsck dry-run runs weekly on the farm and always after a restore (R14); (4) the live-key audit of 10.8; (5) **canary objects**, a fixed set of synthetic keys per hot and cold block class and per bucket mode read through every path (healthy, degraded, each component masked) once an hour; (6) in the simulator, the daemon's exported margin and copy functions must equal the oracle's ground truth, and the simulator's fsck must reproduce the oracle's rows after a rollback, so an audit that cannot fail goes red. `store health` prints two lines: **serving** (can every acknowledged key be read now) and **protected** (how much is below full redundancy, by margin); it adds a third word, `metadata`, that says `ok`, `failover`, `frozen` or `unreachable`.

Pages: unreadable keys > 0; margin-0 or one-copy items older than `drive_delay`; `NodeDownLong`; `NodeDownNeedsDecision` (including `flapping` for 6 h); `RepairBlockedCapacity`; `repair_stalled_on_metadata`; `metadata_frozen` (a node froze its destructive actions, 10.16); the store without a primary for more than 45 s (the client retry window, 10.16); `stale_served_total > 0`. Tickets: `flapping` for 1 h; scrub overrun (a drive not scrubbed within 1.5 periods); `layout_debt_stripes` unchanged for 7 days; `repair_stuck_items`; `repair_targets_flapping`; surplus older than 14 days; an expired maintenance window with the node still away; a `failed` drive uncleared for 24 h; SMART growth; a held burst declaration (24.3 guard 4); `metadata_audit_differences > 0` for an hour; `store_key_conflict_total > 0` (a create race resolved, 10.9); `hot_fenced_unsealed_blocks > 0` for an hour.

### 24.11 Operator verbs
Every verb is an endpoint of one operator API (JSON and gRPC, both complete) and the CLI is generated from its schema; runtime changes go through the API, configuration through files. Mutating verbs take `--reason` (recorded in the node event history and in the `audit` row of the same transaction); destructive ones print the plan and the safety result and act only with `--apply`; none has a flag that skips a safety check. The one escape is the **break-glass held script** of 24.15 item 4 (time-limited, logged, one named check, reviewed afterwards). A step only the owner runs is a held script generated from the same API call. Each refusal and each blocked repair item carries a reason from the closed set of 24.15 item 2. Mutating verbs need the metadata store and say `MetadataUnavailable` (and what to wait for) when it is in failover or unreachable; read-only verbs and `store status --local` do not.
- `store init` (first start of a cluster: reads `membership.toml`, writes the `nodes`, `drives` and settings rows, epoch 1, metadata incarnation 1; refuses on a store that already has a cluster row); `store status`, `store health`; `store node add N` (registers a new node, state `new`, 24.9); `store node list|get N` (state, node incarnation, the last 20 transitions with reasons, flap score, budget used, the time to `lost`); `store node maintenance start N --for 2h` and `end N`; `store node ok-to-stop N`; `store node ok-to-out N`; `store node cordon N` (no placements); `store node drain N` and `store node in N` (set the node's state `drain`, or back to `in`, through a membership row, 10.8 and 21.3); `store drive drain D` and `store drive in D` (the same for a drive); `store node out N` (a prepared change; `--apply` also writes the `lost` row); `store node replace N` (opens a new node incarnation); `store node rejoin-fresh N`; `store drive add D` and the other drive verbs of 24.11.
- `store bucket create NAME --mode immutable|mutable`, `store bucket list` (a bucket's mode is fixed at creation; changing it is a new bucket and a copy, 10.9).
- `store metadata status` (primary, standbys and their lag, the synchronous member, the metadata incarnation, `txn_seq`, frozen or not, the age of the newest backup and of the last restore drill); `store metadata promote-lone` (a held step, R6 and R15: the manual promotion of the lone, asynchronous or lagging member; it raises the metadata incarnation and sets `frozen`, and acts only with `--apply` after the owner has accepted the loss of the commits that member never received; it is followed by `store metadata fsck --apply`, R14); `store metadata migrate` (the additive schema step, run by the explicit verb with the owner role, never on start); `store metadata fsck [--apply]` (10.16; dry-run by default); `store metadata restore --to TIME` (a point-in-time restore from the off-box backups; raises the metadata incarnation; a held step, R14); `store metadata switchover [--to MEMBER]`, a passthrough to Patroni's planned switchover that first checks the target is synchronous and caught up and prints what clients will see (R11).
- `store drive list|get D`, `store drive fail D`, `store drive clear D` (after a verifying scrub), `store drive evacuate D`, `store drive salvage D`, `store drive safe-to-destroy D`, `store drive blink D` (locate the slot), `store drive add` (format plan, held), `store drive replace D` (prints R2).
- `store nvme replace N` (prints R7).
- `store repair status [--class]`, `store repair pause|resume --class ... --for`, `store repair priority STRIPE`, `store repair explain STRIPE|BLOCK` (why it is where it is and what blocks it).
- `store rebalance plan|apply|cancel`; `store audit [--keys FILE]`; `store lost-report`; `store facts list|get` (the `health` rows, read only).
Every state is shown with its reason and the time left ("lost in 6h12m unless it returns; ok-to-out: yes"), and every refusal names what blocks it and the verb that unblocks it.

### 24.12 Runbooks
Each: symptoms, what the system does by itself, what the operator does, how to know it is done, what not to do. Times are LATER defaults at the capacity guard [E]; on today's fill (under 2%) every repair takes minutes.

**R1. A hard drive dies.** *Symptoms:* `DriveFailed` ticket; `store drive get D` shows `failed` and why (EIO count or stalls). *Automatic:* D serves nothing, its stripes read degraded; its margin-1 stripes are already queued (none at `4+4` from one drive alone), at +10 min the reconciler writes `lost(D)` and every stripe on it is queued; rebuild runs at the 25% share, about 2.1 h. *Operator:* watch `store repair status`; find the slot (`store drive blink D`); when `store drive safe-to-destroy D` says yes, swap the drive, run the held format script, `store drive add`; the new drive goes `new`, burns in, becomes `up`. *Done:* the protected line is clean and the new drive is `up`. *Do not:* pull a drive that says `safe-to-destroy: no`; clear `failed` to retry a dying disk. *NOW:* SMR drives shared with silo rebuild in about a day at a 15% share; the exposure page is at 24 h.

**R2. Replace a hard drive on purpose.** A healthy but `suspect-media` drive: `store drive evacuate D` (copies), wait for `safe-to-destroy: yes`, swap, format, `store drive add`. A pulled-by-mistake healthy drive goes `failed` on its EIOs: reinsert it, let it scrub (`store drive clear D` is allowed after a clean verifying scrub), and nothing is rebuilt if it is back within `drive_delay`.

**R3. A storage node crashes and reboots.** *Symptoms:* `NodeDown` (info). *Automatic:* reads reroute; hot writes need both remaining nodes; margin-1 stripes and blocks below `min_acks` are queued at once (24.5); if the node held the metadata primary, Patroni fails over within about 30 s and the store answers `503 Retry-After` meanwhile (R11); on return the node is `indexing` (it takes part in fences, replication and repair at once, 24.2), reconciles its holdings (24.4), repays layout debt by adoption, catches up the dirty hot blocks it missed, pulls tombstones, serves clients when its index is built, and is `up` after its probation (15 min unannounced, skipped after a correlated event); its Postgres member rejoins as a standby (by `pg_rewind` if it was the old primary, R12 if that fails). *Operator:* nothing, unless it flaps (R4) or `NodeDownLong` fires (R6). Read `store node get N` for the crash reason. *Planned reboot:* `store node ok-to-stop N` must say yes (it refuses while the node's Postgres member is the primary or the only synchronous standby: run `store metadata switchover` first, which the update hook of hostkeeper also does, 10.16); `store node maintenance start N --for 30m --reason ...`; stop the daemon with SIGTERM (it seals its open segments, flushes and announces `leaving`, so peers fail over at once); reboot; maintenance ends when the node is `up`. *Done:* `up`, protected clean, `store metadata status` shows three members streaming. *Do not:* reboot a second node before the first is `up` and caught up, and its Postgres member is streaming again.

**R4. A storage node flaps.** *Symptoms:* `flapping` (ticket at 1 h, page at 6 h). *Automatic:* last in reads, not preferred for placements (never below the floor), its unavailable time counts toward the persisted 48 h budget; on three nodes **there is no automatic exit** short of that budget. *Operator:* read the transition history, fix the cause (kernel, NIC, power supply, temperature); park it with `store node cordon N` or `maintenance start N --for 24h`, or decide `store node out N` (R6). If the node holds a Postgres member, a flapping node can also cause repeated failovers (Patroni's leader changes); `store metadata status` shows them, and the cure is the same, with `store metadata switchover` moving the primary to a stable node first. *Done:* stable `up` for its probation and the score below 1. *Do not:* look for a verb that clears `flapping`; there is none.

**R5. Add a storage node.** `store node add` through the validator (24.9; the node must have passed the hardware audit, 24.15 item 9: on LATER a device without power-loss protection is refused); `store node get N` shows `new`, then `up`. No data moves; new writes use it at its weight cap. `store rebalance plan` is optional. The node hosts no Postgres member unless the operator moves one there (R12, T-S12): the store's three members stay where they are.

**R6. A storage node is gone for good (or the decision to treat it so).** *Symptoms:* `NodeDownNeedsDecision` at 4 h. *Decision aid:* `store node ok-to-out N` prints whether the survivors can absorb the rebuild below the 85% auto ceiling, the layout (`4+4` as 4/4 on two nodes), the volume (71.4 TB read, 47.6 TB written), the time (about 14 to 20 h at the default share, about 5 h at the P0 share), whether the node held a Postgres member and what the store looks like without it, and the statement "after this, one more node loss leaves every stripe at margin 0 and no metadata commit possible". *Exits:* (a) **replace**: new hardware joins as `store node replace N` (same id, new node incarnation); debt is repaid by moves and hot catch-up fills its NVMe, and no full rebuild is needed; its Postgres member is created again on it (R12); (b) **out**: `store node out N`; the rebuild runs onto two nodes, and a fourth node is added when hardware exists (R5), after which a third Postgres member is created on it (R12). An automatic `lost` happens at 24 h cumulative only if the guards pass. *Two of three nodes gone:* nothing commits (the store has one member, and strict synchronous replication refuses it, 10.16); hot writes are `503`; immutable-bucket GETs of keys held by the survivor work; cold reads work only for stripes with at least `k` shards on the survivor; this is an availability incident for the owner, not a repair; do not start anything with one node, and promote the lone member only through `store metadata promote-lone`, when the owner has decided to accept the loss of the commits it never received; the verb raises the incarnation and freezes every node, then run R14 steps 4 and 5 (R14 describes what fsck recovers). *NOW:* with six nodes `node out` is routine (one survivor holds two shards of each affected `4+2` stripe; the cluster again survives one further node); if the node held one of the three Postgres members (R12), a new member is built on another combined node.

**R7. Replace the NVMe (the SATA SSD hot partition on NOW).** *Symptoms:* the hot device errors or vanishes; `NodeHotLost`. *Automatic:* the node's hot copies count as gone; blocks below `min_acks` are queued at once; its HDDs are unserved until its index is rebuilt (degraded reads meanwhile); the node's Postgres member and etcd member (on the same device) are lost with it: Patroni fails over if it was the primary, and the store runs on two members (R11, R12). *Operator:* `store node maintenance start N --for 8h --reason nvme`; replace and format (held script: the same node id and a new node incarnation, the Postgres and etcd partitions re-created; `store nvme replace N` records the old incarnation as superseded); start the daemon: it pulls the block, stripe and bucket rows from the store as caches, rebuilds its index from its shards' trailers and `blk/*.idx` and the store's tombstones, adopts its holdings, catches up dirty hot blocks, and allocates block ids above the highest the store and the disks have seen for its node id; then R12 re-creates its Postgres member and etcd member. *Done:* `indexing` over, `hot_blocks_by_copies{copies<2}` clean, adopted shards checked, three Postgres and three etcd members healthy. *Do not:* restore an old NVMe image: its `NODE` file names a superseded incarnation, the node refuses to start with it and it must rejoin fresh, and a stale image of the Postgres member is re-initialised from the primary, never reused (R12).

**R8. Planned maintenance of several nodes.** One node at a time, each with `ok-to-stop` and R3's planned form. A rack or power job that touches two nodes at once is a service window (R10), never a rolling step.

**R9.
