A drive burst or a reordered bus.** *Several drives of one node fail within minutes:* the burst guard holds their `lost` rows and pages; check the HBA, expander, backplane and cabling; if the controller is fixed the drives come back as `missing` then adopted; otherwise treat the node as R6. *Drives renamed after a reboot:* nothing to do (mounted by UUID). *Two drives with one UUID (a clone):* both are `failed` with reason `duplicate-uuid`, never merged.

**R10. Two storage nodes go down together (a power event) [D].** *Symptoms:* two `NodeDown` within a minute; one live node. *Automatic:* nothing commits (the store has one live member and refuses writes: strict synchronous replication); every operation that needs it, including mutable-bucket reads, immutable DELETE and seals, answers `503 Retry-After`; hot reads of segments with a copy on the survivor and immutable-bucket GETs from its index are served; immutable-bucket PUTs cannot gather `min_acks` copies and answer `503`; cold reads fail (the survivor holds at most 3 of 8 shards of a stripe) and the fetch engine waits (non-definitive misses, 4.6), so jobs are delayed, not failed; no repair starts. *On power return:* follow R16's order (etcd, then Patroni and Postgres, then the tierstore daemons are free to start in any order and retry meanwhile); each node starts and is `indexing`, takes part in fences and replication at once; fenced segments are sealed by the CAS as soon as the store has a primary again; hot restart converges segments (adopt sealed rows, cut uncommitted tails); clients are served as each index is built; the downs count as one correlated event (no flap score, no probation). *Operator:* restore power; start the nodes in any order (R16 lists what each unit waits for); check `store metadata status` and then `store health` until all three words are clean. Nothing else is needed: no acknowledged write is lost (every acknowledged record was durable on two devices, and every acknowledged metadata commit on two members). *Do not:* mark anything `out` or start a rebuild while nodes are coming back; do not run a planned reboot on a third node in the same window; do not promote a Postgres member by hand.

**R11. The metadata primary is lost (automatic failover) [D].** *Symptoms:* a burst of `503 Retry-After` from the S3 front on operations that need the store; the alert for a store without a primary if it lasts past the client retry window; Patroni's history shows a leader change. *Automatic:* Patroni's leader key (in etcd) expires after `ttl` (20 s, up to `loop_wait` (5 s) more until a standby looks), the old primary is demoted or reset by its watchdog if it still runs, and the synchronous standby, which holds every acknowledged commit, is promoted: no acknowledged commit is lost; the third member becomes the synchronous standby if the second was the one lost; the metadata clients (`tierstore-meta`, 10.16) reconnect to the new primary by their host list within `metadata.retry_window` and retry their idempotent transactions; the reconciler lease expires and another node takes it; open hot segments continue (their PUTs do not need the store), seals wait. Target and reference: back in 30 s [I, drill 13 measures; with an etcd re-election the detection alone is about 27 to 30 s, so the target is not a derived bound]. The loss of the synchronous standby stalls commits for up to one more Patroni loop (`loop_wait`) before the third member is made synchronous, and on NOW etcd's election timeout is measured under build load (drill 13, T-S23). *What the operator checks:* (1) `store metadata status`: one primary, the synchronous standby streaming, the third member streaming or re-initialising, **the metadata incarnation unchanged and no node frozen** (a frozen node means the promoted member was behind, which strict synchronous replication prevents, and needs R14); (2) `repair_stalled_on_metadata` is clear within minutes and `hot_fenced_unsealed_blocks` drains to zero (the seals that waited are committed); (3) the old primary comes back as a standby (rewound by `pg_rewind`) or shows `needs reinit`, which is R12; (4) the cause of the loss (node, disk, network) is handled by R3, R7 or R4. *Do not:* promote a member by hand while Patroni is running (a manual promotion of a lagging member is only `store metadata promote-lone`, which freezes every node, R6); delete a replication slot to "make it faster"; run `fsck --apply` (nothing needs it unless a node is frozen). A planned switchover (`store metadata switchover`) is the same with a few seconds of `503`.

**R12. A metadata replica is re-created (re-init) [D].** *When:* a member's data is lost or unusable (its disk was replaced, R7; it diverged and `pg_rewind` could not rejoin it; its standby fell behind a stuck slot, R13; a new member for a replaced or added node, R5, R6). *Automatic:* Patroni marks the member `needs reinit` and does not retry forever. *Operator:* check that the **other** standby is healthy and streaming first (a re-init with the store down to two healthy members must not be started while either is unhealthy, because strict synchronous mode then has no second member to acknowledge); run Patroni's reinit for that member (a base backup from the primary, or a delta restore from the pgBackRest repository, which spares the primary's I/O), one member at a time; wait until `store metadata status` shows it streaming and caught up; for a new etcd member, add it with etcd's member-add procedure before Patroni's. *Done:* three members streaming, the synchronous one named, the lag near zero. *Do not:* re-init two members at once; copy a data directory by hand from another node (its identity and slots would be wrong); reuse the data of a member that was restored from an old image (R7). Moving a member from a NOW node to a LATER node (T-S12) is this same procedure: add the new member, wait, remove the old.

**R13. The metadata WAL partition fills, or a replication slot is stuck [D].** *Symptoms:* the partition-fill alert (the hostkeeper's disk alert at 70% of the metadata partition) or `repair_stalled_on_metadata` with the store refusing writes because `pg_wal` is full. *Causes:* a replication slot kept by a standby that is down (it retains WAL for a standby that is not reading it), or a failing archive (the pgBackRest repository unreachable, so archived segments cannot be recycled). *Automatic:* the retention bound of slots (`max_slot_wal_keep_size`, set so that a dead standby's slot cannot fill the partition; the value is [I], T-S23 sets it against the partition size) invalidates a stuck slot instead of the partition filling; Patroni then re-inits that standby when asked; the archive failure shows as an alert on `archive_command` failures. *Operator:* (1) read `store metadata status` for the slot's holder and the archive state; (2) if the archive is failing, fix the repository (the off-box target, its credentials, the network) first: the WAL then drains by itself; (3) if a slot is stuck for a standby that will not return soon, drop that slot (the verb prints what it frees and what the standby will need: R12) and re-init the standby when it is back; (4) if the partition is already full and Postgres has stopped, do not delete WAL files by hand: free space only by removing Postgres server log files (`log/`), rotated Patroni and etcd logs, stale `pg_basebackup` target directories and non-WAL files that are not part of the cluster (core dumps, temporary files), and never anything under `pg_wal`, `pg_xact`, `base/`, `global/`, the data directory, a replication slot or the archive spool and directory (WAL not yet archived is lost if it goes); if that frees too little, extend the partition, and let Postgres replay; if the primary cannot start, fail over to the synchronous standby (R11) and re-init this member (R12). *Done:* writes resume, the partition is under its alert, the archive is current, three members stream. *Do not:* delete files from `pg_wal`; set `fsync` off or `archive_mode` off to escape; ignore the alert, because a full primary partition is a metadata outage.

**R14. A point-in-time restore and `metadata fsck` [D].** *When:* the metadata store is lost entirely (all three members), or its content is wrong (a mistaken operator action, a faulty migration) and the owner decides to go back to a time. A restore **rolls the store back**: rows committed after the target time are gone, while the disks still hold the data those rows described; the incarnation, the rollback detection and fsck exist for exactly this (10.16). *Steps (a held step, owner present):* (1) stop the tierstore daemons on every node (clients get `503`, which they retry for at least 60 s and then fail; announce the window); (2) `store metadata restore --to TIME` restores the pgBackRest backup and WAL to the chosen second into a new cluster, brings up Patroni and etcd as at first start (R16), and **raises the metadata incarnation** in the restored `cluster` row and sets `frozen`; (3) start the daemons: each node sees a different incarnation (or a `txn_seq` below what it remembers) and **freezes every destructive action** (no unlink, no stripe free, no orphan sweep, no trash purge, no block drop, no tombstone deletion, no id reuse); reads, immutable PUTs and repair copies continue, and `metadata_frozen` pages, which is expected; (4) `store metadata fsck` (dry-run by default) scans every node's `NODE` files, segment files and trailers, the records and markers of open and fenced segments with no trailer, `blk/*.idx`, shard footers, large files, commit markers, the bucket ids on disk and the tombstones recorded in each node's `index/tomb-applied`, and prints every difference against the restored rows: blocks, hot copies, stripes and shards that exist on disk with no row (or with a different row), rows with no files (classified *gone* and freed by `--apply`, never rebuilt or repaired: one alert `fsck_rows_without_files`, no P0 jobs, 10.16), live counts, mutable heads, id allocators (blocks, stripes, nodes and buckets) and each node's counter, the sequences, and every bucket id on disk with no row; review the report; (5) `store metadata fsck --apply` (it completes only if facts were read from at least `max(N - hot.min_acks + 1, hot.min_acks)` nodes, 2 of 3 at `min_acks = 2` and 3 of 3 at `min_acks = replicas`; below that floor it stays a dry-run report, and with a node skipped it classifies no row as *gone*) writes the corrections: it rebuilds blocks, hot copies, stripes and shards from footers, live counts from trailers minus tombstones, **mutable heads from commit markers** (the highest version per key whose record exists, so acknowledged mutable writes after the restore point are recovered), and deletions from the tombstones in each node's `index/tomb-applied`; it moves every id allocator and every node's counter above the maximum seen, sets `txn_seq` above every node's remembered value and `ts.head_version` above the maximum marker version, recomputes the live counts of mutable-bucket blocks from the rebuilt heads and markers (the other records are handed to the age-gated sweep), **raises the incarnation again and clears `frozen`**; you bind unbound bucket ids with `store bucket create <name> --mode ... --id <n>` from fsck's report (the nodes' `buckets` record gives name and mode), and `--apply` refuses to complete while an id is unbound unless you name it to skip; nodes adopt the new incarnation and destructive actions resume (the orphan sweeper still waits `destage.orphan_age`, 1 day, unreferenced under an unchanged incarnation, before it deletes anything); (6) run the live-key audit and the canaries. *What can be lost:* rows that existed only in the store and on no disk (membership edits and health facts after the restore point: re-enter them); the repair queue is recomputed. *Done:* `metadata_frozen` clear, `store health` all clean, the fsck dry-run reports no difference. *Do not:* start the daemons with `--no-freeze` (there is no such flag); run `--apply` before reading the dry-run; delete anything while the freeze holds. The restore drill (before production once, then quarterly, ending with fsck) is this runbook on a copy.

**R15. The yearly major upgrade of Postgres [D].** The procedure is the one of 10.16 (build the new packages and stanza; full backup; announce; wait for the asynchronous standby to catch up, then stop it and keep it on the old major as the rollback point; stop Patroni, `pg_upgrade --link` on the primary, start, `ANALYZE`; re-create the synchronous standby from the new primary, run the conformance and drill checks; re-create the third member). *Operator:* schedule it with the owner at a quiet time and announce the window; practise it on a restored copy first (R14's restore into a scratch cluster) and keep the measured duration; during the window (target at most 15 minutes) every operation that needs the store answers `503 Retry-After`, while immutable-bucket PUTs and GETs continue; check `store metadata status` for the new major, the synchronous standby and the backups of the new stanza (take a full backup at once); keep the old-major standby until the checks pass, and do not delete the old data directory for a week; rollback is `store metadata promote-lone` on the old-major standby followed by `store metadata fsck` (R14), never a bare promote. *Do not:* upgrade in the same window as a tierstore upgrade or any node roll; start the upgrade with a member not streaming; skip the full backup. The minor upgrade is a rolling restart, standbys first, then a switchover, then the old primary (10.16), and is part of 24.15 item 10.

**R16. A power event: Postgres and etcd cold start order [D].** *All three nodes down (or two, R10).* *Order that matters:* (1) **etcd** first on at least two of the three nodes: Patroni cannot hold or take a leader key without it, and no member of the store is promoted before it has a quorum; (2) **Patroni and Postgres**: Patroni starts each Postgres as a standby or as the leader as the etcd state says; with strict synchronous replication only the former primary or the standby recorded as synchronous in etcd can be promoted, because only they hold every acknowledged commit, so the cluster waits for one of them rather than promoting a lagging member (a stall is the safe outcome); if both are slow to come back the store stays down and everything that needs it answers `503`; (3) **the tierstore daemons**, in any order and at any time: they retry the store for `metadata.retry_window` and then answer `503` per request, so hostkeeper orders them after Patroni only to keep the logs quiet. *Automatic:* once a primary exists the nodes see an unchanged incarnation and a `txn_seq` at or above theirs (no freeze), fenced segments are sealed by the CAS, hot restart converges, the reconciler lease is taken, `metadata_frozen` stays clear. *If etcd's data is gone as well* (both of its members' disks lost): the operator identifies the Postgres member with the highest committed position and re-creates the Patroni state around it by Patroni's documented procedure (a held step, practised in drill 11), then re-inits the other members (R12); if no member holds the latest commits, R14. *Do not:* start Postgres by hand outside Patroni; start Patroni before etcd has a quorum and "fix" the resulting errors by editing its configuration; let the daemons freeze-loop a restart (a frozen node is a decision, not a crash).

### 24.13 Tests: simulator, fault injection, drills
**Deterministic cluster simulator (T-S14, the primary test).** One process, single-threaded and seeded: N storage nodes whose production state machines (the reconciler, reconciliation, hot replication, the fence and seal protocol, placement, membership validation, the metadata client) run against simulated disk, network and clock traits and a **model of the metadata store**, so any seed reproduces its run (in the manner of FoundationDB's simulation testing). **The store model** is a linearizable table store with the behaviour that matters: transactions with conditional updates on row versions, an outage window, an **ambiguous commit** (the connection is lost during `COMMIT`; the commit may or may not have happened), a **failover** (the primary changes after a delay) with and without the loss of an asynchronous tail (a configured lagging standby promoted), and a **point-in-time rollback** to an earlier state with the disks left as they are; it implements the database clock, the incarnation and the `txn_seq` rule. Faults: a crash at any step boundary (every step of 24.5's commit protocol included), drive EIO, latent sector errors, stalls, symmetric and asymmetric partitions (nodes from nodes and nodes from the store), message delay, duplication and reordering, clock skew, full disks, power loss of all nodes or of two, a long-absent drive, an NVMe replaced or restored from an old image, flapping at chosen periods, a drive burst, and a double fault during rebuild. Workload: PUT, GET and DELETE on both bucket modes with destage, GC and promote, against an oracle of every acknowledged write. **Checked after every event:** I1 (every acknowledged key reads correctly within tolerance, and beyond it fails only as `503` or in `store lost-report`, never with wrong bytes), I2 to I6, I9 and I10; **the oracle checks freeze and fsck:** after any rollback no destructive action runs until fsck has finished, and the rows after fsck equal the oracle's; **liveness** (with faults stopped, every queue empties and margins return to full within a bound set by data and shares in simulated steps); **boundedness** (repair concurrency and shares never exceed their budgets). The same seed gives the same trace; a fixed seed list runs on every PR and a large random batch runs on the farm, not per PR.

Named scenarios, each stating what it proves: (a) a reboot inside the budget copies nothing beyond margin-triggered items, and those are repaid by adoption; (b) a node flapping every 2 minutes for 30 h reaches the persisted budget, and a viewer restart in the middle does not reset it; (c) a node cut off from the store commits nothing and a node on the small side of a replication partition acknowledges no hot write; (d) a node declared lost returns mid-rebuild and the queue shrinks by adoption with no copy-back; (e) a returned shard of a freed stripe is never served; (f) a crash at each commit step leaves only temporary files and never deletes the only copy; (g) a margin-0 rebuild meeting a latent error reports `cell_lost` and serves no wrong byte; (h) a 4/4 layout is accepted only when `m >= max_shards_per_domain` and is repaid by adoption or moves with no decode; (i) a component away for 60 days rejoins and is adopted by its rows, with freed and dropped copies quarantined; (j) hot writes below `min_acks` answer `503`; (k) an old-incarnation NVMe image is refused, and a store rolled back to an earlier state freezes every destructive action until fsck has run (the freeze scenario of T-S15); (l) **an ambiguous commit** retried with the same operation id applies once, whether or not the first attempt had committed, for seal, stripe commit, repair commit, tombstone and mutable PUT; (m) **a margin-1 stripe is queued at once when a node goes down, before any `lost` row**; (n) **two nodes rebooting within a minute (R10)**: no commit while the store lacks a primary, commits resume with two members, no flap score, no probation, no acknowledged write lost; (o) **all nodes flapping together never leave fewer eligible targets than `min_acks`**, and a repair job whose every target is flapping takes the least-flapping one (the X2 cap); (p) **adding a fourth node during a node-loss rebuild** changes no store member, seal or stripe commit and does not stall the rebuild; an unrelated fact does not restart an in-flight item; (q) **a drive burst** on one node holds its declarations; (r) **the membership validator** refuses `in` to `out`, two state changes in one epoch, duplicate ids and UUIDs, a capacity off by more than 2x and the drain of a node that holds a needed Postgres member, and a revert inside `apply_delay` cancels every consequence; (s) **salvage**: a partly readable `failed` drive restores a margin-0 stripe, and `safe-to-destroy` refuses while it is needed; (t) **verified margin after a reboot** returns to its pre-outage value without a full re-scrub; (u) **a node on a fresh hot device allocates no block id it ever used before**, and neither does fsck, and a node on a blank device or with a regenerated node incarnation, restarted with Postgres unreachable, allocates no id and opens no segment until the store is read; (v) **misdirected writes and byte corruption** (on disk and on the wire) are detected and repaired and never served; (w) **mass return and mass restart** (every node and drive back at once, and 50 restarts in a row) trigger no deletion and leave every queue drained in the quiet phase; (x) **the corruption fixture** (a shard corrupted behind the code's back) is a negative case that must fail the run if nothing catches it; (y) **the capacity invariant** stops admission at the post-loss ceiling and a node loss afterwards rebuilds fully; (z) **a slow drive** (a fraction of its speed, no errors) is avoided for reads and evacuated, and a node clock skewed beyond `clock.max_skew` alerts and changes no ordering outcome. Scenarios for the store: (aa) **a seal race**: two sealers under a partition (a stopped writer and a taking-over replica) fence and race one compare-and-set; exactly one sealed row exists and both converge to it; (ab) **a metadata outage**: immutable PUTs continue into new segments, fenced segments stay unsealed and undestaged, `hot_fenced_unsealed_blocks` rises and drains after the outage, and no mutable operation answers anything but `503`; (ac) **failover without loss** (strict synchronous): no acknowledged transaction is missing afterwards, with the clients' retries applied once; (ad) **failover with the loss of an asynchronous tail** (a lagging member promoted, which the configuration forbids and the model can force): the node whose `txn_seq` is ahead freezes and `metadata_frozen` fires, and fsck repairs the rows; (ae) **point-in-time rollback**: no destructive action before fsck, no acknowledged mutable write lost after fsck (heads from commit markers), no deleted immutable key resurrected (tombstones from the node indexes), no block id reused; (af) **relocate against delete** of one record serialize on the source block row, and a delete naming an old record id finds the new one through `relocations`; (ag) **a create race on an immutable bucket** with different bytes (membership views that differ for an epoch change) is detected by the reconciler's per-node index comparison (24.5), the record with the lower record id is kept, the other is tombstoned, `store_key_conflict_total` rises, and with equal bytes only space is wasted; (ah) **a stalled reconciler**: a holder paused past its lease cannot commit, the new holder continues, and no job commits twice.

**Real-code fault injection.** The fake storage node of T-I with scripted hangs, crashes, EIO and slow answers, on real sockets on loopback and real files in the test's scratch directory, with injected clocks; it covers the drive scheduler, the peer protocol and the real file formats. The metadata client is tested against a real Postgres started by the test helper in the test's scratch directory (a failover is a restart on another port, with the client's host list), so its reconnect, retry-window and idempotent-retry paths run against the real driver, and against the simulator's store model for the ambiguous-commit cases.

**Farm drills (T-S15; real hardware, held steps, never per PR).** Each drill is a Rust binary run through hostkeeper or as a held step, first on the LATER cluster before production traffic; on NOW only the reboot and single-drive drills, plus the store drills 13 to 16 once on the three NOW nodes that hold the members. Pass criteria are metric states, never durations (a duration a drill measures, such as the failover time, is reported as a number against its target and reviewed by a person; it never fails a test). 1. `kill -9` the daemon during a rebuild: the queue is recomputed, temporary files removed, margins return to full, I1 holds. 2. Power-cycle one node under load: no full rebuild, debt repaid by adoption, verified margin restored, no unreadable keys. 3. **Power-cycle two nodes together (R10)**: as scenario (n). 4. Offline a drive through the kernel interface during reads: `failed`, `lost` after the timer, the rebuild completes at its share, demand latency within 4.3's targets. 5. Partition one node: the node cut off acknowledges nothing it cannot, the rest proceed, the rows reconverge and nothing is frozen. 6. A flap generator for an hour: `flapping` per the rule, nothing but margin-triggered repair copied. 7. Add a drive and a node: no existing shard moves, the ramp cap holds, a rebalance plan is correct and abortable. 8. NVMe replacement on a spare node: R7 end to end, including the member re-creation of R12. 9. A full node-lost rebuild onto two nodes before production, measured (it replaces the estimates of 24.7). 10. A soak with the simulator's fault schedule, the canaries and the live-key audit: zero invariant violations. 11. **Cold start, once before first production:** power all three storage nodes off and on following R16, including the etcd-lost Patroni re-creation of R12. 12. **Asymmetric partition:** traffic broken in one direction between one pair of nodes (the pair may include a metadata member and its etcd peer): Patroni and the tierstore daemons both end with one primary and no freeze. 13. **Kill the metadata primary under load** (`kill -9` of Postgres, and a power cut of its node): a new primary, no acknowledged commit lost, clients that retry for 60 s see no error, the old primary rejoins (R11); the time to a writable primary is recorded against the 30 s target. 14. **Partition one metadata node** (the primary, then a standby) from the other two, and from etcd only: a minority primary is demoted or reset before the other side promotes (no two primaries), the minority side commits nothing. 15. **Fill the WAL partition** on a standby and on the primary (a stuck slot, a failing archive): the alert fires before the stop, the slot bound protects the primary, R13 restores service. 16. **Restore and fsck:** R14 end to end onto scratch hardware, ending with a clean dry-run; once before production and quarterly. Drills after production run quarterly, owned by whoever owns releases, with an alert when the last run is older than 120 days. Results are metrics and a report, and their numbers replace [E] values here.

### 24.14 What the review required, and where it is resolved
| Review finding | Resolution | Where |
|---|---|---|
| X1: the P0 class and the `lost` timer contradicted each other | Margin-triggered repair: below-threshold items are queued at once, independent of `lost`; the reconciler's queue rows carry the margin | 24.5, scenario (m) |
| X2 / F-FLAP1-3: the damper could remove every target; no exit; budget reset by restarts | Preference with a floor; the reconciler takes the least-flapping target when all are flapping (never zero) and tickets it; correlated downs count once; page at 6 h; the budget is rows with database-clock times, so a restart or a failover does not reset it | 24.3, 24.5, R4, scenarios (b), (o) |
| X3: three-node commits need both survivors | **Closed by construction, stated honestly.** There is no commit protocol of ours that needs "both survivors" any more: a commit is a transaction of the metadata store, which keeps committing on its two surviving members when one node is down (and pauses for a failover of at most about 30 s when the primary or the synchronous standby is the one down). With two nodes down nothing commits, the same outcome as Q5's default; `repair_stalled_on_metadata` pages and a fourth node plus a rebuilt third member is the fix | 24 (opening), 24.5, R10, R11 |
| X4 / F-AMNESIA: a blank or restored acceptor could vote again | **Closed by construction:** there are no acceptors and no votes. A blank node holds no decision state; the one state that decides is the store's rows, and a store restored from a backup (the only analogue of an amnesiac) is caught by the metadata incarnation, the `txn_seq` rollback check and the freeze of destructive actions until fsck | 10.16, 24.4, R14, scenarios (k), (ae) |
| F-VOTERS: voter sets undefined; adding a node could stall commits | **Closed by construction:** there are no voter sets. The store's three members are fixed by Patroni, and adding a storage node changes none of them | 24.8, R5, scenario (p) |
| F-EPOCH / X5: every fact restarted in-flight placements | **Closed by construction:** facts are rows and carry no epoch; only the membership epoch exists, and refusal is by content (only a difference that changes the item's eligibility) | 24.1, scenario (p) |
| F-BOOT1-2 and R10: two nodes rebooting together | Fence and repair work served in `indexing`; probation skipped after a correlated event; the store's cold start order (R16) and the safe stall while a primary is missing; runbook R10 | 24.2, 24.3, R10, R16, scenario (n), drills 3 and 11 |
| F-MINORITY-READ | Stated per bucket mode: an immutable-bucket node serves what its index holds (bytes never change; a deleted key until it pulls the tombstone, bounded by the partition plus `tomb_poll`), a mutable-bucket read answers `503` without the head; nothing is acknowledged that needs the store or `min_acks` | 24.4, scenario (c) |
| F-VALIDATE / F-DELAY: hand-edited membership could do harm | The membership is changed only by verbs, in one transaction with CAS on the epoch, one change per epoch, no direct `out`, duplicate and capacity checks, a 10-minute apply delay with cancel | 24.9, scenario (r) |
| F-SALVAGE: a failed drive was the last source and was forbidden | `store drive salvage`; last-resort source for P0; `safe-to-destroy` refuses while needed | 24.5, R1, scenario (s) |
| F-BURST: an HBA fault became a node loss through drive declarations | Burst guard holds declarations and pages | 24.3, R9, scenario (q) |
| F-ADOPT / F-VERIFY / F-TOMB: about 47 TB of avoidable copies; verified-margin noise; retention wiping cold shards | Debt repaid by adopting the returning copy in one conditional transaction; persisted last-verified time; no age limit at all, because rows define validity and a tombstone lives exactly as long as its block | 24.4, scenarios (d), (i), (t) |
| F-CEIL: auto-lost could leave no room for the next drive | `rebuild.auto_ceiling` 0.85 for automatic `lost` | 24.3 guard 2 |
| Seal-and-roll churn under flapping | No seal-and-roll for a replica `suspect` more than twice in an hour | 24.3 |
| Destage urgency with a node down | Blocks below `hot.replicas` are destaged promptly; nothing else is held back | 24.5 |
| Rollback of the store (a restore, a promoted lagging member) and orphans or id reuse after it | Metadata incarnation, `txn_seq` check, freeze of destructive actions, `metadata fsck`, orphan age | 10.16, 24.5, R14, scenarios (ad), (ae) |
| A paused reconciler committing after its lease | The lease is checked inside every committing transaction by the database clock | 24.5, scenario (ah) |

**What this section changes elsewhere (all in the tasks of section 18):** the `shards` rows carry a blake3 per shard, matching the one the shard footer already carries (schema of T-S22); the `NODE` identity file gains the node incarnation, the metadata incarnation and the highest `txn_seq` seen (T-S2); the `health`, `repair_queue`, `reconciler_lease`, `ops`, `audit` and `trash` tables and the `stripes.layout_debt`, `degraded_since`, `move_to`, `blocks.degraded_since`, `shards.blake3`, `nodes.pending_state`, `pending_effective_at` and `tomb_applied` columns, the `sweep_candidates` and `sync_probe` tables and the `heads` marker columns are part of the schema of T-S22 (10.16); the configuration keys `rebuild.p0_share`, `rebuild.auto_ceiling`, `rebuild.drive_missing_after`, `rebuild.node_down_after` (cumulative), `rebuild.max_items`, `maintenance.max`, `surplus_grace`, `scrub.*`, `rebalance.share`, `membership.apply_delay`, `new_node_weight_cap`, `reconcile.interval`, `reconcile.lease_renew`, `reconcile.lease`, `reconcile.executor_wait` and `health.report_after` (the keys of the metadata store are those of 10.16); the new metrics `metadata_audit_differences{kind}` (kinds `missed_tombstone`, `missing_record` and those of I9) and `metadata_frozen` (registered by T-S24, as in section 12), the stall gauge `repair_stalled_on_metadata` (T-S25), and the alerts on both (T-S25); the metrics join the table of section 12 with the tasks that register them; and 10.13's risks gain "the NVMe holds the metadata store's member of its node (24.6, 10.16)" and "after a node loss `4+4` has no node-loss margin and the store has no spare member until a fourth node exists".

### 24.15 What to build first for operations, and the lessons it rests on [D]
A study of public storage incidents and practice (the S3 outage of 2017, Cloudflare R2's incidents of February and March 2025, DigitalOcean's NYC3 Spaces incident, GitLab's 2017 database outage, Dropbox's Magic Pocket papers on verification, deletion checking and failover testing, Backblaze's drive statistics, TigerBeetle's simulation testing, Huang et al. on gray failure, and Ceph's operations documentation), followed by a review sized to three storage nodes and a small team, gives ten additions in priority order (data loss or silent stall prevented per unit of work). **Scale rule:** each is a small mechanism inside the existing daemon, its verbs and its simulator; nothing here is a second control plane (no plan-hashing framework, no separate referee daemon, no runbook workflow engine, no durability-budget freeze). Each names the failure it prevents and the test that shows it.

1. **The four unknowns, resolved in this document.**
   - *Block ids are never reused.* A `block_id` is (node id, per-node counter) (10.2); the counter is persisted with the replay mark, which lags the ids handed out by up to one flush interval, so **every start** (not only a fresh hot device, a new node incarnation, 24.2, or `metadata fsck`) first learns the highest id its node id ever allocated from the `NODE` file, the replay mark, the segment files, `big/` names and `blk/*.idx` on its own NVMe and, when the store is reachable, the store's rows, and allocates above the maximum before its first allocation (only a node with intact disk history, that is a `NODE` file, a counter or replay mark and its segment files present and an unchanged node incarnation, may allocate while the store is down; a node on a fresh or replaced hot device, with a regenerated node incarnation or with its `NODE` file or counter missing allocates nothing and opens no segment until it has read the maximum block and stripe id for its node id from Postgres and from at least one peer, starts above it and answers reads only, with its new hot writes going to other nodes meanwhile), so an id names one block forever, and no central allocator is needed (a new segment opens while the store is down on every node with intact disk history). Test: simulator scenario (u), including the restart case: crash after allocating ids and before a flush, restart, and assert that no allocated id repeats; and the blank-device case: a node restarts on a blank device with Postgres unreachable and allocates no id and opens no segment until the store is read, then allocates only above the previous maximum (the same case with a regenerated node incarnation).
   - *Bytes at a record id never change.* A record id is `(block id, offset)` (10.2), and no write ever changes bytes in place: an immutable bucket accepts only creates (a PUT of different bytes under a live key is `409 KeyImmutable`, an `If-None-Match: *` PUT of a live key is `412`, 10.9), and a mutable bucket's overwrite writes a new record and moves the head row, so a reader that holds an old record id reads the old bytes until the record is freed. A re-PUT after a DELETE is a new life, and the CAS service's pack keys are content-derived. The deletion guard of item 4 therefore covers every way live bytes can go.
   - *Space is reclaimed* by GC: stripe frees and rewrites (10.5), and the `cold_fill_efficiency` metric shows what is dead.
   - *Index coverage:* on LATER (`hot.replicas = 3` on three nodes) every storage node indexes every key; on NOW (`hot.replicas = 2` on six nodes) each key is indexed on its two replica nodes, so `LOCATE` walks `rank(K)` (10.6).
   - *The durability target and failure model* are the owner's decision (Q5 in the decision table); this document is written against its default.
2. **Margin-first repair (24.5) and a closed set of blocking reasons.** Every item that is not progressing carries exactly one reason from a closed set, each with its one-line next action, shown by `store repair status` and `store repair explain`: `capacity` (add capacity or free space, 24.8), `no-metadata` (a commit waits for the metadata store, which is in failover, unreachable or short of members: R11, R16), `frozen` (the node's destructive actions are frozen: `metadata fsck`, R14), `no-source` (fewer than `k` readable shards: `store drive salvage`, 24.5), `target-ineligible` (every target is `new` or full; flapping targets are taken at the floor, 24.5), `paused` (who paused it and until when), `budget` (waiting for its share: nothing to do), `burst-hold` (operator decision, R9), `maintenance` (the window's end). A new reason is a code change and a new line in this list. Test: every reason is produced by a simulator scenario, and `explain` names it.
3. **The capacity invariant: fill after losing the largest failure domain stays under the ceiling.** The projected fill of the survivors if the largest failure domain (on LATER a node; per drive within a node) were lost and rebuilt must stay below `cold.rebuild_fill_ceiling`; this replaces the flat 92% as the capacity rule. **PUT admission enforces it from day one**: when a new stripe could no longer be placed without breaking it, destage holds and the hot marks of 10.5 take over (503, then 507), so the store refuses writes before it loses the room to repair. `store_days_to_full` (the guard's free logical bytes over the trailing 7-day net write rate) tickets below `capacity.lead_time` (default 60 days, the hardware lead time) and pages below 14 days. The metadata partition has its own alert (R13). Test: a simulator scenario fills the cluster to the invariant and asserts admission stops there, and a node loss afterwards rebuilds fully.
4. **One narrow guard on the four destructive primitives, trash before delete, node-scoped limits.** One `guard` function, with a witness type only it can produce, sits in front of exactly four primitives: deleting a shard or copy, marking a node or drive `lost`, changing the membership, and purging trash. It re-checks current state at apply (no plan hashing): a delete is scoped by `(block, generation, drive)` and needs the `Redundancy` witness (24.5) and an unfrozen node; `lost` passes the guards of 24.3; a membership change passes 24.9; a purge is allowed only for records older than the retention. **Deletes go to `trash/` first:** a deleted shard, copy or large file is renamed into `trash/` on its own drive with a record (what, why, which verb or timer; the `trash` table of the store holds `purge_after` on the database clock), kept 7 days (`trash.retention`), then purged; scrub's audit pass alerts on any unlink that has no trash record. **Node-scoped limits:** no action takes a stripe below margin 1; one node out at a time; the drive-burst hold (24.3). `ok-to-stop` is enforced by the daemon (on SIGTERM it seals and flushes, and if `ok-to-stop` is false it logs the refusal, keeps serving for `stop.grace` and stops only when it becomes true or the grace ends) and by the update hook (which does not stop a node that is not `ok-to-stop`, and which switches a Postgres primary over first). **Break-glass:** a held script, time-limited and logged, that overrides one named guard check once; reviewed afterwards. Tests: a wrong delete injected in a fixture is refused by the guard and, with the guard bypassed in the fixture, caught by the trash audit; a 50-node mass restart in the simulator (scenario (w)) triggers no deletion.
5. **Simulator additions that find real bugs (T-S14):** a two-phase run (a fault phase, then a quiet phase in which every queue must drain and every margin return: the liveness check of 24.13); **misdirected writes** (a write that lands at the wrong offset or file) and **byte corruption** on disk and on the wire; **mass return** (every node and drive back at once after a long outage); **the store model** with its ambiguous commits, failovers and rollbacks (24.13); and **a corruption fixture that must fail the run**: a seed that corrupts a shard behind the code's back, run on every PR, fails the run if no invariant or audit catches it, and a seed that disables the freeze and must lose data (the negative case of T-S15).
6. **Verified reads on every path, and an independent canary prober.** Every byte that moves is verified where it lands: client fills by cell digest (3.3), `digest=none` objects by the serving node's cell crc (9), repair sources and outputs (24.5), evacuation and rebalance copies, hot whole-file copies (`SEG-COPY`), salvage and adoption (24.4) by per-cell crc and per-shard blake3. **The prober** is a separate small process (`ts-prober`, its own crate) that uses only the public paths (the S3 front and a FUSE mount): it keeps a fixed corpus of known-content objects per hot and cold class (and one small mutable-bucket object it overwrites and deletes under a known sequence) and reads each hourly through every path (healthy, degraded, with a component masked by a debug header, through FUSE), and reads a few random old objects verified by hash; its results go to the alerting of hostkeeper, not through tierstore's metrics, and a **dead-man alert** pages when no prober success has arrived for 2 hours. It retries `503` as a client must (section 23) and counts a failover as a retried success. It survives tierstore's failure, not the loss of the box, switch or power it runs on (it runs on a machine outside the storage tier, such as an operator workstation or utility host).
7. **Postgres backups off the storage nodes, and a restore drill before production.** The metadata store is backed up by pgBackRest to a repository off the three storage nodes and independent of tierstore (an S3-compatible bucket or a file server elsewhere, configured by the operator): weekly full, daily differential, continuous WAL archiving with `archive_timeout = 60s`, retention 30 days, so the store can be restored to any second of the last 30 days and a total loss of the three members costs at most about a minute of WAL (10.16). This is the answer to losing all three metadata members at once, and it relies on the disks as the second copy of the truth: the rows written in the lost minute are recovered by `metadata fsck` from footers, commit markers and node indexes (R14). **Drill:** once on the farm before first production, the restore of R14 on scratch hardware ending in a clean fsck dry-run, then quarterly (drill 16 of 24.13), and once the cold start of R16 with all three nodes off and on (drill 11).
8. **An operator path that does not need tierstore, and jitter.** `tierstore store status --local` reads the node's own state files (the `NODE` file, its cached copy of the membership document and bucket table, the last exported metrics, the drive and node states, the event history) directly from disk, with no daemon and no network, so an operator can see a node whose daemon will not start, or whose store is unreachable. **Jitter:** the reconciler lease attempts, the metadata client's reconnect backoff, the backoff of a repair item with no target, client retries (4.6, section 23) and daemon restarts after a crash all add a random 0 to 50% to their delay, so many nodes or clients never retry in step.
9. **Hardware audit.** At start and daily each storage node records, per device: model, firmware, the volatile write-cache setting, whether the device reports power-loss protection, and the flush behaviour; it exports them and alerts on a write cache enabled on a device without power-loss protection whose flushes are not honoured, and on two firmware versions of one model in the cluster. **On LATER the NVMe drives are specified with power-loss protection, and group commit, `hot.min_acks`, `batch_max` and the store's default `synchronous_commit = on` are sized on that assumption: the audit refuses a LATER hot device that does not report it (the node stays `new` and the alert names the device), and does not retune anything silently.** On NOW (SATA SSD without power-loss protection) the audit accepts the device, every `fdatasync` and `fsync` is honest, and the cost is millisecond commit latency; nothing turns `fsync` off on either profile. (DigitalOcean's NYC3 incident was a drive cache setting.)
10. **Upgrades as a rule and a checklist, and a format policy.** Every storage-node upgrade is a controlled single-node failure, scheduled as one: one node at a time; `ok-to-stop` first, which for a node holding the Postgres primary means a Patroni switchover first (the update hook runs it, so a planned roll never fails the primary over by timeout); after the node is back, a completed scrub of its margin-1 stripes before the next node, and the store's three members streaming; the previous binary kept for rollback. **Format policy:** no on-start migration of on-disk formats (a new format is written by new writes and by background rewrite only), **no on-start migration of the store's schema** (additive changes only, run by `store metadata migrate` with its own role, before the binaries that need them), and every release reads the formats of the next one where they changed additively (N reads N+1 for the wire, record, footer and schema formats, and N+1 runs on N's schema until the migrate verb has run), with mixed-version tests for those formats only. Upgrading one node of three is honest about its cost: while it is away, 3-shard stripes run at margin 1, hot writes need both other nodes, and the store runs on two members. The yearly Postgres major upgrade is R15.

**Lessons added to the mechanisms above.**
- **Slow disk, not only dead disk.** The commonest HDD failure is a drive that still answers at a fraction of its speed. Each storage node compares each drive's read service time with the median of the drives of the same model on the node; a drive above 3 times that median for 10 minutes is `slow`: the scheduler stops sending it reads that another copy or a degraded read can serve more cheaply, the client's budget treats it as in decrease (4.3), and after 24 hours as `slow` an evacuation starts (24.7). Peer-observed latency (from `x-ts-load` and part responses) is compared with the drive's own report, so a node that answers but is sick (gray failure) is visible as a gap between the two. The same comparison watches the metadata partition: a standby whose commit latency is far above the others' is reported before it slows every commit (strict synchronous mode waits for it).
- **Clocks.** Every timer on a node, `degraded_since` display and damper window uses the monotonic clock, and every duration decided inside the metadata store (health rows, leases, trash, orphan age) uses the database clock (24.3); wall time on a node is used only for `written_ms` and logs, and never orders anything. Each node compares its wall clock with the database's `now()` returned at every transaction; a difference above `clock.max_skew` (default 1 s) alerts, because it would make `Last-Modified` and logs misleading, and changes no decision; a monotonic jump larger than the ping interval (a resume from suspend, a stalled VM) makes the node re-validate its view, and its reconciler lease (a database-clock fact) is simply not renewed.
- **Asymmetric partitions.** A drill (drill 12 of 24.13) breaks traffic in one direction between one pair of nodes, the gray failure a bad optic produces; the client-local `suspect` of 10.8, Patroni's lease with its watchdog (a primary that cannot renew is demoted before another is promoted) and the rule that a `missing` row needs the reconciler's own view or a second reporter (24.2) are what must hold.
- **Repair and scrub never stall a serving drive.** Background work yields on the drive's queue, not only by a byte budget: no background stream starts on a drive whose demand queue is non-empty or whose demand wait is above its baseline, at most one background stream per drive at a time (24.5), and a running one yields at the next chunk boundary (10.7).

**Deferred until there is evidence or a fourth node:** a durability budget with automatic freezes, a runbook workflow engine, placement spread across drive models, a separate deletion-referee daemon, long-horizon re-reads of sampled writes, and reference models beyond the hot segment append and seal and the shard write-fsync-rename-footer protocol.

- eStargz prioritized files and landmark, and Nydus prefetch table: the links above (6.1)
- Iyer and Druschel, "Anticipatory scheduling", SOSP 2001
- Li, Shen, Papathanasiou, EuroSys 2007: https://www.cs.rochester.edu/~kshen/papers/eurosys2007.pdf
- Schindler et al., "Track-aligned extents", FAST 2002
- Ruemmler and Wilkes, "An introduction to disk drive modeling", IEEE Computer 1994
- Thaler and Ravishankar, "Using name-based mappings to increase hit rates" (rendezvous hashing), IEEE/ACM ToN 1998 (used for the node rank of 10.2 and the drive choice of 10.4)

- Nydus: https://github.com/dragonflyoss/nydus/blob/master/docs/nydus-design.md ; eStargz: https://github.com/containerd/stargz-snapshotter/blob/main/docs/estargz.md

- Linux kernel FUSE documentation and `include/uapi/linux/fuse.h` of the farm kernel (pinned by T-F1)

**Storage tier: published systems this design reuses ("read" means the primary source was read).**
- Beaver et al., "Finding a needle in Haystack: Facebook's photo storage", OSDI 2010 (not re-read in that pass; facts via f4): the segment and needle format, in-memory index, append-only volumes (10.2, 10.3).
- Muralidhar et al., "f4: Facebook's warm BLOB storage system", OSDI 2014: https://www.usenix.org/system/files/conference/osdi14/osdi14-paper-muralidhar.pdf (read): sealing, 1 GB blocks, `n + k` placement invariant and balancer, range-granular degraded read, rebuilders, the journal-delete warning (10.3, 10.4, 10.13).
- Calder et al., "Windows Azure Storage", SOSP 2011 (via the LRC paper): seal an extent at about 1 GB, then erasure-code lazily and delete the replicas; hedged reconstruction on slow reads (10.5, 10.4).
- Huang et al., "Erasure Coding in Windows Azure Storage" (LRC), USENIX ATC 2012: https://www.usenix.org/system/files/conference/atc12/atc12-final181_0.pdf (read): why wide and local-group codes need many domains (10.4).
- Ghemawat, Gobioff, Leung, "The Google File System", SOSP 2003 (not re-read): checksums, scrub and prioritized re-replication (10.4).
- Google Cloud, "A peek behind Colossus, Google's file system" (2021): https://cloud.google.com/blog/products/storage-data-transfer/a-peek-behind-colossus-googles-file-system (read): redundancy chosen per file by the client library; custodians (10.4, 10.14).
- HDFS-7285 (erasure coding in HDFS) and HDFS-8030 (contiguous layout): https://issues.apache.org/jira/browse/HDFS-7285 (read): why striping exists and why contiguous wins when small objects are packed (10.4).
- Ceph erasure-code documentation (read): EC with overwrite is where the complexity lives; failure domain as a profile property (10.4, 10.14).
- Backblaze Vault architecture and Reed-Solomon posts: https://www.backblaze.com/blog/vault-cloud-storage-architecture/ (read): per-shard checksums, recompute-and-rewrite repair, parity as configuration (10.4).
- SeaweedFS: https://github.com/seaweedfs/seaweedfs at `97f49571` (source read) and `weed/storage/erasure_coding/ec_encoder.go` on the current main (re-read for this revision), its wiki (erasure coding, replication, tiered storage, Amazon S3 API), the releases page and issue 8073 (10.12).
- Ceph erasure-code and RGW storage-class documentation (read for 10.12; not run): https://docs.ceph.com/en/latest/rados/operations/erasure-code/ and https://docs.ceph.com/en/latest/radosgw/placement/
- Lamport, "Paxos Made Simple", 2001: the single-decree agreement that the design does not build and why (10.12).

**Metadata store: documentation the design relies on (documentation, not run here; T-S23 pins the released versions and checks each setting named in 10.16 against them).**
- PostgreSQL documentation, high availability and synchronous replication (`synchronous_commit`, `synchronous_standby_names`, replication slots, `max_slot_wal_keep_size`): https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/high-availability.html ; `pg_upgrade` (the major upgrade of R15) and `pg_rewind` (the old primary's rejoin of R11): https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/pgupgrade.html and https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/app-pgrewind.html (10.16, 24.12).
- Patroni documentation (`synchronous_mode`, `synchronous_mode_strict`, `ttl`, `loop_wait`, `retry_timeout`, watchdog support, switchover and reinit): https://patroni.readthedocs.io/ (10.16, R11 to R13, R16).
- etcd documentation (cluster sizing and quorum, member add and remove, disk and heartbeat tuning): https://etcd.io/docs/ (10.16, R12, R16).
- pgBackRest documentation (full, differential and incremental backups, WAL archiving, point-in-time restore, delta restore): https://pgbackrest.org/ (10.16, R14).
- Crates (checked on crates.io, 2026-11-17): `reed-solomon-simd` 3.1.0, `crc32c` 0.6.9, `blake3` 1.8.7, `xxhash-rust` 0.8.21, `io-uring` 0.7.16, `hyper` 1.12.0; `fjall` and `redb` (embedded ordered stores, MIT or Apache-2.0; versions pinned in T-S3); `tokio-postgres` (the driver of `tierstore-meta`, MIT or Apache-2.0; version pinned in T-S22); `ring` (sha256). Cross-check oracles in tests only: ISA-L and Go `klauspost/reedsolomon`.
